P·L DE ERUDITIONE HOMINIS INTERIORIS 196

Patrologia Latina · Vol. 196 ·

DE ERUDITIONE HOMINIS INTERIORIS

PL 196, coll. 1229–1366  ·  Latin from the Migne printing  ·  New English translation  ·  column numbers follow the original plates, not the Garnier reprint

Richard of Saint Victor

Richardus S. Victoris

d. 1173

Canon regular of the Abbey of Saint Victor in Paris — of British, probably Scottish, origin — and its prior from 1162. After Hugh, whose pupil he was, he was the leading theologian of the Victorine school: his De Trinitate reasoned toward the persons of God from the nature of perfect love, and his Benjamin minor and Benjamin major mapped the ascent to contemplation for generations of later readers.

LIBER PRIMUS.

BOOK ONE.

CAPUT PRIMUM. De mystico somnio regis Nabuchodonosor, et summa intentionis totius operis.

  • 1229D

Quid illud Nabuchodonosor somnium juxta litteram designet, potissima illa Danielis expositio docet. Sed displicere non debet, si tropologica discussio ex eadem materia eficiat quod ad veritatis scientiam, vel morum disciplinam lectorem aedificet. Ut ergo summam intentionis ejus paucis perstringam, 1230Dvidetur propheta hoc loco ex mystica visione designare quomodo viros virtutum contingat paulatim defluere, et quibusdam detrimentorum gradibus in ima corruere, et per visitantem gratiam quandoque ad pristinam, vel potius potiorem animi statum resurgere. Haec autem permutationis corruptela, vel reparationis medela accidere utique solet in utraque vita, tam activa videlicet quam contemplativa. Utriusque, ni fallor, in hac una visione typus exprimitur, 1231Asi recte pensetur. Quid enim est quod Nabuchodonosor mysticam visionem accepit, amisit, et postmodum iterum cumulatius agnovit. Nam ipsam cum ipsius interpretatione ad ultimum recipere meruit. Quid, inquam, in his omnibus nobis innuitur, nisi quod contemplationis gratia quandoque divinitus datur, interdum subtrahitur, tandemque multiplicius reparatur. Activae autem vitae detrimenta designantur in statua, quae inchoata ex auro desinit in testa, in summo aurea, in medio argentea, vel aerea, in imo ferrea vel testea. Ecce ut vides quanto inferior, tanto semper invenitur seipsa deterior. In varia permutatione metallorum signantur varia detrimenta regnorum, et in utrisque multiformis defectus morum, vel meritorum. 1231BSed ecce post multiplicem regnorum defectum, regnum illud suscitatur, quod non dissipabitur in aeternum. Sic saepe mens humana post multiplicem ruinam, divina inspiratione compuncta ad justitiam redit, et ex ipso suo casu erudita et humiliata, quanto eruditior quantoque humilior, tanto et fortior resurgit.

Illud quoque notandum quod contemplativae vitae gratia quam activae elabitur, sed multo difficilius reparatur. Hinc est quod Nabuchodonosor somnii visionem quam tam facile amisit, cum tanta difficultate recuperavit. Hinc est quod regnorum detrimentum latius describitur, et gradibus distinguitur, et inexterminabilis regni subintroductio 1231Cpaucis perstringitur, et subito quidem, et sine ulla degradatione subinfertur. Et forte quod de qualibet geminae vitae gratia dicitur, de utraque intelligi datur. Nam Scriptura sacra saepe unum dicit, et aliud innuit. Solet itaque vicissim utrumque contingere, et hominem videlicet in activa exercitatum, subito et inopinate cadere, et item a contemplativa dejectum per visitantem gratiam miranda facilitate resurgere. Sed congrue satis hoc loco, ubi de utriusque vitae lapsu vel reparatione agitur, congrue, inquam, utriusque proprietatem expressit, et qua sibi proportione respondeant ostendit. Nam a contemplativa facilius decidimus, et ad activam facilius resurgimus. Haec autem regis Nabuchodonosor visio (sicut et caetera ipsius) satis evidenter ostendit quam apte 1231Dtale nomen accepit. Nam somnium procul dubio quod vidit futurorum praesagium fuit. Et illud nihilominus quod per praesagium futurum praevidit rerum aliarum signum fuit: nam morum accidentia expressit. Vide ergo quam recte tale nomen sortitur. Nabuchodonosor quippe prophetans istiusmodi signum interpretatur. Nonne Nabuchodonosor per mysticam visionem prophetabat, unde et ejus visio prophetia erat, quae varios regnorum defectus portendebat? Quis autem nesciat quia omnia in figura contingebant illis? Ergo illa regnorum permutatio figura fuit quia aliud aliquid per mysterium significavit; et rerum spiritualium signum fuit. Signum itaque prophetabat, qui in futurorum praesagio rerum spiritualium figuras praevidebat. Sed quale signum? 1232ASignum istiusmodi, hoc est signum tale quod, quale, vel quam verum sit, potest experimento probari, et quasi ad oculum ostendi, vel signum istiusmodi quasi ejus rei quae potest digito ostendi. Si enim in detrimento regnorum morum detrimentum dicitur intelligi, quo, quaeso, in loco, vel tempore hoc non possit ostendi, hoc non possit videri. Et haec quidem illi nominis interpretatio satis congruit, secundum quod ei accidit. Nam est et alia non minus congrua secundum quod fecit: interpretatur namque Nabuchodonosor sessio in agnitione angustiae. Quid est sedere, nisi semetipsum humiliare? Attende autem quantum rex iste seipsum humiliavit, qui Danielem servum sibi ex captivitate addictum adoravit, quando regni sui dejectionem 1232B(quam sine angustia nosse non poterat) ipso exponente agnovit. In agnitione itaque angustiae sedit, qui audito imminenti periculo ad prophetantis se pedes humiliter prostravit. Haec autem quae dicta sunt de nominis interpretatione videntur historiae alludere, sed nihilominus et illud considerandum est quomodo valeant mysterio congruere. Quid enim aliud designat haec tanta persona quae pariter videtur esse rex, atque propheta, nisi animum gemina praeditum gratia et plene exercitatum tam vita activa quam contemplativa? Nonne tibi videtur animus humanus in se regiam quodammodo personam gerere, cum coeperit affectibus et passionibus (tum ex liberi arbitrii jure, tum ex gratiae collatione) fortiter praesidere, et cum auctoritate imperare, et 1232Cillud quod intra nos est regnum coelorum viriliter vendicare? Nonne tibi videtur dono quodam quasi prophetiae pollere, cum jam ex providentiae munere acceperit imminentia pericula posse praevidere atque praedicere, maxime autem cum jam coeperit per contemplationis gratiam mysteriorum profunda rimare, et per mentis excessum, et velut per somnium divinorum judiciorum arcana mirari? Recte autem et Nabuchodonosor dicitur qui praevidentiae dono donatur, et tunc veraciter qui ejusmodi est signum prophetat, cum ex causis praecedentibus morum detrimenta praevideat. Nam semper detrimenta meritorum designant detrimenta praemiorum. Absque dubio namque quantum detrahitur 1232Dmerito, tantum semper detrahitur et praemio. Prophetat igitur signum, et signum istiusmodi, qui ejusmodi signum vel ejus rei signum praedicit quae, qualia sint, possunt quodammodo digito ostendi, et experimento probari. Saepe autem defectus bonorum desideriorum praesignat defectum bonorum operum. Nam, torpente voluntate, necesse est animum postmodum a bono studio tepescere. Solet autem et e converso contingere. Cui vero datum est imminentia pericula, et mala adhuc futura posse praevidere, si curat et satagit cum dolore et animi angustia illa in considerationem adducere, et semetipsum in eorum exspectatione humiliare, potest ei idem nomen secundum aliam interpretationem congruere. Nam Nabuchodonosor, ut dictum est, interpretatur 1233Asessio in agnitione angustiae. Nemo autem miretur quod per regem iniquum animus virilis, et vir virtutum intelligi dicitur. Quis enim nesciat (nisi sacrarum litterarum imperitus) quam saepe in Scripturis significet Christum serpens animal venenosum, et leo animal ferocissimum? Et si exponenti beato Gregorio credimus, in David adultero per mysterium intelligitur Christus, et in innocente Uria intelligitur perfidus ille Judaicus populus. Quid igitur mirum si per regem iniquum quem improbamus, intelligimus rem justam quam approbamus? Et quidem in rege injusto quamvis fuerit voluntas inordinata, erat tamen ei potestas ordinata. Non est enim potestas nisi a Deo. Quae autem a Deo sunt, teste Apostolo, ordinata sunt (Rom. XIII). 1233BRecte itaque satis regia potestas virtutum animum designat, qui regnum illud internum moribus sibi vendicat, sive liberum arbitrium per quod ex cooperante gratia affectionibus et passionibus suis animus imperat.

CHAPTER ONE. On the mystical dream of King Nabuchodonosor, and the sum of the intention of the whole work.

  • 1229D

What that dream of Nabuchodonosor signifies according to the letter, that most weighty exposition of Daniel teaches. But it ought not to displease, if a tropological discussion should effect eficiat out of the same matter something that may build up the reader unto the knowledge of truth or unto the discipline of morals. That I may therefore touch in few words upon the sum of his intention: 1230Dthe prophet in this place seems, out of a mystical vision, to signify how it befalls men of virtues little by little to flow away, and by certain steps of decline to fall down into the lowest things, and at some time, through visiting grace, to rise again to their former, or rather to a stronger, state of spirit. Now this corruption of change, or this remedy of repair, is wont assuredly to happen in either life, that is, as well in the active as in the contemplative. Of both, unless I am mistaken, the type is expressed in this one vision, 1231Aif it be rightly weighed. For what is it that Nabuchodonosor received a mystical vision, lost it, and afterward again came to know it more abundantly. For he deserved at the last to receive it together with its own interpretation. What, I say, is hinted to us in all these things, unless that the grace of contemplation is at one time divinely given, at times withdrawn, and at last repaired more manifoldly. But the declines of the active life are signified in the statue, which, begun from gold, ends in clay: at the top golden, in the middle silver or bronze, at the bottom iron or of clay. Behold, as thou seest, by how much the lower, by so much is it always found worse than itself. In the varied change of the metals are marked the varied declines of kingdoms, and in both the manifold failure of morals or of merits. 1231BBut behold, after the manifold failure of the kingdoms, that kingdom is raised up which shall not be scattered for ever. So often the human mind, after manifold ruin, pricked with compunction by divine inspiration, returns to righteousness; and, instructed and humbled by its own very fall, by how much the more instructed and by how much the more humble, by so much the stronger also does it rise again.

This also is to be noted, that the grace of the contemplative life slips away than [quam; read facilius quam, "more easily than"] that of the active, but is repaired with much greater difficulty. Hence it is that Nabuchodonosor recovered with so great difficulty the vision of the dream which he had lost so easily. Hence it is that the decline of the kingdoms is described more at large, and is distinguished by steps, while the bringing in of the indestructible kingdom 1231Cis touched upon in few words, and is brought in suddenly indeed, and without any gradation. And perhaps what is said of either grace of the twofold life is given to be understood of both. For Holy Scripture often says one thing and hints another. And so both are wont by turns to befall: namely, that a man exercised in the active life should fall suddenly and unlooked for, and likewise that one cast down from the contemplative should rise again, through visiting grace, with wonderful ease. But fittingly enough in this place, where the fall or the repair of either life is treated, fittingly, I say, he has expressed the property of each, and has shown by what proportion they answer to one another. For from the contemplative we fall away more easily, and to the active we rise again more easily. Now this vision of King Nabuchodonosor (like the rest of his own) shows plainly enough how aptly 1231Dhe received such a name. For beyond doubt the dream which he saw was a foretoken of things to come. And that also which through the foretoken he foresaw as future was none the less a sign of other things: for it expressed the accidents of morals. See then how rightly he obtains such a name. For Nabuchodonosor is interpreted prophesying a sign of this sort. Was not Nabuchodonosor prophesying through the mystical vision, whence also his vision was a prophecy, which portended the varied failures of kingdoms? And who does not know that all things befell them in figure? Therefore that change of kingdoms was a figure, because it signified something else through a mystery; and it was a sign of spiritual things. And so he was prophesying a sign, who in the foretoken of things to come was foreseeing figures of spiritual things. But what kind of sign? 1232AA sign of this sort, that is, such a sign that what it is, or how true it is, can be proved by experience and shown as it were to the eye; or a sign of this sort, as it were of that thing which can be shown with the finger. For if in the decline of kingdoms the decline of morals is said to be understood, in what place, I ask, or at what time could this not be shown, could this not be seen. And this interpretation of the name indeed agrees with him well enough, according to what befell him. For there is another also, no less fitting, according to what he did: for Nabuchodonosor is interpreted a sitting in the acknowledgement of anguish. What is it to sit, unless to humble oneself? But consider how greatly this king humbled himself, who adored Daniel, a servant made over to him out of captivity, when he came to know the casting down of his kingdom 1232B(which he could not know without anguish) by that man's own exposition. And so he sat in the acknowledgement of anguish, who, when he had heard of the imminent peril, humbly prostrated himself at the feet of him that prophesied. Now these things which have been said concerning the interpretation of the name seem to allude to the history; but none the less this too is to be considered, how they may be able to agree with the mystery. For what else does this so great person signify, who seems to be at once king and prophet, unless a spirit endowed with a twofold grace and fully exercised as well in the active life as in the contemplative? Does not the human spirit seem to thee to bear in itself in some manner a kingly person, when it has begun to preside strongly over the affections and the passions (both by the right of free will and by the bestowal of grace), and to command with authority, and 1232Cmanfully to claim that kingdom of heaven which is within us? Does it not seem to thee to be strong with a certain gift as it were of prophecy, when now by the endowment of foresight it has received power to foresee and to foretell imminent perils; but most of all when it has now begun, through the grace of contemplation, to search into the deep things of the mysteries, and through excess of mind, and as it were through a dream, to marvel at the secrets of the divine judgments? And rightly is he also called Nabuchodonosor who is endowed with the gift of foresight; and then does he truly prophesy a sign of this kind, when from preceding causes he foresees the declines of morals. For the declines of merits always signify the declines of rewards. For beyond doubt, by how much is taken away from 1232Dmerit, by so much is always taken away from the reward also. He therefore prophesies a sign, and a sign of this sort, who foretells a sign of this kind, or a sign of that thing which — of what sort they be — can in some manner be shown with the finger and proved by experience. But often the failure of good desires foreshows the failure of good works. For, when the will grows torpid, the spirit must afterward grow lukewarm from good zeal. But it is wont to befall conversely also. But to him to whom it is given to be able to foresee imminent perils and evils yet to come, if he takes care and is busy to bring them with grief and anguish of spirit into consideration, and to humble himself in the expectation of them, the same name can fit him according to the other interpretation. For Nabuchodonosor, as has been said, is interpreted 1233Aa sitting in the acknowledgement of anguish. But let no one wonder that by an unjust king a manly spirit and a man of virtues is said to be understood. For who does not know (unless he be unskilled in the sacred letters) how often in the Scriptures the serpent, a venomous animal, and the lion, a most ferocious animal, signify Christ? And if we believe blessed Gregory expounding it, in David the adulterer Christ is understood through a mystery, and in innocent Uriah is understood that faithless Jewish people. What wonder therefore if by the unjust king whom we disapprove we understand a just thing which we approve? And indeed in the unjust king, although the will was disordered, yet the power belonging to him was ordered. For there is no power but from God. And the things which are from God, the Apostle bearing witness, are ordered (Rom. XIII). [Vulg. Rom. 13:1 quae autem sunt, a Deo ordinatae sunt, "the powers that are, are ordained by God"; Migne draws the agent into the relative clause and prints the neuter participle] 1233BAnd so fittingly enough the royal power signifies the spirit of virtues, which claims for itself that inward kingdom by its morals; or free will, through which, by cooperating grace, the spirit commands its own affections and passions.

CAPUT II. Quomodo contemplationis gratia quibusdam modo datur, modo subtrahitur, interimque amissa recuperatur.

In anno secundo regni Nabuchodonosor, vidit Nabuchodonosor somnium, et conterritus est spiritus ejus, et somnium ejus fugit ab eo (Dan. II). Primum studium virilis animi esse debet, ut possit dominari affectionibus; secundum ut possit imperare cogitationibus; tertium ergo animus spiritalia quaeque, 1233Cet vitiorum suorum victrix anima in regno suo extunc jam incipit habere, quando excessibus suis edomitis jam praevalet, non solum affectionibus, verum etiam cogitationibus imperare, ut utrumque possit et illas ab illicita concupiscentia, et istas ab inepta evagatione restringere. Quid est autem somnium videre et futurorum praesagia, seu occultorum mysteria praevidere, nisi gratiam contemplationis divinitus accipere, et per mentis excessum judiciorum divinorum cognitionem ex divina revelatione percipere? Recte autem anno secundo rex iste somnium vidit, quia spiritualis quisque post edomitam concupiscentiam cognitionumque disciplinam, tandem aliquando contemplationis studio vacare 1233Dpraevalet atque assuescit. Saepe autem fit ut, dum humanus animus ad divinorum judiciorum subtilitatem, seu etiam districtionem contemplandam ducitur, ex ea ipsa divinae districtionis consideratione quam percipit, subito timore medullitus concutiatur. Dum enim mens rudis, et coelestibus disciplinis minus erudita, de propria industria, magis quam de gratia divina, adhuc omnia praesumit, facile in hunc timorem ex divinae severitatis contemplatione, aequitatisque illius consideratione cadit. Praesumptuosa namque mens, dum miranda subtilitate conspicit, quam sit supra humanam virtutem divinis beneficiis respondere, et divina severitas pro aequitatis jure quam valeat, vel debeat erga nos districte agere, saepe in immensam formidinem 1234A(imo pene usque in desperationem) solet seipsam praecipitare. Sed dum trepidus animus hujusmodi pavore confunditur (ut taceam quam juste absque ulla dubitatione, tam misericorditer quam utiliter) sub hoc necessitatis articulo, contemplationis gratia subtrahitur, ne a subtilitatis suae nimietate in desperationis foveam totus praecipitetur. Somnium itaque quasi ab eo fugit, dum illam quam per mentis excessum videre consuevit contemplationis lucem amittit.

Praecepit autem rex, ut convocarentur arioli, et magi, et malefici, et Chaldaei, ut indicarent regi sommia sua. Regium profecto animum gerit, qui desideriis suis imperare, et studia, et exercitia sua pro deliberationis arbitrio disponere consuevit. 1234BEjusmodi itaque animus regius omnes sapientes Babylonis in unum convenire facit, et diversas quodammodo sectas in unum per scrutinium cogit, quando omnem sollicitudinem, et indagationem (quas per varia studia, et exercitia dispergere consuevit) uni, et soli coelestis doctrinae negotio impendit. Ab ejusmodi autem sapientibus, et quasi in unum coeuntibus, quasi somnia nostra amissa requirimus, quando subtracta contemplationis nostrae spectacula, per multiplicem et infatigabilem studiorum nostrorum instantiam reparare satagimus. Qui cum venissent, statuerunt coram rege. Ecce quid dicitur; ecce qualis convocatio coram rege stare perhibetur. Regium animum necdum te habere existimes, si subtilissima illa cogitationum tuarum 1234Cacumina undecunque pro voto colligere, et pro arbitrii libertate, cujuscunque intentionis studio immobiliter figere necdum vales. Et dixit ad eos rex: Vidi somnium, et mente confusus ignoro quid viderim. Sapientium nostrorum convocationem alloquimur, quando perscrutationum nostrarum evagationem in intimis increpamus, vel ad sublimium investigationem inflammamus. Studeamus autem juxta hujus similitudinem profectuum nostrorum damna non negligere, quin potius studia nostra ad eorum reparationem incitare.

Illud sane notandum quod superius dicit se esse conterritum, hic autem mente confusum. Sed sciendum quia mens humana, dum ad divinae aequitatis 1234Dardua limpidius contemplanda sublevatur ex infirmitatis suae reverberatione, sub uno eodemque tempore unde terretur, inde confunditur. Nam procul dubio in divinae justitiae consideratione, invaletudinisque humanae comparatione homo infirmitatem suam horrescit, pariter et erubescit. Responderuntque Chaldaei regi, Syriace: Rex, in sempiternum vive. Dic somnium servis tuis, et interpretationem ejus indicabimus. Primo loco considerandum cui et quomodo responderunt. Utique Chaldaico regi, Syriace quod quidem idem est, sicut Hieronymus super hunc eumdem locum diffinivit, quod Chaldaice. Quid itaque est regi in propria lingua respondere, nisi expresse et manifeste quod sciri oportet insinuare? Cum enim cuilibet aliquid in 1235Aaliena et ignota lingua dicitur, strepitus quidem vocis ad aures, sed sensus ejus usque ad animum non ingreditur. Quasi ergo in propria, et nota lingua respondetur, quando id, quod opportunitas exigit, convenienter intimatur. Quod si ad nominis interpretationem recurrimus, in eamdem proculdubio sententiam reducimur. Syria namque interpretatur sublimis. Quid itaque erit respondere Syriace, nisi sublimiter respondere? et quid erit respondere sublimiter, nisi respondere convenienter et sufficienter? Nihil autem melius, nil certius, nihilque sublimius animus agnoscit quam quod per experimentum didicit, et forte hic est proprius atque praecipuus, et omnino sublimissimus humanae animae discendi modus, quando aliquid proprio experimento 1235Bprobamus. Nonne tuae tibi cogitationes seu occupationes, tam proprie quam aperte respondent, et satis sublimiter ad veritatem erudiunt quando ex proprio defectu docent, et quasi per experientiam loquuntur, quam frustra vel temere aliquid supra humanum modum de propria industria praesumitur? Tota sane istorum responsio hoc maxime videtur continere (illud videlicet) quod rex violenter exigebat, omnino non posse. In ejusmodi ergo sapientibus, quales tibi superius ostendimus, hoc erit, ut opinor, eorum respondere suam impossibilitatem proprio experimento docere. Singula autem responsionis eorum pertractemus, et primum de primo videamus.

CHAPTER II. How the grace of contemplation is to some at one time given, at another withdrawn, and meanwhile, when lost, recovered.

In the second year of the reign of Nabuchodonosor, Nabuchodonosor saw a dream, and his spirit was terrified, and his dream fled from him (Dan. II). The first zeal of a manly spirit ought to be that it may have dominion over the affections; the second, that it may command the thoughts; the third, therefore — the spirit, and the soul that is victor over its own vices, 1233Cbegins from that time already to have in its own kingdom all spiritual things whatsoever, when, its excesses being now subdued, it prevails to command not only the affections but also the thoughts, so that it may be able to restrain both, the former from unlawful concupiscence and the latter from unfitting wandering. But what is it to see a dream, and to foresee the foretokens of things to come or the mysteries of hidden things, unless to receive divinely the grace of contemplation, and through excess of mind to perceive out of divine revelation the knowledge of the divine judgments? And rightly did this king see the dream in the second year, because every spiritual man, after the taming of concupiscence and the discipline of his knowings, at length at some time prevails and grows accustomed to be at leisure for the zeal of contemplation. 1233DBut it often comes to pass that, while the human spirit is led to contemplate the subtlety, or even the strictness, of the divine judgments, it is shaken to the marrow with a sudden fear out of that very consideration of the divine strictness which it perceives. For while a mind that is untrained, and less instructed in heavenly disciplines, still presumes all things upon its own effort rather than upon divine grace, it easily falls into this fear from the contemplation of the divine severity and the consideration of that equity. For a presumptuous mind, when with wondrous subtlety it beholds how far it is above human virtue to answer to the divine benefits, and how strictly the divine severity, by the right of equity, may and ought to deal with us, is often wont to hurl itself into an immense dread 1234A(nay, almost even into despair). But while the trembling spirit is confounded with a dread of this sort (to say nothing of how justly, beyond all doubt, and as mercifully as profitably), under this crisis of necessity the grace of contemplation is withdrawn, lest by the excess of its own subtlety it be hurled wholly into the pit of despair. And so the dream as it were flees from him, while he loses that light of contemplation which he was wont to see through excess of mind.

But the king commanded that the soothsayers, and the magicians, and the sorcerers, and the Chaldeans should be called together, that they might declare to the king his dreams sommia. He assuredly bears a kingly spirit who is wont to command his own desires, and to dispose his zeals and his exercises according to the judgment of deliberation. 1234BAnd so a kingly spirit of this sort makes all the wise men of Babylon come together into one, and in a manner drives diverse sects into one by scrutiny, when it spends upon the one and only business of heavenly doctrine all that solicitude and searching which it was wont to scatter through varied zeals and exercises. But from wise men of this sort, coming together as it were into one, we seek back as it were our lost dreams, when we busy ourselves to repair, by the manifold and untiring urgency of our zeals, the spectacles of our contemplation that have been withdrawn. Who, when they had come, set up before the king. [Vulg. Dan. 2:2 steterunt, "they stood"; the word Migne prints is transitive and leaves the clause without an object] Behold what is said; behold what manner of assembly is declared to stand before the king. Thou mayest reckon that thou hast not yet a kingly spirit, if thou art not yet able to gather from every side, at thy wish, those most subtle points of thy thoughts, 1234Cand, by the liberty of thy judgment, to fix them unmovably upon the zeal of whatever intention thou wilt. And the king said to them: I saw a dream, and being confounded in mind I know not what I saw. We address the assembly of our wise men when in our inmost parts we rebuke the wandering of our searchings, or inflame it to the investigation of sublime things. But let us be zealous, after the likeness of this man, not to neglect the losses of our advances, but rather to stir up our zeals unto their repair.

This surely is to be noted, that above he says that he was terrified, but here that he was confounded in mind. But it is to be known that the human mind, while it is lifted up to contemplate more clearly the steep things of the divine equity, 1234Dis, by the beating back of its own infirmity, at one and the same time confounded from that same source whence it is terrified. For beyond doubt, in the consideration of the divine justice and in the comparison of human weakness, a man shudders at his own infirmity, and equally blushes at it. And the Chaldeans answered the king in Syriac: O king, live for ever. Tell the dream to thy servants, and we will declare the interpretation thereof. In the first place it must be considered to whom and in what manner they answered. Namely, to a Chaldean king, in Syriac — which indeed is the same thing, as Jerome has determined upon this same place, as in Chaldee. What then is it to answer the king in his own tongue, unless expressly and manifestly to convey that which it behooves to be known? For when anything is said to a man in 1235Aa foreign and unknown tongue, the noise of the voice indeed comes to the ears, but the sense of it does not enter as far as the spirit. Therefore answer is made as it were in one's own and known tongue, when that which the occasion demands is fittingly made known. But if we have recourse to the interpretation of the name, we are beyond doubt brought back to the same meaning. For Syria is interpreted sublime. What then will it be to answer in Syriac, unless to answer sublimely? and what will it be to answer sublimely, unless to answer fittingly and sufficiently? But the spirit knows nothing better, nothing more certain, and nothing more sublime than what it has learned through experience; and perhaps this is the proper and chief, and altogether the most sublime, manner of learning belonging to the human soul, when by our own experience 1235Bwe prove something. Do not thy own thoughts or occupations answer thee as properly as openly, and instruct thee unto the truth sublimely enough, when out of their own failure they teach, and as it were through experience declare, how vainly and how rashly anything above the human measure is presumed upon one's own effort? Surely the whole answer of these men seems chiefly to contain this (namely, that) they were altogether unable to do what the king was violently demanding. In wise men of this sort therefore, such as we have showed thee above, this will be, as I judge, their answering: to teach their own impossibility by their own experience. But let us handle the several parts of their answer, and first let us see concerning the first.

CAPUT III. Quod in omne quod agimus, intentioni bonae studere et gratiae divinae inniti debeamus.

  • 1235C

Rex, inquiunt, in sempiternum vive (Dan. II). Ut itaque ad invicem conferamus exteriora cum interioribus, corporalia cum spiritualibus, utinam internam illam familiam, tam disciplinatam habeamus quam disciplinatam, istam exteriorem agnovimus. Audivimus quid prima fronte suo regi respondeant, et forte hoc ipsum eos ipse docuerat, doceamus et nostram (in omni videlicet nostro conamine) simillima respondere: Rex, in sempiternum vive. Quid tamen hoc erit, quaeso, exoptatio, an admonitio? Exoptatio, ut hoc valeat, an admonitio, ut in hoc ipsum omne studium impendat? Quae est autem 1235Dvita illius interni principis, et internae illius familiae dominatoris? Quae, inquam, est illius principalis in anima (humanae videlicet mentis) vita, nisi intentio bona? Quidquid homo agat, etiamsi bonum sit, inutile tamen erit, nisi bona intentione fiat. Tunc autem mens hominis etiam inter bona opera moritur, quando intentio depravatur. Veritatis attestatio est: Quia, si oculus tuus fuerit nequam, totum corpus tuum tenebrosum erit (Matth. VI). Quid igitur erit in sempiternum vivere, nisi in omni opere bono intentionem bonam habere? Utinam ad omnem conatum nostrum, ad omne incoeptum bonum, haec nobis responsio primo occurrat, in hoc omnis nostra cogitatio suspiret, et satagat, ut in aeternum vivamus, quod tunc quidem fiet, 1236Asi nihil nisi bonum, et sine bona intentione agamus. Ut enim de caeteris taceamus, o quoties ipsam veritatem, non pro veritate, sed pro vanitate quaerimus, et inventam veritatem, non in veritate, sed ad vanitatem diligimus, et quod miserrimum est, in verbis vitae ad mortis lucra negotiamur, vel certe interioris hominis vitam recte intelligimus gratiam divinam! Sicut enim corpus nihil potest sine vita sua, id est sine anima, sic interior homo noster nihil boni potest sine gratia divina: Gratia, Dei, inquit, apostolus, sum id quod sum (Cor. XV). Et Veritas ipsa de se: Sine me nihil potestis facere (Joan. XV). Quid ergo est dicere, in sempiternum vivere, nisi indesinenter gratiae adhaerere? Quidquid disponas non de tua industria, sed de divina gratia 1236Bpraesumas. Quidquid peragas, illi, non tibi ascribas hoc utique regi illi, et eo maxime tempore dicendum erat, quod illud quod divinitus acceperat humanitus quaerebat, et ad aliud quod sperabatur, humanae potius industriae, quam divinae gratiae innitebatur. Sed illud est valde mirum (imo supra humanum modum stupendum) quod saepe cum haec apud nosmetipsos tacite loquimur, et intime sentimus, nihilominus tamen, et sub eodem tempore propriae industriae innitimur, ac si cooperantem gratiam penitus ignoremus. Saepe cum de divinae gratiae commendatione, subtiliter aliquid apud proximos loquimur de nostrae locutionis subtilitate apud nosmetipsos gloriamur. O infinita hominis stultitia, 1236Cet recte quidem dicis hominem nil posse sine gratia? Sed si veritatem loqueris, unde gloriaris? Doceamus itaque occultorum perscrutatrices subtilissimas, illas videlicet nostras cogitationes, ut in omni nostro studio, in omni negotio hoc primum, hoc praecipue satagant, hanc nobis sollicitudinem primo ingenerant, ut studeamus totum quod proponimus de gratia praesumere, totum quod efficimus gratiae divinae ascribere, et hoc pertinet ad sempiternum vivere.

CHAPTER III. That in all that we do we ought to be zealous for a good intention and to lean upon divine grace.

  • 1235C

O king, they say, live for ever (Dan. II). That we may therefore compare together outward things with inward, bodily with spiritual: would that we had that inward household as well disciplined as we have known this outward one to be disciplined. We have heard what they answer at the first to their king, and perhaps he himself had taught them this very thing; let us also teach our own household (namely, in every endeavour of ours) to answer things most like: O king, live for ever. Yet what will this be, I ask, a wish, or an admonition? A wish, that he may avail for this; or an admonition, that he may spend all zeal upon this very thing? But what is 1235Dthe life of that inward prince, and lord of that inward household? What, I say, is the life of that which is chief in the soul (namely, of the human mind), unless a good intention? Whatever a man may do, even if it be good, yet it will be unprofitable unless it be done with a good intention. But then the mind of a man dies even amid good works, when the intention is corrupted. It is the Truth's own attestation: Because, if thine eye be evil, thy whole body shall be darksome (Matth. VI). What therefore will it be to live for ever, unless to have a good intention in every good work? Would that at every endeavour of ours, at every good beginning, this answer might first meet us; that all our thought might sigh and busy itself for this, that we may live for ever, which indeed will then come to pass, 1236Aif we do nothing but what is good, and nothing without a good intention. For, to say nothing of the rest: oh, how often do we seek the truth itself, not for truth but for vanity, and the truth once found we love, not in truth, but unto vanity; and, what is most wretched, in the words of life we traffic for the gains of death; or at any rate we rightly understand the life of the inner man to be divine grace! For as the body can do nothing without its own life, that is, without the soul, so our inner man can do no good thing without divine grace: By the grace, of God, says the apostle, I am what I am (Cor. XV). And the Truth himself concerning himself: Without me you can do nothing (Joan. XV). What therefore is it to say, to live for ever, unless unceasingly to cleave to grace? Whatever thou mayest purpose, presume not of thine own effort, but of divine grace. 1236BWhatever thou accomplishest, ascribe to him, not to thyself this assuredly was to be said to that king, and at that time above all, because he was seeking in a human way that which he had received divinely, and for the other thing that was hoped for was leaning upon human effort rather than upon divine grace. But this is very marvellous (nay, astonishing beyond the human measure), that often, when we say these things silently within ourselves and feel them inwardly, none the less, and at the very same time, we lean upon our own effort, as if we were wholly ignorant of cooperating grace. Often, when we say something subtly to our neighbours concerning the commendation of divine grace, we glory within ourselves over the subtlety of our own speaking. O infinite folly of man, 1236Cand dost thou indeed rightly say that a man can do nothing without grace? But if thou speakest the truth, whence dost thou glory? Let us therefore teach those most subtle searchers-out of hidden things, namely, those thoughts of ours, that in all our zeal, in every business, they busy themselves first and chiefly with this, that they first beget in us this solicitude: that we be zealous to presume of grace all that we propose, and to ascribe to divine grace all that we accomplish; and this belongs to living for ever.

CAPUT IV. Quomodo secundum varios studiorum suorum successus, quidam se, vel enerviter dejiciunt, vel inaniter extollunt.

Dic, inquit, somnium servis tuis, et interpretationem 1236Dejus indicabimus (Dan, II). Attende quid dicunt. Unum exigunt, et aliud promittunt. Exigunt a rege visionem, et ejus pollicentur interpretationem. Et tamen quam vane exigunt, tam falso promittunt. Unum autem erat quod veraciter dicere possent, si vera confiteri voluissent, visionem videlicet minime nosse, nec per se scire penitus potuisse. Qui ergo falsa profitentur, cur saltem vera non confitentur? Communis pudentia generalis impudentia. Communis pudentia impossibilitatem suam celare, generalis impudentia, ultra possibilitatem praesumere. Et respondens rex, ait Chaldaeis: Sermo recessit a me. Nisi indicaveritis mihi somnium, et conjecturam ejus, peribitis vos et domus vestrae publicabuntur. Si autem somnium, et conjecturam 1237Aejus narraveritis, praemia, et dona, et honorem multum a me accipietis. Quis, quaeso, regem, Nabuchodonosor libere non reprehendat, quod sapientes suos pro tali causa perditioni addicat, cum forte et ipse simile, vel pejora faciat? Quid enim est sapientes suos ultro perdere, nisi sapientiae studia penitus omittere, et subtilissima illa ingenii sui acumina in doctrinae alicujus investigatione ulterius exercere jam nolle? Quid autem est sapientium domos publicare, et ad communem omnium usum redigere? Domos autem, illum habitationis nostrae locum dicere consuevimus, ad quem semper post multiplices labores atque discursus quiescendi gratia recurrere solemus. Quid igitur per sapientium domum rectius datur intelligere 1237Bquam privata illa atque secreta quietis suae otia quibus solent studiosi quique doctrinae studiis vacare? Sapientium igitur nostrorum domus, quodammodo publicantur, quando studiosa illa quietis nostrae otia, ad omnem dissolutionis evagationem exponuntur, doctrinaeque secreta penetralia ad omnem curam vel curiositatem prostituuntur. Quam multos videmus post praeclara studiorum suorum insignia, scientiaeque magnalia, cum forte ad qualemcunque honoris gradum promoti fuerint, vel forte administrationis cujuscunque curam susceperint, omnem statim doctrinarum suarum disciplinam despicere, saecularibus negotiis solum semperque vacare, cunctos sapientiae cultores sub uno die perdere, eorumque abdita secreta publicare? 1237CQuid isti facerent si Nabuchodonosor regnum acciperent? Quam multos itidem videmus, post multum spiritualium exercitiorum instantiam, post acceptam denique contemplationis gratiam, cum forte ab illo sublimitatis suae statu aliqua tentatione, vel qualicunque fatigatione dejecti fuerint, et ad illam celsitudinis suae artem sine aliquo, vel forte sine magno labore redire non posse perspexerint, omnem protinus spiritualium studiorum industriam deponere, vel damnare, et seipsos sub omni hora ad omnes vagos, vanosque discursus exponere. Videas illos non solum illa contemplationis suae otia non repetere, verum etiam omnem meditationis, seu lectionis instantiam penitus omittere. Quid 1237Daliud isti quam sapientes suos occidunt, qui omnes spiritales intellectus in semetipsis varia desideriorum suorum fluctuatione obducunt? Ecce audivimus de minis, videamus de promissis. Si autem somnium, inquit, et conjecturam ejus narraveritis, praemia et dona, et honorem multum accipietis a me. Praemium est quando illud accipimus quod meruimus; donum, quando illud accipimus, quod necdum meruimus; honor autem quando et illud obtinemus, ad quod promerendum minus idonei sumus. Praemium itaque est quod meritis redditur, donum quod gratis impenditur, honor quod ex sola dignitate confertur: praemia, inquit, et dona, et honorem multum accipietis a me. Nihil, fratres, verius: quam libenter sapientibus nostris stipendia meritorum 1238Aimpendimus, quam gratanter eis honorem debitum vel gratuitum conferimus? Et ut inde quid sentiam plenius absolvam, nihil certius exspecto, nihil amplius timeo quam ut nimium honorem et majorem reverentiam quam oporteat ejusmodi sapientibus impendi, quisque satagat, cum forte suis desideriis satisfecerint, et votis suis pro voto responderint. Quis suis studiis et disciplinis non amplius ascribat, quam ipsa Veritas dictat? Quis, quaeso, opinionem scientiae et sapientiae suae, non solum, juxta aequitatis modum, sed et praeter, imo supra aequitatis debitum nolit extendere? Quoties itaque animus studiis suis libenter insistit, et inde sibi famae et gloriae dilatationem promittit, loquitur quodammodo sapientibus suis, et praemia, et dona; 1238Bet honorem multum accipietis.

CHAPTER IV. How, according to the varied successes of their zeals, some either feebly cast themselves down or emptily exalt themselves.

Tell, says he, the dream to thy servants, and we will declare the interpretation 1236Dthereof (Dan, II). Mark what they say. One thing they demand, and another they promise. They demand of the king the vision, and they promise its interpretation. And yet as vainly as they demand, so falsely do they promise. But there was one thing which they might have said truly, if they had been willing to confess the truth: namely, that they in no wise knew the vision, nor could of themselves have known it at all. They therefore who profess false things, why do they not at least confess true ones? A common modesty; a general shamelessness. It is common modesty to conceal one's own impossibility; it is general shamelessness to presume beyond possibility. And the king answering said to the Chaldeans: The word has departed from me. Unless you declare to me the dream and the conjecture of it, you shall perish, and your houses shall be made public. But if you shall relate the dream and the conjecture 1237Aof it, you shall receive from me rewards, and gifts, and much honour. Who, I ask, would not freely rebuke King Nabuchodonosor, that for such a cause he consigns his wise men to destruction, when perhaps he himself does the like, or worse? For what is it to destroy one's own wise men of one's own accord, unless utterly to lay aside the zeals of wisdom, and to be now unwilling to exercise those most subtle points of one's wit any further in the investigation of some doctrine? But what is it to make public the houses of the wise men, and to reduce them to the common use of all? Now by houses we are wont to mean that place of our dwelling to which, after manifold labours and runnings to and fro, we are always accustomed to return for the sake of rest. What therefore is more rightly given to be understood by the house of the wise men 1237Bthan those private and secret leisures of their rest, wherein all the studious are wont to be free for the zeals of doctrine? The houses of our wise men therefore are in a manner made public, when those studious leisures of our rest are exposed to every wandering of dissoluteness, and the secret inner chambers of doctrine are prostituted to every care or curiosity. How many do we see, after the glorious marks of their zeals and the great things of their knowledge, when perchance they have been promoted to some step of honour, or perchance have taken up the care of some administration, straightway despise all the discipline of their doctrines, be free for secular affairs only and always, destroy in one day all the cultivators of wisdom, and make public their hidden secrets? 1237CWhat would these men do if they received the kingdom of Nabuchodonosor? How many likewise do we see, after much urgency of spiritual exercises, after having at last received the grace of contemplation, when perchance they have been cast down from that state of their sublimity by some temptation or by weariness of whatever kind, and have perceived that they cannot return to that art of their height without some, or perchance without great, labour, straightway lay down or condemn all the effort of spiritual zeals, and expose themselves at every hour to all vagrant and empty runnings to and fro. Thou mayest see them not only not seek again those leisures of their contemplation, but even utterly lay aside all urgency of meditation or of reading. What 1237Delse do these men do than slay their own wise men, who overspread all spiritual understandings within themselves by the varied fluctuation of their own desires? Behold, we have heard concerning the threats; let us see concerning the promises. But if the dream, says he, and the conjecture of it you shall relate, you shall receive rewards and gifts, and much honour from me. A reward is when we receive that which we have deserved; a gift, when we receive that which we have not yet deserved; but an honour, when we obtain even that for the deserving of which we are less fit. And so a reward is what is rendered to merits; a gift, what is bestowed freely; an honour, what is conferred from dignity alone: rewards, says he, and gifts, and much honour you shall receive from me. Nothing, brethren, is truer: how gladly do we bestow upon our wise men the wages of their merits, 1238Ahow thankfully do we confer upon them honour due or gratuitous? And that I may more fully declare what I think thereon: I look for nothing more certain, I fear nothing more, than that every man should busy himself that too much honour, and greater reverence than is fitting, be bestowed upon wise men of this sort, when perchance they have satisfied his desires and have answered his wishes according to his wish. Who would not ascribe more to his own zeals and disciplines than the Truth itself dictates? Who, I ask, would not wish to extend the reputation of his own knowledge and wisdom, not only according to the measure of equity, but even beyond, nay above, what equity owes? And so as often as the spirit gladly presses on in its own zeals, and thence promises itself an enlargement of fame and glory, it speaks in a manner to its own wise men: both rewards, and gifts; 1238Band much honour you shall receive.

CAPUT V. Quam multis modis animus seipsum decipit, et suum sibi vitium non invitus abscondit.

Proposito praemio, statim rex subjungit, et dicit: Somnium igitur, et interpretationem ejus indicate mihi (Dan. II). Duo quaerit, somnium, et interpretationem ejus. Aliud est divinorum judiciorum arcana ex divina revelatione cognoscere, et aliud est eorumdem rationem perpendere vel penetrare potuisse. Hoc ad somnii cognitionem, illud pertinet ad ejus interpretationem. Quam multa divinorum judiciorum cognoscimus, quorum rationem nulla nostra discussione penetramus. Responderunt secundo, 1238Catque dixerunt: Rex somnium servis suis dicat, et interpretationem ejus indicabimus. Quid est iterum respondere, nisi impossibilitatem suam iterato conanimis sui defectu plenius perfectiusque docere? O quam difficile humana impudentia, a sua praesumptione reprimitur. Ecce post plura experimenta adhuc impossibilia exiguntur, adhuc impossibilia promittuntur. Sed videamus quid sequitur: Respondit rex, et ait: Certe novi quia tempus redimitis, scientes quod recesserit a me sermo. Si igitur somnium non indicaveritis mihi, una est de vobis sententia, quod interpretationem quoque fallacem et deceptione plenam composueritis, ut loquamini mihi donec tempus pertranseat. Somnium itaque dicite mihi ut sciam, quod interpretationem 1238Dquoque ejus veram loquamini. Ecce ex sua instantia rex non nihil profecit, qui tandem aliquando eorum duplicitatem deprehendit, et te quidem non putes parum profecisse, cum cordis tui duplicitatem poteris ad liquidum deprehendere. Quam multa saepe simulamus, quam multa dissimulamus. Infirma nostra libenter dissimulamus, et illa quae omnino non possumus, posse vel habere nos bona simulamus: et mirum valde quod libenter animus seipsum decipit, et suum sibi vitium non invitus abscondit. Ecce utrumque vitium simulationis et dissimulationis rex in suis subjectis deprehendit, et dissimulationis quidem in eo quod dicit: Certe novi quia tempus redimitis. Simulationis in eo quod post pauca adjungit, una est de vobis sententia, quod interpretationem 1239Aquoque fallacem composueritis. Ignorantiam suam dissimulabant, et aperte fateri volebant, et in hoc tempus redimebant, et tamen de somnii interpretatione falsa promittebant. Sed dissimulationis malum in eis rex experiendo probavit, simulationis vero malum ex ratione cognovit, dum unum vitium ex alio collegit. Si enim nec illud poterant, quod minus erat, quomodo illud potuissent quo majus erat? Perpendis ex his, ut opinor, et animadvertis, quod alia in nobis experimento probamus, et alia utique ex ratione colligimus. Responderunt ergo Chaldaei coram rege, et dixerunt: Non est homo super terram, qui sermonem tuum, rex, possit implere. Ecce jam tertio respondent, et tandem aliquando defectum suum manifeste docent. Et ut 1239Btres responsionis vicissitudines gradibus distinguamus, vide ne forte prima illa responsio sit dubitationis, secunda timoris, tertia desperationis. Et ad primum quidem conatum quando aliquid incipimus, nec tamen perficimus, incipimus saepe de incoepti nostri consummatione dubitare. Ad secundum autem amplius formidare. Ad tertium vero conatum, cum nullum consequimur effectum, solemus penitus desperare.

Et nota quod dubitatio et formido habent responsionem ambiguam, desperatio autem habet responsionem manifestam. Sic utique illi primo et secundo ignorantiam suam magis minuunt quam loquuntur. Ad tertiam autem, lucem jam palam profitentur. Audiamus tamen quam falso vera loquantur: Non 1239Cest, inquiunt, homo super terram, qui sermonem tuum possit implere. Non dicunt simpliciter, nos non possumus, quod utique verum erat, sed non est homo super terram qui possit, quod absque dubio falsum erat. Nam et Daniel homo erat, et super terram erat, qui hoc potuit, sicut postea rei exitus probavit. Potuit tamen non ex propria industria, sed ex gratia divina. Quis eum dicat non posse quod orando potuit obtinere? Vide quot modis, et quam saepe seipsum animus humanus decipiat, sed quis hoc plene explicare sufficiat? Quam multa saepe nos posse praesumimus quae tamen omnino non possumus, et quam multa quae omnino non possumus neminem posse putamus? Quid, quaeso, est 1239Dquod animus humanus secundum bonum quod in se probat alios omnino non praedicat, et tamen secundum malum quod in seipso invenit, alios adjicit? Ecce sapientes isti quod de somnii interpretatione praesumebant, non tamen et aeque omnibus in commune attribuebant. Verumtamen quod de somnii recuperatione non poterant, neminem posse confirmant. Sed ut totum hoc secundum unum eumdemque animum consideremus, nihil sane in nobis debemus vel temere praesumere, vel temere desperare. Nam saepe quod uno modo non possumus, alio modo facile explicamus. Quam multa saepe quae non possumus actitando, obtinemus orando.

CHAPTER V. In how many ways the spirit deceives itself, and not unwillingly hides its own vice from itself.

The reward being set forth, the king straightway subjoins and says: Tell me therefore the dream and the interpretation of it (Dan. II). Two things he seeks: the dream, and the interpretation of it. It is one thing to know the secrets of the divine judgments out of divine revelation, and another to have been able to weigh or to penetrate the reason of the same. This belongs to the knowledge of the dream, that to the interpretation of it. How many of the divine judgments do we know, whose reason we penetrate by no discussion of ours. They answered a second time 1238Cand said: Let the king tell the dream to his servants, and we will declare the interpretation of it. What is it to answer again, unless to teach their own impossibility more fully and more perfectly by the repeated failure of their own conanimis? O how hardly is human shamelessness restrained from its own presumption. Behold, after many experiences, still impossible things are demanded, still impossible things are promised. But let us see what follows: The king answered and said: I know for certain that you buy up the time, knowing that the word has departed from me. If therefore you shall not declare to me the dream, there is one sentence concerning you, that you have framed an interpretation also that is deceitful and full of deception, that you may speak to me until the time pass away. Tell me therefore the dream, that I may know that you speak a true interpretation 1238Dof it also. Behold, from his urgency the king profited not nothing, who at length at some time detected their duplicity; and do not thou think that thou hast profited but little, when thou shalt be able clearly to detect the duplicity of thine own heart. How many things do we often feign, how many do we dissemble. Our weaknesses we gladly dissemble, and those good things which we are altogether unable to do we feign that we can do or have: and it is very marvellous that the spirit gladly deceives itself, and not unwillingly hides its own vice from itself. Behold, the king detected both vices, of simulation and of dissimulation, in his subjects: that of dissimulation indeed in that he says, I know for certain that you buy up the time. That of simulation, in that after a few words he adds, There is one sentence concerning you, that you have framed a deceitful interpretation 1239Aalso. They dissembled their own ignorance, and were willing [volebant; read nolebant, "were unwilling"] to confess it openly, and in this they were buying up the time, and yet concerning the interpretation of the dream they were promising false things. But the evil of dissimulation in them the king proved by experience, while the evil of simulation he knew from reason, gathering the one vice out of the other. For if they could not do even that which was the less, how could they have done that whereby it was the greater? From these things, as I judge, thou weighest and observest that some things in us we prove by experience, and others assuredly we gather from reason. The Chaldeans therefore answered before the king and said: There is not a man upon earth that can fulfil thy word, O king. Behold, now a third time they answer, and at length at some time they manifestly teach their own failure. And that 1239Bwe may distinguish by steps the three alternations of the answer, see whether perchance that first answer be of doubt, the second of fear, the third of despair. And at the first endeavour indeed, when we begin something and yet do not finish it, we often begin to doubt of the consummation of what we have begun. But at the second, to dread the more. But at the third endeavour, when we obtain no effect, we are wont utterly to despair.

And note that doubt and dread have an ambiguous answer, but despair has a manifest answer. So assuredly at the first and the second those men rather lessen their own ignorance than speak it. But at the third, they now openly profess the light. Yet let us hear how falsely they speak true things: There 1239Cis not, say they, a man upon earth that can fulfil thy word. They do not say simply, We cannot, which assuredly was true, but, There is not a man upon earth that can, which beyond doubt was false. For Daniel also was a man, and was upon earth, who was able to do this, as the outcome of the matter afterward proved. Yet he was able, not of his own effort, but of divine grace. Who would say that he could not do what he was able to obtain by praying? See in how many ways, and how often, the human spirit deceives itself; but who would suffice to explain this fully? How many things do we often presume that we can do, which yet we altogether cannot; and how many things which we altogether cannot do, do we think that no one can? What is it, I ask, 1239Dthat the human spirit, according to the good which it approves in itself, does not at all proclaim others, and yet, according to the evil which it finds in itself, adds others? Behold, what these wise men presumed concerning the interpretation of the dream, they did not yet attribute equally to all in common. But nevertheless, what they could not do concerning the recovery of the dream, they affirm that no one can do. But that we may consider all this according to one and the same spirit: surely we ought neither rashly to presume anything in ourselves, nor rashly to despair. For often what we cannot do in one way, we easily accomplish in another. How many things which we often cannot get by doing, we obtain by praying.

CAPUT VI. Quam temere, vel frustra ex humana industria praesumimus quod ex angelica solum, seu divina revelatione exspectare debemus.

  • 1240A

Videamus tamen quid isti adhuc addant, subtiliter discutientes quid vitiose, vel quid forte veraciter dicant. Nam et incaute prolata ad nostram cautelam valent, et recte prolata ad imitationis studium informare habent. Sed neque regum quisquam, inquiunt, magnus, et potens verbum hujusmodi sciscitatur ab omni ariolo, et mago, et Chaldaeo (Dan. II). Rex recte dicitur qui ad regnum sublimatur. Sed quid ait rex ille, vere Rex regum et Dominus dominantium? Regnum caelorum, inquit, intra vos est (Luc. XVII). Ad hoc sane regnum sublimari possumus, 1240Bsi perfecte id volumus. Habet sane hoc regnum intra nos existens incolas multos et habitatores infinitos. Quanto autem quisque ejusmodi regnum amplius dilatat, fidelius dispensat, districtius regit, prudentius disponit, tanto potentior efficitur, tanto major judicatur. Ad hoc autem regnum magis provehit atque conducit paupertas spiritus quam copia sensus: Beati enim pauperes spiritu, quoniam ipsorum est regnum coelorum (Matth. V). Qui ejusmodi regnum accipit fiducialiter psallit, cum dicit: Ego autem constitutus sum rex ab eo (Psal. II). Bene autem de ejusmodi regibus dicitur. Sed neque regum quisquam magnus, et potens verbum hujuscemodi sciscitatur ab omni ariolo, et mago, et Chaldaeo. In hoc autem dicti reges magni sunt, et suam sibi 1240Cpotentiam defendunt, quod nihil de propria virtute praesumunt, quanto minus ea quae supra hominem sunt? Certe illud verbum rex divinitus acceperat, et omnino tale erat quod nisi ex Dei revelatione sciri non poterat. Quod ergo sciri non poterat nisi a Deo, qua temeritate praesumitur ab homine ariolo, mago, vel Chaldaeo? Quid igitur est hujuscemodi verbum ab ejusmodi quovis sapiente sciscitari nolle, nec debere, nisi ea quae supra hominem sunt ex nulla humana industria, vel arte debere praesumere? Hinc est quod additur, cum dicitur: Sermo enim quem tu, rex, quaeris gravis est, nec reperietur quisquam qui indicet illum in conspectu regis, exceptis diis quorum non est cum hominibus conversatio. Certe 1240Dquod supra hominem erat, rex ab homine exigere non debebat. Quid autem gravius, quidve molestius quam hominem ab homine, homini impossibilia exigere, et quae supra humanum modum sunt ab humana possibilitate extorquere velle? Sed quae impossibilia sunt apud homines, apud Deum possibilia sunt. Exceptis, inquiunt, diis quorum non est cum hominibus conversatio. Recte hoc loco satis per deos sanctos intelligimus angelos. Si enim propter imaginis, seu etiam similitudinis participationem homines dicti sunt dii, quanto rectius angeli? Ego dixi: dii estis et filii Excelsi (Psal. LXXXI), et angeli quidem omni hora, imo omni pene momento inter homines versantur, et tamen cum hominibus non conversantur, quia et tunc quidem cum inter homines sunt, 1241Anon humanitus, sed coelitus, et vere divinitus degunt. Quis neget vel nesciat angelos hominibus multa quae supra homines sunt revelare potuisse, et revelare solere? Sed ut adhuc de exterioribus ad intima nostra similitudinem trahamus, sic saepe quae nulla nostra investigatione penetramus, ex divinis revelationibus et inspirationibus agnoscimus. Si autem per deos quis intelligi velit non angelos, sed homines divinitus aspiratos quorum conversatio jam non sit cum hominibus (hoc est humana sapientibus) eo quod jam veraciter dicere possunt: nostra conversatio in coelis est, nihil verius dici potest. Multa enim ex divina inspiratione agnoscunt, quae humanam investigationem excedunt. Si vero sententia illa superior ad hunc sensum accommodatur, 1241Bnihil in ea nisi rectum sonare videtur ubi dicitur. Non est homo super terram qui sermonem tuum, rex, possit implere, quasi dicatur: Nemo eorum qui super terram sunt, nisi illi tantum qui coelesti conversatione coelos ascendunt.

CHAPTER VI. How rashly, or how vainly, we presume upon human effort for that which we ought to await from angelic revelation alone, or from divine.

  • 1240A

Yet let us see what these men further add, subtly discussing what they say faultily, or what perchance they say truly. For things uttered incautiously avail for our caution, and things rightly uttered have wherewith to form us unto the zeal of imitation. But neither doth any of the kings, say they, great and mighty, inquire such a word of every soothsayer, and magician, and Chaldean (Dan. II). He is rightly called a king who is exalted unto a kingdom. But what says that King, truly King of kings and Lord of lords? The kingdom of heaven, says he, is within you (Luc. XVII). [Vulg. Luc. 17:21 reads regnum Dei, "the kingdom of God"] Unto this kingdom surely we can be exalted, 1240Bif we perfectly will it. This kingdom, existing within us, surely has many inhabitants and dwellers without number. But by how much the more each man enlarges such a kingdom, more faithfully dispenses it, more strictly rules it, more prudently orders it, by so much the mightier is he made, by so much the greater is he judged. But unto this kingdom poverty of spirit advances and conducts more than abundance of sense: for Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven (Matth. V). He who receives such a kingdom sings confidently, when he says: But I am appointed king by him (Psal. II). And well is it said of kings of this sort. But neither doth any of the kings, great and mighty, inquire a word of this kind of every soothsayer, and magician, and Chaldean. But in this the said kings are great, and defend their own power for themselves, 1240Cthat they presume nothing of their own virtue: how much less those things which are above man? Certainly the king had received that word divinely, and it was altogether such that it could not be known unless from God's revelation. That therefore which could not be known save from God, with what rashness is it presumed of a man, a soothsayer, a magician, or a Chaldean? What therefore is it to be unwilling, and not to be bound, to inquire a word of this kind of any wise man of this sort, unless that those things which are above man ought to be presumed of no human effort or art? Hence it is that it is added, when it is said: For the word which thou, O king, seekest is grievous, neither shall there be found any one that may declare it in the sight of the king, except the gods, whose conversation is not with men. Certainly 1240Dwhat was above man, the king ought not to have demanded of man. But what is more grievous, or what more burdensome, than that a man should demand of a man things impossible for a man, and should wish to wrest from human possibility things that are above the human measure? But the things that are impossible with men are possible with God. Except, say they, the gods, whose conversation is not with men. Rightly enough in this place do we understand by the gods the holy angels. For if because of the participation of the image, or even of the likeness, men have been called gods, how much more rightly the angels? I have said: you are gods and sons of the Most High (Psal. LXXXI); and angels indeed are engaged among men at every hour, nay at almost every moment, and yet they do not converse with men, because even then, when they are among men, 1241Athey live not in a human way, but in a heavenly, and truly in a divine way. Who would deny or not know that angels have been able to reveal to men many things which are above men, and are wont to reveal them? But that we may still draw a likeness from outward things to our inmost parts, so often the things which we penetrate by no investigation of ours, we come to know from divine revelations and inspirations. But if any one should wish that by the gods there be understood not angels, but men divinely inspired, whose conversation is now not with men (that is, with such as savour of human things), in that they can now truly say, Our conversation is in heaven, nothing truer can be said. For they come to know many things from divine inspiration which exceed human investigation. But if that former sentence be accommodated to this sense, 1241Bnothing in it seems to sound but what is right, where it is said. There is not a man upon earth that can fulfil thy word, O king: as if it were said, None of those who are upon earth, save only those who by a heavenly conversation ascend the heavens.

CAPUT VII. Quomodo post praeclara saepe studiorum suorum insignia mens tam saecularium quam spiritalium doctrinarum curam deponit; et se totam ad sola exteriora negotia exponit.

Quo audito, rex in furore et in ira magna praecipit ut perirent omnes sapientes Babylonis. Et egressa sententia, sapientes interficiebantur (Dan. II). Ecce quod superius jam praelocuti sumus. Postquam rex de somnii sui recuperatione desperavit, in furore 1241Cet ira omnes sapientes suos perire decrevit. Uti jam diximus, hoc est sapientes suos perdere, per acutos illos atque subtiles scientiae et sapientiae intellectus secundum pristinam consuetudinem et assuetam exercitationem ulterius non requirere, et cujuscunque doctrinae studiis nullam penitus operam dare, vel curam impendere. Quasi somnium visum amittitur, quando illa per quam ad sublimia mens frequenter sublevabatur gratia contemplationis subtrahitur, et tunc quidem velut de ejus recuperatione desperamus, quando diu multumque laborantes, nihilque proficientes spiritalium illam spectaculorum nostrorum jucunditatem ulterius nos recuperare posse diffidimus. Sed fit saepe ut, dum illam quam 1241Damare consueverat divinarum revelationum speculationem amittit animus, diutina fatigatione jam fractus, non solum ejus in cujus reparatione defecit, sed quorumcunque studiorum curam deponat, et omnes sapientes suos quodammodo perdere disponat. In furore, inquit, et ira magna. Furor est perturbatio mentis, totius expers rationis. Ira est mentis perturbatio magna, sed a ratione non penitus aliena. Quasi ex furore agitamur, et actitamur, quando sine respectu rationis in id, quod fieri non oportet, nos ipsos praecipites damus. Ira saepe commoti intelligimus quid faciendum sit, cum tamen refrenari non possit, si magna fuerit. In primo itaque perturbationis nostrae impetu saepe, nec saltem ad rationem respicimus, postmodum autem rationem 1242Aquidem videmus, sed sequi non possumus, tandem vero intuemur et sequimur. Recte ergo prius furor, et postea ira ponitur.

Notandum sane quod sapientes Babylonis dicuntur, qui hoc loco perire praecipiuntur. Puto alios esse sapientes Babyloniorum, et alios esse sapientes Judaeorum. Illi sane occupantur circa terrena, isti circa divina. Illi sane sectantur sapientiam mundi, quae stultitia est apud Deum, isti scrutantur etiam profunda Dei, utpote spiritales, et spiritualibus spiritualia comparantes. Quid igitur per sapientes Babyloniorum, nisi scientias saeculares? et quid per sapientes Judaeorum intelligimus, nisi doctrinas spiritales? Quasi ergo sapientes Babylonis damnationi addicimus, quando saeculares disciplinas a nostris 1242Bstudiis amputamus. Harum amputationi conscientia nostra facilius acquiescit, et eo levius earum abjectioni consentit, quo eas (quamvis ad aliquid utiles humanae saluti) minime necessarias attendit. Primo ergo sapientes Babylonis trucidantur, unde est quod de eis dicitur: Et egressa sententia sapientes interficiebantur. Sententia egreditur, quando id quod quisque mente deliberaverat atque statuerat, tandem aliquando ex opere manifestat. Quasi adhuc intrinsecus est, quod adhuc versatur, et latet in mente, et velut jam exercitus esse incipit, quando jam apparet in opere. Sequitur: Quaerebanturque Daniel et socii ejus, ut perirent. Ecce quomodo malum grassatur. Audivimus superius quomodo sapientes Babylonis interficiebantur, ecce jam sapientes 1242CJudaeorum quaeruntur, ut interficiantur. Ecce quibus gradibus studiosa mens saepe a sua vivacitate resolvitur, et a summis paulatim ad deteriora relabitur, prius saecularium, postea spiritalium doctrinam curam deponit, et se totam ad sola exteriora negotia exponit, et in se quasi omnes scientiae et sapientiae cultores interficit, dum post humanarum artium despectum, contemplationis, meditationis, lectionis et orationis instantiam penitus deserit.

Notandum tamen quod de sapientibus Judaeorum dicitur, quia ad perditionem quaerebantur. Quid, quaeso, est locum ubi ejusmodi sapientes ad perditionem inveniri valeant quaerere, nisi occasionem 1242Dseu etiam excusationis alicujus opportunitatem captare, quo tutius atque liberius et cum securiori conscientia sacrae etiam eruditionis studia dissoluta mens possit abjicere? saepe enim infirmitatem corporis praetendimus, cum spiritualia exercitia abjicimus. Saepe charitatem hoc esse fingimus, quod contemplativam vitam deserentes exterioribus negotiis nos ipsos funditus exponimus. In hunc modum occasio, et quasi loci opportunitas quaeritur, ubi Daniel cum caeteris sapientibus Judaeorum ad interficiendum inveniatur. Si hujus Danielis facta consulimus, quid rectius quam cordis devotionem in ipso figurari inveniemus? Vide quomodo sub omni adversitate, et qualicunque necessitate semper ad Dominum recurrit et consulit, auxilium divinum implorat et accipit. 1243AQuis autem nesciat verae devotionis esse sub omni necessitatis articulo, ad divina praesidia confugere, et tam in ambiguis quam adversis hinc consilium, illinc auxilium humiliter petere, et efficaciter impetrare? Scimus autem, quia intime et devote nunquam mens orat, quae se ad devotionem studiosis praemeditationibus prius non excitat. Hi sunt enim verae devotionis socii inseparabiles, circumspectae, discretae et providae cogitationes. Sine hujusmodi enim sociis et cooperatoribus, quaelibet nostra devotio non novit vel valet orare quomodo oportet.

CHAPTER VII. How, after the often illustrious distinctions of its own studies, the mind lays aside the care of doctrines both secular and spiritual; and exposes itself wholly to outward business alone.

And when this was heard, the king in fury and in great wrath commands that all the wise men of Babylon should perish. And when the sentence had gone forth, the wise men were being slain (Dan. II). Behold what we have already spoken of above. After the king had despaired of the recovery of his dream, in fury 1241Cand wrath he decreed that all his wise men should perish. As we have already said, this is to destroy his wise men: no longer to seek out, according to the former custom and the accustomed exercise, those keen and subtle apprehensions of knowledge and of wisdom, and to spend no labor at all upon the studies of any teaching whatever, nor to bestow care upon them. The dream that was seen is as it were lost when that grace of contemplation by which the mind was frequently lifted up to sublime things is withdrawn; and then indeed we as it were despair of its recovery, when, laboring long and much and profiting nothing, we distrust that we can any longer recover that delight of our spiritual sights. But it often happens that, when the spirit loses that gazing upon divine revelations which it had been 1241Dwont to love, being now broken by long weariness, it lays aside the care not only of that in whose restoration it failed, but of studies of every kind, and in a manner disposes to destroy all its wise men. In fury, he says, and in great wrath. Fury is a disturbance of the mind wholly devoid of reason. Wrath is a great disturbance of the mind, yet not utterly alien from reason. We are driven and driven about as it were out of fury, when, with no regard to reason, we cast ourselves headlong into that which ought not to be done. Often, moved by wrath, we understand what ought to be done, though it cannot be reined in, if it be great. And so in the first onset of our disturbance we often do not so much as look back to reason; but afterwards we do indeed see reason, 1242Ayet cannot follow it; and at last we both behold it and follow it. Rightly therefore is fury set first, and wrath afterwards.

It is certainly to be noted that those who in this place are commanded to perish are called the wise men of Babylon. I think that the wise men of the Babylonians are one thing, and the wise men of the Jews another. The former, indeed, are occupied about earthly things, the latter about divine. The former follow after the wisdom of the world, which is foolishness with God; the latter search even the deep things of God, being spiritual, and comparing spiritual things with spiritual. What then do we understand by the wise men of the Babylonians, but secular sciences? and what by the wise men of the Jews, but spiritual teachings? So then we as it were consign the wise men of Babylon to condemnation, when we cut off the secular disciplines from our 1242Bstudies. Our conscience acquiesces more easily in the cutting off of these, and the more lightly consents to their being cast away, the more it observes them (however useful for something to human salvation) to be in no way necessary. First, then, the wise men of Babylon are slaughtered; whence it is that of them it is said: And when the sentence had gone forth, the wise men were being slain. The sentence goes forth when at length a man manifests in deed what he had deliberated and determined in his mind. That is as it were still within which is still turned over and lies hidden in the mind; and it begins as it were to be already put into practice when it now appears in deed. There follows: And Daniel and his companions were sought, that they might perish. Behold how the evil spreads. We have heard above how the wise men of Babylon were being slain; behold, now the wise men 1242Cof the Jews are sought, that they may be slain. Behold by what steps a studious mind is often loosened from its own liveliness, and slips back little by little from the highest things to worse ones: first it lays aside the care of secular, afterwards of spiritual teachings doctrinam, and exposes itself wholly to outward business alone, and slays within itself as it were all the cultivators of knowledge and of wisdom, while after its contempt for the human arts it utterly forsakes the urgency of contemplation, of meditation, of reading and of prayer.

It is to be noted, however, that of the wise men of the Jews it is said that they were sought for destruction. What, I ask, is it to seek a place where wise men of this sort may be found for destruction, but to snatch at an occasion, 1242Dor even at the opportunity of some excuse, whereby a dissolute mind may the more safely and the more freely, and with a more secure conscience, cast away even the pursuits of sacred instruction? For we often plead infirmity of the body when we cast away spiritual exercises. Often we pretend this to be charity, that, forsaking the contemplative life, we utterly expose ourselves to outward business. In this way an occasion, and as it were the opportunity of a place, is sought, where Daniel with the rest of the wise men of the Jews may be found for slaying. If we consult the deeds of this Daniel, what shall we more rightly find figured in him than the devotion of the heart? See how, under every adversity and in whatever necessity, he always has recourse to the Lord and consults him, implores and receives the divine aid. 1243AAnd who does not know that it belongs to true devotion, under every crisis of necessity, to flee to the divine defenses, and in doubtful matters as well as in adverse ones humbly to ask counsel from the one side and aid from the other, and effectually to obtain it? But we know that the mind never prays inwardly and devoutly which does not first rouse itself to devotion by diligent premeditations. For these are the inseparable companions of true devotion: circumspect, discerning and provident thoughts. For without companions and fellow workers of this kind, whatever devotion is ours neither knows nor is able to pray as it ought.

CAPUT VIII. Quomodo in omni proposito, vel instituto debeamus aequitatis regulam consulere, et improvide statuta ad saniorem sententiam revocare.

  • 1243B

Tunc Daniel requisivit de lege, atque sententia ab Arioch principe militiae regis, qui egressus fuerat ad interficiendos sapientes Babylonis, et interrogavit eum qui a rege acceperat potestatem, quam ob causam tam crudelis sententia a facie regis esset egressa. Cum ergo rem indicasset Arioch Danieli, Daniel ingressus rogavit regem, ut tempus daret sibi ad solutionem indicandam regi (Dan. II). Sicut in Daniele intelligitur fervor devotionis, sic per principem militiae recte, ut arbitror, intelligimus zelum rectitudinis et rigorem districtae severitatis. Certe sine rigore justae severitatis, nec illud quidem regnum licet in pace vel securitate possidere, in quo solent homines hominibus imperare, et in quo solent homines possidentium 1243Cpacem perturbare. Quis imperantium unquam pace diutina frui, vel laetari potuit, qui pacis adversarios, cum potuit et oportuit districte judicare, et fortiter ferire noluit? Quanto magis autem in illo utique regno, in quo non est nobis colluctatio adversus carnem et sanguinem, sed adversus spiritualia nequitiae in coelestibus, quanto magis, inquam, in ejusmodi regno oportet nos fortiter agere, et omnes perstrepentium cogitationum, vel affectionum tumultus, et quoslibet internae pacis perturbatores districta animadversione ferire? Bonus sane iste princeps militiae (rigor videlicet; et severitas justitiae) cujus legibus, vel disciplinis quisquis non obtemperat, ab illa coelestis militiae societate se 1243Dalienat.
