Recte autem, ut arbitror, iste princeps militiae Arioch dicitur. Nam Arioch ad solitudinem redigens interpretatur. Scimus quia regnum coelorum intra nos est. Debet itaque iste princeps militiae quodcunque regnum vel regionem videt contra hoc regnum exsurgere, vel rebellare, et aeterni regis legibus non obtemperare, forti impugnatione evertere, et ad sempiternam solitudinem redigere, et si fieri potest, in aeternum delere. Si igitur per Danielem devotionem, per Arioch zelum rectitudinis debemus intelligere, quid est Danielem ab Arioch de lege, et sententia regis quaerere, nisi compunctam mentem aequitatis judicium consulere, et ex magna devotione vehementer discutere, an forte justum vel injustum sit, quod meminit se 1244Aprius per spiritus sui impetum statuisse? Multa enim tempore compunctionis et orationis mens hominis mala et injusta esse invenit, quae prius ex animi impetu, et quasi praecipitanter definivit, quae tamen injusta esse tunc temporis non advertit, et multa saepe ex eodem spiritus sui impetu homo contra conscientiam suam dictat, et tamen eamdem animi sui praecipitationem, postmodum sub orationis et devotionis suae tempore temperat, et ad saniorem sententiam reformat. Debemus itaque ea quae praecipitanter, magis quam prudenter statuimus, et mala esse pertimescimus, postmodum diligenter discutere, et utrum justa an injusta, utilia an inutilia sint, sagaci retractatione perquirere, et in hunc modum quasi per Danielem officium 1244BArioch de lege vel sententia data requiritur, dum de quolibet minus consulto mentis proposito ex magna devotione, tandem aliquando verae aequitatis, et summae discretionis regula consulitur. Quid sit autem ad interficiendos sapientes egredi, ex superioribus facile potest perpendi. Sciendum autem quod inconsulte sententiae definitionem vel qualitatem saepe tunc facilius deprehendimus, ampliusque formidamus, cum jam qualis sit, ex ipso opere et quasi per experimentum probamus. Hinc est quod Arioch hoc loco tunc primum de sententiae datione consulitur, cum ad ejus occasionem jam jamque egreditur.

CHAPTER VIII. How in every purpose or ordinance we ought to consult the rule of equity, and to call back what has been improvidently decreed to a sounder judgment.

  • 1243B

Then Daniel inquired concerning the law and the sentence from Arioch, the prince of the king's soldiery, who had gone forth to slay the wise men of Babylon; and he questioned him who had received power from the king, for what cause so cruel a sentence had gone forth from the king's face. When therefore Arioch had made the matter known to Daniel, Daniel went in and asked the king that he would give him time to declare the solution to the king (Dan. II). As in Daniel the fervor of devotion is understood, so by the prince of the soldiery we rightly, as I judge, understand the zeal of rectitude and the rigor of strict severity. Certainly, without the rigor of just severity, not even that kingdom may be possessed in peace or in security in which men are wont to command men, and in which men are wont to disturb the peace 1243Cof those who possess it. What ruler was ever able to enjoy a lasting peace, or to rejoice in it, who was unwilling strictly to judge and strongly to strike the adversaries of peace, when he could and when he ought? But how much more in that kingdom, in which our wrestling is not against flesh and blood, but against the spiritual things of wickedness in the heavenly places; how much more, I say, in a kingdom of this sort ought we to act strongly, and with strict punishment to strike all the tumults of clamorous thoughts or affections, and whatever disturbers there be of inward peace? Good indeed is this prince of the soldiery (namely rigor; and the severity of justice), whose laws or disciplines whoever does not obey estranges himself 1243Dfrom that fellowship of the heavenly soldiery. And rightly, as I judge, is this prince of the soldiery called Arioch. For Arioch is interpreted reducing to solitude. We know that the kingdom of heaven is within us [Vulg. Luke 17:21 regnum Dei intra vos est, "the kingdom of God is within you"]. And so this prince of the soldiery ought, whatever kingdom or region he sees rising up or rebelling against this kingdom and not obeying the laws of the eternal King, to overthrow it by a strong assault, and to reduce it to everlasting solitude, and, if it can be done, to blot it out forever. If therefore by Daniel we ought to understand devotion, and by Arioch the zeal of rectitude, what is it for Daniel to inquire of Arioch concerning the law and the sentence of the king, but for a mind stricken with compunction to consult the judgment of equity, and out of great devotion vehemently to examine whether that be perhaps just or unjust which it remembers itself 1244Ato have formerly determined through the impulse of its own spirit? For in the time of compunction and of prayer the mind of man finds many things to be evil and unjust which it had formerly defined out of the impulse of its spirit, and as it were headlong, and which nevertheless it did not perceive at that time to be unjust; and often, out of that same impulse of his spirit, a man dictates many things against his own conscience, and yet afterwards, in the time of his prayer and of his devotion, he tempers that same headlong haste of his spirit, and reforms it to a sounder judgment. And so we ought afterwards diligently to examine those things which we have decreed headlong rather than prudently, and which we greatly fear to be evil, and to search out by shrewd reconsideration whether they be just or unjust, useful or useless; and in this way the office 1244Bof Arioch is as it were inquired of through Daniel concerning the law or the sentence given, when at length, out of great devotion, the rule of true equity and of the highest discretion is consulted concerning any less considered purpose of the mind. But what it is to go forth to slay the wise men can easily be weighed from what has gone before. It is to be known, however, that we often more easily detect, and more fully dread, the determination or the quality of an ill-considered sentence at that time when we now prove by the deed itself, and as it were by experiment, what sort it is. Hence it is that Arioch in this place is consulted about the giving of the sentence then first, when he is already going forth on occasion of it.

CAPUT IX Quod quidquid contra nos accidere solet, dispensante Deo, libero mentis arbitrio, juxta meritorum qualitatem respondet.

  • 1244C

Sed si per Arioch districtio aequitatis recte intelligitur, mirum fortassis, nec immerito, alicui videbitur, quomodo ejusmodi sententiae exsecutio ad Arioch officium spectare dicatur, cum eadem utique sententia, nec juxta historiam, nec juxta mysticam intelligentiam justa judicetur. An forte rex juxta historiam furore turbatus justum esse credidit, quod veraciter justum non fuit, eo quod tunc veraciter dicere potuerit: Turbatus est a furore oculus meus? Sic sane quidam juxta hujus similitudinis typum, zelum quidem rectitudinis generantes, sed secundum scientiam non habentes, rectum 1244Dcredunt esse vel fingunt quod sapientes suos occidunt, majora sibi meritorum lucra proponentes vel promittentes ex corporalibus exercitiis acquirere, quam ex spiritualibus quidem, in quibus interim non possunt quantum vel quam leviter volunt, proficere. Sed si adhuc profundius inspiciamus, et ad divina judicia recurramus, statim videbimus quia multa quae homo injuste disponit vel agit, Deus nonnisi juste fieri permittit. Cur ergo et illa non dicantur ad Arioch officium pertinere, quae juxta hanc considerationem ex divinae districtionis aequitate, nostris probantur meritis respondere? Et interrogavit, inquit, eum qui a rege acceperat potestatem. Attende ergo quod Arioch iste a rege potestatem accepit, quia quidquid 1245Acirca nos utiliter seu inutiliter accidit, ex liberi arbitrii merito vel vitio descendit. Accipit autem Arioch exercendae modo utilis, modo inutilis sententiae potestatem a rege, aliquando a sciente et volente, aliquando a sciente et nolente quandoque a non sciente et volente, quandoque a nesciente et nolente. Quemadmodum enim dictum est, quod cuique accidere solet, dispensante Deo, libertatis arbitrio juxta meritorum qualitatem respondet. Quidquid ergo circa nos agitur pro meritorum retributione aequitatisque jure, justo Dei judicio moderatur. Sed providus ille animi obtutus, saepe sub magnae perturbationis suae impetu, Omnipotentis beneplacitum seu judicium agnoscit et acquiescit. Aliquando agnoscit, nec acquiescit, quandoque 1245Bautem sic effectus est ut voluntatem Dei solam circa se fieri concupiscat, quamvis eam interim ad plenum non intelligat, modo autem sic afficitur ut voluntati Dei omnino acquiescere non possit, etiamsi illam (quam interim scire non potest) ad plenum scire potuisset. Sed quid mirum si humanus animus non potest semper acquiescere etiam cognitae divinae voluntati, quando quidem saepe nec suae potest acquiescere utilitati? Saepe enim per impatientiam sententiam dictat quam suae utilitati esse contrariam penitus non ignorat. Non igitur tunc tantum dictus ille princeps militiae a suo rege exercendae sententiae potestatem accipit, quando ejusmodi rex aliquid juste et utiliter disponit, sed etiam nunc quidem, quando aliquid per impudentiam seu etiam 1245Cper impatientiam ordinat quod Dei dispositione, praecedentibus ejus meritis, aequitatis retributione respondeat, imo etiam tunc quando aliquid agit unde odiosam, quamvis justam, sententiam incurrit.

CHAPTER IX That whatever is wont to befall against us answers, by God's dispensation, to the free will of the mind according to the quality of merits.

  • 1244C

But if by Arioch the strictness of equity is rightly understood, it will perhaps seem strange to someone, and not without reason, how the execution of a sentence of this kind is said to belong to the office of Arioch, since that same sentence is by no means judged just, either according to the history or according to the mystical understanding. Or did the king perhaps, according to the history, being troubled with fury, believe that to be just which was not truly just, in that he might then truly have said: My eye is troubled with fury? Just so, indeed, certain men after the type of this likeness, begetting a zeal of rectitude but not having it according to knowledge, believe it to be right, or feign it to be right, 1244Dthat they slay their own wise men, setting before themselves or promising themselves greater gains of merit to be acquired from bodily exercises than from spiritual ones, in which meanwhile they cannot make as much progress, or as easily, as they wish. But if we look still more deeply, and have recourse to the divine judgments, we shall at once see that many things which a man disposes or does unjustly, God permits to be done in no way but justly. Why then should not those things also be said to pertain to the office of Arioch which, according to this consideration, are proved by the equity of the divine strictness to answer to our merits? And he questioned, he says, him who had received power from the king. Mark, then, that this Arioch received power from the king, because whatever 1245Abefalls us, whether profitably or unprofitably, comes down from the merit or the fault of free will. And Arioch receives from the king the power of executing a sentence now profitable, now unprofitable: sometimes from one who knows and wills, sometimes from one who knows and does not will, sometimes from one who does not know and wills, sometimes from one who neither knows nor wills. For, as has been said, what is wont to befall each man answers, by God's dispensation, to the free choice of liberty according to the quality of merits. Whatever therefore is done concerning us is regulated, for the requital of merits and by the right of equity, by the just judgment of God. But that provident gaze of the spirit often, under the onset of its own great disturbance, recognizes the good pleasure or judgment of the Almighty and acquiesces in it. Sometimes it recognizes it and does not acquiesce; but sometimes 1245Bit is so wrought that it desires the will of God alone to be done concerning itself, although meanwhile it does not fully understand it; and sometimes it is so affected that it cannot at all acquiesce in the will of God, even if it had been able fully to know that will (which meanwhile it cannot know). But what wonder if the human spirit cannot always acquiesce even in a divine will that is known, since indeed it often cannot acquiesce even in its own advantage? For often through impatience it dictates a sentence which it is in no way ignorant to be contrary to its own advantage. Therefore that prince of the soldiery which we have named receives from his king the power of executing a sentence not then only, when a king of this sort disposes something justly and usefully, but even now indeed, when he ordains something through shamelessness or even 1245Cthrough impatience which, by God's disposition and his own preceding merits, answers to the requital of equity; nay, even then, when he does something whereby he incurs a hateful, though just, sentence.

CAPUT X. Quibus considerationibus pensanda sunt quae circa nos accidunt.

Videamus adhuc autem quid illud sit quod Daniel ab Arioch iterum inquirit. Quam ob causam tam crudelis sententia a facie esset regis egressa. Ordo congruus, et diligenter observandus, prius interrogatur de lege et sententia, postea de ejusdem sententiae causa. Procul dubio diligenter inquirendum, 1245Det vehementer discutiendum (in rebus utique dubiis et maxime periculosis) prius de qualitate actionis, et consequenter quidem de qualitate intentionis. Nam saepe culpa quae ex actione contrahitur, ex qualitate intentionis nonnihil levigatur. Quod tamen inquirit de causa sententiae, ad duo, ut arbitror, possumus imo et debemus referre, qua videlicet intentione fiat, vel qua Dei dispositione fieri contingat. Et circa actionem quidem quaeritur utrumnam sit commoda an incommoda, justa vel injusta. Circa intentionem vero an forte sit pia vel impia, discreta vel indiscreta. Circa Dei autem dispositionem id maxime quisque in dirae vel durae sententiae investigatione perquirat, utrum ad utilitatem an ad ultionem nos feriat. Nam sane cum nos percutit 1246Avel feriri permittit, modo ad nostram eruditionem, modo ad nostram correctionem, modo ad correptionem, modo ad damnationem, vel alio adhuc quolibet modo id agit. Haec omnia diligenter inquirenda et subtiliter discutienda. Haec omnia utilem timorem incutiunt, et mentem in oratione compungunt. Haec omnia mentem humiliant, et nostram devotionem excitant, et ad supplicationem inflammant. Vides certe quam recte, quam discrete haec omnia Daniel inquirat antequam precibus insistat, vel supplicationi incumbat. Hinc est quod dicitur, cum subditur: Cum ergo rem indicasset Arioch Danieli, Daniel ingressus rogavit regem Quid est autem praedicta illa Danielem ab Arioch discere, nisi diligenter et devote consulta districte 1246Baequitatis regula tandem aliquando post multam discussionem quaesitam sententiae, et causae qualitatem devotam mentem plenius agnoscere.

CHAPTER X. By what considerations the things that befall us are to be weighed.

But let us further see what that is which Daniel again inquires of Arioch. For what cause so cruel a sentence had gone forth from the king's face. A fitting order, and diligently to be observed: first he is questioned concerning the law and the sentence, afterwards concerning the cause of that same sentence. Without doubt there must be diligent inquiry 1245Dand vehement examination (in matters doubtful, at all events, and most perilous) first concerning the quality of the action, and consequently concerning the quality of the intention. For often the fault that is contracted from the action is not a little lightened by the quality of the intention. Yet that he inquires concerning the cause of the sentence we can, as I judge, nay we ought, to refer to two things: namely, with what intention it is done, or by what disposition of God it happens to be done. And concerning the action it is asked whether it be advantageous or disadvantageous, just or unjust. But concerning the intention, whether it be perhaps pious or impious, discerning or undiscerning. And concerning God's disposition let each man especially search this out in the investigation of a dread or a hard sentence: whether he strikes us for our profit or for vengeance. For certainly, when he smites us 1246Aor permits us to be struck, he does it now for our instruction, now for our correction, now for our rebuke, now for our condemnation, or in yet some other way whatever. All these things are to be diligently inquired into and subtly examined. All these things strike in a profitable fear, and prick the mind with compunction in prayer. All these things humble the mind, and rouse our devotion, and inflame it to supplication. Thou seest surely how rightly, how discreetly Daniel inquires into all these things before he presses on in prayers or applies himself to supplication. Hence it is that it is said, when there is added: When therefore Arioch had made the matter known to Daniel, Daniel went in and asked the king But what is that aforesaid learning of Daniel from Arioch, but that the devoted mind, the rule of equity having been diligently and devoutly 1246Band strictly consulted, should at length, after much examination, more fully recognize the quality of the sentence sought and of its cause.

CAPUT XI. Quomodo perturbationis nostrae tempore debeamus ad orationis remedia confugere, et ejusmodi suffragium in usum adducere.

Restat modo illud quaerere quid sit Danielem ad regem intrare. Recte fortassis per regem qui in omnibus praesidet et imperat, intelligimus hoc loco vehementem animi motum, et validum libere voluntatis impetum, cui facile quaelibet alia affectio cedat vel obediat. Et quam multis (cum sunt vehementer affecti, vel acriter commoti) omnino non occurrit rationem vel discretionem consulere, quanto minus 1246Cad orationem confugere, vel ullam devotionem admittere? Sed quid aliud hoc dicimus esse, quam sapientium neminem coram rege audere apparere? Saepe tamen qui ejusmodi sunt, post sedatum animi impetum, cum ad semetipsos redeunt, ad discretionis judicium et devotionis officium recurrunt, satagentes corrigere in quibus perturbationis tempore excesserunt. Sed si circa cordis disciplinam studiosi sumus quemadmodum esse debemus, doceamus et assuescamus animum nostrum sub quovis impetu et perturbationis tempore ad discretionis consulta confugere, et hoc est sapientes ante conspectum regis amittere. Sed quia saepe contigit ut nulla rationis attestatione praecipitem perturbationis suae impetum 1246Dlenire possit, debet mens sub ejusmodi necessitatis articulo ad devotionis auxilia recurrere, et ad pacandam perturbationis suae violentiam divinae miserationis suffragia impetrare, et ejusmodi devotionis remedium oportet in usum adducere, et hoc est Danielem ad regem intrare. Quasi enim ad regem Daniel ingreditur, quando ad pacandum animi impetum, sub ipso etiam praecipitationis impulsu devotio suffragatur.

Notandum autem quod superius dicitur introductus, hic vero simpliciter ponitur quod sit ingressus. Puto autem quia aliud, et aliud sit ab alio introduci, vel per semetipsum ingredi, sicut ex superioribus patet, sub omni vehementi animi impetu devotae orationis suffragium praesto habere, hoc est 1247ADanielem in praesentia furentis regis non deesse. Sed, ut omnes communiter et sollicitudine efficimus, alia orando, et Deo supplicando precibus obtinemus. Quando igitur humana sagacitate et exercitatione devotionis praesidium ad sedandum animi impetum assuescitur, ille noster Daniel quodammodo ante dicti regis praesentiam, alieno officio introducitur. Quando autem hoc non tam meditando quam orando efficitur, Daniel procul dubio ad regem per semetipsum ingreditur. Debemus itaque studiosa et sagaci praemeditatione mentem nostram ad devotionem excitare, et hoc erit Danielem alieno officio introducere. Item, quia saepe etiam cum necessitas exigit et urget, devote orare non possumus, divinae visitationis et nostrae compunctionis tempore 1247Boremus, ut necessitatis tempore orare possimus. Si itaque orandi facultas oratione obtinetur, Daniel pro certo per semetipsum ingreditur. Sed nec illud quidem negligenter praetereundum quod sub tempore regiae perturbationis et furoris legitur ingressus. Nam introductionis tempore nullum profecto regiae perturbationis vel indicium accipimus. Non enim credere oportet hunc regem semper esse iratum, atque aliquo furore turbatum. Aliud est animi impetum esse vehementem, aliud est esse praecipitem. Aliud, inquam, validum esse atque invictum, atque aliud esse ira et insania plenum. Aliud denique invincibilem et pene immobilem, aliud vero intolerabilem et plane irrationabilem. 1247CSed quis, quaeso, Danielem sub regii furoris tempore praesumeret, vel auderet ante regem adducere? Supra humanam industriam, et virtutem esse judico tantae et tam intimae perturbationis tempore orationi incumbere, cum vix, vel nullo potius modo tunc temporis contingat ejus saltem meminisse. Oportet ergo, eo quo supra diximus modo, Danielem per semetipsum coram rege turbato intrare, si vult sub tantae necessitatis articulo periculo repugnare. Mens itaque studiosa, et circa salutem suam sollicita, quae adhuc, cum fuerit perturbata, perturbationi suae orando repugnare non novit, et hujusmodi usum, vel gratiam necdum obtinuit, oret, et impetret tempore tranquillitatis divinitus sibi dari gratiam orandi sub tempore perturbationis. 1247DIn hunc modum forsitan dabitur Danieli quandoque ad regem per semetipsum intrare, et adhuc ipso furente, et tunc forte poterit non semel tantum, sed saepe et assidue malae sententiae differendae inducias petere et obtinere, ad hoc ut eam possit postmodum penitus delere. Quando intimae perturbationis suae tempore devota mens humiliter petit et supplicat, ne sub concitati furoris impetu divina eum pietas aliquid per impatientiam agere permittat, unde postmodum poenitere oporteat, si impetrat quod optat, quid aliud devotionis nostrae Daniel videtur tibi facere, quam furentis regis insaniam mitigare? Cum autem ad aliquid praecipitis affectionis suae vehementia trahi se sentit, quod interim sub tantae perturbationis suae nube, vel turbine utrumne 1248Arectum sit agnoscere ad plenum non possit, si interim patientiam sibi petit, donec quid faciendum sit tranquillitatis suae tempore rectius possit discernere, accipiendo quod petit quid aliud tibi videtur efficere, quam inducias petere et accipere? Daniel, inquit, ingressus rogavit regem ut tempus daret sibi ad solutionem indicandam regi. Vides certe quia devotio pie et utiliter de Domini pietate saepe praesumit, quae nulla sua sagacitate humana industria efficere possit, et quam juste de Domini pietate magna praesumimus, si magnanimitatem nostram pro amore ejus, cum ejus adjutorio vincamus.

CHAPTER XI. How in the time of our disturbance we ought to flee to the remedies of prayer, and to bring a support of this kind into use.

It remains now to ask what it is for Daniel to go in to the king. Rightly perhaps by the king, who presides and commands over all, we understand in this place the vehement motion of the spirit and the strong impulse of free will, to which any other affection whatever readily yields or gives obedience. And to how many (when they are vehemently affected or sharply moved) does it not occur at all to consult reason or discretion, how much less 1246Cto flee to prayer, or to admit any devotion? But what else do we say this to be, than that none of the wise men dares to appear before the king? Yet often those who are of this sort, after the impulse of the spirit has been settled, when they come back to themselves, have recourse to the judgment of discretion and to the office of devotion, busying themselves to correct the things in which they went beyond measure in the time of disturbance. But if we are diligent about the discipline of the heart, as we ought to be, let us teach and accustom our spirit, under any impulse whatever and in the time of disturbance, to flee to the counsels of discretion; and this is to lose the wise men before the king's sight. But because it often happens that by no attestation of reason can it soothe the headlong impulse of its own 1246Ddisturbance, the mind ought, under a crisis of necessity of this kind, to have recourse to the helps of devotion, and to obtain the supports of the divine mercy for the pacifying of the violence of its disturbance; and a remedy of devotion of this kind must be brought into use; and this is for Daniel to go in to the king. For Daniel as it were goes in to the king when, for the pacifying of the impulse of the spirit, devotion lends its support even under the very drive of headlong haste.

But it is to be noted that above he is said to have been brought in, whereas here it is simply set down that he went in. And I think that to be brought in by another and to go in by oneself are one thing and another; as is plain from what has gone before, to have the support of devout prayer ready at hand under every vehement impulse of the spirit, this is 1247Athat Daniel is not wanting in the presence of the raging king. But, as we all in common effect things by carefulness too, other things we obtain by prayers, by praying and beseeching God. When therefore by human shrewdness and by exercise the defense of devotion is made habitual for settling the impulse of the spirit, that Daniel of ours is in a manner brought into the presence of the aforesaid king by another's office. But when this is effected not so much by meditating as by praying, Daniel without doubt goes in to the king by himself. And so we ought by diligent and shrewd premeditation to rouse our mind to devotion; and this will be to bring Daniel in by another's office. Likewise, because often even when necessity demands and presses we cannot pray devoutly, let us pray in the time of the divine visitation and of our own compunction, 1247Bthat in the time of necessity we may be able to pray. If, then, the faculty of praying is obtained by prayer, Daniel assuredly goes in by himself. But neither is this to be passed over negligently, that he is read to have gone in during the time of the king's disturbance and fury. For at the time of his being brought in we assuredly receive not even any indication of the king's disturbance. For we must not believe that this king is always angry and troubled with some fury. It is one thing for the impulse of the spirit to be vehement, another for it to be headlong. One thing, I say, to be strong and unconquered, and another to be full of wrath and madness. One thing, finally, to be invincible and almost immovable; but another to be intolerable and plainly irrational. 1247CBut who, I ask, in the time of the royal fury would presume, or would dare, to lead Daniel before the king? I judge it to be above human effort and virtue to apply oneself to prayer in the time of so great and so inward a disturbance, since scarcely, or rather in no way at all, does it happen at that time even to remember it. It behooves therefore, in the manner we have said above, that Daniel go in by himself before the troubled king, if he wishes under the crisis of so great a necessity to fight back against the peril. And so let the mind that is diligent and solicitous about its own salvation, which as yet, when it has been disturbed, does not know how to fight back against its disturbance by praying, and has not yet obtained the practice or the grace of this kind, pray and obtain in a time of tranquillity that the grace of praying in a time of disturbance be given it from God. 1247DIn this way it will perhaps be granted to Daniel at some time to go in to the king by himself, and while he is still raging; and then he will perhaps be able, not once only but often and assiduously, to ask and to obtain a respite for the deferring of an evil sentence, to this end, that he may afterwards utterly blot it out. When, in the time of its inward disturbance, the devoted mind humbly asks and beseeches that the divine goodness not permit him, under the impulse of a roused fury, to do anything through impatience whereof he must afterwards repent, if it obtains what it desires, what else does the Daniel of our devotion seem to thee to do, than to soothe the madness of the raging king? But when it feels itself drawn by the vehemence of its headlong affection to something which meanwhile, under the cloud or the whirlwind of so great a disturbance of its own, it cannot fully recognize whether 1248Ait be right, if meanwhile it asks patience for itself until in the time of its tranquillity it can more rightly discern what is to be done, then, by receiving what it asks, what else does it seem to thee to accomplish, than to ask and receive a respite? Daniel, he says, went in and asked the king that he would give him time to declare the solution to the king. Thou seest surely that devotion piously and profitably presumes often upon the Lord's goodness for things which human effort could by no shrewdness of its own bring about; and how justly we presume great things upon the Lord's goodness, if for love of him, with his help, we conquer our own magnanimity.

CAPUT XII Qualibus meditationibus oportet animum ad devotionem inflammare, vel quomodo oporteat studiorum nostrorum instantiam reformare.

  • 1248B

Sed videamus quid post haec Daniel fecerit, vel quid de eo scriptum sit: Et ingressus domum suam, Ananiae, Misaeli, Azariae sociis suis indicavit negotium, ut quaererent misericordiam a facie Dei coeli super sacramento isto, et non perirent Daniel, et socii ejus, cum caeteris sapientibus Babylonis (Dan. II). Per tres istos, Danielis socios individuos, et comites inseparabiles, circumspectionem, discretionem, deliberationem intelligimus, sine quibus verae devotionis officium nullo modo posse expleri quotidiano experimento probamus. Si enim intime et devote orare volumus, primum est ut vitam nostram 1248Ccircumspiciamus. Videamus quibus studiis vel exercitiis occupari consuevimus, vel quibus adhuc occupari sumus, vel quale propositum in his de futuro habemus. Sed quid ista juvat in considerationem adducere, nisi eadem sollicitudine satagamus singula quaeque forti examinatione discernere, et bona a malis vigili sagacitate secernere, ut sciamus pro quibus debeamus Domino supplicare, seu etiam gratias agere? Cognitis autem seu bonis, seu malis nostris, restat adhuc provida deliberatione perquirere qua occupatione vel sollicitudine possimus, et pro praeteritis malis satisfacere, et de praesentibus se explicare, et de futuris cautelam curiosius exhibere. Primum est circumspectionis, secundum est discretionis, tertium est deliberationis. Circumspectionis 1248Dest morum nostrorum modum in considerationem adducere; discretionis est in omnibus judicium facere; deliberatio autem consilium quaerere, et invenire. Circumspectio dicit: Recogitabo tibi omnes annos meos in amaritudine animae meae (Isai. XXXVIII). Discretio fatetur: Feci judicium et justitiam (Psal. XCVIII). Deliberatio loquitur: Quid retribuam Domino pro omnibus quae retribuit mihi? (Psal. CXV.) Ad circumspectionis studium revocatur, cui dicitur: Leva in circuitu oculos tuos (Isai. XLIX), et vide tunc ubi prostrata sis, meretrix. Ad discretionis examen animare nos voluit, qui dixit: Si nos ipsos dijudicaremus, non utique judicaremur (I Cor. XI). Deliberationis officium praecipitur, seu etiam commendatur cum per sapientem dicitur: Omnia fac cum consilio, 1249Aet in aeternum non poenitebis (Eccli. XXXII). Quis autem non videat horum officia verae utique devotioni quam sint necessaria? Quis enim unquam intime et devote orare potuit, si vitam suam circumspicere, discutere, disponere postposuit? Quoties igitur necessitas urget devotae orationi vehementius incumbere, oportet nos ad circumspectionis, et discretionis, seu etiam deliberationis officia recurrere, et ejusmodi studiis negotiosisque meditationibus animum nostrum ad devotionem inflammare, et hoc est Danielem sociorum suorum suffragia in orationis suae usum asciscere. Daniel itaque quasi ad domus suae penetralia recurrit, et ad orationis suae negotia perfectius explenda velut cooperatores quaerit, quando, instante jam orationis tempore, seu etiam urgentis 1249Bnecessitatis articulo, devota mens solitam et in id officii necessariam quietis suae opportunitatem repetit, et providis quibusdam, et in ejusmodi usum idoneis meditationibus devotionem suam animare seu inflammare contendit. Sociis suis, inquit, indicavit negotium. Quasi a Daniele Danielis sociis negotium indicatur, quando ex devotione agitur ut imminens negotium in considerationem, examinationem, consultationem adducatur. Misericordia autem impetranda proponitur, si compuncta mens in eo quod devote exsequitur non suis meritis, sed divinis miserationibus innititur.

Notandum sane juxta historiam quod Daniel propheta negotium illud sacramentum vocet, unde in eo mysticam rerum intelligentiam latitare quis dubitet? 1249CEt non perirent, inquit, Daniel, et socii ejus cum caeteris sapientibus Babylonis. Juxta hujus rei exemplum, cum viderimus mentem nostram post saecularium studiorum contemptum ad spiritualia etiam exercitia torpere, et paulatim deficere, et sapientum nostrorum periculum imminere, debemus et nos modis omnibus illud a Domino petere, unde possimus consuetam studiorum nostrorum instantiam reparare, vel confirmare. Est autem, ut pariter novimus, tam in studio orandi quam meditandi labor pergrandis, et mentibus vagis, et inquietis pene intolerabilis. In contemplatione vero jucunditas est mirabilis et pene singularis. Quando igitur contemplationis gratia studiosis mentibus 1249Dconfertur labor ille caeterorum studiorum ex ejus dulcedine, in quantum dulcoratur, in tantum levigatur, seu temperatur. Si ergo ejusmodi gratiam nondum accepimus, vel subtractam dolemus, debemus illam ad studiorum nostrorum confirmationem vel reformationem a Domino expetere. Cum igitur haec agimus, quid aliud, quaeso, fecisse videmur quam divinarum revelationum somnia quaerere, ut habeamus unde possimus nostros illos superius designatos sapientes in sua integritate conservare? Tunc Danieli per visionem nocte mysterium revelatum est. Mysterii revelatio quasi nocte percipitur, quando veritatis illustratio tenuiter quidem, et ex parte, et velut ad lunae lumen videtur. Mysterii revelationem quasi nocte percipimus, quando de aliquo 1250ADivinitatis arcano quid sentiendum sit ipsi quidem intelligimus, sed rationem qua illud et aliis probare possumus in promptu necdum habemus. Revelationis lumen quasi in nocte et velut in mediis tenebris apparet, quando quis mysterii declarationem quam per intelligentiam videt, sermone explicare, et quasi in apertam lucem proferre, non valet. Sed cum per diutinam retractationem postmodum agimus, ut acceptam veritatis intelligentiam quam ipsi prius vix vel tenuiter videre potuimus, perspicua demonstratione aliis declarare possimus, quid aliud quam quod nocte percipimus sub diei luce proferimus? Recte ergo nulla postmodum fit mentio noctis, ubi Daniel quod prius nocte accepit, tandem aliquando palam loquitur in praesentia regis.

CHAPTER XII With what meditations the spirit must be inflamed to devotion, and how the urgency of our studies must be reformed.

  • 1248B

But let us see what Daniel did after these things, or what is written of him: And having gone into his house, he made the matter known to Ananias, Misael, Azarias, his companions, that they might seek mercy from the face of the God of heaven concerning this sacrament, and that Daniel and his companions might not perish with the rest of the wise men of Babylon (Dan. II). By these three, Daniel's undivided companions and inseparable comrades, we understand circumspection, discretion, deliberation; without which we prove by daily experience that the office of true devotion can in no way be fulfilled. For if we wish to pray inwardly and devoutly, the first thing is that we look about upon our own 1248Clife. Let us see with what studies or exercises we have been accustomed to be occupied, or with what we are occupied still, or what purpose we have in these for the time to come. But what does it profit to bring these things into consideration, unless with the same solicitude we busy ourselves to discern each single thing by strong examination, and to separate the good from the bad by watchful shrewdness, that we may know for what things we ought to beseech the Lord, or even to give thanks? But when our good things or our evil ones are known, it still remains to search out by provident deliberation by what occupation or carefulness we may both make satisfaction for past evils, and extricate oneself from present ones, and more carefully exhibit caution concerning things to come. The first belongs to circumspection, the second to discretion, the third to deliberation. It belongs 1248Dto circumspection to bring the measure of our morals into consideration; it belongs to discretion to make judgment in all things; but to deliberation, to seek and to find counsel. Circumspection says: I will recount to thee all my years in the bitterness of my soul (Isai. XXXVIII). Discretion confesses: I have done judgment and justice (Psal. XCVIII). Deliberation speaks: What shall I render to the Lord for all the things that he hath rendered to me? (Psal. CXV.) She is called back to the pursuit of circumspection to whom it is said: Lift up thine eyes round about (Isai. XLIX), and see then where thou hast lain prostrate, harlot [Vulg. Jer. 3:2 vide ubi non prostrata sis, "see where thou hast not lain prostrate"; the received text carries the negative, which is absent here]. He wished to encourage us to the examination of discretion who said: If we judged ourselves, we should not indeed be judged (I Cor. XI). The office of deliberation is enjoined, or even commended, when it is said through the wise man: Do all things with counsel, 1249Aand unto eternity thou shalt not repent (Eccli. XXXII) [Vulg. Eccli. 32:24 Fili, sine consilio nihil facias, et post factum non poeniteberis, "Son, do nothing without counsel, and after the deed thou shalt not repent"]. But who does not see how necessary the offices of these are to true devotion? For who was ever able to pray inwardly and devoutly, if he has put off looking about upon his life, examining it, ordering it? As often therefore as necessity urges us to apply ourselves more vehemently to devout prayer, we must have recourse to the offices of circumspection and of discretion, or even of deliberation, and by such pursuits and busy meditations inflame our spirit to devotion; and this is for Daniel to take up the supports of his companions for the use of his prayer. And so Daniel as it were runs back to the inner chambers of his house, and seeks fellow workers, as it were, for the more perfect fulfilling of the business of his prayer, when, the time of prayer now pressing, or even the crisis of urgent 1249Bnecessity, the devoted mind seeks again the accustomed opportunity of its own quiet, necessary for that office, and strives to animate or to inflame its devotion by certain provident meditations, apt for such a use. To his companions, he says, he made the matter known. The matter is as it were made known by Daniel to Daniel's companions, when out of devotion it is brought about that the impending matter is brought into consideration, examination, consultation. But mercy is set forth as a thing to be obtained, if the mind stricken with compunction, in that which it devoutly performs, leans not upon its own merits, but upon the divine mercies.

It is certainly to be noted, according to the history, that Daniel the prophet calls that matter a sacrament; whence who would doubt that a mystical understanding of things lies hidden in it? 1249CAnd that Daniel, he says, and his companions might not perish with the rest of the wise men of Babylon. After the example of this thing, when we have seen our mind, after its contempt of secular studies, grow torpid toward the spiritual exercises also, and little by little fail, and the peril of our wise men press upon us, we too ought by all means to ask that of the Lord whereby we may be able to repair or to confirm the accustomed urgency of our studies. But there is, as we alike know, a very great labor both in the pursuit of praying and in that of meditating, and to minds that wander and are restless it is almost intolerable. But in contemplation the delight is wonderful and almost singular. When therefore the grace of contemplation is conferred upon diligent minds, 1249Dthat labor of the other studies is by its sweetness lightened or tempered just so far as it is sweetened. If therefore we have not yet received grace of this kind, or grieve that it has been withdrawn, we ought to seek it from the Lord for the confirming or the reforming of our studies. When therefore we do these things, what else, I ask, do we seem to have done than to seek the dreams of divine revelations, that we may have wherewith we can preserve in their integrity those wise men of ours designated above? Then the mystery was revealed to Daniel by a vision in the night. The revelation of a mystery is as it were perceived by night, when the illumination of truth is seen thinly indeed, and in part, and as it were by the light of the moon. We perceive the revelation of a mystery as it were by night, when concerning some 1250Asecret of the Godhead we ourselves indeed understand what is to be thought, but do not yet have ready to hand the reason by which we can prove it to others also. The light of revelation appears as it were in the night and as though in the midst of darkness, when a man is not able to unfold in speech, and as it were to bring forth into the open light, the declaration of the mystery which he sees through understanding. But when afterwards by long reconsideration we bring it to pass that we can declare to others by clear demonstration the received understanding of the truth, which we ourselves formerly could scarcely or but thinly see, what else do we bring forth under the light of day than what we perceived by night? Rightly therefore is no mention made afterwards of night, where Daniel at length speaks openly in the presence of the king what he had formerly received by night.

CAPUT XIII. Quomodo semper ad innovationem intelligentiae debeamus non nostram industriam, sed Domini magnificentiam magnificare.

  • 1250B

Sed accepta mysterii revelatione videamus adhuc quid Daniel agat vel dicat: Et Daniel benedixit Deo coeli, et locutus ait: Sit nomen Domini benedictum a saeculo, et usque in saeculum, quia sapientia et fortitudo ejus sunt, et ipse mutat tempora et aetates, transfert regna atque constituit (Dan. II). Exemplo discamus quid ipsi facere debeamus, cum revelantis gratiae lumen accipimus. Satagamus itaque juxta Danielis exemplum illi gloriam, et honorem, et gratiarum actionem referre, a quo habemus sapere, 1250Cet intelligere, et quidquid boni nos constat habere. Quod multi sunt qui cum lumen veritatis accipiunt, statim hoc suis meritis, vel studiis ascribunt, seipsos magnificant, et attollunt, caeteros omnes sui comparatione despiciunt. Loquitur apud se anima ejusmodi, super omnes docentes me intellexi. Nititur illud Salomonicum ad se tam imprudenter quam inaniter trahere: Praecessi sapientia omnes qui fuerunt ante me (Eccle. I). Econtra nos demus illi gloriam a quo accepimus intelligentiam. Studeamus semper statim in ipso exortu revelantis gratiae, in ipsa innovatione illuminatae intelligentiae, digna Deo laudum praeconia referre. Buccinate in neomenia tuba in insigni die solemnitatis vestrae. Neomenia, nova luna, nova luna intelligenti innovata, et divinitus 1250Daspirata. Scimus quia luna lumen a seipsa non habet, sed a sole accipit ut lucere possit. Sic sane humana intelligentia divinitus accepit, ut veri luminis radiis renitere possit. Quoties igitur novos divinae illustrationis radios accipimus, et aliquid quod humanam intelligentiam excedit ex divina revelatione cognoscimus, debemus statim in gratiarum actionem assurgere, et magnificentiae tuba in divinae bonitatis praeconia reboare: Magnificate, inquit, Dominum mecum, et exaltemus nomen ejus in idipsum (Psal. XXXIII). Ecce quomodo buccina personuit; ecce quomodo ad clangorem buccinae secum et alios animavit. Ejusmodi buccinae clangorem in nova luna fieri voluit, vel se facturum spopondit, cum dixit: Benedicam Dominum qui tribuit mihi intellectum 1251A(Psal. XV). Sic Daniel cum revelantis gratiae lumen accepit, quasi ad novae lunae exortum magnificentiae tuba personuit, cum ait: Sit nomen Domini benedictum a saeculo, et usque in saeculum, quia sapientia, et fortitudo ejus sunt.

CHAPTER XIII. How at the renewing of understanding we ought always to magnify, not our own effort, but the Lord's magnificence.

  • 1250B

But, the revelation of the mystery having been received, let us further see what Daniel does or says: And Daniel blessed the God of heaven, and speaking said: Blessed be the name of the Lord from everlasting and even unto everlasting, for wisdom and fortitude are his, and he changeth times and ages, transferreth kingdoms and establisheth them (Dan. II). By this example let us learn what we ourselves ought to do when we receive the light of revealing grace. And so let us busy ourselves, after Daniel's example, to render glory and honor and thanksgiving to him from whom we have it to be wise 1250Cand to understand, and whatever of good it is agreed that we have. For there are many who, when they receive the light of truth, at once ascribe this to their own merits or studies, magnify and exalt themselves, and despise all others in comparison with themselves. A soul of this kind speaks within herself: I have understood above all my teachers. She strives to draw to herself that saying of Solomon, as imprudently as emptily: I have gone before in wisdom all who were before me (Eccle. I). On the contrary, let us give glory to him from whom we have received understanding. Let us be zealous always, at the very rising of revealing grace, at the very renewing of an illumined understanding, to render proclamations of praise worthy of God. Blow up the trumpet on the new moon, on the notable day of your solemnity. Neomenia, the new moon; the new moon, renewed for the understanding and divinely 1250Dinspired. We know that the moon has not light from itself, but receives it from the sun that it may be able to shine. So indeed the human understanding has received from God, that it may be able to shine back with the rays of the true light. As often therefore as we receive new rays of divine illumination, and know from divine revelation something that exceeds human understanding, we ought at once to rise up into thanksgiving, and with the trumpet of magnificence to resound in the proclamations of the divine goodness: Magnify, he says, the Lord with me, and let us exalt his name together (Psal. XXXIII). Behold how the trumpet has sounded; behold how at the blast of the trumpet he has roused others also along with himself. He wished a blast of such a trumpet to be made at the new moon, or promised that he would make it, when he said: I will bless the Lord who hath given me understanding 1251A(Psal. XV). So Daniel, when he received the light of revealing grace, sounded as it were the trumpet of magnificence at the rising of the new moon, when he said: Blessed be the name of the Lord from everlasting and even unto everlasting, for wisdom and fortitude are his.

CAPUT XIV. Quod triplex sit verae devotionis officium.

Notandum sane quod triplex sit officium verae et perfectae devotionis. Primum est confessio criminis, secundum postulatio miserationis, tertium glorificatio miserentis. Primum est accusatio culpae, secundum supplicatio veniae, tertium magnificatio divinae munificentiae. Haec omnia iste noster Daniel magnifice exsequitur, sicut ex ejus verbis cognoscitur. Confessio et accusatio est culpae, ubi alio loco Domino 1251Bloquitur in oratione: Peccavimus, iniquitatem fecimus, impie egimus, et recessimus, et declinavimus a mandatis tuis ac judiciis (Dan. IX). Supplicatio, et imploratio est veniae, ubi legis eodem ipso dicente: Exaudi, Domine; placare, Domine; attende, et fac (ibid.). Glorificatio et magnificatio sui Creatoris sibique miserentis est procul dubio quod hoc in loco legis: Sit nomen Domini benedictum a saeculo, et usque in saeculum, quia sapientia, et fortitudo ejus sunt, et ipse mutat tempora et aetates, transfert regna, atque constituit (Dan. II). Sapientia et fortitudo sane accipientis quidem bona, sed procul dubio largientis Dei bona sunt, quia ex ejus utique munere acceperunt quicunque sapientiam et fortitudinem habent, 1251Cquicunque sapienter et fortiter agere potuerunt. Quid enim habes, inquit Apostolus, quod non accepisti? (I Cor. XIV). Ipse, inquit, mutat tempora et aetates. Scimus quia omnia tempus habent, et suis spatiis transeunt universa sub coelo. Tempus plantandi, et tempus evellendi quod plantatum est. Juxta hunc modum, et in aliis rebus considerari potest. Ex occasione itaque temporis opportunitatem accipimus, ut modo haec, modo illa utilius vel expeditius agere valeamus. Nostris itaque usibus tempora mutat, quando nunc harum, illarum virtutum exercitiis opportunitatem accommodat. Sed quid, quaeso, juvat exercendorum accepisse opportunitatem, nisi ex ejusdem largitatis munere accipiamus et exsequendi facultatem? Scimus autem 1251Dquia aetates diversae aliis, et aliis effectibus sunt magis aptae. Certe quae in pueritia discimus profundius retinemus, et expeditius proferimus. In juvenili aetate est virtus corporis robustior, et animus audacior, et idcirco ea aetas ad exercitationis usum efficacior. Usque ad provectiorem aetatem tum multiplex doctrina, tum multiplex rerum experientia quotidianis provectibus scientiam auget, et idcirco ea aetas prae caeteris omnibus consiliis viget, prudentia pollet. Prima itaque dictarum aetatum ad discendum, secunda ad exercitandum, tertia vero praevalet ad discernendum. Juxta similitudinem annorum et meritorum, ut sic dicam, aetates consideramus, et secundum diversorum provectuum differentiam, diversarum virtutum efficaciam nos habere cognoscimus. 1252ADifferentes itaque efficaciae, facultates diversae quodammodo sunt annorum aetates. Temporum ergo et aetatum modos meritorum ille optimus moderator alternat, qui nunc harum, nunc illarum virtutum tam facultatem, quam opportunitatem pietatis suae dispensatione administrat. Transfert regna atque constituit, quando eversa vitiorum tyrannide virtutes regnare facit. De his vitiorum, virtutumve populis, et tam de horum destructione quam illarum exaltatione mystice scribitur, cum per Prophetam dicitur: Vineam de Aegypto transtulisti, ejecisti gentes, et plantasti eam (Psal. LXXIX). Vinea plantatur quando bonum propositum in bona intentione figitur. Gentes ejiciuntur, quando confessionis edicto, professionis voto interminatur peccatis, vitiis abrenuntiatur. 1252BIn eamdem sententiam currit, quod alios idem Propheta de vitiorum exstirpatione, vel virtutum plantatione cavit: Ejecisti gentes, et plantasti eos, afflixisti populos, et expulisti eos (ibid.). Odiosus ille vitiorum populus afflictionis magnitudine expellitur, cum tandem aliquando tam contritione corporis quam compunctione cordis a carnalium desideriorum dominatione anima diu multumque oppressa liberatur. In hunc itaque modum salutis nostrae auctor et protector, dum vitiorum in nobis dominium destruit, virtutes vero in eorum locum restituit, transfert regna, atque constituit. Et recte quidem, quia Domini est regnum, ipse dominabitur gentium: Dat, inquit, sapientiam sapientibus, et scientiam intelligentibus disciplinam. Ipse revelat 1252Cprofunda et abscondita, et novit in tenebris constituta, et lux cum eo est. Nemo ergo de suo ingenio praesumat, nemo interiorum vel exteriorum scientiam suis studiis vel meritis ascribat. Ipse est enim qui dat sapientibus sapientiam, ipse aeque qui dat scientiam intelligentibus disciplinam. In eamdem procul dubio sententiam pergit qui dicit: Omnis sapientia a Domino Deo est, et cum eo fuit semper, et est ante aevum (Eccle. I). Utrique consentit, qui ait: Omne datum optimum, et omne donum perfectum de sursum est, descendens a Patre luminum (Jac. I). Ipse revelat profunda et abscondita, et novit in tenebris constituta. Pro illis quae vere profunda sunt, et humanae intelligentiae modum excedunt, Apostolus exclamat, 1252Dcum dicit: O altitudo sapientiae et scientiae Dei, quam incomprehensibilia judicia ejus, et investigabiles viae ejus! (Rom. XI.) De his tamen ipsis sublimibus et profundis revelat utique per Spiritum suum quantum sciri oportet, vel expedit nobis. Revelat ergo profunda et arcana secreti sui, nec haec tantum, sed et occulta consilii alieni quae nobis aliena malignitas abscondit, ut facilius decipere nos possit. Audisti quid de ejus revelatione optare vel sperare debeamus, audi adhuc quid de ejus scientia timeamus. Novit in tenebris constituta; novit sane intima nostra et quaelibet cordis nostri occulta, nec solum illa quae soli ipsi de nobis scimus, et alios latere volumus, sed ea etiam quae in conscientiae nostrae latebris ipsi penetrare non sufficimus. Revelat ergo sua, revelat 1253Aaliena, novit nostra. Revelat arcana sua, revelat fraudulenta aliena, novit occulta nostra. Revelat profunda dispensationis suae, revelat abscondita fraudis diabolicae, novit tenebrosa conscientiae humanae. Revelat profunda sua, ut sciamus quid de illo credere, quid ab illo sperare debeamus. Revelat abscondita aliena, ut sciamus quid timere, quid cavere debeamus. Novit tenebrosa nostra, ne illum aliquid latere posse credamus, et nos ipsos spe vana decipiamus. Revelat profunda et abscondita, et novit in tenebris constituta: Incerta et occulta sapientiae tuae manifestasti mihi (Psal. L). Ecce revelatio profundorum, et alibi orat in idipsum: Revela oculos meos, et considerabo mirabilia de lege tua (Psal. CXVIII). De absconditis alienis timebat qui dicebat: Educes 1253Bme de laqueo hoc quem absconderunt mihi (Psal. XXX). Ad idipsum respicit quod alias psallit: Linguis suis dolose agebant, venenum aspidum sub labiis eorum (Psal. XIII). Nonne abscondere volebant venenum quod sub lingua portabant? Latebrosa nostra eum latere non posse credebat, qui dicebat: Omnia autem nuda, et aperta sunt oculis Dei (Hebr. IV). Salomonis sententia est quod solus ipse scit omnia, vel sciat omnia corda filiorum hominum (Sap. IX). Cujus sententiae concinit, qui dicit: Scrutans corda et renes Deus (Psal. XII). Sed quid sibi voluit quod dicit, et lux cum eo est. Absque dubio Deus lux est, et in eo tenebrae non sunt ullae. Cur ergo non dicit quod veraciter dicere potuit, quia Deus lux est, sed ait, et cum eo lux est? An forte hoc docere nos voluit, 1253Cquia ubi ipse per inhabitantem gratiam manserit, necessariae cognitionis lumen deesse non possit? Nam lumen quidem quod ipse est, prout ipsum est, in hac utique vita interim videre non possumus; sed lumen quod cum ipso est, non solum ad solatium, sed etiam ad suffragium accipere consuevimus. Quale autem hoc lumen esse dicimus, per quod quid agere, quid vitare, quid diligere, quid odio habere, quid credere vel sperare debemus agnoscimus? Qualis, quaeso, est lux ista quae novit profunda et abscondita, ipso utique revelante, ipso denique inspirante, qui dat sapientibus sapientiam, et scientiam intelligentibus disciplinam? Hucusque Daniel glorificavit Deum pro communi bono, nunc autem pro speciali beneficio sibi sociisque suis specialiter 1253Dimpenso. Tibi, inquit, Deus patrum meorum confiteor, teque laudo, quia sapientiam et fortitudinem dedisti mihi, et nunc ostendisti mihi quae rogavimus te, quia sermonem regis aperuisti nobis. Superius dixit, dat sapientiam sapientibus. Hic autem dicit: Quia sapientiam et fortitudinem dedisti mihi; et item: Quia sermonem regis aperuisti nobis. Praecipuus ordo charitatis bona communia praeferre privatis. Charitas enim non quaerit quae sua sunt, sed quae aliorum. Qui ergo communia bona charitatis instinctu propriis anteposuit, quid mirum si prius hostiam laudis obtulit pro illis quae plus dilexit?

Notandum autem quod tria illa, quae Danielis officio superius assignavimus in hoc uno versu pariter 1254Anotare possumus. Nota vocem confessionis et glorificationis in eo quod legis: Tibi, Deus patrum meorum, confiteor, teque laudo; supplicationis autem preces se obtulisse ostendit in eo quod dicit: Et nunc ostendisti nobis quae rogavimus te. In his autem tribus, ni fallor, verae devotionis officium continetur vel expletur.

CHAPTER XIV. That the office of true devotion is threefold.

It is certainly to be noted that the office of true and perfect devotion is threefold. The first is the confession of the crime, the second the request for mercy, the third the glorification of him who shows mercy. The first is the accusing of the fault, the second the supplication for pardon, the third the magnifying of the divine munificence. All these things this Daniel of ours performs magnificently, as is known from his words. It is the confession and the accusing of fault, where in another place he speaks 1251Bto the Lord in prayer: We have sinned, we have done iniquity, we have acted impiously, and we have departed, and we have turned aside from thy commandments and thy judgments (Dan. IX). It is the supplication and the imploring of pardon, where thou readest, the same man saying: Hear, O Lord; be appeased, O Lord; attend, and do (ibid.). It is without doubt the glorification and magnifying of his Creator, and of him who shows mercy to him, that thou readest in this place: Blessed be the name of the Lord from everlasting and even unto everlasting, for wisdom, and fortitude are his, and he changeth times and ages, transferreth kingdoms, and establisheth them (Dan. II). Wisdom and fortitude are indeed goods of the one who receives them, but without doubt they are the goods of God who bestows them, since whoever have wisdom and fortitude, 1251Cwhoever have been able to act wisely and strongly, have received them from his gift. For what hast thou, says the Apostle, which thou hast not received? (I Cor. XIV). He, he says, changeth times and ages. We know that all things have their time, and that all things under heaven pass in their own spaces. A time to plant, and a time to pluck up that which is planted. After this manner it can be considered in other things also. And so from the occasion of the time we receive an opportunity, that we may be able to do now these things, now those, more profitably or more readily. And so he changes the times for our uses, when now he accommodates opportunity to the exercises of these virtues, of those. But what, I ask, does it profit to have received the opportunity of the things to be practiced, unless from the gift of that same bounty we receive also the faculty of performing them? But we know 1251Dthat different ages are more apt for different effects. Certainly the things we learn in boyhood we retain more deeply and bring forth more readily. In the age of youth the strength of the body is more robust, and the spirit bolder; and therefore that age is more effective for the practice of exercise. Up to a more advanced age both manifold teaching and manifold experience of things increase knowledge by daily advances; and therefore that age, beyond all the rest, is vigorous in counsels and strong in prudence. And so the first of the ages spoken of prevails for learning, the second for practicing, but the third for discerning. After the likeness of years we consider, so to speak, the ages of merits also; and according to the difference of the various advances we know that we have the efficacy of various virtues. 1252AAnd so differing efficacies and diverse faculties are in a manner the ages of years. That best governor of merits therefore alternates the measures of times and of ages, who by the dispensation of his goodness administers both the faculty and the opportunity, now of these virtues, now of those. He transferreth kingdoms and establisheth them, when, the tyranny of vices being overthrown, he makes the virtues to reign. Of these peoples of vices or of virtues, and of the destruction of the one as well as the exaltation of the other, it is mystically written, when it is said through the Prophet: Thou hast brought a vineyard out of Egypt; thou hast cast out the nations, and planted it (Psal. LXXIX). The vineyard is planted when a good purpose is fixed in a good intention. The nations are cast out when, by the edict of confession and the vow of profession, sins are threatened and vices are renounced. 1252BTo the same purport runs that which the same Prophet alios took heed of concerning the extirpation of vices, or the planting of virtues: Thou hast cast out the nations, and planted them; thou hast afflicted the peoples, and driven them out (ibid.) [Vulg. Ps. 43:3 opens Manus tua gentes disperdidit, "Thy hand destroyed the nations"; the opening clause as printed here is that of Ps. 79:9 just quoted, whose object is the vine, eam]. That hateful people of vices is driven out by the greatness of affliction, when at length the soul, long and much oppressed, is set free from the dominion of carnal desires as much by contrition of body as by compunction of heart. And so in this manner the author and protector of our salvation, while he destroys the dominion of vices in us and restores the virtues in their place, transferreth kingdoms and establisheth them. And rightly indeed, for the kingdom is the Lord's, and he shall rule over the nations: He giveth, he says, wisdom to the wise, and knowledge to them that understand instruction. He revealeth 1252Cdeep and hidden things, and knoweth what is set in darkness, and light is with him. Let no one, then, presume upon his own genius; let no one ascribe the knowledge of inward or of outward things to his own studies or merits. For it is he who gives wisdom to the wise, he equally who gives knowledge to them that understand instruction. To the same purport without doubt goes he who says: All wisdom is from the Lord God, and hath been with him always, and is before all time (Eccle. I). With both agrees he who says: Every best gift, and every perfect gift, is from above, coming down from the Father of lights (Jac. I). He revealeth deep and hidden things, and knoweth what is set in darkness. For those things which are truly deep, and exceed the measure of human understanding, the Apostle cries out 1252Dwhen he says: O the depth of the wisdom and of the knowledge of God, how incomprehensible are his judgments, and his ways past finding out! (Rom. XI.) [Vulg. O altitudo divitiarum sapientiae et scientiae Dei, "O the depth of the riches of the wisdom and of the knowledge of God"] Yet of these very sublime and deep things he does reveal, through his Spirit, as much as it behooves us to know, or is expedient for us. He reveals therefore the deep things and the secrets of his own hiddenness; nor these only, but also the hidden things of another's counsel, which an alien malice conceals from us, that it may the more easily deceive us. Thou hast heard what we ought to desire or to hope for concerning his revelation; hear further what we should fear concerning his knowledge. He knoweth what is set in darkness; he knows indeed our inmost things and whatever is hidden of our heart, and not only those things which we alone know of ourselves and wish to be hidden from others, but also those which we ourselves are not able to penetrate in the hiding places of our conscience. He reveals therefore his own things, he reveals 1253Aanother's, he knows ours. He reveals his own secrets, he reveals another's frauds, he knows our hidden things. He reveals the deep things of his own dispensation, he reveals the hidden things of the devil's fraud, he knows the dark things of the human conscience. He reveals his own deep things, that we may know what we ought to believe of him, what to hope from him. He reveals another's hidden things, that we may know what we ought to fear, what to beware of. He knows our dark things, lest we believe that anything can lie hidden from him, and deceive ourselves with a vain hope. He revealeth deep and hidden things, and knoweth what is set in darkness: The uncertain and hidden things of thy wisdom thou hast made manifest to me (Psal. L). Behold the revelation of deep things; and elsewhere he prays to the same effect: Unveil mine eyes, and I will consider the wondrous things of thy law (Psal. CXVIII). He feared another's hidden things who said: Thou wilt bring 1253Bme out of this snare which they have hidden for me (Psal. XXX). To the same thing looks what he sings elsewhere: With their tongues they acted deceitfully; the venom of asps is under their lips (Psal. XIII). Did they not wish to hide the venom which they carried under their tongue? He believed that our lurking places could not lie hidden from him, who said: But all things are naked and open to the eyes of God (Hebr. IV) [Vulg. Heb. 4:13 oculis ejus, "to his eyes"]. It is Solomon's judgment that he alone knows all things, or knows all the hearts of the sons of men (Sap. IX). With whose judgment he agrees who says: God searcheth the hearts and the reins (Psal. XII). But what did he mean by saying, and light is with him. Without doubt God is light, and in him there are no darknesses at all. Why then does he not say what he could truly have said, that God is light, but says, and with him is light? Or did he perhaps wish to teach us this, 1253Cthat where he himself has abided through indwelling grace, the light of necessary knowledge cannot be lacking? For the light indeed which he himself is, as it is in itself, we certainly cannot meanwhile see in this life; but the light which is with him we have been accustomed to receive, not only for solace, but also for support. But of what sort do we say this light to be, through which we recognize what we ought to do, what to avoid, what to love, what to hold in hatred, what to believe or to hope? Of what sort, I ask, is that light which knows deep and hidden things, he indeed revealing, he finally inspiring, who gives wisdom to the wise, and knowledge to them that understand instruction? Thus far Daniel has glorified God for the common good; but now for the special benefit specially bestowed 1253Dupon himself and upon his companions. To thee, he says, God of my fathers, I confess, and thee I praise, because thou hast given me wisdom and fortitude, and now thou hast shown me the things we asked of thee, because thou hast opened to us the word of the king. [Vulg. Dan. 2:23 Deus patrum nostrorum, "God of our fathers"] Above he said, he giveth wisdom to the wise. But here he says: Because thou hast given me wisdom and fortitude; and again: Because thou hast opened to us the word of the king. The chief order of charity, to prefer common goods to private ones. For charity seeketh not the things that are her own, but the things of others. He therefore who by the instinct of charity set common goods before his own, what wonder if he first offered the sacrifice of praise for those things which he loved more?

But it is to be noted that those three things which we assigned above to Daniel's office we can equally 1254Amark in this one verse. Mark the voice of confession and of glorification in that which thou readest: To thee, God of my fathers, I confess, and thee I praise; and he shows that he offered the prayers of supplication in that which he says: And now thou hast shown to us the things we asked of thee. [Vulg. Dan. 2:23 has "hast shown ME" here, and the lemma printed above has it so too; this re-quotation gives "to us"] But in these three, unless I am mistaken, the office of true devotion is contained or fulfilled.

CAPUT XV. Quomodo debeamus aequitatis regulam arctius consulere, eo maxime tempore quo oportet quodlibet sublimitatis officium explere.

Post haec Daniel ingressus ad Arioch, quem constituerat rex ut perderet sapientes Babylonis, sic ei locutus est: Sapientes Babylonis ne perdas, introduc me in conspectu regis, et solutionem regi narrabo 1254B(Dan. II). Si per Danielem recte fervor devotionis, per Arioch intelligitur districtio aequitatis, quid erit aliud ad Arioch velle intrare, nisi secretioria aequitatis arcana profundius quaerere, et quid districta justitia exigat, intima perscrutatione perspicere velle? Hoc autem negotium Danieli tunc recte ascribitur, quando mens humana in idipsum ex magna animi devotione urgetur. Daniel itaque ad Arioch intrat, quando aequitatis consulta devotio quaeritat. Est et alia adhuc intrandi ad eum causa (nec forte minus necessaria) quando ei scilicet medullitus infundere volumus, quod ex divina revelatione in ejus sensibus corrigendum agnovimus. Ibi ad eum intramus, ut ex multa investigatione, justitiaeque examinatione, quid sit rectum agnoscamus; hic ad illum 1254Cingredimur, ut rectum divinitus agnitum rectitudinis zelo arctius imprimamus. Ibi itaque intramus causa discendi, hic causa docendi; vel illic causa quaerendi, hic causa persuadendi. Sed ingressionis causam citius advertimus, si quid dicat attendimus: Sapientes Babylonis ne perdas. Ecce quod rex turbatus fieri praecepit, Daniel divinitus illuminatus fieri prohibuit, et fit saepe, ut quod turbati, et per impetum animi velut rectum discernimus, et quasi Arioch officio fieri posse vel debere praesumimus, postmodum ad preces et orationem conversi, et inter compunctionis nostrae lacrymas divinitus illuminati, injustum esse invenimus quod prius rectum putabamus. Sic devotio saepe iniquum esse invenit, fierique defendit, quod violentus animi impetus quasi 1254Dsub regio furore fieri decrevit: Sapientes, inquit, Babylonis ne perdas. Et addit: Introduc me in conspectu regis, et solutionem regi narrabo. Quid, quaeso, hoc loco ab alio se introduci petit, qui jam superius ante regis conspectum per semetipsum intravit? Sed superius intravit, ut regi supplicaret; hic vero, ut regem doceret. Illud fuit humilitatis, istud magnae sublimitatis. Magnae, inquam, sublimitatis fuit illud regem docere, quod ab alio nemine poterat didicisse. Ad humilitatis ergo obsequia libenter seipsum ingessit, ad celsitudinis officia impudenter se ingerere declinavit. Exemplo discamus quid in ejusmodi facere debeamus. Cum locum magisterii sive ex praecepto, sive pro officio suspicere 1255Aoportet, prius mens ad Arioch introeat. Justitiae consulta requirat, et ut bonum opus bene et juste exsequatur, sollicita examinatione praevideat. Fac, inquit, bene bonum. Bonum tum fit, quando opus bonum ex bona intentione surgit, et modo congruo procedit. Facile est ut ambitio vel cenodoxia ad conspicuum et praeclarum nos opus impellat. Facile est ut fermentum Pharisaeorum, totam boni operis nostri massam corrumpat. Nullo modo ab Arioch introducimur, quando ad opus bonum sinistra intentione impellimur. Quoties igitur docendi locum suscipimus, praevideamus quam juste, quam ordinate susceptum officium expleamus. Videamus ne aliquid falsum vel frivolum, ne aliquid ineptum vel inordinatum dicamus. Hanc, ni fallor, discendi vel docendi regulam 1255Bcustodit, cui illud Psalmistae convenire potuit: Jucundus homo qui miseretur et commodat, disponet sermones suos in judicio (Psal. CXI). Et forte hoc erit Arioch exterius consulere, contra sermonis vel operis vitia nos ipsos rectitudinis consiliis praemunire. Tunc autem quasi intrinsecus consulitur, quando de interioribus quaeritur, et contra cogitationum vel afflictionum corruptelam, justitiae institutis anima praemunitur. Maxime eo tempore debemus ad Arioch intrare, et ejus manuductionem quaerere, et nihil sine justitiae praemeditatione vel munitione agere; maxime, inquam, eo tempore ubi debemus celsitudinis loco apparere, vel sublimitatis officium explere. Introduc me, inquit, in conspectu regis, et solutionem 1255Cregi narrabo. Tunc autem Daniel noster veraciter dicti Arioch manuductione utitur, quando ex recordatione divinae districtionis, veraeque justitiae consideratione animus compungitur. Et tunc quasi ante regiam praesentiam ab ejusmodi duce introducitur, quando sub quovis animi impetu, et quasi sub regiae potestatis praesentia, per districti examinis respectum cor hominis ad devotionem facile inflammatur.

CHAPTER XV. How we ought to consult the rule of equity the more strictly, at that time above all when some office of sublimity must be fulfilled.

After this Daniel, going in to Arioch, whom the king had appointed to destroy the wise men of Babylon, spoke to him thus: Destroy not the wise men of Babylon; bring me in before the king, and I will tell the king the solution 1254B(Dan. II). If by Daniel the fervor of devotion is rightly understood, and by Arioch the strictness of equity, what else will it be to wish to go in to Arioch than to seek out more deeply the secretioria secrets of equity, and to wish to discern by innermost searching what strict justice demands? Now this business is rightly ascribed to Daniel at that time when the human mind is urged to this very thing out of a great devotion of spirit. Daniel therefore goes in to Arioch when devotion seeks out the counsels of equity. There is also yet another cause for going in to him (and perhaps not a less necessary one), namely when we wish to pour into him, to the very marrow, what we have recognized from divine revelation as needing correction in his senses. There we go in to him that, from much investigation and examination of justice, we may recognize what is right; here we enter in to him 1254Cthat what has been divinely recognized as right we may press home the more strictly with a zeal for rectitude. There, then, we enter for the sake of learning, here for the sake of teaching; or there for the sake of inquiring, here for the sake of persuading. But we perceive the cause of his entering the sooner if we attend to what he says: Destroy not the wise men of Babylon. Behold, what the king in his trouble commanded to be done, Daniel, divinely enlightened, forbade to be done; and it often happens that what, being troubled and carried by an impulse of the spirit, we discern as though it were right, and presume can or ought to be done as by the office of Arioch, afterwards, having turned to prayers and supplication and been divinely enlightened amid the tears of our compunction, we find to be unjust, which before we thought right. So devotion often finds that to be iniquitous, and forbids its being done, which a violent impulse of the spirit had decreed should be done as it were 1254Dunder a royal fury: Destroy not, he says, the wise men of Babylon. And he adds: Bring me in before the king, and I will tell the king the solution. Why, I ask, does he in this place beg to be brought in by another, who already above entered before the king's sight by himself? But above he entered that he might make supplication to the king; here, however, that he might teach the king. That was of humility, this of great sublimity. Of great sublimity, I say, was it to teach the king that which he could have learned from no one else. To the services of humility, therefore, he thrust himself willingly; to the offices of eminence he declined to thrust himself shamelessly. By his example let us learn what we ought to do in matters of this kind. When the place of mastery must be looked up to, whether from a command or for an office, 1255Alet the mind first enter in to Arioch. Let it seek out the counsels of justice, and by anxious examination let it look ahead, that it may carry out a good work well and justly. Do the good well, he says. The good is then done, when a good work rises from a good intention and proceeds in a fitting manner. It is easy for ambition or vainglory to drive us to a conspicuous and illustrious work. It is easy for the leaven of the Pharisees to corrupt the whole lump of our good work. In no way are we brought in by Arioch when we are driven to a good work by a sinister intention. As often, therefore, as we take up the place of teaching, let us look ahead to how justly, how orderly we are to fulfil the office we have taken up. Let us see to it that we say nothing false or frivolous, nothing inept or disordered. This rule of learning and of teaching, unless I am mistaken, 1255Bhe keeps to whom that word of the Psalmist could apply: Pleasant is the man who has mercy and lends; he shall order his words in judgment (Psal. CXI). And perhaps this will be to consult Arioch outwardly: to fortify ourselves beforehand with counsels of rectitude against the vices of speech or of work. But he is consulted as it were inwardly when inquiry is made concerning inward things, and the soul is fortified beforehand by the ordinances of justice against the corruption of thoughts or of afflictions. Above all at that time ought we to go in to Arioch, and to seek his leading by the hand, and to do nothing without the forethought or the fortification of justice; above all, I say, at that time when we must appear in a place of eminence, or fulfil an office of sublimity. Bring me in, he says, before the king, and I will tell the solution 1255Cto the king. Now our Daniel truly uses the leading by the hand of the said Arioch when, from the recollection of the divine strictness and the consideration of true justice, the spirit is pricked with compunction. And then he is brought in as it were before the royal presence by such a leader, when under any impulse of the spirit whatever, and as it were in the presence of royal power, the heart of man is easily inflamed to devotion by the regard of a strict examination.

CAPUT XVI. Quibus gradibus mysteriorum cognitio proficit, et ad quantam scientiae perfectionem animus saepe merito devotionis ascendit.

Et solutionem, inquit, regi narrabo. Quae est ista mysticae visionis solutio, nisi sacramentorum mysteriorumque divinorum facilis enodatio, et prompta 1255Dexplicatio? Si autem per regem liberi arbitrii potestatem oportet intelligere, quid aliud erit ejusmodi solutionem ad regiam notitiam pervenire, quam mysteriorum divinorum rationem palam et in promptu habere? Si igitur liberum, et promptum, et in potestate nostra habemus occultorum enodationem in commune proferre cum voluimus, jam mysteriorum divinorum scientiam, et enodationem in nostro arbitrio esse, et quasi ad regiam potestatem jam pervenisse non dubitamus. Haec autem tanta scientiae vel doctrinae dilatatio Danieli tunc recte ascribitur, quando ejusmodi gratia merito devotionis acquiritur et possidetur. Videamus ergo quibus promotionis gradibus divinorum cognitio dilatatur. Primo omnium Daniel mysticae visionis notitiam ex divina revelatione 1256Aaccepit. Postea etiam ejus utique sociis res eadem innotuit, tandem autem ejusdem rei scientia usque ad regis notitiam pervenit. De primo dicitur: Tunc Danieli per visionem nocte mysterium revelatum est. Postmodum autem tam pro se, quam pro sociis suis Daniel profitetur: Quia sermonem, inquit, regis aperuisti nobis. Et nunc de propalanda regi ejusdem rei notitia idem ipse pollicetur. Introduc me in conspectum regis, et solutionem regi narrabo. Et ut breviter absolvam quid super his sentiam, alii in sua oratione, alii in sua meditatione. alii autem in ipsa sua saepe locutione, revelationem divinam accipiunt, et sublimia quaelibet atque profunda ex divina inspiratione cognoscunt. Verumtamen non solum illud quod orando, sed et illud etiam quod meditando 1256Bvel loquendo subtiliter penetramus, tunc etiam recte Danieli attribuimus, quando horum quodlibet merito devotionis acquirimus.
