Quidam ita que sunt qui nunquam nisi post intimam compunctionem, et quasi post cordis emundationem ad sublimia contemplanda vel penetranda ascendere possunt, qualium personam Job in seipso expressit, cum dicit: Antequam comedam suspiro, et tanquam inundantes aquae sic rugitus meus (Job. III). Antequam comedat suspirare habet necesse, qui nisi per compunctionis gratiam, et post lacrymarum effusionem, ad intimam animae refectionem, veritatisque pabula nullo modo potest pertingere. Alii in suis meditationibus divinis revelationibus copiose 1256Caffluunt, et forte adhuc tales sunt, qui sine praemeditatione, vel subtilia penetrare, vel subtiliter disserere omnino non possunt. Priores itaque illi suis orationibus, isti autem suis meditationibus ignem sibi divinae revelationis accendunt: Et in meditatione mea exardescet ignis, ait Psalmista (Psal. XXXVIII). Ignis in meditatione exardescit, quando animus et propriis investigationibus ad divinae revelationis lucem, arcanorumque manifestationem proficit. Ignis in meditatione exardescit, quando ex propria inquisitione ad veritatis cognitionem, et amorem animus incandescit. Quosdam autem constat ad tantam eruditionem profecisse, ut possint omni hora de subtilibus quibusque subtiliter disserere, et ad quaelibet interrogata (sine praemeditatione 1256Daliqua) pene semper sufficienter respondere, parati semper, quemadmodum scriptum est, ad satisfactionem omni poscenti rationem et ea quae in nobis est fide, et spe (I Petr. III). Quidam autem ex eis qui ejusmodi sunt divinae revelationis, lucem tam familiarem habent, ut multa eis etiam inter loquendum revelentur, ex illis quae ipsi prius ignorabant. Quandiu igitur per solam cordis compunctionem divinam revelationem animus obtinet, quasi solus Daniel usque adhuc mysteriorum notitiam habet. Quando autem meditationibus nostris tam divina revelatio occurrit, jam mysticae visionis agnitio usque ad Danielis socios pervenit. Cum autem jam coeperit mens illustrantem gratiam tam familiarem, et quasi ad manum habere, ut 1257Apro arbitrio et voluntate possit, cum placuerit, ad quaelibet discutienda, astruenda, defendenda sub omni pene hora sufficere, jam occultorum enodatio incipit se usque ad regiam notitiam dilatare. Nonne regio quodam jure in facultate profundiorum scientia, vel eruditio possidetur, quando pro voluntatis arbitrio de quibuslibet mysticis et profundis, facile et absque ulla difficultate disseritur? Ecce ad quantam scientiae perfectionem saepe animus merito devotionis perducitur, ecce qualis fructus saepe veram devotionem sequitur. Hunc devotionis suae fructum provida mens antequam obtineat saepe et prudenter praevidet, et fidenter exspectat. Unde fit maxime, ut devotionis studiis vehementius incumbat. Solutionem, inquit, regi narrabo. Et additur: 1257BTunc Arioch festinus introduxit Danielem ad regem, et dixit ei: Inveni hominem de filiis transmigrationis Judae qui solutionem regi significet. Ecce, ut audis, accepta promissione solutionis illius quam rex multum desideravit, Danielem Arioch ad regem introduxit. Ut superius dictum est, Daniel ad Arioch manuducitur, quando ex conspectu districtae aequitatis fervor devotionis inflammatur. Multis autem modis atque respectibus humanus compungitur animus. Optimus autem pene modus compunctionis qui surgit ex consideratione districti examinis. Daniel itaque non a quovis, sed quasi ab Arioch tunc introducitur, quando ex consideratione verae justitiae minus servatae, animus intime compungitur. Ad regem 1257Cintroducitur, et quasi regiae potestati assistitur, quando vera devotio ex virtute possidetur, et ea animus pro arbitrio utitur. Festine introducitur, quando facile, et absque mora cum oportuerit, ad cordis contritionem animus emollitur. Studeat ergo ejusmodi facultatem, seu facilitatem compunctionis et devotionis habere, qui ad eam quam superius assignavimus scientiae dilatationem cupit attingere. Prius est enim ut Daniel per Arioch, et cum festinatione introeat, quam regi sermo desideratus innotescat.

CHAPTER XVI. By what steps the knowledge of mysteries advances, and to how great a perfection of learning the spirit often ascends by the merit of devotion.

And I will tell the king, he says, the solution. What is this solution of the mystic vision, but the easy unravelling and ready 1255Dexplication of the divine sacraments and mysteries? But if by the king we must understand the power of free will, what else will it be for such a solution to come to the royal cognizance, than to have the account of the divine mysteries openly and ready at hand? If therefore we have it free and ready and in our own power to bring forth in common the unravelling of hidden things whenever we will, we do not doubt that the learning and unravelling of the divine mysteries is already in our own choosing, and has already come, as it were, to the royal power. Now so great an enlargement of learning or of teaching is rightly ascribed to Daniel at that time when such grace is acquired and possessed by the merit of devotion. Let us see, then, by what steps of advancement the knowledge of divine things is enlarged. First of all Daniel received cognizance of the mystic vision from divine 1256Arevelation. Afterwards the same thing became known to his companions also, and at last the learning of that same thing came even to the king's cognizance. Of the first it is said: Then to Daniel the mystery was revealed by a vision in the night. [Vulg. per visum nocte, "by a sight in the night"] But afterwards Daniel professes, as much for himself as for his companions: Because the word, he says, of the king thou hast opened to us. And now the same man promises concerning the publishing to the king of the cognizance of that same thing. Bring me in before the king, and I will tell the king the solution. And that I may briefly discharge what I think upon these things: some in their prayer, some in their meditation. and others often in their very speaking, receive divine revelation, and come to know whatever things are sublime and profound out of divine inspiration. Nevertheless not only that which we penetrate subtly by praying, but that also which we penetrate by meditating 1256Bor by speaking, we then rightly attribute to Daniel, when we acquire any of these by the merit of devotion. There are certain men, then ita que, who can never ascend to the contemplating or the penetrating of sublime things save after inward compunction and as it were after a cleansing of the heart; whose person Job expressed in himself when he says: Before I eat I sigh, and like overflowing waters, so is my roaring (Job. III). He must needs sigh before he eats, who in no way can reach the inward refreshment of the soul and the pastures of truth save through the grace of compunction and after a pouring out of tears. Others in their meditations abound copiously 1256Cin divine revelations; and perhaps there are still such as cannot at all, without premeditation, either penetrate subtle things or discourse subtly. Those former, then, kindle for themselves the fire of divine revelation by their prayers, but these by their meditations: And in my meditation a fire shall flame out, says the Psalmist (Psal. XXXVIII). The fire flames out in meditation when the spirit advances, even by its own investigations, to the light of divine revelation and to the manifestation of hidden things. The fire flames out in meditation when, out of its own inquiry, the spirit grows white-hot toward the knowledge and the love of truth. But it is agreed that some have advanced to so great an instruction that they can at every hour discourse subtly upon any subtle matters whatever, and can answer sufficiently, almost always, to whatever is asked them (without any premeditation 1256D), being always ready, as it is written, to give satisfaction to everyone that asks a reason, and with that faith and hope which is in us (I Petr. III) [Vulg. de ea, quae in vobis est, spe, "concerning that hope which is in you"; the received text has no word for faith here, and reads "in you" for "in us"]. But certain of those who are of this kind have the light of divine revelation so familiar that many things are revealed to them even while they are speaking, out of those things which they themselves before knew not. As long, therefore, as the spirit obtains divine revelation through compunction of heart alone, Daniel as it were alone has thus far the cognizance of the mysteries. But when so divine a revelation occurs to our meditations, then the recognition of the mystic vision has come even to Daniel's companions. But when the mind has begun to have the illuminating grace so familiar and as it were at hand that 1257Ait can, at its own choosing and will, whenever it please, be sufficient at almost every hour for whatever things are to be discussed, established, defended, then the unravelling of hidden things begins to enlarge itself even to the royal cognizance. Is not the learning, or the instruction, of deeper things possessed as a faculty by a kind of royal right, when at the choice of the will one discourses easily and without any difficulty upon whatever mystic and profound matters? Behold to how great a perfection of learning the spirit is often led by the merit of devotion; behold what fruit often follows true devotion. This fruit of its devotion the provident mind often and prudently foresees before it obtains it, and confidently awaits. Whence it comes above all that it presses the more vehemently upon the zeal of devotion. I will tell the king, he says, the solution. And it is added: 1257BThen Arioch in haste brought Daniel in to the king, and said to him: I have found a man of the sons of the transmigration of Judas, who may signify the solution to the king. [Vulg. annuntiet, "may announce" — the reading Richard's own exposition re-quotes twice at 1257D and again at 1259A] Behold, as thou hearest, when the promise of that solution which the king greatly desired had been received, Arioch brought Daniel in to the king. As was said above, Daniel is led by the hand to Arioch when, from the sight of strict equity, the fervor of devotion is inflamed. Now the human spirit is pricked with compunction in many ways and in many regards. But almost the best mode of compunction is that which rises from the consideration of the strict examination. Daniel therefore is then brought in not by anyone whatever, but as it were by Arioch, when from the consideration of true justice too little kept, the spirit is inwardly pricked with compunction. He is brought in to the king 1257Cand stands as it were beside the royal power, when true devotion is possessed out of virtue, and the spirit uses it at its own choosing. He is brought in in haste when easily, and without delay when need requires, the spirit is softened to contrition of heart. Let him therefore be zealous to have such a faculty, or facility, of compunction and of devotion, who desires to attain to that enlargement of learning which we assigned above. For it comes first that Daniel enter in through Arioch, and with haste, before the desired word becomes known to the king.

CAPUT XVII. Quod ille summum devotionis culmen attingit, qui quidquid sibi molestiae etiam praeter meritum accidit, suis meritis ascribit.

Inveni, inquit Arioch, hominem de filiis transmigrationis 1257DJudae, qui solutionem regi annuntiet. Ab Arioch invenitur, quod aequitatis judicio justum et rectum esse deprehenditur. Invenit itaque Arioch Danielem idoneum, et dignum qui tantam acciperet gratiam, qui mysteriorum revelationem divinitus perciperet, qui solutionem regi annuntiaret. Et forte quod de filiis transmigrationis Judae fuit, quam longam captivitatem patienter sustinuit, Danielis meritum in idipsum adjuvit, et eum ad tantae gratiae susceptionem aptiorem exhibuit. Iste Daniel utique de Jerusalem in Babylonem captivatus fuerat, et fortassis nihil tale commiserat unde ejusmodi captivitatem incurreret, nisi eum communis casus involveret, et aliena malitia injuste opprimeret. Nihilominus tamen meritis suis ascribebat captivitatem 1258Aquam sine culpa sustinebat. Peccavimus, inquit, iniquitatem fecimus, impie egimus, et recessimus, et declinavimus a mandatis tuis ac judiciis (Dan. IX). Recte ergo de filiis transmigrationis Judae dicitur, qui etiam inter immeritae oppressionis flagella Domino humiliter confitetur. Nam et Judas confitens interpretatur. Vide ergo quam recte divinitus glorificari meruit qui divinam gloriam (non propriam) in tanta, et tali afflictione vivendo, et confitendo quaesivit. Quam multi non tam propria culpa, quam aliena malitia notam suspicionis incurrunt, et quasi de Hierusalem ad Babylonem, et veluti de loco pacis et quietis ad locum confusionis et captivitatis transeunt. Hierusalem namque visio pacis, Babylon confusio interpretatur. Quid itaque est de Jerusalem in 1258BBabylonem injuste captivum transire, nisi sub sinistra, sed tamen falsa, suspicione laborare? Pravorum est semper bonorum vitam lacerare, et cum vera non possunt, falsa tamen criminatione impetere, et cum ad operum infamationem deficiunt, simplicem illum intentionis eorum oculum nota, si possunt, sinistrae suspicionis obducunt. Velut igitur in modum Danielis mens justi sub injusta captivitate apud Babylonem tenetur, quando falsa suspicione laborans, injustae confusionis opprobrio tam impudenter quam irreverenter affligitur. Nonne sub Babylonica, ut sic dicam, afflictione gemebat, qui dicebat: Tota die verecundia mea contra me est, et confusio faciei meae cooperuit me (Psal. XLIII). Cogita 1258Ctamen illam confusionis suae captivitatem quam tunc sustinuit, quam sine culpa tunc temporis portavit, qui secura conscientia dicere potuit: Haec omnia venerunt super nos, nec obliti sumus te, et inique non egimus in testamento tuo (ibid.) Sed cum inter instantia flagella praesentem in nobis culpam minime deprehendimus, nec tunc quidem securi esse debemus. Ad hoc nos suo exemplo erudivit, qui ait: Nihil mihi conscius sum, sed non in hoc justificatus sum (I Cor. IV). Saepe etenim pro praecedenti, saepe pro praesenti, sed latenti, culpa percutimur, et pro tali quidem quam omnino nullam, vel forte satisfactione deletam credebamus. Et quidem, quod omnino fatendum est, non semper ad correptionem, seu ad purgationem percutimur. Sed modo ad eruditionem, 1258Dmodo ad probationem, seu alio quolibet modo flagellamur. Sed verae et perfectae devotionis est poenam quam patitur suis semper meritis ascribere, et culpam quam in se minime deprehendit, ex occulto timere. Ille itaque devotionis fervor quasi summum perfectionis culmen jam attigit, qui quodlibet injustum confusionis opprobrium, et quidquid sibi etiam praeter meritum accidit, suis meritis ascribit, qui in omni afflictione sua Dominum glorificat, et semetipsum in omnibus sine simultate accusat. Quam recte ille devotionis fervor inter filios transmigrationis Judae deputatur, qui inter quaelibet injustae exprobrationis ignominiosa semper Domino humiliter confitetur. Saepe autem qui ejusmodi sunt qui divinam prae caeteris gloriam quaerunt, qui scipsos 1259Ainglorios vident, et videri non refugiunt, supra caeteros gratiam divinitus percipiunt. Inveni, inquit, hominem de filiis transmigrationis, qui solutionem regi annuntiet.

CHAPTER XVII. That he attains the highest summit of devotion who ascribes to his own merits whatever trouble befalls him, even beyond his desert.

I have found, says Arioch, a man of the sons of the transmigration 1257Dof Judas, who may announce the solution to the king. That is found by Arioch which by the judgment of equity is discerned to be just and right. Arioch therefore found Daniel fit, and worthy to receive so great a grace, to perceive the revelation of the mysteries from God, to announce the solution to the king. And perhaps the fact that he was of the sons of the transmigration of Judas, and bore that long captivity patiently, helped Daniel's merit toward this very thing, and showed him the fitter for the receiving of so great a grace. This Daniel had assuredly been carried captive from Jerusalem into Babylon, and perhaps had committed nothing such as to incur a captivity of this kind, save that the common lot enfolded him and another's malice unjustly oppressed him. Nonetheless he ascribed to his own merits the captivity 1258Awhich he bore without guilt. We have sinned, he says, we have committed iniquity, we have done wickedly, and we have departed, and we have turned aside from thy commandments and thy judgments (Dan. IX). Rightly, then, is he said to be of the sons of the transmigration of Judas, who even amid the scourges of an undeserved oppression humbly confesses to the Lord. For Judas too is interpreted confessing. See therefore how rightly he deserved to be divinely glorified, who in so great and such an affliction sought the divine glory (not his own) by living and by confessing. How many incur the mark of suspicion not so much by their own fault as by another's malice, and pass as it were from Hierusalem to Babylon, and as though from a place of peace and quiet to a place of confusion and captivity. For Hierusalem is interpreted a vision of peace, Babylon confusion. What, then, is it to pass unjustly, as a captive, from Jerusalem into 1258BBabylon, but to labor under a sinister, yet false, suspicion? It belongs to the depraved always to tear at the life of the good, and, when they cannot do it with true charges, to assail them nevertheless with false accusation; and when they fail at the defaming of their works, they overspread that simple eye of their intention, if they can, with the mark of a sinister suspicion. Just so, therefore, after the manner of Daniel, the mind of the just man is held under an unjust captivity at Babylon, when, laboring under a false suspicion, it is afflicted as shamelessly as irreverently by the reproach of an unjust confusion. Was he not groaning under a Babylonian affliction, so to speak, who said: All the day my shame is against me, and the confusion of my face has covered me (Psal. XLIII). Consider 1258Cnevertheless that captivity of his confusion which he then bore, which at that time he carried without guilt, who with a secure conscience could say: All these things came upon us, and we have not forgotten thee, and we have not dealt unjustly in thy testament (ibid.) But when, amid pressing scourges, we detect in ourselves no present fault, not even then ought we to be secure. To this he instructed us by his own example who says: I am conscious to myself of nothing, but I am not hereby justified (I Cor. IV). For often we are struck for a preceding fault, often for a present but hidden one, and indeed for such a fault as we believed to be no fault at all, or perhaps blotted out by satisfaction. And indeed, as must be altogether confessed, we are not always struck for correction or for purgation. But we are scourged now for instruction, 1258Dnow for testing, or in some other way whatever. But it belongs to true and perfect devotion always to ascribe to its own merits the punishment it suffers, and to fear from what is hidden the fault which it does not detect in itself. That fervor of devotion, therefore, has already reached as it were the highest summit of perfection, which ascribes to its own merits any unjust reproach of confusion, and whatever befalls it even beyond its desert; which glorifies the Lord in all its affliction, and accuses itself in all things without pretense. How rightly is that fervor of devotion reckoned among the sons of the transmigration of Judas, which amid whatever ignominies of unjust reproach always humbly confesses to the Lord. But often those who are of this kind, who seek the divine glory before all others, who see themselves scipsos 1259Awithout glory and do not shrink from being so seen, receive grace from God above the rest. I have found, he says, a man of the sons of the transmigration, who may announce the solution to the king.

CAPUT XVIII. Quod de virtute devotionis saepe minus praesumimus quam debeamus, et quod aliud est munus mysticae intelligentiae, et aliud munus mysticae doctrinae.

Respondit rex, et dixit Danieli, cujus nomen erat Balthasar: Putasne vere potes indicare mihi somnium, quod vidi, et interpretationem ejus? Regem Danieli respondere, suamque diffidentiam ostendere, nihil aliud est quam ejusmodi animi vitium, veram devotionem non posse latere. Saepe enim vitia quaedam quae alio tempore non possumus, 1259Bcompunctionis nostrae hora, et inter devotionis nostrae lacrymas facile deprehendimus. Perfecta siquidem devotio omnia discutit, omnia dijudicat. Spiritualis enim dijudicat omnia, et ipse a nemine judicatur.

Notandum sane quod Daniel dum profunda et mystica requirit, cum sublimia et subtilia pollicetur, Balthasar appellatur, quod capillus capitis interpretatur. Est autem capillus capitis, ut nostis, longus admodum atque subtilis. Quid itaque capillus capitis, nisi subtilis profunditas, et profunda subtilitas mentis? Et perfecta devotio profecto mentem et ad profunditatem illuminat, et ad omnem subtilitatem exacuit. Est autem et capillus capitis ornatus, et mens utique hominis ex vera devotione in 1259Cquantum a vitiis purgatur, in quantum ad veritatem illuminatur, in quantum ad virtutem inflammatur, in tantum procul dubio decoratur, vel ornatur. Nota ergo quod et ille de Balthasar promissione diffidit, qui ei tale nomen imposuit. Nam a rege utique ejusmodi nomen accepit. Et fit quidem ut mens ad miram subtilitatem ex multa devotione exacui se sentiat atque intelligat, et tamen sub tempore necessitatis minus quam valeat, vel debeat de virtute devotionis praesumat. Putasne vere, inquit, potes indicare mihi somnium quod vidi, et interpretationem ejus? Et Daniel superius: Introduc me in conspectu regis et solutionem regi narrabo. Ecce unde Daniel praesumit, fidenterque promittit, regia 1259Dsollicitudo diffidit. Totum hoc saepe apud unum eumdemque animum agitur, et pene quot affectibus tangitur, tot sententiis variatur. Unam eamdemque rem modo sperat, modo desperat, et secundum varios effectus sive respectus, sententias suae aestimationis alternat. Sed ista alternantium vicissitudinum fluctuatio, tandem utili exitu optimoque fine concluditur, quando quod de humana industria desperatur, tandem aliquando devotionis magisterio totum de gratia divina praesumitur. Quid enim est responsio Danielis, nisi magisterium verae devotionis? Et respondens Daniel coram rege ait: Mysterium quod rex interrogat sapientes, magi, et arioli, et aruspices nequeunt indicare regi. Sed est Deus in coelis revelans mysteria, qui indicavit tibi Nabuchodonosor quae 1260Aventura sunt novissimis temporibus. Sed quia Daniel mysticam jam visionem agnoverat quam adhuc regia sollicitudo quaerebat, cupit sibi quis fortassis apertius ostendi quomodo una, eademque res ab eodem animo possit juxta aliquid sciri, et juxta aliquid nesciri. Sed aliud est mysteriorum profunda per solam intelligentiam capere, aliud est autem quoties volueris facile explicare, et explanare posse. Vide quam rari sunt qui mystica Scripturarum sine aliena opera et expositione penetrare valeant, multo tamen pauciores qui earum explanationem verbo, vel scripto ad aliorum notitiam propalare queant. Perpendis ergo, ut arbitror, quia aliud est munus mysticae intelligentiae, atque aliud est munus mysticae doctrinae. Alii in uno, alii in alio praepollent, 1260Balii autem in utroque eminent. Si super hoc Moysi dicta consulimus, ipsum sapientia, fratremque ejus eloquentia praeeminere cognoscimus. Si ad David respicimus, in uno utrumque invenire poterimus: Incerta, et occulta sapientiae tuae manifestasti mihi (Psal. L). Ecce de uno. Itemque pro alio: Aperiam in parabolis os meum, loquar propositiones ab initio (Psal. LXXVII). Quidam autem jam primum perceperunt et de secundo quidem penitus diffidunt. Mysticam jam visionem Jeremias cognoverat, qui super hoc ipsum interrogatus, dicebat: Virgam vigilantem ego video; et item: Ollam succensam ego video, et faciem ejus a facie aquilonis (Jer. I). Ecce jam audis de uno munere profitentem, audi eumdem de alio adhuc diffidentem: A, a, a, 1260CDomine, nescio loqui. Ejusmodi sane diffidentia exprimitur, cum cognoscenti visionem jam dicitur. Putasne vere potes indicare mihi somnium quod vidi? Talis diffidentia redarguitur, et unde ejusmodi gratia desperari, vel sperari debeat ostenditur, cum dicitur: Mysterium quod rex interrogat sapientes, magi, et arioli, et aruspices nequeunt indicare regi. Sed est Deus in coelis revelans mysteria. Docet itaque nos in his verbis Daniel noster, ea quae supra hominem sunt, et humanum sensum excedunt, de humana industria penitus desperare, et de sola Domini revelatione exspectare. Quod autem dicit ostensa sibi quae futura sunt novissimis temporibus, ad illud procul dubio respicere videtur 1260Dquod in fine visionis post caetera adjungitur: Lapis autem qui percusserat statuam, factus est mons magnus, et implevit universam terram. Quam sint magna, quamve profunda, et quanta veneratione digna ea quae visio ista juxta allegoricum, imo pene juxta historicum sensum loquitur, nemo Scripturarum sanctarum peritus ignorare permittitur. Sed sic ista ad nostram seu aliorum aedificationem juxta moralem sensum discutimus, ut de tanti mysterii auctoritate juxta sacratiorem intelligentiam, nihil minuere vel attenuare praesumamus. Illud itaque stupendum, et multa admiratione dignum occurrit, quomodo contemplationis oculis tam late pateat, qui divinae revelationis cooperatione se usque ad ea quae in novissimo fiunt perlustranda dilatat. 1261AQui indicavit tibi, rex Nabuchodonosor, quae ventura sunt novissimis temporibus, non ait quae ventura sunt tibi, sed quae ventura sunt novissimis temporibus.

Quaerendum itaque est, ne forte aliquid in his verbis lateat quod non uni alicui tantum, sed pluribus, seu etiam praefectis pene omnibus juxta aliquid convenire queat. Neque enim alibi quam apud electos regnum coelorum suscitatur. Regnum quod secundum Danielis sententiam in aeternum non dissipatur, regnum quod intra nos esse dicitur: Regnum, inquit Dominus, coelorum intra vos est (Luc. XVII). Hoc profecto regnum in electis suis Dominus in novissimis eorum suscitat, qualicunque modo eos in primordiis suis per varia tentamenta fluctuare 1261Bpermittat.

CHAPTER XVIII. That we often presume less upon the virtue of devotion than we ought, and that the gift of mystic understanding is one thing, and the gift of mystic teaching another.

The king answered, and said to Daniel, whose name was Balthasar: Thinkest thou that thou canst truly tell me the dream which I saw, and the interpretation thereof? For the king to answer Daniel and to show his own diffidence is nothing else than that such a vice of the spirit cannot lie hidden from true devotion. For often certain vices which at another time we cannot detect, we detect easily 1259Bin the hour of our compunction and amid the tears of our devotion. For perfect devotion sifts all things, discerns all things. For the spiritual man discerns all things, and he himself is judged by no one.

It is surely to be noted that Daniel, while he seeks out things profound and mystic, when he promises things sublime and subtle, is called Balthasar, which is interpreted a hair of the head. Now a hair of the head, as you know, is exceedingly long and subtle. What, then, is a hair of the head, but the subtle profundity and the profound subtlety of the mind? And perfect devotion assuredly both illuminates the mind unto profundity and sharpens it unto every subtlety. And a hair of the head is also an ornament; and the mind of man, assuredly, out of true devotion, in 1259Cso far as it is purged from vices, in so far as it is illuminated unto truth, in so far as it is inflamed unto virtue, in so far without doubt is it adorned, or ornamented. Note therefore that even he distrusted Balthasar's promise who had laid such a name upon him. For it was from the king assuredly that he received such a name. And it does happen that the mind feels and understands itself to be sharpened unto a wondrous subtlety out of much devotion, and yet in a time of necessity presumes less upon the virtue of devotion than it is able, or than it ought. Thinkest thou, he says, that thou canst truly tell me the dream which I saw, and the interpretation thereof? And Daniel above: Bring me in before the king, and I will tell the king the solution. Behold: where Daniel presumes and confidently promises, the royal 1259Danxiety distrusts. All this is often enacted within one and the same spirit; and by almost as many affections as it is touched, by so many opinions is it varied. One and the same thing it now hopes for, now despairs of, and according to its various effects or regards it alternates the opinions of its estimation. But this fluctuation of alternating vicissitudes is at last concluded with a useful issue and a best end, when what is despaired of from human effort is at length, by the mastery of devotion, wholly presumed upon from divine grace. For what is Daniel's answer, but the mastery of true devotion? And Daniel, answering before the king, said: The mystery which the king asks of, the wise men, the magi, and the soothsayers, and the diviners are not able to tell the king. But there is a God in the heavens revealing mysteries, who has shown to thee, Nabuchodonosor, what things 1260Aare to come in the last times. [Vulg. tibi, rex Nabuchodonosor — the royal title is absent here, though Richard's own re-quotation at 1261A restores it; the received text also has the singular in caelo, "in heaven"] But because Daniel had already recognized the mystic vision which the royal anxiety was still seeking, someone perhaps desires to have it shown him more openly how one and the same thing can, by the same spirit, in one respect be known and in another respect not be known. But it is one thing to grasp the depths of the mysteries by understanding alone, and another to be able to explicate and expound them easily as often as thou shalt wish. See how rare they are who are able to penetrate the mystic things of the Scriptures without another's labor and exposition; yet far fewer are they who can publish the exposition of them, by word or by writing, to the cognizance of others. Thou weighest therefore, as I judge, that the gift of mystic understanding is one thing, and the gift of mystic teaching another. Some are strong in the one, some in the other, 1260Bbut others are eminent in both. If upon this point we consult what is said of Moses, we recognize that he stands pre-eminent in wisdom, and his brother in eloquence. If we look to David, we shall be able to find both in one: The uncertain and hidden things of thy wisdom thou hast made manifest to me (Psal. L). Behold, for the one. And likewise for the other: I will open my mouth in parables, I will utter propositions from the beginning (Psal. LXXVII). But certain men have already received the first, and yet utterly distrust as to the second. Jeremiah had already known the mystic vision, who, being questioned upon this very thing, said: I see a rod that watches; and likewise: I see a pot set burning, and the face thereof from the face of the north (Jer. I). Behold, thou already hearest him professing concerning the one gift; hear the same man still distrusting concerning the other: Ah, ah, ah, 1260CLord, I know not how to speak. Such a diffidence, surely, is expressed when it is said to one who already knows the vision. Thinkest thou that thou canst truly tell me the dream which I saw? Such diffidence is refuted, and it is shown whence such a grace ought to be despaired of, or hoped for, when it is said: The mystery which the king asks of, the wise men, the magi, and the soothsayers, and the diviners are not able to tell the king. But there is a God in the heavens revealing mysteries. Our Daniel therefore teaches us in these words utterly to despair of human effort as to those things which are above man and exceed human sense, and to look for them from the Lord's revelation alone. But in saying that there have been shown to him the things that are to come in the last times, he seems without doubt to look to that 1260Dwhich is added at the end of the vision after the rest: But the stone which had struck the statue became a great mountain, and filled the whole earth. How great, and how profound, and how worthy of much veneration are those things which this vision speaks according to the allegorical, nay almost according to the historical sense, no one skilled in the Holy Scriptures is permitted to be ignorant. But we sift these things according to the moral sense, for our own edification or that of others, in such a way that we presume to diminish or to weaken nothing of the authority of so great a mystery according to its more sacred understanding. This therefore presents itself as astonishing and worthy of much wonder: how widely there lies open to the eyes of contemplation the man who, by the cooperation of divine revelation, enlarges himself even to the surveying of those things which come to pass at the last. 1261AWho has shown to thee, king Nabuchodonosor, what things are to come in the last times: he does not say, what things are to come to thee, but what things are to come in the last times.

It must therefore be asked whether perhaps something lie hidden in these words which may in some respect fit not one man only, but many, or even almost all prefects. For the kingdom of heaven is not raised up anywhere else than among the elect. A kingdom which, according to Daniel's word, is not scattered for ever; a kingdom which is said to be within us: The kingdom, says the Lord, of heaven is within you (Luc. XVII) [Vulg. regnum Dei, "the kingdom of God" — the received text of Luke 17:21 does not read "of heaven"]. This kingdom assuredly the Lord raises up in his elect at their last, in whatever manner he may permit them in their beginnings to waver 1261Bthrough various trials.

CAPUT XIX. Quod amissam contemplationis gratiam, melius devotione, quam investigatione recuperamus, et quod id quoque gratis obtinemus quod merito devotionis acquirimus.

Sed ecce jam Daniel ad visionis narrationem vel expositionem accedit, cum adjungit: Somnium tuum, et visiones capitis tui in cubili tuo, hujuscemodi sunt (Dan. II). Quia per somnium sopitur sensus corporeus, recte per illum mentis excessum intelligimus, et per somnium, ea quae per mentis excessum videmus. Caput est summum et praecipuum membrum corporis, et mens est principale hominis 1261Cinterioris. Visiones itaque capitis sunt contemplationes mentis. In cubili autem quiescere consuevimus, et idcirco in ipso quietis otium recte intelligimus. In hoc cubili quasi ad quiescendum interior homo noster locatur, quando animus ad summam tranquillitatem componitur. In hoc cubili tunc quodammodo somnium videtur, quando inter intimae quietis suae, summaeque tranquillitatis otia mens ad sublimia contemplanda sublevatur. Somnium, inquit, tuum et visiones capitis tui hujuscemodi sunt. Nihil aliud est Danielem nostrum ista dicere, amissumque somnium retractare, regemque docere, quam devotam mentem saepe post amissam contemplationis gratiam, qualis in ea aliquando fuerit, ad memoriam revocare, et per multam compunctionis instantiam 1261Diterum eam reparare, firmiusque tandem atque perfectius possidere. Firmius namque atque perfectius rex per Danielis officium visionem suam cognovit, quam prius per semetipsum accepit. Ad hoc namque saepe ejusmodi gratia quibusdam in suis primordiis datur, atque subtrahitur, ut per experientiam agnoscant quid cum quanta aviditate expetere debeant. Ad hoc iterum (nec tamen sine magno labore) datur recuperari, ut ex eo melius innotescat, quam stricte oporteat custodiri. Sed ut ad sequentia transeamus, ecce Daniel antequam visionem explicet, duo quidem praemittere studet, quomodo videlicet eam rex prius obtinuerit, vel ad eam postmodum ipse Daniel recuperare possit. Et de primo quidem: Tu rex, inquit, cogitare coepisti 1262Ain stratu tuo quid esset futurum post te. Et qui revelat mysteria ostendit tibi quae ventura sunt. Ac si aperte dicat: Quia de futuris studiose quaesisti, futurorum revelationem accipere meruisti. De secundo quoque adjungit: Mihi autem quoque non in sapientia quae est in me plus quam in cunctis viventibus, sacramentum hoc revelatum est, sed ut interpretatio regi manifesta fieret, et cogitationes mentis tuae scires. Ac si regi apertius dicat: Mihi quoque ipsum est revelatum, non per sensum proprium, non ob meritum meum, sed ad profectum tuum. Palam autem omnibus puto hoc ipsum, quantum juxta historiam placeat, quod aliud quidem quod precibus obtinuerat industriae, vel meritis suis nullo modo ascribat. Tu rex, inquit, coepisti. Et tu cogita, et 1262Bexcogita, si vales, quantum futurorum cogitatio apud Deum valeat, quae futurorum revelatione divinitus remunerari soleat. Audi hominem de futuris sollicitum, et in ejusmodi cogitationibus vehementer occupatum: Nunquid in aeternum projiciet Deus, et non apponet ut complacitior sit adhuc? Aut in finem misericordiam suam abscindet a generatione in generationem? (Psal. LXXVI.) Audi eumdem de ejusmodi edoctum, et de his quae quaesierat profunde illuminatum: Miserator, et misericors Dominus, longanimis, et multum misericors, non in perpetuum irascetur, neque in aeternum comminabitur (Psal. CII). Forte tunc futurorum revelationem amiserat, et amisisse dolebat, cum diceret: Jam non est propheta, 1262Cet nos non cognoscet amplius (Psal. LXXIII). Et item: Et lumen oculorum, et ipsum non est mecum (Psal. XXXVII). Ad pristinam, ni fallor, gratiam aspirabat, qui dicebat: Quis mihi det, ut sim juxta dies pristinos? (Job. XXIX.) Solitam mysteriorum revelationem repetebat, qui a Domino postulabat: Revela oculos meos, et considerabo mirabilia de lege tua (Psal. CXVIII). Tu quoque qui haec audis, vel legis, si ab ejusmodi gratia excidisse te sentis, attende diligenter quid tecum tacitus agis, cogita quibus cogitationibus vehementer occuparis. Si sola vana, sola instantia meditaris, quid mirum si profundorum, si futurorum revelatione fraudaris? In initio conversionis tuae, quando sola aeterna, sola spiritualia et futura cogitasti, quando sola sublimia 1262Dsuspirasti, quid mirum si secundum desiderium tuum consolari meruisti? Desiderium pauperum exaudivit Dominus. Desisti jam ad Domini pedes sedere et quiescere, ad similitudinem Mariae. Turbaris jam erga plurima, ad similitudinem Marthae. Memento ergo unde excideris, et age poenitentiam, et priora opera fac. Si pristinam illam gratiam cupis reformare, si visiones capitis tui in cubili tuo quaeris recuperare, recurre ad Danielem, renova devotionem. Ad cordis sane renovationem, ad amissae gratiae reformationem melius proficis, citius promoveris intima devotione quam profunda investigatione, nisi forte investigatio tua sit circa detrimenta tua, detrimentorumque remedia. Consonat in haec verba Danielis illa sententia. Mihi quoque non in sapientia 1263Aquae est in me plus quam in cunctis viventibus, sacramentum hoc revelatum est. Certe omne verbum sapientiae, et intellectus sciscitans rex, decuplum super omnes ariolos, et magos in Daniele invenit. Verumtamen vir tantae sapientiae, non tam investigatione, quam prece, quod quaesivit obtinuit. Nemo ergo quaerat per humanam sapientiam ea quae sunt supra humanam intelligentiam. Ad profundiorem fortassis intelligentiam nos vocat, quod nec illud sibi Daniel ascribat quod merito devotionis obtinuerat. Sed ut interpretatio, inquit, regi manifesta fieret. Forte et illud gratis obtinetur, quod merito devotionis acquiritur. Nam et ipsam devotionem quidem non a nobis habemus, sed ex munere largientis accipimus: Quid enim habes, inquit Apostolus, quod non 1263Baccepisti? (I Cor. IV). Ergo et devotione, et gratia quidem: Gratis, inquit, accepistis, gratis date (Matth. X). Si igitur devotione gratis accipimus, gratuitum est et id quod ejus merito obtinemus. Quando gratia pro gratia impenditur, sicut primum, ita et secundum munus gratis obtinetur. Non ergo dona Dei percipimus, quia meruimus, sed ut ex eis proficere, vel prodesse possimus. Et veritatis quidem intelligentiam accipimus ad profectum nostrum; veritatis autem doctrinam ad profectum aliorum. Ex utraque autem liberum arbitrium ad bonum adjuvatur, et ad boni cooperationem utraque illi divinitus impenditur. Per regem vero, ut diximus, arbitrii libertatem accipimus. Danieli itaque perplexorum 1263Cinterpretatio, divinorum judiciorum ratio, quasi ad hoc datur, ut regi manifestetur, quando ad hoc devotio ad veritatem illuminatur, ut liberum arbitrium habeat, et agnoscat in quo se exercere, quid cavere, quid eligere debeat.

CHAPTER XIX. That we recover a lost grace of contemplation better by devotion than by investigation, and that we obtain freely that also which we acquire by the merit of devotion.

But behold, Daniel now comes to the narration, or the exposition, of the vision, when he adds: Thy dream, and the visions of thy head upon thy bed, are of this kind (Dan. II). Because by the dream the bodily sense is lulled, we rightly understand by it the excess of mind, and by the dream those things which we see through the excess of mind. The head is the highest and chief member of the body, and the mind is the principal part of the inner 1261Cman. The visions of the head, therefore, are the contemplations of the mind. Now in a bed we are wont to rest, and therefore in it we rightly understand the leisure of rest. In this bed our inner man is laid as it were to rest, when the spirit is composed to the highest tranquillity. In this bed a dream is then in a manner seen, when amid the leisures of its inmost rest and highest tranquillity the mind is lifted up to the contemplation of sublime things. Thy dream, he says, and the visions of thy head are of this kind. It is nothing else for our Daniel to say these things, and to go over again the lost dream, and to teach the king, than that the devout mind, often after the grace of contemplation has been lost, calls back to memory what that grace once was in it, and by much insistence of compunction 1261Drepairs it again, and at last possesses it more firmly and more perfectly. For the king came to know his vision more firmly and more perfectly through Daniel's office than he had before received it by himself. For to this end such grace is often given to some in their beginnings, and withdrawn, that they may recognize by experience what they ought to seek after, and with how great avidity. To this end again it is granted to be recovered (yet not without great labor), that from it may better become known how strictly it must be guarded. But that we may pass on to what follows: behold, before Daniel explicates the vision, he is zealous to set down two things first, namely how the king had first obtained it, and how Daniel himself might afterwards recover it. And of the first: Thou, O king, he says, didst begin to think 1262Aupon thy bed what should be after thee. And he that reveals mysteries showed thee what things are to come. [Vulg. post haec, "after these things"] As if he said openly: Because thou didst diligently seek concerning things to come, thou hast deserved to receive a revelation of things to come. Of the second also he adds: To me moreover too, not in the wisdom which is in me above all living, has this sacrament been revealed, but that the interpretation might be made manifest to the king, and that thou mightest know the thoughts of thy mind. As if he said to the king more openly: To me also it has been revealed, not through my own sense, not on account of my merit, but for thy advancement. And I think this very thing is plain to all, how much it pleases according to the history, that he in no way ascribes to his own effort or to his merits that other thing which he had obtained by prayers. Thou, O king, he says, didst begin. And do thou consider, and 1262Bconsider well, if thou art able, how much the thought of things to come avails with God, which is wont to be divinely rewarded with a revelation of things to come. Hear a man anxious concerning things to come, and vehemently occupied in thoughts of this kind: Will God then cast off for ever, and will he not add that he be more favorable yet? Or will he cut off his mercy unto the end, from generation to generation? (Psal. LXXVI.) Hear the same man taught concerning such things, and deeply enlightened as to those things which he had sought: The Lord is compassionate and merciful, long-suffering and very merciful; he will not be angry for ever, nor will he threaten unto eternity (Psal. CII). Perhaps he had then lost the revelation of things to come, and grieved to have lost it, when he said: There is now no prophet, 1262Cand he will know us no more (Psal. LXXIII). And likewise: And the light of the eyes, even that is not with me (Psal. XXXVII) [Vulg. lumen oculorum meorum, "the light of my eyes"]. He was aspiring, unless I am mistaken, to the former grace, who said: Who will grant me that I may be according to the former days? (Job. XXIX.) [Vulg. Quis mihi tribuat, ut sim juxta menses pristinos, "the former months"] He was seeking again the accustomed revelation of mysteries, who asked of the Lord: Unveil mine eyes, and I will consider the wondrous things of thy law (Psal. CXVIII). Thou too who hearest or readest these things, if thou feelest that thou hast fallen from such grace, attend diligently to what thou dost silently within thyself; consider with what thoughts thou art vehemently occupied. If thou meditatest on empty things alone, on pressing things alone, what wonder if thou art cheated of the revelation of deep things, of things to come? In the beginning of thy conversion, when thou didst think on eternal things alone, on spiritual and future things alone, when thou didst sigh after sublime things alone, 1262Dwhat wonder if thou didst deserve to be consoled according to thy desire? The Lord has heard the desire of the poor. Thou hast now ceased to sit and rest at the Lord's feet, after the likeness of Mary. Thou art now troubled about many things, after the likeness of Martha. Remember therefore whence thou hast fallen, and do penance, and do the former works. If thou desirest to restore that former grace, if thou seekest to recover the visions of thy head upon thy bed, run back to Daniel, renew thy devotion. For the renewal of the heart, surely, for the restoration of a lost grace, thou advancest better and art promoted more swiftly by inward devotion than by profound investigation, unless perhaps thy investigation be about thy own declines, and the remedies of those declines. That saying of Daniel agrees with these words. To me also, not in the wisdom 1263Awhich is in me above all living, has this sacrament been revealed. Assuredly the king, inquiring after every word of wisdom and of understanding, found in Daniel tenfold above all the soothsayers and magi. Nevertheless a man of so great wisdom obtained what he sought not so much by investigation as by prayer. Let no one, therefore, seek through human wisdom those things which are above human understanding. It calls us perhaps to a deeper understanding, that Daniel does not ascribe to himself even that which he had obtained by the merit of devotion. But that the interpretation, he says, might be made manifest to the king. Perhaps that also is obtained freely which is acquired by the merit of devotion. For devotion itself indeed we have not from ourselves, but we receive it from the gift of the bestower: For what hast thou, says the Apostle, which thou hast not 1263Breceived? (I Cor. IV). Therefore both by devotion, and indeed by grace: Freely, he says, you have received, freely give (Matth. X). If therefore by devotion we receive freely, that too is gratuitous which we obtain by its merit. When grace is bestowed for grace, then, just as the first, so also the second gift is obtained freely. We do not therefore receive the gifts of God because we have deserved them, but that from them we may be able to advance, or to profit others. And the understanding of truth indeed we receive for our own advancement, but the teaching of truth for the advancement of others. Now by both is free will helped toward the good, and both are divinely bestowed upon it for a cooperation in the good. But by the king, as we have said, we understand the liberty of the will. To Daniel, therefore, the interpretation 1263Cof perplexed things, the account of the divine judgments, is given as it were for this end, that it may be made manifest to the king, when devotion is for this end illuminated unto truth, that free will may have and recognize wherein it ought to exercise itself, what to beware of, what to choose.

CAPUT XX. Compendiosa recapitulatio praedictorum cum similitudinis attestatione.

Juvat hoc loco in considerationem adducere quomodo ista lectio, et evangelica videantur sibi mystica locutione concinere. Refert Lucas evangelista quomodo voluerit Dominus itinerantibus discipulis praesentiam suam in peregrina specie exhibere, et 1263Dpostmodum subtrahere, sed cum rogantibus manere, ad compellentes intrare, et tandem aliquando inter epulas speciem quam recognoscerent ostendere (Luc, XXIV). Quid itaque est Christum, hoc est Dei sapientiam, in peregrina specie praesentem habere, nisi veritatem non in sua simplicitate, sed per speculum, vel aenigmate videre? Extra domum autem et quasi in itinere haec visio ostenditur, quando sublimium contemplatio per mentis excessum et velut in transitu videtur. Quasi duo exeuntes sunt, qui et ambulantes ejusmodi conspiciunt, quando ratio, et intelligentia humanum modum excedentes furtim quidem, et raptim sublimium spectacula percurrunt. Tunc autem Dominus longius se ire fingit, nec tamen penitus recedit, 1264Aquando contemplationis gratiam quam misericorditer impendere consuevit, non minus utiliter ad tempus subtrahit, ut melius per experientiam doceat cum quanta aviditate requiri, vel retineri debeat. Manere autem, et ad eos intrare compellitur, quando multa precum instantia amissa gratia reparatur, ut visionem exterius oblatam introrsum trahat, et per excessum visa, animus ad se ipsum reversus memoriter retineat, et ad contemplationis gratiam de caetero familiarius possideat. Cibos quales Dominus amat, et delectari solet, apponimus, quando eum pro recuperatione vel accumulatione gratiae in voce exsultationis et confessionis laudamus, honoramus, magnificamus: In voce, inquit, exsultationis, et 1264Bconfessionis sonus epulantis (Psal. XLI). Inter hos saepe exsultationis et confessionis nostrae hymnos sinus mentis dilatatur, sensus acuitur, et ad mystica penetranda, et usque ad Divinitatis arcana saepe rimanda, intelligentia illuminatur: Sacrificium, inquit, laudis honorificabit me, et illic iter quo ostendam salutare Dei (Psal. XLIX). Panis coram nobis procul dubio a Domino frangitur, quando mysteriorum profunditas mystica interpretatione aperitur, ipsoque revelante cognoscitur. Ex hac panis fractione, mysteriorumque revelatione agitur, ut Dominus in propria specie recognoscatur, et ipsa Veritas (utpote figurarum velamine amoto) in sua simplicitate perspiciatur, Dominique praesentia ex 1264Cgratiae ipsius infusione perpendatur. Dominus autem (qui et Dei sapientia) ab oculis intuentium evanescit, quando ex contemplatione et admiratione divinae sapientiae contemplantis et admirantis animo innotescit, quantum ejus incomprehensibilitas omnem humanam intelligentiam excedit: O altitudo, inquit, divitiarum, sapientiae, et scientiae Dei, quam incomprehensibilia sunt judicia ejus, et investigabiles viae ejus (Rom. XI). Humanus siquidem animus, quanto subtilius ad Dei sapientiam contemplandam illuminatur, tanto validius in semetipsum luminis illius immensitate reverberatur, quod est ab intuentis oculis evanescere, et seipsam quasi ostendendo subducere. Ex his itaque licet perpendere, quanta similitudinis proportione haec gemina lectio videatur 1264Dsibi respondere. Quod enim Dominus se in peregrina specie ostendit, hoc est, quod Nabuchodonosor veritatis revelationem sub mystico velamine accepit. Quod ibi Dominus longius iter se finxit, hoc est, quod visio regi ostensa ad modicum, se ab ejus memoria elongavit. Quod ibi qui apparuerat remanere, et intrare compellitur, hoc est, quod hic elongata visio multa instantia, quaeritur, et tandem Danielis sollicitudine multo profundius, multo perfectius percipitur vel retinetur. Quod ille qui illic apparuerat, tandem aliquando manifesta visione cognoscitur, hoc est, quod hoc in loco ablatae visionis obnubilata veritas prophetica demum interpretatione manifestatur. In his, et aliis quibusdam hanc geminam lectionem sibi invicem concinere 1265Avidebit, si quis est, qui haec altius perscrutari velit. Habet tamen illa lectio sua quaedam propria, et ista de quibus non est modo dicendum per singula. His itaque per excessum excussis, et pro similitudinis attestatione collatis, ad visionis explicationem, seu explanationem redeamus.

CHAPTER XX. A concise recapitulation of what has been said, with the attestation of a likeness.

It is profitable at this point to bring into consideration how this reading and the Gospel reading seem to accord together in a mystical manner of speech. Luke the evangelist relates how the Lord willed to show his presence to the disciples on their journey in the appearance of a stranger, and 1263Dafterwards to withdraw it; but to remain with them as they asked, to enter in to them as they constrained him, and at last, amid the feast, to show that appearance which they might recognize (Luc, XXIV). What then is it to have Christ, that is, the Wisdom of God, present in the appearance of a stranger, but to see the truth not in its own simplicity, but through a glass, or in an enigma? But this vision is shown outside the house and as it were on a journey, when the contemplation of sublime things is seen through an excess of mind and as it were in passing. There are, as it were, two going out, who also as they walk behold such things, when reason and intelligence, exceeding the human measure, run through the spectacles of sublime things, stealthily indeed and hurriedly. But then the Lord feigns to go further, and yet does not wholly withdraw, 1264Awhen the grace of contemplation, which he is wont mercifully to bestow, he withdraws for a time, no less profitably, that he may better teach by experience with how great eagerness it ought to be sought and to be held fast. And he is constrained to remain and to enter in to them, when by much instancy of prayers the lost grace is repaired, so that he may draw inward the vision offered outwardly, and that the spirit, returned to itself, may retain in memory the things seen through excess, and thereafter possess the grace of contemplation more familiarly. We set before him such foods as the Lord loves and is wont to be delighted with, when, for the recovery or the accumulation of grace, we praise, honor, and magnify him in the voice of exultation and of confession: In the voice, he says, of exultation, and 1264Bof confession the sound of one feasting (Psal. XLI). Amid these hymns of our exultation and confession the bosom of the mind is often enlarged, the sense is sharpened, and the intelligence is enlightened for penetrating mystical things, and for often searching out even the secrets of the Godhead: The sacrifice, he says, of praise shall honor me, and there is the way by which I will show the salvation of God (Psal. XLIX). [Vulg. ostendam illi salutare Dei, "will show him the salvation of God"] Beyond doubt the bread is broken before us by the Lord, when the profundity of the mysteries is opened by mystical interpretation, and is known by his own revealing. From this breaking of bread and revelation of the mysteries it comes about that the Lord is recognized in his own appearance, and that Truth itself (the veil of figures being removed) is discerned in its own simplicity, and the Lord's presence is weighed from 1264Cthe infusion of his grace. But the Lord (who is also the Wisdom of God) vanishes from the eyes of those beholding, when, from the contemplation and admiration of the divine wisdom, it becomes known to the spirit of him who contemplates and admires how far its incomprehensibility exceeds every human intelligence: O the depth, he says, of the riches, of the wisdom, and of the knowledge of God: how incomprehensible are his judgments, and his ways unsearchable (Rom. XI). For the human spirit, the more subtly it is enlightened for contemplating the wisdom of God, so much the more strongly is it beaten back upon itself by the immensity of that light; and this is to vanish from the eyes of the beholder, and, as it were, in showing itself to withdraw itself. From these things, therefore, one may weigh with how great a proportion of likeness this twin reading seems 1264Dto answer to itself. For that the Lord showed himself in the appearance of a stranger, this is that Nabuchodonosor received the revelation of the truth under a mystical veil. That there the Lord feigned for himself a longer journey, this is that the vision shown to the king withdrew itself for a little from his memory. That there he who had appeared is constrained to remain and to enter in, this is that here the withdrawn vision is sought with much instancy, and at last, by Daniel's solicitude, is perceived and retained far more profoundly, far more perfectly. That he who had appeared there is at length known in a manifest vision, this is that in this place the clouded truth of the vision taken away is at last made manifest by prophetic interpretation. In these things, and in certain others, he will see this twin reading to accord with itself, 1265Aif there be any who is willing to search these matters out more deeply. Yet that reading has certain things proper to itself, and this one too, of which there is no need to speak now one by one. These things, therefore, having been shaken out by way of digression, and brought together for the attestation of the likeness, let us return to the explication, or explanation, of the vision.

CAPUT XXI. Quomodo oporteat unumquemque a praesumptionis suae altitudine detumescere, qui cupit in vera stabilitate quandoque fixus manere.

Tu rex videbas, et ecce quasi statua una grandis statua illa magna, et statura sublimis stabat contra te, et intuitus ejus erat terribilis. Hujus statuae caput ex auro optimo erat. Pectus autem et brachia de argento. Porro venter et femora ex aere, tibiae autem ferreae. 1265BPedum quaedam pars erat ferrea, quaedam fictilis. Videbas ita, donec abscisus est lapis sine manibus, et percussit statuam in pedibus ejus ferreis, et fictilibus, et comminuit eos. Tunc contrita sunt pariter ferrum, testa, aes, argentum et aurum, et redacta quasi in favillam aestivae areae, quae rapta sunt vento, nullusque locus inventus est eis. Lapis autem qui percussit statuam, factus est mons magnus, et implevit universam terram. Hoc est somnium (Dan. II). Ecce, ut videre potes, imago illa, quae per visionem cernitur, varia permutatione semper in deteriora ducitur, et quae in somno a pretiosissima materia incipit, per multiplicia detrimenta decurrens, tandem in vilissimam materiam desinit. Quod enim inchoatur ex auro, ad 1265Cultimum consummatur in luto. Ut igitur breviter perstringam quid super his, juxta generalem quamdam considerationem, sentiam, unumquemque oportere puto, juxta propositam ostensae visionis formam, apud semetipsum a propria aestimatione decrescere, et a sui confidentia magis magisque deficere, praesumptionemque temperare, si regnum coelorum intus apud semetipsum optat aedificari, et illud utique regnum, quod nunquam possit finiri. Omnis homo naturaliter seipsum amat, et approbat, et sane in quantum diligit, in tantum et magni pendit. Semper autem ex magnitudine dilectionis, pendet mensura aestimationis, quia modus dilectionis est sine modo, sine mensura est aestimationis suae magnitudo. O quam rarum ut homo inveniri possit, 1265Dqui contra Apostoli sententiam non se putet aliquid esse, cum nihil sit! Sed nunquid saltem aliquid parvum? Imo vere magnum, et valde magnum supra modum, et, ut figurate loquar, pene plusquam aureum, cum veraciter sit vile lutum, utquid superbis terra et cinis? Si exaltatus fueris ut aquila, et si inter sidera coeli posueris nidum tuum, inde detraham te, dicit Dominus (Abd. IV). Oportet itaque eum qui adhuc apud se in immensum tumet, ab enormi illa aestimationis propriae altitudine detumescere, et ad verae humiliationis ima gradatim descendere, et tandem aliquando seipsum velut nihilum et inane reputare: velut vile lutum despiciendo calcare, et quasi favillam et cinerem omni hora aspicere, si illud aeternae stabilitatis regnum cupit in seipso consurgere. 1266AConcinit in hanc sententiam cui non erat similis super terram: Comparatus sum luto, et assimilatus sum favillae et cineri (Job XXX). Vultis autem adhuc apertius audire quam omnino ab omni confidentia sui dejectus fuit ille qui inter omnes orientales magnus apparuit? Desperavi, inquit, nequaquam ultra jam vivam (Job VII). Desperasse se dicit, sed dubitas forte de quo, de Domino an de seipso? Audi ergo quid alibi dicat, et agnosce quam fortiter in Domini confidentia fixus manebat: Etiamsi occiderit me, in ipso sperabo (Job XIII). Sperabat itaque, sed de Domino. Desperabat nihilominus, sed de seipso. O quanta perversitas de seipso praesumere, et de Domini pietate desperare! Si multum de tua virtute confidis, ibi te in altum erigis, et 1266Bquasi ad instar montis intumescis, ubi te submittere, et velut vallem facere debuisses. Bonum est ergo dicere huic monti tollere, et mittere in mari. Propter ejusmodi montes conturbabitur terra, et transferentur montes in cor maris. Econtra si de Domini protectione diffidis, ibi procul dubio satis prave atque perverse vallem efficis, ubi collem, imo montem, et montem excelsum valde habere debueras. Ut igitur sint prava in directa, et aspera in vias planas, ejusmodi omnis vallis implebitur, omnis mons et collis humiliabitur, et tunc temporis elevabitur mons domus Domini supra verticem montium. O quanta mentis altitudo! o quanta fortitudo vel firmitas in sola Domini pietate confidere, et in ejusmodi 1266Cconfidentia fixum manere! Hic est ille mons qui post statuae dejectionem propriaeque confidentiae extenuationem vel exterminationem ex modico in immensum crescit, ut in aeternum moveri non possit: Qui confidit, inquit, in Domino sicut mons Sion, non commovebitur in aeternum qui habitat in Hierusalem (Psal. CXXIV). Hic est mons quem oportet universam terrae superficiem implere, ita ut nihil audeat homo de sua industria, nihil omnino nisi de Domini virtute praesumere. Puto, quia non potest abscondi civitas supra hunc montem sita. Si mihi credis, aedificabis tibi in hoc monte structuram illam, quae aedificatur ut civitas, cujus participatio ejus in idipsum, ut sit firmamentum in terra in summis montium. In hoc sane eminenti metropolim, et sedem 1266Dimperii habere debet regnum charitatis, et regni ejus non erit finis. Regnum siquidem charitatis, ni fallor, est illud regnum secundum Danielis sententiam quod in aeternum non dissipabitur. Ecce quid tibi statuae hujus erectio, vel dejectio proponat, ecce qualia, vel quanta tibi per similitudinem repraesentat.

CHAPTER XXI. How everyone who desires one day to remain fixed in true stability must subside from the height of his own presumption.

Thou, O king, wast seeing, and behold, as it were one great statue that statue, the great one, and lofty in stature, stood over against thee, and its look was terrible. The head of this statue was of the finest gold. But the breast and the arms of silver. Further, the belly and the thighs of bronze, but the shins of iron. 1265BA certain part of the feet was of iron, a certain of earthenware. Thou wast seeing thus, until a stone was cut out without hands, and struck the statue upon its feet of iron and of earthenware, and crushed them. Then were crushed together the iron, the clay, the bronze, the silver and the gold, and were reduced as it were into the cinders of a summer threshing-floor, which were carried off by the wind, and no place was found for them. But the stone which struck the statue became a great mountain, and filled the whole earth. This is the dream (Dan. II). [Vulg. abscissus est lapis de monte sine manibus, "was cut out of a mountain without hands"] Behold, as thou canst see, that image which is discerned through the vision is by manifold change always led into worse things; and that which in the dream begins from the most precious material, running down through manifold declines, at last ends in the vilest material. For what is begun from gold is at 1265Cthe last consummated in mire. That I may therefore briefly touch upon what I think concerning these things, according to a certain general consideration: I judge that everyone must, according to the form set forth of the vision shown, decrease within himself from his own estimation, and fail more and more in confidence in himself, and temper his presumption, if he desires the kingdom of heaven to be built within him, and that kingdom indeed which can never be ended. Every man naturally loves and approves himself, and assuredly, in so far as he loves, so far also does he esteem himself great. But always the measure of esteem hangs upon the greatness of love; because the mode of love is without mode, the greatness of his own esteem is without measure. O how rare it is that a man can be found 1265Dwho does not think himself, against the Apostle's sentence, to be something, when he is nothing! But is he at least something small? Nay, truly great, and exceedingly great beyond measure, and, that I may speak figuratively, almost more than golden, while in truth he is vile mire. Why art thou proud, earth and ashes? If thou be exalted as an eagle, and if thou set thy nest among the stars of heaven, thence will I draw thee down, saith the Lord (Abd. IV). [Vulg. et si inter sidera posueris nidum tuum, with no coeli, "among the stars"] It behooves him therefore who still swells immeasurably within himself to subside from that enormous height of his own esteem, and to descend by steps to the depths of true humiliation, and at length to reckon himself as a nothing and a void: to trample himself in contempt as vile mire, and to look upon himself every hour as cinder and ashes, if he desires that kingdom of eternal stability to rise up within himself. 1266AHe accords with this sentence to whom there was none like upon the earth: I am compared to mire, and am made like to cinders and ashes (Job XXX). But do you wish to hear yet more plainly how utterly he was cast down from all confidence in himself, who appeared great among all the men of the East? I have despaired, he says, by no means shall I live any longer now (Job VII). He says that he has despaired, but perhaps thou doubtest of what: of the Lord, or of himself? Hear therefore what he says elsewhere, and know how strongly he remained fixed in confidence in the Lord: Even if he slay me, I will hope in him (Job XIII). He hoped, therefore, but of the Lord. He despaired none the less, but of himself. O how great a perversity, to presume of oneself and to despair of the Lord's mercy! If thou trustest much in thine own virtue, there thou raisest thyself on high, and 1266Bswellest up after the likeness of a mountain, where thou oughtest to have lowered thyself and made thyself as a valley. It is good therefore to say to this mountain: be lifted up, and be cast into the sea. Because of such mountains the earth shall be troubled, and mountains shall be carried into the heart of the sea. On the contrary, if thou distrustest the Lord's protection, there beyond doubt thou makest, wickedly and perversely enough, a valley where thou oughtest to have had a hill, nay a mountain, and a mountain exceeding high. That therefore the crooked things may be made straight, and the rough ways plain, every such valley shall be filled, every mountain and hill shall be brought low, and at that time the mountain of the house of the Lord shall be raised above the top of the mountains. O how great a height of mind! o how great a fortitude or firmness, to trust in the Lord's mercy alone, and to remain fixed in such 1266Cconfidence! This is that mountain which, after the casting down of the statue and the attenuation or extermination of confidence in oneself, grows from small to immense, so that it cannot be moved for ever: He that trusteth, he says, in the Lord, as mount Sion, shall not be moved for ever, he that dwelleth in Jerusalem (Psal. CXXIV). [Vulg. Qui confidunt in Domino, plural, "they that trust in the Lord"] This is the mountain which must fill the whole surface of the earth, so that a man may dare nothing of his own effort, may presume nothing at all save of the Lord's virtue. I think that a city set upon this mountain cannot be hid. If thou believest me, thou wilt build for thyself on this mountain that structure which is built as a city, whose participation is in itself, that there may be a firmament in the earth on the tops of the mountains. On this eminence assuredly the kingdom of charity ought to have its metropolis and the seat 1266Dof its empire, and of its kingdom there shall be no end. For the kingdom of charity, if I mistake not, is that kingdom which, according to Daniel's sentence, shall not be destroyed for ever. Behold what the raising up, or the casting down, of this statue sets before thee; behold what things, and how great, it represents to thee by way of likeness.

CAPUT XXII. De modo conditionis, vel corruptibilitatis humanae, et quod in quolibet virtutum culmine oporteat morum ruinam timere.

Quid est statua, nisi effigies humana? Quid itaque est statuam, hoc est similitudinem humanam per visionem cernere, nisi humanae vitae modum, vel ordinem, quasi per speculum, et in aenigmate 1267Aoculo contemplationis aspicere? Tu rex, inquit, videbas, et ecce quasi statua una grandis (Dan. II). Si modum vel qualitatem humanae vitae perscrutari velis, si ad dignitatem conditionis, si ad facilitatem reparationis, ad quodlibet horum te verteris, videbis te in singulis deficere, et nihil horum ad plenum posse comprehendere. Vide ergo in intuentis oculis quam grande est, quod ab ipsius aspectu omnino comprehendi non potest: Statua, inquit, illa magna, et statura sublimis, stabat contra te, et intuitus ejus erat terribilis. Bene autem postquam magnam dixit, et sublimem forte adjungit. Siquidem aliud est esse magnum, et aliud est esse altum. Nam et ager magnus dicitur, qui tamen nulla altitudine attollitur. Sic sane quaedam pene incomprehensibilia 1267Bsunt, quae tamen multa investigatione, vel admiratione digna non sunt. Sed quod hanc statuam grandem, sublimem, terribilem audivimus, ad triplicem considerationem recte accommodare possumus. Humanae siquidem vitae conditio, quae in hac statua figuratur, quamvis unumquodque secundum aliquid in singulis invenitur, specialius tamen, et expressius, ex uno grandis, ex altero sublimis, et ex alio invenitur terribilis. Una itaque humanae conditionis consideratio et immanis est, ex ea parte qua subjacet corruptioni; et sublimis est ex ea parte qua reservatur reparationi; et terribilis ex ea parte qua patet perditioni. Vultis audire quanta immanitas corruptionis? Cui comparabo te, vel cui 1267Cassimilabo te, filia Hierusalem; cui exaeguabo te, et consolabor te, virgo filia Sion? Magna velut mare contritio tua. Quis medebitur tui? (Thren. II). Vultis agnoscere quanta sit sublimitas reparationis? Nam quos praescivit, et praedestinavit conformes fieri imaginis Filii sui, ut sit ipse primogenitus in multis fratribus. Quos praedestinavit, hos et vocavit, et quos vocavit, hos et justificavit; quos autem justificavit, illos et magnificavit (Rom. VIII). Vultis et illud nosse quantus sit terror divinae percussionis? Quis, inquit, novit potestatem irae tuae, et prae timore tuo iram tuam dinumerare? (Psal. LXXXIX). Revelatur enim ira Dei de coelo super omnem impietatem, et injustitiam hominum eorum, qui veritatem Dei in injustitia detinent. Attende ergo magnitudinem statuae 1267Dnostrae in consideratione culpae; altitudinem, in consideratione gratiae; terrorem, in consideratione poenae. Quaere culpae magnitudinem, et secundum culpam originalem, et secundum culpam actualem. Et pro culpa originali audi quid de homine dicitur homini: Homo cum in honore esset, non intellexit, comparatus est jumentis insipientibus, et similis factus est illis (Psal. XLVIII). Et de culpa actuali, accipe vocem improperii: In omni colle sublimi, et sub omni ligno frondoso tu prosternebaris meretrix (Jer. II). Unde est quod ei dicitur: Leva in circuitu oculos tuos, et vide ubi nunc prostrata sis meretrix. O vere gratiae altitudinem, tam secundum gratiam justificantem quam secundum gratiam confirmantem, quam secundum gratiam glorificantem! 1268AMetire si vales sublimitatis celsitudinem, secundum gratiam justificantem: Nam quantum distat ortus ab occidente, longe fecit a nobis iniquitates nostras (Psal. CII). Suscipe et eminentiae altitudinem, secundum gratiam confirmantem. Quoniam secundum altitudinem coeli terra corroboravit misericordiam suam super timentes se. Aestima, si potes, dignitatis sublimitatem, secundum gratiam glorificantem: Quicunque enim Spiritu Dei aguntur, hi filii sunt Dei. Si autem filii, et haeredes. Haeredes quidem Dei, cohaeredes autem Christi (Rom. XIV). O statuae nostrae aspectum vere terribilem, secundum poenae addictionem; terribilem, secundum judicium corruptionis; terribilem, secundum judicium reprobationis; terribilem, secundum judicium damnationis! O aspectum 1268Bterroris, secundum judicium corruptionis! Quoniam circumdederunt me mala quorum non est numerus, comprehenderunt me iniquitates meae, et non potui ut viderem. Multiplicatae sunt super capillos capitis mei. O aspectum plenum horroris secundum judicium reprobationis! Et sicut non probaverunt, inquit Apostolus, Deum habere in notitia, tradidit illos Deus in reprobum sensum, ut faciant ea quae non conveniunt, repletos omni iniquitate, malitia, fornicatione, avaritia, nequitia (Rom. I). O aspectum plenum terroris, plenum horroris, secundum judicium damnationis! Terribilis enim, secundum eumdem Apostolum, quaedam exspectatio judicii, et ignis aemulatio quae consumptura 1268Cest adversarios. Ignis enim eorum non exstinguetur, et vermis eorum non morietur. Eorum utique qui ducunt in bono dies suos, et in puncto ad inferna descendunt. Ex his omnibus, ut arbitror, perpendis quam sit horrendum incidere in manus Dei viventis. Quid his terribilius dici, quid his horribilius potest excogitari? Et intuitus, inquit, ejus erat terribilis. Quod autem de statua dicitur, stabat contra te, non multa eget expositione. Tunc procul dubio contra unum quemlibet hujusmodi statua erigitur, quando speculantis animi foeditas, infirmitas, calamitas oculis ejus ingeritur, et qualis sit, vel qualis adhuc esse possit, ei per revelationem ostenditur, ut ejus impudentia, superbia, duritia confundatur, humilietur, conteratur: Arguam 1268Dte, inquit, et statuam contra faciem tuam (Psal. XLIX). Divinitus arguitur, quando divina inspiratione jam tactus animus conscientiae propriae accusatione confunditur. Et quasi contra faciem propriam praevaricatrix anima statuitur, quando in foeditatis suae consideratione contra semetipsum per increpationem servire videtur. Contra speculantis itaque animum statua quodammodo statuitur, quando ad sui ipsius confusionem, et humiliationem divino quodam nutu suae ei corruptibilitatis imago proponitur. Disce itaque, homo, disce confidentiam tui (si subito, et semel non potes, gradatim saltem et per temporum intervalla), deponere stude; satage de te humiliora semper magis magisque sentire, ut ad veram perfectamque humilitatem possis vel 1269Asero pertingere. Haec sunt consummatae charitatis inconcussa fundamenta, haec sunt aeternae securitatis regnantis animae firma munimenta. Depone arrogantiam, deprime superbiam, contere perversam illam de tua virtute fiduciam, jacta in Domino curam tuam. Omnem virtutem, omnem fortitudinem, omnem industriam tuam reputa velut cinerem et favillam. Quid aliud notavit favillam quam omni turbini cedere, imo ad quemlibet, vel tenuem flatum dissilire, quietisque suae locum deserere? Congrua satis corruptelae nostrae forma, et imago convenienter expressa in cinere et favilla. Vide, quisquis es, ne forte oporteat velis nolis, quandoque interius vel exterius talem forte, interius apud te in tua existimatione, vel exterius etiam coram 1269Baliis in tua actione. Interius, inquam, ex proposito et studio voluntatis, vel exterius ex defectu et fluctu humanae corruptibilitatis, et quam multi sunt qui nullo modo ab altitudine propriae aestimationis descendunt, nisi tunc primum cum exterius in actione turpiter cadunt. Ad hoc namque procul dubio nonnulli permittuntur in actione deformiter cadere, et aurum suum in argentum, argentum in aes, aes in ferrum, ferrum in testam mutare, ut ex proprio defectu, et quasi per experimentum discant, quid de propria virtute et fortitudine sentire debeant. Videamus ergo quomodo quosdam contingat, juxta ostensae imaginis formam, quasi de summis bonis pene usque in extrema mala per varios corruptionum 1269Cdefectus corruere, et velut aureum caput in testeos pedes terminare, ut discamus, in quolibet virtutum culmine morum ruinam timere, ut satagamus semper et ubique verae et perfectae humilitatis formam modumque tenere.

CHAPTER XXII. On the measure of the human condition, or of human corruptibility, and that on any summit of virtues one must fear the ruin of morals.

What is a statue, but a human effigy? What then is it to discern a statue, that is, a human likeness, through a vision, but to look upon the measure, or the order, of human life as through a glass, and in an enigma, 1267Awith the eye of contemplation? Thou, O king, he says, wast seeing, and behold, as it were one great statue (Dan. II). If thou wouldst search out the measure or the quality of human life, if to the dignity of its condition, if to the ease of its reparation, to whichever of these thou turn thyself, thou wilt see that thou failest in each, and canst not fully comprehend any of them. See therefore how great that is in the eyes of the beholder which cannot at all be comprehended by his own sight: That statue, he says, the great one, and lofty in stature, stood over against thee, and its look was terrible. But rightly, after he said "great," he perhaps adds "lofty" also. For it is one thing to be great, and another to be high. For a field also is called great which yet is raised by no height. So indeed some things are almost incomprehensible 1267Bwhich yet are not worthy of much investigation or admiration. But that we have heard this statue to be great, lofty, terrible, we can rightly accommodate to a threefold consideration. For the condition of human life, which is figured in this statue, although each of these is found in some respect in each, is yet more especially and more expressly found great from one thing, lofty from another, and terrible from another. One consideration therefore of the human condition is monstrous, on that side by which it lies subject to corruption; and it is lofty on that side by which it is reserved for reparation; and terrible on that side by which it lies open to perdition. Do you wish to hear how great is the monstrousness of corruption? To what shall I compare thee, or to what shall I 1267Cliken thee, O daughter of Jerusalem; to what shall I make thee equal exaeguabo, and comfort thee, O virgin daughter of Sion? Great as the sea is thy crushing. Who shall heal thee? (Thren. II).
