[Vulg. magna est enim velut mare contritio tua, "for thy crushing is great as the sea"] Do you wish to know how great is the loftiness of reparation? For whom he foreknew, he also predestinated to be made conformable to the image of his Son, that he himself might be the firstborn among many brethren. Whom he predestinated, them he also called; and whom he called, them he also justified; but whom he justified, them he also magnified (Rom. VIII). [Vulg. glorificavit, "glorified"] Do you wish to know this also, how great is the terror of the divine stroke? Who, he says, knoweth the power of thy wrath, and for fear of thee can number thy anger? (Psal. LXXXIX). For the wrath of God is revealed from heaven against all ungodliness and injustice of those men who detain the truth of God in injustice. Consider therefore the greatness of our statue 1267Din the consideration of fault; its height, in the consideration of grace; its terror, in the consideration of punishment. Seek the greatness of fault both according to original fault and according to actual fault. And for original fault, hear what is said of man to man: Man, when he was in honor, did not understand; he is compared to senseless beasts, and is become like to them (Psal. XLVIII). And concerning actual fault, receive the voice of reproach: On every high hill, and under every leafy tree thou didst prostrate thyself, a harlot (Jer. II). Whence it is that it is said to her: Lift up thine eyes round about, and see where thou now liest prostrate, a harlot. O truly the height of grace, both according to justifying grace and according to confirming grace, and according to glorifying grace! 1268AMeasure, if thou art able, the loftiness of that sublimity, according to justifying grace: For as far as the rising is distant from the west, so far hath he put our iniquities from us (Psal. CII). Receive also the height of eminence, according to confirming grace. For according to the height of heaven from the earth, he hath strengthened his mercy over them that fear him. Estimate, if thou canst, the sublimity of dignity, according to glorifying grace: For whosoever are led by the Spirit of God, they are the sons of God. And if sons, heirs also. Heirs indeed of God, but joint-heirs with Christ (Rom. XIV). O the aspect of our statue, truly terrible according to the adjudging of punishment; terrible according to the judgment of corruption; terrible according to the judgment of reprobation; terrible according to the judgment of damnation! O the aspect 1268Bof terror, according to the judgment of corruption! For evils without number have surrounded me; my iniquities have overtaken me, and I was not able to see. They are multiplied above the hairs of my head. O the aspect full of horror, according to the judgment of reprobation! And as they did not approve, says the Apostle, to have God in their knowledge, God delivered them up to a reprobate sense, to do those things which are not fitting, being filled with all iniquity, malice, fornication, avarice, wickedness (Rom. I). O the aspect full of terror, full of horror, according to the judgment of damnation! For there is, according to the same Apostle, a certain terrible expectation of judgment, and rage of fire which shall consume 1268Cthe adversaries. For their fire shall not be extinguished, and their worm shall not die. Of those, namely, who lead their days in good things, and in a moment go down to hell. From all these things, as I judge, thou weighest how horrible it is to fall into the hands of the living God. What can be said more terrible than these, what more horrible can be devised? And its look, he says, was terrible. But that which is said of the statue, it stood over against thee, needs no long exposition. Then beyond doubt is such a statue raised up against any single man, when the foulness, the infirmity, the calamity of the beholding spirit is thrust upon his eyes, and it is shown him by revelation what he is, or what he may yet become, so that his shamelessness, his pride, his hardness may be confounded, humbled, crushed: I will reprove thee, 1268Dhe says, and I will set thee against thy face (Psal. XLIX). He is divinely reproved, when the spirit, already touched by divine inspiration, is confounded by the accusation of his own conscience. And the transgressing soul is set as it were against its own face, when in the consideration of its own foulness it seems to serve against itself by rebuke. A statue therefore is in a manner set against the spirit of him who beholds, when, for his own confusion and humiliation, by a certain divine nod the image of his own corruptibility is set before him. Learn therefore, O man, learn to lay aside confidence in thyself (if suddenly and at once thou canst not, then at least by degrees and through intervals of time); be earnest to do it; be busy to think of thyself ever more and more humbly, that thou mayest attain, even 1269Alate, to true and perfect humility. These are the unshaken foundations of consummate charity; these are the firm defenses of the eternal security of the reigning soul. Lay aside arrogance, press down pride, crush that perverse trust in thine own virtue, cast thy care upon the Lord. Reckon all thy virtue, all thy fortitude, all thine effort as ash and cinder. What else has marked the cinder than to yield to every whirlwind, nay, to fly apart at any breath, however slight, and to forsake the place of its rest? A form fitting enough for our corruption, and an image suitably expressed in ash and cinder. See, whoever thou art, lest perhaps it behoove thee, willing or unwilling, to be such perhaps at some time inwardly or outwardly: inwardly with thyself in thine own estimation, or outwardly also before 1269Bothers in thine action. Inwardly, I say, from the purpose and zeal of the will, or outwardly from the failing and the fluctuation of human corruptibility. And how many there are who in no way come down from the height of their own estimation, save then first, when outwardly they fall shamefully in action. For to this end beyond doubt are some permitted to fall deformedly in action, and to change their gold into silver, silver into bronze, bronze into iron, iron into clay, that from their own failing, and as it were by experiment, they may learn what they ought to think of their own virtue and fortitude. Let us see therefore how it befalls some, according to the form of the image shown, to fall as it were from the highest goods almost to the utmost evils through the various failings of corruptions, 1269Cand to end a head of gold, as it were, in feet of clay; that we may learn, on any summit of virtues, to fear the ruin of morals, and may be busy always and everywhere to keep the form and the measure of true and perfect humility.

CAPUT XXIII. Quod de magno saepe virtutum culmine soleat meus humana gradatim in ima corruere.

Qui stat, ait Apostolus, videat ne cadat (I Cor. X). De quantumlibet celso fastigio facile potest in ima corrui, vel in profundum demergi, imo procul dubio quanto gradus altior, tanto casus deterior. Debet nos semper istud Nabuchodonosor somnium, et cautos reddere, et sollicitos efficere. Vide, obsecro, visionis 1269Dejus statuam; vide, inquam, a quanta sublimitate incipitur, et in qualem tandem finem deducitur. Habet sane caput aureum, et finem testeum. Quid auro charius, quid testa vilius? Attende et illud quomodo gradatim ad deteriora vergit, et quandoque in foedissimo fine finem facit. Prima pars aurea, secunda argentea, tertia aerea, quarta ferrea, et quinta describitur testea. In auro intelligitur charitas, in argento veritas, in aere simultas, in ferro crudelitas, in testa fictili fragilitas. Testa enim facile frangitur, et per eam recte impatientiae fragilitas figuratur. Ferrum quod caetera omnia metalla comminuit et domat, competenter quidem crudelitatis violentiam designat, quae resistentia quaeque premit, ut opprimat. Aes autem argentum imitatur 1270Ain sono, sed multum dissonat in pretio. Sic simulatio suavem adulationis tinnitum habet in sermone, et suaviter reboat, et dulciter resultat in opere; sed quantum per verbum, et exercitium demulcet extrinsecus, tantum per consilium et desiderium pallet intrinsecus. Argentum quoque quo frequentiori usu atteritur, eo utique clarior redditur: sic veritas vehementi discussione quo magis eo melius commendatur, si veritatis consilia experientiae usu certiora, eo clariora redduntur. Charitatis claritatem auri fulgor exprimit, quae coelestis desiderii igne flammescit; et, sicut aurum caeteris metallis praefertur, sic charitas caeteras virtutes supergreditur. O quantum est inter testam et aurum, inter statuae finem et statuae principium! Statua, quae ex auro incipit, testam desinit. 1270BPuto, quia nemo repente fit turpissimus. Sed qui minima negligit, paulatim defluit. Sicut quibusdam profectuum gradibus ad alta conscenditur, sic rursum gradatim ad ima descenditur. Attende quomodo in hac statua prius aurum, postea argentum, post haec mediante aere, sequitur et ferrum. O quanta differentia auri et ferri! O quantum in se differunt charitas et crudelitas! Quid tamen de his ait Veritas? Abundabit, inquit, iniquitas, et refrigescet charitas multorum (Matth. XXIV). Vide, quaeso, unde quo pervenitur de abundantia charitatis ad abundantiam iniquitatis. Charitas ad aurum, iniquitas pertinet ad ferrum. O mirabilem (imo detestabilem) commatationem de charitate ad iniquitatem! Si averterit, inquit, 1270Cjustus justitia sua, et fecerit iniquitatem, omnes justitiae ejus quas fecerat, non recordabuntur (Ezech. XVIII). Opus detestabile et dignum confusione, quod inchoatur ex auro, et consummatur in testa et ferro. Sic namque describitur pedum pars ferrea, et quaedam testea. O confusibiles operarios, qui, in initio conversionis suae, opus suum inchoant ex auro, et tandem consummant luto. Scimus, quia saepe cum immundus spiritus exierit de homine, quaerens requiem et non inveniens, assumptis secum septem spiritibus nequioribus se, revertitur in domum suam, et ingressus habitat ibi, et fiunt novissima hominis illius pejora prioribus. Quam multos videmus in initio suae conversionis spe gaudentes, spiritu ferventes, in tribulatione patientes, sollicitos in opere, 1270Dstudiosos in lectione, devotos in oratione. Quid isti videntur tibi, nisi in auro operari, et in charitate initiari? Charitas enim, ut scriptum est, patiens est, benigna est, omnia suffert, omnia credit, omnia sperat, omnia sustinet (I Cor. XIII). Nonne operari tibi videtur in auro, cui dictum est a Domino: Novi opera tua, et charitatem, et fidem, et ministerium, et patientiam tuam? (Apoc. VIII.) Sed sunt multi qui ad tempus credunt, et in tempore tentationis recedunt. Nec tamen qui hujusmodi sunt, statim se in infima demergunt, sed prius de bono in pejus, dehinc de minus bono in malum, et demum de malo in deterius corruunt. Mons enim cadens paulatim defluit, et terra alluvione consumitur. Siquidem quae hujusmodi sunt, paulatim incipiunt a pristino desiderio 1271Atepescere, et priori fervore magis magisque deficere.

CHAPTER XXIII. That from a great summit of virtues the human mind meus is often wont to fall by degrees into the depths.

He that standeth, says the Apostle, let him take heed lest he fall (I Cor. X). [Vulg. qui se existimat stare, "he that thinketh himself to stand"] From however lofty a summit one may easily fall into the depths, or be plunged into the deep; nay, beyond doubt, the higher the step, the worse the fall. That dream of Nabuchodonosor ought always both to render us cautious and to make us solicitous. See, I beseech thee, the statue of his vision; 1269Dsee, I say, from how great a loftiness it begins, and to what an end it is at last brought down. It has indeed a head of gold, and an end of clay. What is dearer than gold, what viler than clay? Note this also, how by degrees it inclines to worse things, and at length makes its end in a most foul ending. The first part is described as of gold, the second of silver, the third of bronze, the fourth of iron, and the fifth of clay. In the gold is understood charity; in the silver, truth; in the bronze, pretense; in the iron, cruelty; in the earthenware clay, fragility. For clay is easily broken, and by it the fragility of impatience is rightly figured. Iron, which crushes and tames all other metals, fittingly designates the violence of cruelty, which presses upon whatever resists, that it may crush it. But bronze imitates silver 1270Ain sound, yet is much at variance in price. So simulation has the sweet ring of flattery in speech, and resounds sweetly, and echoes pleasantly in work; but as much as by word and practice it soothes without, so much by counsel and desire it grows pale within. Silver too, the more frequent the use by which it is worn, so much the brighter is it made: so truth, the more it is shaken by vehement discussion, so much the better is it commended, if the counsels of truth, made more certain by the use of experience, are thereby rendered the brighter. The splendor of gold expresses the brightness of charity, which blazes with the fire of heavenly desire; and as gold is preferred to the other metals, so charity surpasses the other virtues. O how great is the distance between clay and gold, between the end of the statue and the beginning of the statue! The statue which begins from gold ends in clay. 1270BI think that no one becomes most base of a sudden. But he who neglects the least things slips away little by little. As one climbs to the heights by certain steps of progress, so again one descends by degrees to the depths. Note how in this statue there is first gold, afterwards silver, and after these, with bronze between, iron also follows. O how great the difference of gold and iron! O how greatly do charity and cruelty differ from one another! Yet what says the Truth concerning these? Iniquity shall abound, he says, and the charity of many shall grow cold (Matth. XXIV). [Vulg. quoniam abundavit iniquitas, "because iniquity hath abounded"] See, I pray, from whence one comes to what: from the abundance of charity to the abundance of iniquity. Charity belongs to gold, iniquity belongs to iron. O the wonderful (nay, the detestable) change commatationem from charity to iniquity! If the just man, he says, 1270Cturn away from his justice, and do iniquity, all his justices which he had done shall not be remembered (Ezech. XVIII). A detestable work, and worthy of confusion, which is begun from gold and consummated in clay and iron. For thus is described a part of the feet as of iron, and a certain part as of clay. O shameful workmen, who at the beginning of their conversion begin their work from gold, and at last consummate it in mire. We know that often, when the unclean spirit has gone out of a man, seeking rest and not finding it, having taken with him seven other spirits more wicked than himself, he returns into his house, and entering in dwells there, and the last things of that man become worse than the first. How many do we see at the beginning of their conversion rejoicing in hope, fervent in spirit, patient in tribulation, solicitous in work, 1270Dstudious in reading, devout in prayer. What do these seem to thee to do, but to work in gold, and to be initiated in charity? For charity, as it is written, is patient, is kind, beareth all things, believeth all things, hopeth all things, endureth all things (I Cor. XIII). Does he not seem to thee to work in gold, to whom it was said by the Lord: I know thy works, and thy charity, and thy faith, and thy ministry, and thy patience? (Apoc. VIII.) [Vulg. et fidem, et caritatem tuam, faith before charity] But there are many who believe for a time, and in the time of temptation fall away. Yet those who are such do not at once plunge themselves into the lowest things, but fall first from the good into the worse, then from the less good into the bad, and at last from the bad into the yet worse. For a falling mountain slips away little by little, and the earth is consumed by a flood. For such as these begin little by little to grow lukewarm from their former desire, 1271Aand to fail more and more from their earlier fervor.

CAPUT XXIV. Quomodo vel quibus corruptionis gradibus de summis in ima corruat saepe animus humanus.

Nec statim tamen inchoata deserunt, sed agunt adhuc bona quae possunt. Refrigescente namque charitate, operantur sane bona ex deliberatione. Quid putamus tamen interesse inter priorem illum statum, et istum secundum? Multum per omnem modum. Aliud est bonum sequi ex desiderio, atque aliud ex solo consilio. Aliud, inquam, est bonum operari cum magna delectatione, atque aliud ex sola deliberatione. Istud bonum, sed illud optimum. Istud ad argentum, illud pertinet ad aurum. Pretiosum 1271Bquidem metallum in argento, licet longe vilius sit auro. Studeat ergo in argento operari, cui forte deest copia auri: Si non potest bona agere ex delectatione, age tamen saltem ex veritate, et veritas liberabit te. Vultis autem nosse opus quod fit ex hujusmodi argento, quomodo luceat, vel quantum placeat, et possessorem suum quam divitem vel securum reddat? Omnis, inquit, qui facit veritatem, venit ad lucem, ut manifestentur opera ejus, quoniam bona sunt (Joan. III). Bonum est itaque argento hujusmodi abundare, sed nec minus stultum aurum suum in argentum mutare. Omnis enim homo mittens manum suam in aratrum, et respiciens retro, non est aptus regno Dei. Vultis autem nosse quomodo divinus eum sermo per increpationem feriat, 1271Cqui aureum opus in argentum commutarat? Scio, inquit, opera tua et laborem, et patientiam tuam (Luc. IX). Ecce opus bonum, ecce brachia, ecce pectus argenteum. Sed videamus quid sequitur, cum postmodum subinfertur: Sed habeo, inquit, adversum te, quod charitatem tuam primam dereliquisti (Apoc. VIII). Arguitur ergo qui adhuc bonum agit, quod charitatem primam reliquit, et aureum caput in pectus argenteum deflexit. Quicunque hujusmodi te esse perpendis, memor esto unde excideris, et age poenitentiam, et prima opera fac. Alioqui time illud quod sequitur, quod minime resipiscendi divinus sermo minatur: Sin autem, inquit, venio tibi, et movebo candelabrum tuum de 1271Dloco suo, nisi poenitentiam egeris (Apoc. II). Time ergo nisi ad priora bona citius redeas, ne adhuc in deteriora cadas. Elige quid malis; ascendere sive descendere. Non enim potest homo in eodem fixus diutius stare. Fugit enim velut umbra, et nunquam in eodem statu permanet. Memoriter tene, nec mediocriter time, quoniam qui ad primam charitatem non revertitur, cito ejus candelabrum de loco suo transfertur. Quid per candelabrum gestatorium lucis, nisi opus bonum, opus veritatis? Hujusmodi candelabrum stat in loco suo, quando bonum opus surgit ex proposito bono. Sed hujusmodi candelabrum de loco suo transfertur, cum bona boni operis intentio in malam commutatur. Sic opus argenteum transit in aereum. Et est quidem frequens 1272Ahoc malum inter filios hominum. Saepe enim quod prius quis agebat ex veritate, ad laudem hominum resolutus, postmodum actitat pro humano favore: Popule, inquit, meus, qui beatum te dicunt, ipsi te decipiunt, et viam gressuum tuorum dissipant (Isa. III). Qui igitur blandum se exhibet in sermone, benignum in opere, pravum autem atque perversum in cogitatione et voluntate, iste profecto abjecto argento jam operatur in aere. Aeris enim more suaviter tinnit extrinsecus, sed voluntatis depravatione pallescit intrinsecus. Nunquidnam tibi non tale videtur opus illorum, qui loquuntur pacem cum proximo suo, mala autem in cordibus eorum. In hujusmodi opere, ut arbitror, desudabat, quem Dominus increpando arguit, cum dicat: Scio opera tua, quia 1272Bnomen habes, quod vivas, et mortuus es (Apoc. III). Sed quisquis hujusmodi es, non te existimes diu posse latere: Sed si exaltatus fueris ut aquila, et inter sidera posueris nidum tuum: inde detraham te, dicit Dominus (Abd. I). Quandoque oportet te propheticam imo Dominicam vocem audire: Denuda turpitudinem tuam, discooperi humerum, revela crura, transi flumina. Et tunc revelabitur ignominia tua, et videbitur opprobrium tuum (Isa. XLVII). Denudata autem turpitudine tua, cum jam coeperint venter et femora aerea apparere, incipies mox laudatoribus tuis, imo jam derisoribus tuis sordescere, et de die in diem magis magisque vilescere. Jam tunc illis incipies irasci, indignari, illos detestari et crudeliter persequi, quibus 1272Cantea multum placere studuisti. Et quam blandus eras prius, tam eris postmodum asper et durus. Sic aes tuum mutabitur in ferrum, diceturque tibi: Quomodo conversa es in amaritudinem vitis aliena? (Jer. II.) Haud dubium est opus aereum mutasse in ferrum. Lenitatem in severitatem, quorum os maledictione et amaritudine plenum est, qui prius linguis suis dolose agebant, venenum aspidum sub labiis eorum. Qui prius vos tam blandos, tam benignos exhibuistis, unde jam bella et lites in vobis? Jam zelum amarum habetis, et contentiones in vobis. Occiditis et zelatis, litigatis et belligeratis, et unde haec tam saeva crudelitas et ferri duritia in vobis? Quid putas causae est, quod sunt tam veloces pedes 1272Deorum ad effundendum sanguinem, nisi quod tibias et plantas ferreas habent, qui laetantur cum male egerint, et exsultant in rebus pessimis? Sed hoc est in istis prae omnibus mirabile, imo supra omnia miserabile, quod sicut sunt prompti et audaces ad mala inferenda, sic sunt pusillanimes et supra modum impatientes ad toleranda illata, ut recte mirari possis quonam modo convenire possit ferrum cum testa, tanta crudelitas cum tanta impatientia. Ecce statua nostra in qualem finem desinit, quae post caput aureum fictiles plantas accepit. Filii Sion inclyti, et amicti auro primo, quomodo reputati sunt in vasa testea, opus manuum figuli? Quid auro pretiosius, quid testa vilius? Quomodo ergo obscuratum est aurum, mutatus est color optimus? 1273AQuid auro tractabilius, quid testa fragilius? Quomodo charitas se attrectare; quomodo attenuare permittit, quae omnia suffert, omnia credit, omnia sperat, omnia sustinet? Quid autem voluntate contemptibilius, quid delicata mente dejectius, quae super et in prosperis per concupiscentiam resolvitur, et in adversis per impatientiam frangitur? Vae illis, qui perdiderunt sustinentiam, qui dereliquerunt vias rectas, et diverterunt in pravas (Eccle. II). Contritio enim et infelicitas in viis eorum. Contritio propter testae corruptibilitatem, et infelicitas propter ferri rigorem, eo quod pedum pars quaedam sit testea, pars vero quaedam sit ferrea. Quid autem est, quod hujusmodi homines ad sua iterum desideria convertuntur, quod post opera aeris et ferri ad 1273Bopus figuli trahuntur, nisi quod non habeant (praeterquam in concupiscentiis suis) ubi delectentur, eo quod per simulationem prius perdiderint gaudium bonae conscientiae, et postmodum per crudelitatem gloriam famae. Ut autem ea quae diffusius jam diximus, ad compendium revocemus, in capite aureo, intelligitur devotio, in membris argenteis discretio, in aereis simulatio, in ferreis indignatio, in testeis dissolutio.

CHAPTER XXIV. How, or by what steps of corruption, the human spirit often falls from the highest things into the lowest.

Yet they do not at once forsake what they have begun, but still do the good things they can. For when charity has grown cold, they do indeed work good things out of deliberation. Yet what do we think there is between that former state and this second one? Much in every way. It is one thing to follow the good out of desire, and another out of counsel alone. It is one thing, I say, to work the good with great delight, and another out of deliberation alone. This is good, but that is best. This belongs to silver, that belongs to gold. A precious 1271Bmetal indeed is in silver, though it be far viler than gold. Let him therefore study to work in silver who perchance lacks abundance of gold: if he cannot do good things out of delight, yet do it at least out of truth, and the truth shall make thee free. But do you wish to know the work that is made out of such silver, how it shines, or how much it pleases, and how rich or how secure it renders its possessor? Everyone, he says, that doeth truth cometh to the light, that his works may be made manifest, because they are good (Joan. III). [Vulg. Qui autem facit veritatem … quia in Deo sunt facta, "But he that doeth truth … because they are done in God"] It is good therefore to abound in silver of this kind, but no less foolish to change one's gold into silver. For every man that putteth his hand to the plough and looketh back is not fit for the kingdom of God. But do you wish to know how the divine word strikes with rebuke 1271Chim who had changed a golden work into silver? I know, he says, thy works and thy labor, and thy patience (Luc. IX). Behold a good work; behold the arms, behold the breast of silver. But let us see what follows, when afterwards it is subjoined: But I have, he says, against thee, that thou hast forsaken thy first charity (Apoc. VIII). He therefore who still does good is reproved because he has left his first charity, and has bent the golden head down into a breast of silver. Whosoever thou art that judgest thyself to be of this kind, be mindful whence thou art fallen, and do penance, and do the first works. Otherwise fear that which follows, which the divine word threatens against him who by no means comes to his senses: But if not, he says, I come to thee, and will move thy candlestick out of 1271Dits place, unless thou do penance (Apoc. II). Fear therefore, unless thou return the more speedily to thy former good things, lest thou fall yet into worse. Choose which thou wouldst rather, to ascend or to descend. For a man cannot stand fixed in the same state for long. For he flees as a shadow, and never continues in the same state. Hold it in memory, and fear it not moderately, that he who does not return to his first charity, his candlestick is quickly moved out of its place. What is meant by the candlestick, the bearer of light, but a good work, a work of truth? Such a candlestick stands in its place, when a good work rises out of a good purpose. But such a candlestick is moved out of its place, when the good intention of a good work is changed into an evil one. So a work of silver passes into one of bronze. And this evil is indeed frequent 1272Aamong the sons of men. For often what one formerly did out of truth, being dissolved toward the praise of men, he afterwards keeps doing for human favor: O my people, he says, they that call thee blessed, the same deceive thee, and destroy the way of thy steps (Isa. III). He therefore who shows himself bland in speech, kind in work, but crooked and perverse in thought and in will, he assuredly, having cast away silver, works now in bronze. For after the manner of bronze he rings sweetly without, but by the depravity of his will he grows pale within. Does not the work of those seem to thee to be such, who speak peace with their neighbor, but evils are in their hearts. In such a work, as I judge, he sweated whom the Lord reproved in rebuking him, when he says: I know thy works, that 1272Bthou hast a name that thou livest, and thou art dead (Apoc. III). But whosoever thou art that art such, do not think that thou canst long lie hid: But if thou be exalted as an eagle, and set thy nest among the stars, thence will I draw thee down, saith the Lord (Abd. I). One day thou must hear the prophetic, nay the Lord's, voice: Uncover thy shame, lay bare thy shoulder, make bare thy legs, pass over the rivers. And then shall thy ignominy be revealed, and thy reproach shall be seen (Isa. XLVII) [Vulg. Isa. 47:3 runs on without Et tunc, transi flumina, revelabitur ignominia tua; the same verse is printed at 1361A of this work without the added words]. But when thy shame has been uncovered, when the belly and the thighs of bronze have begun to appear, thou wilt soon begin to grow foul to thy praisers, nay now to thy mockers, and from day to day to grow viler and viler. Then wilt thou begin to be angry with them, to be indignant, to detest them and cruelly to persecute them, whom 1272Cbefore thou hadst studied much to please. And as bland as thou wast before, so harsh and hard wilt thou be afterwards. So thy bronze shall be changed into iron, and it shall be said to thee: How art thou turned into bitterness, O strange vine? (Jer. II.) [Vulg. conversa es mihi in pravum, "turned unto me into that which is depraved"] There is no doubt that he has changed a work of bronze into iron. Gentleness into severity: whose mouth is full of cursing and bitterness, who before dealt deceitfully with their tongues, the venom of asps under their lips. You who before showed yourselves so bland, so kind, whence now are wars and quarrels among you? Now you have bitter zeal, and contentions among you. You kill and you envy, you go to law and you make war; and whence is this so savage cruelty and hardness of iron in you? What thinkest thou is the cause that their feet are so swift 1272Dto shed blood, unless that they have shins and soles of iron, who rejoice when they have done evil, and exult in the worst things? But this in them is above all wonderful, nay above all things pitiable, that as they are prompt and bold in inflicting evils, so are they faint-hearted and beyond measure impatient in bearing the evils inflicted; so that thou mayest rightly wonder in what manner iron can agree with clay, so great a cruelty with so great an impatience. Behold to what an end our statue comes, which after a head of gold received soles of earthenware. The noble sons of Sion, and clothed with the finest gold, how are they esteemed as vessels of clay, the work of the potter's hands? What is more precious than gold, what viler than clay? How then is the gold become obscured, the finest color changed? 1273AWhat is more tractable than gold, what more fragile than clay? How does charity permit herself to be handled; how to be drawn thin, she who beareth all things, believeth all things, hopeth all things, endureth all things? But what is more contemptible than the will, what more abject than a delicate mind, which both above and in prosperous things is dissolved through concupiscence, and in adverse things is broken through impatience? Woe to them that have lost endurance, that have forsaken the right ways, and have turned aside into crooked ones (Eccle. II). For crushing and unhappiness are in their ways. Crushing, because of the corruptibility of clay; and unhappiness, because of the rigor of iron, in that a certain part of the feet is of clay, but a certain part is of iron. But what is it that men of this kind are turned again to their own desires, that after the works of bronze and of iron they are drawn to 1273Bthe work of the potter, unless that they have not (save in their own concupiscences) wherein to be delighted, because by simulation they first lost the joy of a good conscience, and afterwards, by cruelty, the glory of reputation. But that we may recall to a summary the things we have now said more diffusely: in the head of gold is understood devotion; in the members of silver, discretion; in those of bronze, simulation; in those of iron, indignation; in those of clay, dissolution.

CAPUT XXV. De praedictorum differentia graduum, et qualitate singulorum.

Notandum sane, quid de quibus membris dicitur, et qualia quaeque describantur. Describatur 1273Cautem caput aureum, pectus argenteum, brachia autem argentea, venter et femora aerea, tibiae ferreae, pedes ferrei, vel testei pro parte. Capite totum corpus regitur, unde per ipsum recte intentio designatur, quia per ipsam omne actionis nostrae corpus, sive in bonum, sive in malum finem deducitur. Consilium ad cor pertinet, et cor sub pectore latet. Brachiis opera nostra explicamus, cum aliquid a nobis fieri volumus. Venter tenerior pars corporis et velamentum fetoris obsequium portendit compassionis. Femur pudendis subjicitur et perfectam videri se vult humilitas, quae ad vilia et indigna inclinatur. Tibiae caeteris membris ossa longiora et robustiora portant, unde et recte severitatis rigorem designant. Pedibus terram tangimus, etiam cum 1273Derecti stamus, et quantumcunque mens se ad coelestia extendat, sine hujus vitae subsidiis necessitatem suam non sustentat. Per caput itaque, ut dictum est, intelligitur intentio, per pectus deliberatio, per brachium exercitatio, per ventrem compassio, per femur humilitas, per tibiam severitas, per pedem necessitas, per caput desiderium intentionis, per pectus studium deliberationis, per brachium exercitium actionis, per ventrem obsequium pietatis, per femur servitium subjectionis, per tibiam flagellum severitatis, per pedem subsidium necessitatis. Si ergo caput intentio, et aurum devotio, quid aliud est aureum caput, nisi devota intentio? Si pectus deliberatio, brachium actio, argentum vero discretio, quid aliud erit pectus et brachia argentea quam 1274Aconsilia et opera discreta? In ventre compassio, in femore subjectio, et in aere simulatio. Quid itaque aliud est venter et femora aerea, nisi pietas et humilitas ficta? Si per tibiam severitas, si per ferrum crudelitas, quid aliud intelligitur in tibiis ferreis, nisi severitas crudelis? Pes necessitatem, testa voluptatem. Planta ergo testea designat voluptuosam superfluitatem. Pes autem ex parte ferreus, et ex parte testeus describitur, quia delicata mens nunc in eorum odium obduratur, per quos forte a suis desideriis restringitur, nunc vero cum concupita suppetunt, in eorum luxu resolvitur. Quid itaque est fulgor capitis aurei, nisi fervor coelestis desiderii? Claritas pectoris argentei et brachii argentei, certitudo recti consilii et rectitudo operis certi. Sonoritas 1274Baerei ventris et femoris, blandimentum falsae pietatis, et fictae humilitatis. Robur ferreae tibiae, rigor immoderatae disciplinae, fragilitas testei pedis, effrenatio superfluitatis. Caput itaque aureum, sancta desideria faciunt, pectus et brachium argenteum, consilia et opera recta. Ventrem et femur aereum, obsequia et servitia ficta, tibias ferreas flagella saeva, plantam testeam subsidia delicata.

CHAPTER XXV. On the difference of the aforesaid steps, and the quality of each.

It is indeed to be noted what is said of which members, and of what sort each of them is described. Now there is described 1273Ca golden head, a silver breast, silver arms too, a belly and thighs of bronze, iron shins, feet of iron, or in part of clay. By the head the whole body is ruled, whence by it intention is rightly signified, because by intention the whole body of our action is led to an end, whether good or evil. Counsel belongs to the heart, and the heart lies hidden beneath the breast. With the arms we carry out our works, when we will something to be done by us. The belly, the more tender part of the body and the covering of foulness, portends the service of compassion. The thigh is set beneath the shameful parts, and humility, which bends down to base and unworthy things, wishes to be seen as perfect. The shins carry bones longer and sturdier than the other members, whence they also rightly signify the rigor of severity. With the feet we touch the earth, even when we 1273Dstand upright; and however much the mind stretches itself out toward heavenly things, without the supports of this life it does not sustain its own necessity. By the head, therefore, as has been said, intention is understood; by the breast, deliberation; by the arm, exercise; by the belly, compassion; by the thigh, humility; by the shin, severity; by the foot, necessity; by the head, the desire of intention; by the breast, the zeal of deliberation; by the arm, the exercise of action; by the belly, the service of piety; by the thigh, the servitude of subjection; by the shin, the scourge of severity; by the foot, the support of necessity. If then the head is intention, and gold devotion, what else is a golden head but a devout intention? If the breast is deliberation, the arm action, and silver discretion, what else will a silver breast and silver arms be than 1274Adiscreet counsels and works? In the belly, compassion; in the thigh, subjection; and in the bronze, simulation. What else, then, is a bronze belly and bronze thighs but feigned piety and feigned humility? If by the shin severity, if by iron cruelty, what else is understood in iron shins but a cruel severity? The foot signifies necessity, the clay pleasure. The clay sole, therefore, signifies voluptuous superfluity. But the foot is described as in part of iron and in part of clay, because the pampered mind is now hardened into hatred of those by whom it is perhaps restrained from its own desires, and now, when the things it has coveted are at hand, is dissolved in the luxury of them. What, then, is the gleam of the golden head but the fervor of heavenly desire? The brightness of the silver breast and of the silver arm is the certainty of right counsel and the rightness of a certain work. The resonance 1274Bof the bronze belly and thigh is the allurement of false piety and of feigned humility. The strength of the iron shin is the rigor of immoderate discipline; the fragility of the clay foot, the unbridling of superfluity. The golden head, therefore, holy desires make; the silver breast and arm, right counsels and works. The bronze belly and thigh, feigned services and servitudes; the iron shins, savage scourges; the clay sole, dainty supports.

CAPUT XXVI. Dictum est quomodo defluat homo saepe de bono in malum, nunc dicitur quomodo corruat saepe de malo in malum et vitio in vitium.

Si quis autem totum quod de statua dicitur velit accipere in malo, ejus assertioni absque dubio facile 1274Ccedo. Sed sic tamen sensum suum qui ejusmodi est studeat approbare, ut praedictum minime audeat condemnare, eo quod Spiritus sanctus soleat saepe in uno quod loquitur plura designare. Nam, sicut aliquid est in hoc scriptione quod facilius accommodatur ad lectionem illam, sic et aliquid invenitur in eadem quod levius applicatur ad istam. Nam aurum sane hujus statuae, cum optimum legatur, accommodantius in meliorem partem accipi posse videtur. Sed, sicut hoc secundum praedictam lectionem recte accipitur juxta rei veritatem, sic in hac lectione quidem convenienter satis referri potest ad suam de se superbientis animae aestimationem. Sic quod statua non animal describitur hoc quod per revelationem cernitur, huic secundae lectioni alacrius 1274Darridet, ubi totum quod dicitur in malam partem accipere placet. Quid enim aliud est hujusmodi statua nisi humana figura, sive humana vita? Et quid aliud est humana figura sine rationabili anima, nisi actio congrua sine intentione recta? Toties itaque non dicam animal nutritur, sed statua juxta humanam effigiem fabricatur, quoties homo in ordine conversationem suam secundum carnis prudentiam (quae inimica est Deo) moderatur. Videamus itaque hujusmodi statuae qualia quaecunque membra divinus sermo describat, cum dicat, hujus statuae caput ex auro optimo erat, etc. Secundum hanc itaque acceptionem qua totum referimus in malam partem, per aurum potest intelligi arrogantia. per argentum jactantia, per aes pertinacia, per 1275Aferrum saevitia, per testam impatientia. Aurum siquidem omnia metalla pretii sui dignitate praecedit, et superba mens caeteros omnes in sui comparatione despicit. Argentum suavem, et admodum dulcem sonum habet, et jactantia in sui laudis praeconio blandam jucundamque se exhibet. Aes eo quod valde durabile existat, recte satis pertinaciam figurat, quae a suo consilio, vel proposito vix vel nunquam deflecti queat. Ferrum quod caetera soleat metalla domare convenienter satis saevitiam designat, quae solet caeteros premere. Per testam autem quae facile frangitur, recte satis impatientia designatur. Aureum itaque sibi caput fabricat, qui sibi sanctitatem arrogat. Pectus argenteum et brachia argentea circumferre videtur, qui de consilii et operis 1275Bsui rectitudine gloriatur. Quasi ventrem et femora quodammodo aerea habet, qui in his etiam pertinax permanet quae propter vilitatem despicere, vel propter inhonestatem erubescere deberet. Tibias ferreas habere se ostendit, qui et contra superiores se per audaciam erigit, et inferiores per saevitiam premit. Testeis plantis innititur qui terrenorum amori immoderatius incumbens ad impatientiam effrenatur.

Notandum sane quibus corruptionis gradibus defectus nostri malum incrementum accipiat, quomodo gradatim ad deteriora devergens usque in ima descendat. Vide quomodo arrogantiam sequitur jactantia, jactantiam pertinacia, pertinaciam saevitia, saevitiam impatientia. Jactantiam sane arrogantia praecedit, quia nisi prius elata mens de se alta sentiret, 1275Cnunquam tam impudenter de scientia, vel sanctitate jactaret. Cum autem quod in semetipsa de sua scientia, vel sanctitate sentit, aliis etiam persuasum esse credit, quid jam mirum si in suo consilio, vel proposito pertinax perstiterit? Nisi vero in suo consilio, vel proposito prius pertinaciter persisteret, nunquam pro eorum defensione in alios tam improbe saeviret. Saevitia impatientiam generat, quia quo quisque proximum suum minus amat, eo utique minus infirma molestius tolerat. Scimus autem quia amoris alveus quo amplius erga proximum obstruitur extrinsecus, eo altius erga semetipsum intumescit intrinsecus. Et quanto quisque miserabilius per privatum amorem in semetipso 1275Dlanguet, tanto utique molestias suas molestius sustinet, tantoque difficilius desideria sua cohibet. Hinc est quod erga alios severus, et in semetipso resolutus, dummodo in alios furit, modo in semetipso torpescit, pedibus ex parte ferreis, et ex parte testeis incedit. Et quidem saepe quaedam adeo ex magnis conatibus, quos in principio conversionis audacter praesumpserunt, tam enormiter inanescunt, ut in tanta dissolutione qua tandem tam enerviter vivunt, nec tenue quidem non dicam exprobrationis sed nec correctionis, imo blandae admonitionis verbum patienter audire possunt: Vae miseris. Quid, quaeso, jam de his poterit fieri, vel quae jam spes ulterius haberi, qui propter arrogantiam nullo modo possunt a suo tumore dejici, 1276Anec propter pertinaciam de suo errore convinci, nec propter ferri duritiam a suo rigore deflecti, nec propter testeam fragilitatem de suo corpore redargui?

CHAPTER XXVI. It has been said how a man often flows down from good into evil; now it is said how he often falls headlong from evil into evil and from vice into vice.

But if anyone should wish to take the whole of what is said of the statue in an evil sense, I readily yield, without doubt, 1274Cto his assertion. Yet let him who is of this mind be zealous so to approve his own meaning that he by no means dare to condemn what has been said before, in that the Holy Spirit is often wont to signify many things in the one thing he speaks. For as there is something in this writing which is more easily fitted to that reading, so also something is found in the same which is more lightly applied to this one. For the gold of this statue indeed, since it is read as the best, seems able to be taken more fittingly in the better sense. But as this is rightly taken, according to the aforesaid reading, in keeping with the truth of the matter, so in this reading it can indeed be referred suitably enough to the estimate which the soul that is proud of itself makes of itself. So the fact that what is beheld through the revelation is described as a statue and not as a living creature smiles the more eagerly 1274Dupon this second reading, where it is pleasing to take the whole of what is said in the evil sense. For what else is such a statue but a human figure, or a human life? And what else is a human figure without a rational soul but a fitting action without a right intention? So often, therefore — I will not say a living creature is nourished, but a statue is fashioned after the human likeness — as often as a man in an order regulates his manner of life according to the prudence of the flesh (which is an enemy to God). Let us see, then, what sort of members the divine discourse describes for such a statue, when it says: the head of this statue was of the best gold, and so on. According to this acceptation, then, by which we refer the whole to the evil sense, by gold arrogance can be understood. by silver boastfulness, by bronze obstinacy, by 1275Airon savagery, by clay impatience. For gold goes before all metals in the dignity of its price, and the proud mind despises all others in comparison with itself. Silver has a sweet and exceedingly pleasant sound, and boastfulness shows itself soothing and delightful in the proclamation of its own praise. Bronze, in that it is exceedingly durable, figures obstinacy rightly enough, which can scarcely or never be turned aside from its own counsel or purpose. Iron, because it is wont to tame the other metals, suitably enough signifies savagery, which is wont to crush others. But by clay, which is easily broken, impatience is rightly enough signified. He therefore fashions a golden head for himself who arrogates holiness to himself. He is seen to carry about a silver breast and silver arms who glories in the rightness of his own counsel and 1275Bwork. He has, as it were, a belly and thighs after a manner of bronze who remains obstinate even in those things which he ought to despise for their baseness, or to blush at for their dishonor. He shows himself to have iron shins who both raises himself against his superiors through boldness and crushes his inferiors through savagery. He leans upon clay soles who, bearing down too immoderately upon the love of earthly things, is unbridled into impatience.

It is indeed to be noted by what steps of corruption our failing takes on an evil increase, how, sinking gradually toward worse things, it descends even to the lowest. See how boastfulness follows arrogance; obstinacy, boastfulness; savagery, obstinacy; impatience, savagery. Arrogance indeed goes before boastfulness, because unless the mind, lifted up, first thought lofty things of itself, 1275Cit would never so shamelessly boast of its knowledge or of its holiness. But when it believes that what it thinks within itself of its own knowledge or holiness has been persuaded to others as well, what wonder is it now if it has stood obstinate in its own counsel or purpose? But unless it first persisted obstinately in its own counsel or purpose, it would never rage so wickedly against others in their defense. Savagery begets impatience, because by how much the less each man loves his own neighbor, by so much the less, assuredly, does he bear weak things the more troublesomely. But we know that the channel of love, the more it is blocked outwardly toward the neighbor, the higher it swells inwardly toward itself. And by how much the more wretchedly each man languishes in himself through private love, by so much the more troublesomely 1275Ddoes he endure his own troubles, and by so much the more hardly does he restrain his own desires. Hence it is that, severe toward others and slack in himself, so long as he rages against others, and now grows torpid in himself, he walks with feet in part of iron and in part of clay. And indeed certain ones often become so enormously empty of the great efforts which at the beginning of their conversion they boldly presumed upon, that in the great dissolution in which at last they live so nervelessly they cannot patiently hear even a slight word, I will not say of reproach, but not even of correction, nay, of gentle admonition: Woe to the wretched. What, I ask, can now be done with these, or what hope can now be had hereafter, who by reason of arrogance can in no way be cast down from their swelling, 1276Anor by reason of obstinacy be convicted of their error, nor by reason of the hardness of iron be turned aside from their rigor, nor by reason of their clay fragility be reproved concerning their own body?

CAPUT XXVII. Quibus reparationis gradibus mens saepe ex divina operatione resurgit, quae de magnis bonis in ima cadit.

Sed nec horum tamen ferream, seu lapideam duritiam debemus despicere, potens est enim Dominus de lapidibus istis suscitare filios Abrahae. Quae enim impossibilia sunt apud homines, apud Deum possibilia sunt. Vide enim quod sequitur: Videbas, inquit, ita, donec abscissus est lapis sine manibus, et percussit statuam in pedibus ejus ferreis, et fictilibus, 1276Bet comminuit eos (Dan. II). Ecce lapis sine manibus abscinditur, unde ferri duritia conteratur, et quidem recte. Quae enim manus talem lapidem concidere, seu etiam abscindere posset, qui ferrum conterere vel comminuere valeret? Sed quod non potest humana opera vel quaelibet humana industria, fit in sola voluntate divina. Solus utique Deus est, qui sine aliquo conatu, sine aliquo sui motu, quoties et quaecunque voluerit potest operari, stabilisque manens dat cuncta moveri. Sed quid, quaeso, est, qui hoc potuit et voluit facere manibus, minime voluit haec perficere? An forte in hujusmodi ferreis et obduratis mentibus melius operatur inspiratione quam percussione, corrigiturque saepe interna illustratione, quem non possunt flagella corrigere? Percussi, 1276Cinquit, filios vestros, et non doluerunt, renuerunt recipere disciplinam (Jer. V). Sed cum multo sit levius apud nos dicere quam facere, saepe tamen in his etiam mentibus, in quibus videtur percutientis manus opera defecisse, operatur quidem miro et incomprehensibili modo interno suo verbo vel sibilo: Verbum, inquit, quod egredietur de ore meo, non redibit ad me vacuum, sed faciet quaecunque volui, et prosperabit in his ad quae misi illud (Isa. LV). Sic et Spiritus sanctus ubi vult spirat. Verbo enim Domini, coeli firmati sunt, et spiritu oris ejus omnis virtus eorum. Lapis itaque sine manibus abscissus, timor Domini divinitus inspiratus, in ictu hujus lapidis statua dejicitur, et monstruosum opus destruitur. 1276DSicut per timorem mens dejicitur a proposito bono, sic per timorem sane excutitur a proposito malo. Sicut enim initium omnis peccati superbia, sic initium sapientiae timor Domini. In hujusmodi itaque lapide statua concutitur, ut confusibilis mens confundatur et humilietur. Quid est hujusmodi percussio, nisi interna compunctio? Quid autem est percussae statuae comminutio, nisi compuncti cordis contritio et humiliatio? Sed scimus quia cor contritum et humiliatum Deus non despiciet. Melius itaque sane conteri in hoc lapide quam erectum stare et superbum sapere. Percussit, inquit, statuam in pedibus ejus ferreis et fictilibus, et comminuit eos. In inferioribus et deterioribus suis utique statua concutitur, quando mens elata atque 1277Aperversa per spiritum timoris Domini ad iniquitatis et infirmitatis suae considerationem reverberatur. Haec pia Domini percussio tam misericorditer intentatur quam utiliter in hac vita in nobis completur, cum dicitur: Arguam te, et statuam contra faciem tuam (Psal. XLIX). In hunc utique modum redargutus, et in infirma sui parte percussus fuerat, et per ejusmodi percussionem seipsum contra seipsum erexerat, qui dicebat: Quoniam iniquitatem meam ego cognosco, et peccatum meum contra me est semper (Psal. L). Sed postquam ferrum et testa concutitur, etiam caetera conteruntur. Tunc contrita sunt, inquit, pariter ferrum, testa, aes, argentum, et aurum. Vidisti superius, quibus gradibus bonitas defecit, crevit perversitas. Vide nunc quomodo gradatim 1277Bproficiat totius bonitatis fundamentum, humilitas: prius erubescimus vera mala, postea ficta bona. Novissime etiam vera bona cum sint imperfecta et inconsummata. Sic sane primum conteritur ferrum et testa, deinde membra aerea, novissime argentea et aurea. In malis facilius erubescimus iniquitatem quam infirmitatem. In bonis autem citius erubescimus imperfectionem quam intermissionem. Hinc est quod in infimis membris prius conteritur ferrum quam testa. Superius autem citius comminuuntur argentea, quam aurea membra. Primo itaque in ferri contritionem damnamus mala, quae per iniquitatem commisimus. In testae autem contritione, ea quae per infirmitatem contraximus. Aeris autem 1277Ccontritio quid est quam simulati boni reprobatio? In argenteis membris hoc est, quod nobis minus placet, et quod minime placere debet, non quod sint bona, sed quod non sint optima. In aureo enim opere hoc solum sane reprehensibile cernitur, quod optimi operis inchoatio, usque ad digni vel debiti finis talum non perducitur. In argenti igitur contritione boni operis imperfectio. In auri autem contritione arguitur optimi operis intermissio. Hoc nunc de hujus statuae contritione jam diximus, secundum eam lectionem quam primo posuimus, ubi partim in bono partim in malo accepimus.

CHAPTER XXVII. By what steps of repair the mind that falls from great goods into the lowest often rises again by divine working.

But neither ought we for all that to despise the iron or stony hardness of these men, for the Lord is able of these stones to raise up sons to Abraham. For the things that are impossible with men are possible with God. For see what follows: Thou wast beholding, he says, thus, until a stone was cut out without hands, and struck the statue upon its feet of iron and of earthenware, 1276Band broke them small (Dan. II) [Vulg. adds de monte after lapis: the stone is "cut out of a mountain"]. Behold, a stone is cut out without hands, whereby the hardness of iron may be crushed; and rightly indeed. For what hand could cut down, or even cut out, such a stone as would be strong enough to crush or to break iron small? But what human work, or any human effort whatever, cannot do is done by the divine will alone. It is assuredly God alone who, without any exertion, without any motion of himself, can work as often and whatsoever he will, and, remaining stable, grants all things to be moved. But what, I ask, is it, that he who was able and willed to do this, by no means willed to accomplish these things with hands? Or does he perhaps work better in such iron and hardened minds by inspiration than by striking, and is he often corrected by an inward illumination whom scourges cannot correct? I have struck, 1276Che says, your sons, and they grieved not; they refused to receive discipline (Jer. V) [Vulg. Jer. 5:3 reads Percussisti eos … attrivisti eos, et renuerunt accipere disciplinam, "Thou hast struck them … thou hast bruised them"; the opening words are those of Jer. 2:30]. But although with us it is much lighter to say than to do, yet often even in those minds in which the work of the striking hand seems to have failed, he works indeed in a wondrous and incomprehensible manner by his own inward word or whisper: The word, he says, that shall go forth from my mouth shall not return to me void, but shall do whatsoever I have willed, and shall prosper in those things for which I have sent it (Isa. LV). So too the Holy Spirit breathes where he wills. For by the word of the Lord the heavens were established, and all their power by the spirit of his mouth. The stone, therefore, cut out without hands is the fear of the Lord divinely inspired; at the stroke of this stone the statue is cast down, and the monstrous work is destroyed. 1276DAs by fear the mind is cast down from a good purpose, so by fear indeed it is shaken out of an evil purpose. For as pride is the beginning of every sin, so the fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom. By such a stone, then, the statue is shaken, that the mind that can be confounded may be confounded and humbled. What is such a striking but inward compunction? And what is the breaking small of the stricken statue but the contrition and humiliation of a heart pierced with compunction? But we know that God will not despise a contrite and humbled heart [Vulg. Ps. 50:19 addresses God, Cor contritum et humiliatum, Deus, non despicies, "thou, O God, wilt not despise"]. It is therefore indeed better to be crushed upon this stone than to stand erect and to be high-minded. It struck, he says, the statue upon its feet of iron and of earthenware, and broke them small. The statue, assuredly, is shaken in its lower and worse parts, when the mind, lifted up and 1277Aperverse, is beaten back by the spirit of the fear of the Lord to the consideration of its own iniquity and weakness. This loving striking of the Lord is threatened as mercifully as it is profitably completed in us in this life, when it is said: I will reprove thee, and will set against thy face (Psal. XLIX). In this manner assuredly he had been reproved and struck in the weak part of himself, and by such a striking had raised himself against himself, who said: For I acknowledge my iniquity, and my sin is always against me (Psal. L). But after the iron and the clay are shaken, the rest also are crushed. Then were broken in pieces together, he says, the iron, the clay, the bronze, the silver, and the gold. Thou hast seen above by what steps goodness failed and perversity grew. See now how the foundation of all goodness, humility, 1277Badvances step by step: first we blush at true evils, afterward at feigned goods. Last of all, at true goods also, since they are imperfect and unconsummated. So indeed first the iron and the clay are crushed, then the bronze members, last of all the silver and the golden. Among evils we blush more easily at iniquity than at weakness. But among goods we blush more quickly at imperfection than at interruption. Hence it is that in the lowest members the iron is crushed before the clay. But higher up the silver members are broken small more quickly than the golden. First, therefore, in the crushing of the iron we condemn the evils which we have committed through iniquity. But in the crushing of the clay, those things which we have contracted through weakness. And what is the crushing 1277Cof the bronze but the reprobation of a simulated good? In the silver members this is what pleases us less, and what ought least to please, not that they are good, but that they are not the best. For in the golden work this alone is indeed discerned as blameworthy, that the beginning of the best work is not carried through even to the heel of a worthy or due end. In the crushing of the silver, therefore, the imperfection of a good work. But in the crushing of the gold the interruption of the best work is arraigned. This we have now already said concerning the crushing of this statue, according to that reading which we set down first, where we took it partly in a good and partly in an evil sense.

CAPUT XXVIII. Quibus promotionis gradibus saepe divinitus reparetur mens quae de magno malo in deteriora labitur.

  • 1277D

Nunc autem secundum eam lectionem, quae secundo loco ponitur, aliquid loquamur. Sicut nihil facilius erubescimus quam malitiam, sic nihil difficilius cognoscimus vel erubescimus quam arrogantiam. Arrogantia enim oculum mentis obnubilat vel excaecat, ut nec seipsam agnoscere valeat. Qualem enim seipsam mens elata per aestimationem fingit, talem procul dubio veraciter esse se credit. Vide ergo quam difficile sit illa nos in nobis reprehendere, et arguere, et per increpationem et reprobationem conterere, in quibus dudum credidimus nos veraciter debere et aliis placere. Sed quoniam jactantia, dum gloriam suam foras dilatare studet, veris falsa permiscet, et non solum 1278Aea quae de se vera, verum etiam quae novit falsa, aliis de sui aestimatione persuadet. Ibi procul dubio facilius in confusionem dejicitur, ubi de falsa ostentatione, conscientiae etiam proprio testimonio convincitur. Unde utique est, quod jactantiae argentum quam aurum arrogantiae, quo facilius eo et citius atteritur. Sed, sicut multo difficilius arrogantiam quam jactantiam, sic multo laboriosius erubescimus jactantiam quam pertinaciam. Ex jactantia siquidem famam nostram saepe crevisse, semper autem ex pertinacia decrevisse non dubitamus. Nam nihil aliud pertinaciam dicimus, nisi cum in nostra sententia contra aliorum consilia impudenter persistimus. Quis autem illud malum facilius non erubescat, in quo et aliis se multum displicuisse 1278Bminime ignorat? Recte igitur prius aes quam argentum contritum esse describitur, quia facilius, ut dictum est, mens de pertinacia quam de jactantia confunditur. Pertinet autem ad contritionem testae, confusio de propriae infirmitatis et impatientiae consideratione. Cum autem perversa mens ad tantam infirmitatem et impatientiam pervenit, ut passionibus suis, etiam cum voluerit, omnino resistere non possit, quis non videat quam facile in hac fragilitatis suae consideratione erubescat? Facilius autem, ut superius dictum est, illa excusamus quae per infirmitatem contrahimus, quam quae ex sola malitia committimus, unde et illa citius erubescimus. Recte ergo, ut legitur, prius comminuitur ferrum quam 1278Ctesta, sicut et post ista, aes, argentum et aurum recte describuntur comminuta. Hujusmodi ergo contritionis ordo describitur, cum dicitur: Tunc contrita sunt pariter ferrum, testa, aes, argentum, et aurum. Ubi et adducitur, et redacta quasi in favillam aestivae areae, quae rapta sunt vento; nullusque locus inventus est in eis.

CHAPTER XXVIII. By what steps of advancement the mind that slips from great evil into worse things is often divinely repaired.

  • 1277D

But now let us say something according to that reading which is set in the second place. As there is nothing at which we blush more easily than malice, so there is nothing which we acknowledge or blush at more hardly than arrogance. For arrogance clouds over or blinds the eye of the mind, so that it cannot even recognize itself. For such as the mind lifted up feigns itself to be by its own estimate, such, beyond doubt, it believes itself truly to be. See then how difficult it is for us to reprehend in ourselves, and to arraign, and by rebuke and reprobation to crush, those things in which we have long believed that we truly ought to please others also. But since boastfulness, while it is zealous to spread its own glory abroad, mixes false things with true, and persuades others, concerning the estimate of itself, not only 1278Aof those things which it knows to be true of itself, but even of those which it knows to be false. There beyond doubt it is more easily cast down into confusion, where concerning its false ostentation it is convicted even by the proper testimony of its own conscience. Whence assuredly it comes that the silver of boastfulness, rather than the gold of arrogance, is worn away the more quickly by how much the more easily. But as we blush at arrogance much more hardly than at boastfulness, so we blush much more laboriously at boastfulness than at obstinacy. For we do not doubt that our reputation has often grown from boastfulness, but has always decreased from obstinacy. For we call nothing else obstinacy, save when we shamelessly persist in our own opinion against the counsels of others. But who would not blush more easily at that evil in which he is by no means ignorant that he has greatly displeased others as well? 1278BRightly, therefore, is the bronze described as crushed before the silver, because, as has been said, the mind is more easily confounded over obstinacy than over boastfulness. But to the crushing of the clay belongs the confusion that comes of the consideration of one's own weakness and impatience. But when the perverse mind has come to such weakness and impatience that it cannot at all resist its own passions, even when it has willed to, who would not see how easily it blushes in this consideration of its own fragility? But more easily, as has been said above, do we excuse those things which we contract through weakness than those which we commit out of malice alone, whence also we blush at them the more quickly. Rightly then, as it is read, the iron is broken small before the 1278Cclay, just as after these the bronze, the silver, and the gold are rightly described as broken small. Such an order of crushing, then, is described when it is said: Then were broken in pieces together the iron, the clay, the bronze, the silver, and the gold. Where also it is brought in: and they were reduced as it were into the ashes of a summer threshing-floor, which were carried away by the wind; and no place was found in them. [Vulg. nullusque locus inventus est eis, "no place was found for them"; chunk 0016 of this work prints the verse without in]

CAPUT XXIX. Quomodo vel ad quantam perfectionem saepe homo post multiplicem lapsum ex divina operatione resurgat.

Quid per aestivum tempus nisi fervorem spiritus, et quid per aream nisi prudentiam spiritus intelligimus? Quid itaque aestiva area nisi spiritalis intelligentia? In hac area veritatis grana a figurarum 1278Dpaleis vehementis discussionis trituratione excutiuntur. In hac area virtutis annona a vitiorum aristis districte examinationis ventilatione separatur. In hujus areae tritura laboramus, et quasi grana a paleis separamus, quoties vera et aeterna bona a falsis et transitoriis secernimus. Nec nobis sufficiat paleas excutere, sed studeamus etiam earum cumulum igne supposito in favillam vertere, et transeuntium nitorem in nigredinem mutare, et affectum nostrum in eorum contemptum vel odium transformare. In hac area desudat quisquis spiritalis esse desiderat. Spiritalis enim judicat omnia, et ipse a nemine judicatur. Audi quemdam spiritalem in hac area laborantem: Proposui, inquit, in corde meo quaerere, et investigare sapienter de omnibus 1279Aquae fiunt sub sole (Eccle. I.) Ecce area, ecce tritura, et ubi est palea, ubi est arista? Vanitas, inquit, vanitatum, et omnia vanitas. Ecce palea, et vidi quod in omnibus, inquit, esset vanitas, et afflictio spiritus (ibid.). Ecce palea cum arista. Sed ubi est favilla? Quem ergo, ait Apostolus, fructum habuistis in quibus nunc erubescitis? (Rom. VI.) Quid est nigredo favillae, nisi confusio verecundiae? Vide ergo quomodo stipularum nitorem in abominationis nigredinem verterant qui jam erubescebant in quibus aliquando delectari consueverant. Audi et illum qui vanitatum paleas non solum in nigredinem, sed etiam in fetorem verterat, cum dicebat: Omnia arbitratus sum ut stercora, ut Christum lucrifacerem (Philip. III). Dolor autem poenitentiae est 1279Bignis ille quo solent spiritales viri vanitatum aristas comburere, et in abominationum favillas vertere. Has utique favillas in conscientiae nostrae adhuc area toleramus, quandiu de praeteritis culpis confusi vel suspecti sumus; sed, flante vento, favillae, tolluntur, cum ad Spiritus sancti inspirationem reatus nostri formido omnis atque suscipio absorbetur, et per spem veniae ab omnis confusionis nigredine conscientiae nostrae area mundatur, ita ut veraciter Domino psallere possimus: projecisti post tergum omnia peccata mea. Nonne in favillam, non dicam membra caetera ferrea vel aerea, sed et caput ejus aureum redactum erat, cum Propheta dicebat: Et confusio faciei meae cooperuit me (Psal. XLIII). Nonne pro favillarum eventilatione 1279CDomino supplicabit, cum Domino in oratione proclamaret: In te speravi, non confundar in aeternum (Psal. XXX). Nonne nigrae illae confusionum suarum favillae vento raptae atque sublatae fuerant, cum per veniae praesumptionem culparum suarum maculis emundatus in laudis confessionem erumperet, dicens: Benedic, anima mea, Domino, et noli oblivisci omnes retributiones ejus, qui propitiatur omnibus iniquitatibus tuis (Psal. CII). Vide ergo quia corruptionis opibus in favillam redigitur, et postmodum vento raptatur et tollitur; quia, nisi praevaricatrix anima prius confusiones suas cognoscat, et erubescat, et digno poenitentiae dolore exurat, et absumat, conscientiae suae aream a culparum suarum maculis non 1279Demundat, et ad plenam veniae securitatem nunquam se sublevat. Raptis autem vento nullus locus eis invenitur; quia post diu quaesitam, vixque impetratam veniam contra similes tentationes mens non solum cautior sed et robustior efficitur, imo contra similium corruptionum detrimenta insuperabilis redditur. Nullus sane locus eis potest inveniri, cum ad similem corruptelam nec sensus decipi, nec affectus potest corrumpi. Sed quid illud sibi vult quod lapis ille in quo statua percutitur, percussaque comminuitur; quid, inquam, est quod lapis non crescit in rupem sed mutatur in montem, nec lapis in lapidem grandem sed potius excrescit in montem magnum et eminentem? Sed scimus quia perfecta charitas mittit timorem. Nescit enim perfectio charitatis 1280Aaugere formidinem, sed mutat et magnificat in confidentiae montem. Qui confidunt in Domino sicut mons Sion, non commovebitur in aeternum qui habitat in Hierusalem. Videsne, quaeso, quomodo concurrunt sibi propheticae sententiae, quomodo concordet David cum Daniele, veritas cum figura, Prophetae sententia cum visione prophetica? Lapis autem, ait, qui percussit statuam factus est mons magnus, et implevit universam terram. Quam magnus, quaeso, mons, qui implevit universam terram, qui non commovebitur in aeternum? Vultis audire quam magnus, quam firmus, quam grandis, quam immobilis? Audi alium hunc confidentiae montem tenentem, et de ejus magnitudine, seu immobilitate proponentem: Certus sum, inquit, quia neque mors, 1280Betc., usque, quae est in Christo Jesu Domino nostro (Rom. VIII).

CHAPTER XXIX. How, and to how great a perfection, a man often rises again after a manifold lapse by divine working.

What do we understand by the summer season but the fervor of the spirit, and what by the threshing-floor but the prudence of the spirit? What, then, is a summer threshing-floor but spiritual understanding? On this floor the grains of truth are beaten out from the 1278Dchaff of figures by the threshing of a vehement examination. On this floor the corn of virtue is separated from the beards of the vices by the winnowing of a strict examination. In the threshing of this floor we labor, and, as it were, separate the grains from the chaff, as often as we sunder true and eternal goods from false and transitory ones. Nor let it suffice us to beat out the chaff, but let us be zealous also to turn the heap of it into ash by setting fire beneath it, and to change the brightness of passing things into blackness, and to transform our affection into contempt or hatred of them. On this floor sweats whoever desires to be spiritual. For the spiritual man judges all things, and he himself is judged by no man. Hear a certain spiritual man laboring on this floor: I purposed, he says, in my heart to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things 1279Athat are done under the sun (Eccle. I.) [Vulg. in animo meo, "in my mind"] Behold the floor, behold the threshing; and where is the chaff, where is the beard? Vanity, he says, of vanities, and all is vanity. Behold the chaff, and I saw that in all things, he says, there was vanity, and affliction of spirit (ibid.). Behold the chaff with the beard. But where is the ash? What fruit therefore, says the Apostle, had you in those things whereof you are now ashamed? (Rom. VI.) What is the blackness of the ash but the confusion of shame? See then how they had turned the brightness of the stubble into the blackness of abomination, who were now blushing at the things in which they had once been wont to take delight. Hear him too who had turned the chaff of vanities not only into blackness but even into stench, when he said: I have counted all things as dung, that I might gain Christ (Philip. III) [Vulg. arbitror … ut Christum lucrifaciam, present, "I count … that I may gain"]. But the sorrow of penitence is 1279Bthat fire with which spiritual men are wont to burn up the beards of vanities, and to turn them into the ashes of abominations. These ashes assuredly we endure still on the threshing-floor of our conscience, so long as we are confounded or suspect concerning past faults; but, when the wind blows, the ashes are taken away, when at the inspiration of the Holy Spirit all dread of our guilt and suscipio is swallowed up, and through the hope of pardon the floor of our conscience is cleansed of all the blackness of confusion, so that we may truly sing to the Lord: thou hast cast behind thy back all my sins. Had it not been reduced to ash, I will not say the other members, the iron or the bronze, but even his golden head, when the Prophet said: And the confusion of my face hath covered me (Psal. XLIII). Will he not make supplication to the Lord 1279Cfor the winnowing away of the ashes, when he cried out to the Lord in prayer: In thee have I hoped, let me never be confounded (Psal. XXX). Had not those black ashes of his confusions been carried away and taken up by the wind, when, cleansed from the stains of his faults through the presuming upon pardon, he broke forth into the confession of praise, saying: Bless the Lord, O my soul, and forget not all his benefits, who forgiveth all thy iniquities (Psal. CII). See then that by the riches of corruption it is reduced to ash, and afterward is carried off and taken away by the wind; because, unless the transgressing soul first acknowledge its confusions, and blush at them, and burn them up and consume them with a worthy sorrow of penitence, it does not 1279Dcleanse the floor of its conscience from the stains of its faults, and never raises itself to the full security of pardon. But when they have been carried off by the wind, no place is found for them; because after a pardon long sought and scarcely obtained the mind is made not only more cautious but also stronger against like temptations, nay is rendered insuperable against the declines of like corruptions. No place indeed can be found for them, when neither can the sense be deceived nor the affection corrupted unto a like corruption. But what does that mean, that that stone by which the statue is struck, and being struck is broken small; what, I say, is it that the stone does not grow into a crag but is changed into a mountain, nor the stone into a great stone but rather grows out into a great and eminent mountain? But we know that perfect charity sends fear. [Vulg. I Joan. 4:18 perfecta caritas foras mittit timorem, "casteth fear out"] For the perfection of charity knows not 1280Ahow to increase dread, but changes and magnifies it into a mountain of confidence. They that trust in the Lord, as mount Sion: he shall not be moved for ever that dwelleth in Jerusalem. Dost thou see, I pray, how the prophetic sentences run together with one another, how David agrees with Daniel, truth with figure, the sentence of the Prophet with the prophetic vision? But the stone, he says, that struck the statue became a great mountain, and filled the whole earth. How great a mountain, I pray, is that which filled the whole earth, which shall not be moved for ever? Will you hear how great, how firm, how vast, how immovable? Hear another who holds this mountain of confidence, and sets forth its greatness and immovability: I am sure, he says, that neither death, 1280Betc., as far as, which is in Christ Jesus our Lord (Rom. VIII).

CAPUT XXX. Qualem libertatem naturaliter habemus, et quatem potestatem ex gratia accipimus, et quid sit libertatis vel potestatis.

Ecce somnium audivimus. Sed nunquid adhuc interpretatione indigemus? Sicut superius jam dixit, aliud est divinorum judiciorum arcana, et divina revelatione cognoscere, et aliud est eorumdem rationem perpendere, vel penetrare potuisse. Aliud est, inquam, perspicua contemplatione videre quid in rerum universitate soleat vel oporteat accidere, atque aliud est quid cui personae, quali de causa, quave illius cui accidit utilitate debeat 1280Ccontingere. Unum quasi ad somnii visionem, aliud pertinet ad somnii interpretationem, et quid regi Nabuchodonosor datum est ex divina revelatione per similitudinem videre, ex quanto sublimitatis fastigio contingat hominem in ima corruere, et iterum ad integritatis statum, perfectionisque culmen resurgere, sed non aequa accepit qualitatem personae agnoscere cui debeat tale aliquid accidere. Sed quod per semetipsum videre non potuit, ab alio accepit, cum per Danielem audit: Tu es caput aureum, et post te consurget aliud regnum minus te. Alia itaque est gratia per quam cognoscimus judicia divina, atque alia est illa per quam dijudicamus merita propria vel aliena. Illud est contemplationis, 1280Distud est meditationis, seu potius devotionis. Nam illa sane meditatio devotioni recte ascribitur, per quam vitae nostrae modus meritorumque qualitas dijudicatur. Et quidem infirmitatis nostrae corruptelam nunquam melius cognoscimus, nusquam plenius deprehendimus, quam cum inter compunctionis lacrymas totum cor nostrum Domino effundimus. Si ergo per Danielem devotio recte intelligitur, merito illi gratia illa ascribitur per quam vitae nostrae status, vel corruptela deprehenditur. Contingit autem saepe unum in una illarum quas dixi, et alium in alia gratia perfectum esse, et quod quisque per semetipsum non potest, ab alterutro discere. Quandoque vero unus idemque homo utriusque gratiae plenitudinem accipiet, et tamen in uno eodemque 1281Aanimo quasi unus alium consulit, quando illud quod per unum mentis statum non potuit, per alium addiscit. Nam multa quae ex contemplationis gratia non possumus, merito devotionis obtinemus. Hoc est quod Daniel a rege consulitur, hoc est quod rex per Danielem eruditur: Hoc est, inquit, somnium interpretationemque ejus dicemus coram te, rex. Felix cui eo adhuc tempore visio haec ostenditur, ejusque interpretatio revelatur. Dum adhuc rex est, passionibus suis dominari potest: Tu, inquit, Rex regum es. Scimus quia motui corporis praesidet corporeus sensus, sensui appetitus, appetitui affectus, et his omnibus rationalis consensus, secundum quod scriptum est, quia excelso alius excelsior est, et super hos quoque eminentiores sunt 1281Balii, insuper et universae terrae rex imperat servienti. Rex itaque regum est rationalis consensus, qui omnibus cordis corporisque motibus praesidet, et intra ditionis suae jura (accepta tamen divinitus potestate) coercet. Audi enim quod dicitur, et intellige a quo potestas haec datur: Tu, inquit, rex regum es, et Dominus coeli, regnum et fortitudinem, et imperium, et gloriam dedit tibi. Liberum arbitrium hominis in eo regnum accepit et habet quod aliis omnibus quae in homine sunt praesidere debet; fortitudinem accepit, et habet quandiu passionibus suis fortiter resistere valet; imperium accepit, et habet quandiu actiones et voluntates, cogitationesque in bono exercet, vel exercere valet. Gloriam accepit, et habet quandiu adhuc de bona conscientia gaudet: Gloria, inquit, 1281Cnostra haec est, testimonium conscientiae nostrae (II Cor. I). Scimus quis dicat: Sine me nihil potestis facere (Joan. XV). Tantum ergo possumus, quantum posse accepimus. Quid enim habes quod non accepisti, nihil enim aliud est habere hujusmodi potestatem et fortitudinem, quam habere gratiam cooperantem. Potestas tua, cooperatrix gratia. Quantum habes gratiae, tantum habes et potentiae; et quantum gratia accepta crescit, tantum et potentia tua proficit; et quantum gratia minuitur, tantum et potentiae tuae detrahitur; et si quando subtrahitur gratia, simul perit et potentia. Patet itaque ex his quia aliud est arbitrii potestas, atque aliud est arbitrii libertas. Aliud est enim magna posse, atque aliud est liberum 1281Desse. Et liber, et infirmus esse potes, sicut econtrario et sanus, et servus esse potes. Si sanus et robustus es, et multa, et magna facere potes; verumtamen si liber es, ad nihil cogi potes, vel debes. Et si amittis sanitatem, amittis priorem potestatem, non tamen et libertatem. Potestatis itaque est quod bonum facere possumus quandiu id facere valemus. Libertatis autem quod ad nullum seu bonum, seu malum inviti cogi possumus. Quam multi in definitione liberi arbitrii errant, quia inter potestatem et libertatem distinguere ignorant vel dissimulant. Vide tamen quid intersit cum potestatem homo saepe amittat, libertatem vero nunquam amittere possit.
