Item nemo ad omnia bona accipit potestatem, sed omnis homo ad omnia bona vel mala accipit libertatem. 1282ANam, uti jam dictum est, ad nullum bonum, vel malum invitus cogi potest. Sed, cum sit liber omnium, non tamen omnium potestatem habet, alioqui omnipotens esset. Multum est autem per omnem modum cum ad illa accipit potestatem, quae sibi sufficiunt ad salutem. Quid autem cum et illa suppetunt quae sufficiunt ad perfectionem. Quemadmodum hic dictum est, et omnia in quibus habitant filii hominum, et bestias agri, volucresque coeli dedit in manu tua, et sub ditione tua universa constituit? Omnia in quibus habitant filii hominum quasi in manu sua tenet, cum ad illa gratiam cooperantem habet quae ex ejus adjutorio humanis consiliis et studiis subjacere solent. Bestias agri inter potestatis suae jura possidet, cum saecularium desideriorum 1282Bimpetus intra aequitatis terminos fortiter coercere valet. Volucribus coeli potenter imperat, cui cogitationes suas quoties voluerit, bonis meditationibus spiritualibusque theoriis occupat. In hunc itaque modum sub ditione sua universa constitui meruit, qui ad singula horum quae diximus, gratiam divinitus accepit.

CHAPTER XXX. What kind of liberty we have by nature, and quatem power we receive from grace, and what belongs to liberty or to power.

Behold, we have heard the dream. But do we still need an interpretation? As he has already said above, it is one thing to know the secrets of the divine judgments, and to know them by divine revelation, and another to have been able to weigh or to penetrate the reason of the same. It is one thing, I say, to see by clear contemplation what is wont or ought to happen in the universe of things, and another what ought to 1280Cbefall to which person, for what cause, and with what profit to him to whom it happens. The one belongs, as it were, to the vision of the dream, the other to the interpretation of the dream; and what was given to king Nabuchodonosor out of divine revelation was to see by a likeness from how great a height of loftiness it may befall a man to fall down into the lowest places, and again to rise up to the state of integrity and to the summit of perfection; but he did not receive equal things, to recognize the quality of the person to whom such a thing ought to happen. But what he could not see through himself he received from another, when he hears through Daniel: Thou art the head of gold, and after thee shall rise up another kingdom less than thee. One grace, therefore, is that by which we know the divine judgments, and another that by which we judge our own merits or those of others. The former belongs to contemplation, 1280Dthe latter to meditation, or rather to devotion. For that meditation is indeed rightly ascribed to devotion by which the measure of our life and the quality of our merits are judged. And indeed we never better know the corruption of our weakness, nowhere more fully detect it, than when amid the tears of compunction we pour out our whole heart to the Lord. If, then, devotion is rightly understood by Daniel, deservedly is that grace ascribed to him by which the state or the corruption of our life is detected. But it often happens that one man is perfect in one of those graces which I have named, and another in the other, and that what each cannot do through himself he learns from the other. But sometimes one and the same man will receive the fullness of both graces, and yet in one and the same 1281Aspirit the one, as it were, consults the other, when what he could not attain through one state of mind he learns through another. For many things which we cannot do out of the grace of contemplation we obtain by the merit of devotion. This is why Daniel is consulted by the king, this is why the king is instructed through Daniel: This is, he says, the dream; and its interpretation also we will tell before thee, O king. Happy is he to whom, while there is yet time, this vision is shown and its interpretation revealed. While he is still a king, he can have dominion over his own passions: Thou, he says, art the King of kings. We know that bodily sense presides over the motion of the body, appetite over sense, affection over appetite, and rational consent over all these, according to what is written, that another is higher than the high, and over these there are yet 1281Bothers more eminent, and moreover the king rules over all the earth that serves him. The king of kings, therefore, is rational consent, which presides over all the motions of the heart and of the body, and restrains them within the rights of its own dominion (the power having nonetheless been received from God). For hear what is said, and understand by whom this power is given: Thou, he says, art king of kings, and the Lord of heaven hath given thee a kingdom, and strength, and empire, and glory. [Vulg. Dan. 2:37 Deus caeli, "the God of heaven"; chunk 0025 of this work prints the verse with Deus] The free will of man has received and has a kingdom in this, that it ought to preside over all other things that are in man; it has received strength, and has it so long as it is able bravely to resist its own passions; it has received empire, and has it so long as it exercises, or is able to exercise, its actions and wills and thoughts in good. It has received glory, and has it so long as it still rejoices in a good conscience: Our glory, he says, 1281Cis this, the testimony of our conscience (II Cor. I). We know who it is that says: Without me you can do nothing (Joan. XV). So much, then, can we do as we have received the power to do. For what hast thou that thou hast not received, for to have such power and strength is nothing else than to have cooperating grace. Thy power, cooperating grace. As much grace as thou hast, so much power also hast thou; and as much as the grace received grows, so much also does thy power advance; and as much as grace is diminished, so much also is taken from thy power; and if ever grace is withdrawn, power perishes together with it. It is plain, then, from these things that the power of the will is one thing, and the liberty of the will another. For it is one thing to be able to do great things, and another to be 1281Dfree. Thou canst be both free and weak, just as, on the contrary, thou canst be both sound and a slave. If thou art sound and strong, thou canst do both many and great things; but nevertheless, if thou art free, thou canst not, nor oughtest to, be compelled to anything. And if thou losest soundness, thou losest thy former power, yet not thy liberty as well. It belongs, therefore, to power that we can do good so long as we are able to do it. But to liberty, that we cannot be compelled unwilling to anything, whether good or evil. How many err in the definition of free will, because they know not, or dissemble, how to distinguish between power and liberty. See nevertheless what difference there is, since a man often loses power, but can never lose liberty. Likewise no one receives power unto all goods, but every man receives liberty unto all goods or evils. 1282AFor, as has already been said, he can be compelled unwilling to no good or evil. But although he is free for all things, he does not for all that have power over all things, otherwise he would be omnipotent. But it is much in every way when he receives power unto those things which suffice him for salvation. And what when those things also are at hand which suffice for perfection. As it is said here: and all things in which the children of men dwell, and the beasts of the field, and the fowls of the air he hath given into thy hand, and hath set all things under thy dominion? He holds, as it were, in his own hand all things in which the children of men dwell, when he has cooperating grace unto those things which, by his help, are wont to lie subject to human counsels and zeals. He possesses the beasts of the field among the rights of his own power, when he is able bravely to restrain the onsets of worldly desires 1282Bwithin the bounds of equity. He powerfully commands the fowls of the air, to whom he occupies his own thoughts, as often as he wills, with good meditations and spiritual theoriae. In this manner, then, he has deserved that all things be set under his dominion who has received grace from God unto each of these things which we have named.

CAPUT XXXI. De diversis corruptionis gradibus, eorumque proprietatibus descendentium de bono in malum.

Sed quaeramus quid illud sibi vult quod ad ista subjungitur: Tu es ergo, inquit, caput aureum, et post te consurget regnum aliud minus te (Dan. II). Attende, quaeso, quid dicat, quid sua nos interpretatione docere proponat: Tu es, inquit, caput aureum. 1282CUt enim de caeteris taceam, hoc vel solum puto in quo interprete opus habemus, et utinam interpretantem intelligere, et hoc vel per interpretem nosse possimus. Nonne statua una unius hominis personam repraesentat, et illa, ut exposuimus, cujuslibet de summis in ima ruentis praevaricationem designat? Si unus est qui ejusmodi est, habens caput aureum, pectus argenteum, caetera autem membra aerea vel ferrea, cur, quaeso, si ipse cui hoc dicitur, est caput aureum, cur non et ipse sit pectus argenteum, et caetera in hunc modum? An forte in ea parte qua aureus es, ipse es, et ipse non es, in ea parte qua aureus non es? Quisquis ergo es, cum post caput aureum jam coeperis pectus argenteum habere, vide, 1282Dquaeso, et diligenter attende ne forte ipse, jam non sis ipse. Si post multam charitatem, magnamque animi devotionem desieris esse devotus, destiterisque esse aureus, nunquid ipse eris qui prius? Homo, inquit, cum in honore esset non intellexit, comparatus est jumentis insipientibus, et similis factus est illis (Psal. XLVIII). Simile esse Deo, mira ile decus; similem esse pecoribus, miserabile dedecus. Ergo alius, et alius similis Deo et similis pecoribus. Homo bonus factus est, et ad bonitatem creatus est: Solummodo hoc inveni, inquit Ecclesiastes, quia fecerit Deus hominem rectum, et ipse immiscuerit se infinitis quaestionibus (Eccli. VII). Si ergo charitate ferves, et in boni dilectione permanes, aureum caput habes, et tu ipse es, quia id quod, et ad quod factus es, et 1283Aesse debes. Sed, charitate in te refrigescente, cum jam coeperis a pristino fervore deficere, nunquid adhuc eris ipse, et non potius alius minor te? Tu es, inquit, caput aureum, et post te consurget regnum aliud minus te. Nunquid non es jam minor te ipso, cum coeperis facere ex solo consilio, quod prius actitabas ex magno consilio, et multo desiderio? Et post te, inquit, consurget regnum aliud minus te. Nonne aliud regnum est cum mens regitur solo consilio, quam cum agitur etiam boni desiderio? Nonne est minus regnum sine desiderio solum consilium, quam cum prudenti consilio fervens desiderium? Nonne minus est bonum facere ex sola deliberatione, quam etiam ex magna dilectione seu delectatione. Recte ergo dicitur: Et post te consurget regnum 1283Baliud minus te. Et postea de tertio regno subinfertur, cum dicitur: Et regnum tertium aliud aereum quod imperabit universae terrae. Sicut ad aurum pertinet quod fit ex dilectione, et sicut ad argentum quod fit ex sola deliberatione, sic ad aes pertinet bonum quod fit ex simulatione. In primo regnat charitas, in secundo veritas, in tertio regnat simultas. Vide ergo, quaeso, quam recte videtur huic tertio regno convenire quod imperare describitur universae terrae. Neque enim habet hujus regni imperator sortem in regno coelorum qui in bono opere exercet corpus suum, sed negligit in bona voluntate nutrire cor suum: Regnum coelorum, inquit, intra vos est (Luc. XVII). Intus sane, intus in corde regnat charitas, intus regnat veritas, nec his 1283Ctamen finibus contentae per bonum opus erumpunt foras. Vide ergo ne forte duo illa praedicta regna pertineant ad regnum coelorum, si omnino regnum coelorum intra nos est, eo quod utrumque illorum intra nos inveniri potest. Sed ille tertii regni rex et imperator, non habens partem in regno coelorum, de summo depulsus et foras ejectus, quia in coelo non potest, regnum sibi vindicat in terra. Projecit enim intima sua in vita sua, et non potest dicere, nostra conversatio in coelis est. Quanto magis ergo ejus dominatio in coelis esse non potest? Imperat tamen universae terrae exercens carnem in bono opere, mentem relinquens vacuam bona intentione. Et quartum regnum, inquit, erit velut ferrum. Quomodo 1283Dferrum comminuit, et domat omnia, sic comminuet haec omnia et conteret. Quid est quod dicit haec omnia, nisi quod hoc regnum conterit caetera regna praedicta. Quomodo caetera metalla comminuere solet ferrum, ea videlicet, aurum et argentum? Sicut in auro charitas, in argento veritas, in aere simultas, sic, ut superius dictum est, in ferro signatur crudelitas. Quis autem nesciat quomodo rigor crudelitatis dissipare, et destruere soleat sanctum desiderium, quod pertinet ad aurum; verum consilium, quod pertinet ad argentum; pium sed simulatum obsequium, quod pertinet ad opus aereum? Bene ergo dictum est, et regnum quartum erit velut ferrum.

CHAPTER XXXI. On the various steps of corruption, and the properties of those who descend from good into evil.

But let us ask what this means, that to these things there is subjoined: Thou art therefore, he says, the head of gold, and after thee shall rise up another kingdom less than thee (Dan. II). Give heed, I pray, to what he says, to what he sets out to teach us by his interpretation: Thou art, he says, the head of gold. 1282CFor, to say nothing of the rest, this alone, I think, is the thing in which we have need of an interpreter; and would that we might understand the one interpreting, and know this at least through an interpreter. Does not one statue represent the person of one man, and does it not, as we have expounded, signify the transgression of anyone falling from the highest things into the lowest? If he who is of this sort is one, having a golden head, a silver breast, but the other members of bronze or of iron, why, I pray, if he to whom this is said is himself the golden head, why is he not himself also the silver breast, and the rest in this manner? Or art thou perhaps thyself in that part in which thou art golden, and not thyself in that part in which thou art not golden? Whosoever therefore thou art, when after the golden head thou hast now begun to have a silver breast, see, 1282DI pray, and diligently give heed, lest perhaps thou thyself be now not thyself. If, after much charity and a great devotion of spirit, thou hast ceased to be devout and left off being golden, wilt thou be the same as thou wast before? Man, he says, when he was in honor did not understand; he hath been compared to senseless beasts, and is become like to them (Psal. XLVIII). To be like to God, mira ile glory; to be like to cattle, a miserable disgrace. Therefore it is one man and another, he that is like to God and he that is like to cattle. Man was made good, and was created unto goodness: This only I have found, says Ecclesiastes, that God made man upright, and that he himself hath entangled himself with infinite questions (Eccli. VII). If, then, thou glowest with charity and abidest in the love of the good, thou hast a golden head, and thou art thyself, because thou art that which, and unto which, thou wast made and 1283Aoughtest to be. But when charity grows cold in thee, when thou hast now begun to fail from thy former fervor, wilt thou still be thyself, and not rather another less than thee? Thou art, he says, the head of gold, and after thee shall rise up another kingdom less than thee. Art thou not now less than thyself, when thou hast begun to do out of counsel alone what before thou wast doing out of great counsel and much desire? And after thee, he says, shall rise up another kingdom less than thee. Is it not another kingdom when the mind is ruled by counsel alone, than when it is driven also by the desire of the good? Is not counsel alone, without desire, a lesser kingdom than a fervent desire with prudent counsel? Is it not less to do good out of deliberation alone than also out of great love or delight. Rightly, then, is it said: And after thee shall rise up another kingdom 1283Bless than thee. And afterward concerning the third kingdom it is inferred, when it is said: And another third kingdom of bronze, which shall rule over the whole earth. As what is done out of love belongs to the gold, and as what is done out of deliberation alone belongs to the silver, so the good that is done out of simulation belongs to the bronze. In the first charity reigns, in the second truth, in the third reigns pretense. See then, I pray, how rightly it seems to fit this third kingdom that it is described as ruling over the whole earth. For the emperor of this kingdom has no lot in the kingdom of heaven, who exercises his body in good work but neglects to nourish his heart in good will: The kingdom of heaven, he says, is within you (Luc. XVII) [Vulg. Luc. 17:21 regnum Dei, "the kingdom of God"]. Within indeed, within in the heart charity reigns, within truth reigns; yet not content with these 1283Cbounds they break forth outward through good work. See then whether perhaps those two aforesaid kingdoms belong to the kingdom of heaven, if the kingdom of heaven is altogether within us, in that either of them can be found within us. But that king and emperor of the third kingdom, having no part in the kingdom of heaven, driven down from the height and cast forth outside, because he cannot in heaven, claims a kingdom for himself on earth. For he hath cast forth his inmost parts in his own life, and cannot say, our conversation is in heaven. How much more, then, can his dominion not be in heaven? Yet he rules over the whole earth, exercising the flesh in good work, leaving the mind empty of good intention. And the fourth kingdom, he says, shall be as iron. As 1283Diron breaketh in pieces and tameth all things, so shall it break in pieces and crush all these things. What is it that he says, all these things, unless that this kingdom crushes the other aforesaid kingdoms. How is iron wont to break in pieces the other metals, namely those, the gold and the silver? As in the gold charity, in the silver truth, in the bronze pretense, so, as has been said above, in the iron cruelty is signified. But who could fail to know how the rigor of cruelty is wont to scatter and to destroy holy desire, which belongs to the gold; true counsel, which belongs to the silver; the pious but simulated service, which belongs to the bronze work? Well, therefore, has it been said: and the fourth kingdom shall be as iron.

CAPUT XXXII. De diversis corruptionis gradibus, eorumque proprietatibus, descendentium de malo in malum.

  • 1284A

Sed oportet nos item paululum ad superiora redire, et quomodo haec interpretatio aliae lectioni etiam conveniat ostendere. Secundum autem eam lectionem, primum regnum est arrogantiae, secundum est jactantiae, tertium pertinaciae, quartum saevitiae. Arrogantia siquidem pertinet ad opus aureum, jactantia ad opus argenteum, pertinacia ad opus aereum, saevitia ad ferreum. Videamus quid interpres noster de aureo capite dicat, quidve sentiat, vel quomodo illud ei conveniat: Tu, inquit, rex regum es: et Deus coeli, regnum et fortitudinem et imperium et gloriam dedit tibi, et omnia in quibus habitant 1284Bfilii hominum, et bestias agri volucresque coeli dedit in manu tua, et sub ditione tua universa constituit. Saepe cum talem tantamque gratiam accipimus qualem hic descriptam, vel superius expositam audivimus, fit ut inde contra Deum superbiamus, unde devotiores vel magis obnoxii Deo esse debuimus. Sed illud, quaeso, quam pium quantave admiratione dignum, quod cum tumida mens nostra contra Deum superbit, non statim nos districta animadversione percutit, nec gratiam suam illico subtrahit. Sed prius nos pia admonitione convenit, modo interius, modo exterius; intus per divinam revelationem, exterius per humanam exhortationem. Intus, per internam aspirationem, exterius per humanam rationem, sicut hic prius in occulto per somnium, postea in manifesto 1284Cper verbum. Tu, inquit, rex regum es, quia fortibus fortioribusque fortior, fortissimus dominari potes, quia adhuc gratiam adjutricem habes, poenitentiam ergo age, dum potes, dum gratiam adhuc cooperantem habes. Adhuc rex regum es et principaliter etiam vitiis adhuc principari, passionibusque tuis adhuc dominari potes. Quia gratiam licet deserens, nondum ab illa desertus es. Noli, inquit, superbire, noli altum sapere, cordis tui arrogantiam citius depone, et quod de Dei munere potes, noli tibi ascribere: Deus enim coeli, regnum et fortitudinem, et imperium, et gloriam dedit tibi. Adhuc in cordis vel corporis tui motibus habes regnum, quia bonos disponere; habes fortitudinem, quia malis resistere; 1284Dimperium, quia negligentius urgere; gloriam, quia potes rebelles revincere. Non est tamen unde superbias, non est quod tibi ascribas: Deus enim coeli dedit omnia ista tibi, et omnia in quibus habitant filii hominum, et bestias agri, volucresque coeli dedit in manu tua, et sub ditione tua universa constituit. Quod bestiis agri, quod volucribus coeli praesides, quod appetitibus carnis, quod cogitationibus mentis dominari potes, quod bona operari, quod utilia meditari vales non de tua virtute, sed de divino munere habes. Omnia enim in quibus filii hominum habitant, et quae rationi rectisque consiliis obtemperant, ex ejus munere fiunt, ejus cooperatione in actum prodeunt, bene proveniunt, prospere succedunt. Quidquid ergo utiliter potes, 1285Aex ipso, et per ipsum potes, qui nihil non potest, quia omnia potest. Omnis itaque possibilitas tua ex ejus munere venit, qui sub ditione tua haec omnia constituit. Tu ergo, inquit, caput aureum es, qui, adhuc comitante gratia, et mala corrigere, et bona exercere potes. Preme ergo superbiam, ne perdas et gratiam: Deus enim superbis resistit, humilibus autem dat gratiam. Scimus autem quia initium omnis peccati superbia. Qui autem facit peccatum, servus est peccati. Quid ergo? Nunquidnam jam non erit aliud regnum, cum jam coeperit dominari peccatum, et tu exhibere famulatum, quam tunc erat cum tu poteras peccatum premere; ita ut non regnaret in tuo mortali corpore? Puto ergo quia cum coeperis gratiam per superbiam amittere, et teipsum 1285Bperdere, sine qua nihil poteris facere; puto, inquam, quia jam consurget aliud regnum minus te, secundum quod scriptum est: Tu, inquit, caput aureum es, et post te consurget regnum aliud minus te. Sed quia arrogans quisque gloriatur in seipso, jactantia autem in favore alieno, recte secundum aliquid de aureo capite, hoc est de arrogante, dici potest, quia adhuc ipse est, eo quod seipsum necdum deseruit, qui gloriam suam in seipso constituit. Recedit autem a seipso, cum gloriam ponit in altero. Cogita autem quanto minus sit gloriari in sibilo alieni oris, et laudibus falsis, quam in propriis bonis, et saepe veris, quamvis ab alio acceptis. Recte ergo dicitur: Tu caput aureum 1285Ces, et post te consurget regnum aliud minus te, et additur, et regnum aliud tertium aereum quod imperabit universae terrae. Aes, eo quod valde durabile existat, recte, ut superius diximus, pertinaciam designat. Ventrem itaque, et femora quae pudendis adjacent quasi aerea quis tunc habet, quando his etiam pertinaciter inhaeret quae etiam erubescere deberet. Vide ergo quam apte videatur huic regno congruere quod dicitur imperare universae terrae. Quomodo enim universae terrae non imperat quod usque ad ignominiosa imperanda tyrannidem suam dilatat? Et quartum, inquit, regnum erit velut ferrum. Quomodo ferrum comminuit et domat omnia, sic comminuet omnia haec, et conteret. Si in ferro saevitiam debemus intelligere, quomodo, 1285Dquaeso, hujusmodi regnum regna caetera queat comminuere atque conterere? Absque dubio quantumcunque mens impudens sit, gloriam tamen quam in seipsa per arrogantiam, vel in altero per jactantiam habere consuevit omnino (nec mirum) perturbat atque confundit. Quis enim inter tot saevitiae suae tam apertas insanias vel sanctum se credat, vel ab alio hoc posse credi confidat? Regnum autem pertinaciae adeo solent violenta illa saevitiae jura conterere atque confundere, ut in nullo proposito (quamvis recto, quamvis honesto) furoris sui tempore mens possit perseverare. Sed quidquid tranquillitatis tempore mens de pace seu patientia, etiam cum voto vel sacramento deliberat atque disponit, perturbationis suae tempore furor superveniens 1286Adissipat atque dissolvit. Ea quae de visionis hujus expositione hucusque jam diximus, secundum duplicem considerationem, juxta intelligentiae nostrae modum pertractavimus. Caetera autem quae sequuntur utramque lectionem in eamdem sententiam concurrunt, geminam expositionem (ut ea quae praemissa sunt) non requirunt. Quid enim est saevitia nisi crudelitas? Sic et impatientia, quid aliud quam miseranda fragilitas? Sic sub diversis nominibus idem intelligere debemus.

CHAPTER XXXII. On the diverse steps of corruption, and their properties, in those who descend from evil to evil.

  • 1284A

But we must again return a little to what has gone before, and show how this interpretation also fits the other reading. Now according to that reading, the first kingdom is that of arrogance, the second that of boasting, the third of obstinacy, the fourth of savagery. For arrogance belongs to the golden work, boasting to the silver work, obstinacy to the bronze work, savagery to the iron. Let us see what our interpreter says of the head of gold, or what he understands, or how it fits him: Thou, he says, art king of kings: and the God of heaven hath given thee a kingdom, and strength, and empire, and glory, and all things in which 1284Bthe children of men dwell, and the beasts of the field and the birds of heaven he hath given into thy hand, and hath set all things under thy dominion. Often, when we receive such and so great a grace as we have heard described here or expounded above, it comes about that we grow proud against God from that very thing for which we ought to have been more devout and more beholden to God. But that, I ask, how merciful and how worthy of wonder it is, that when our swollen mind grows proud against God he does not at once strike us with strict punishment, nor withdraw his grace on the spot. But first he approaches us with a devout admonition, now inwardly, now outwardly; within, by divine revelation, without, by human exhortation. Within, by inward inspiration; without, by human reasoning, as here first in secret by a dream, afterwards openly 1284Cby a word. Thou, he says, art king of kings, because, stronger than the strong and than the stronger, thou art strongest and canst rule, because thou still hast grace as thy helper; do penance therefore while thou canst, while thou still hast grace working with thee. Thou art still king of kings, and thou canst still in princely wise be prince over the vices too, and still lord it over thy passions. For although thou art deserting grace, thou art not yet deserted by it. Be not proud, he says, be not high-minded, lay aside the sooner the arrogance of thy heart, and what thou canst do of God's gift, ascribe not to thyself: for the God of heaven hath given thee a kingdom, and strength, and empire, and glory. Still in the motions of thy heart or of thy body thou hast a kingdom, because thou canst dispose the good; thou hast strength, because thou canst resist the evil; 1284Dempire, because thou canst urge the more remissly; glory, because thou canst overcome the rebellious. Yet there is nothing whence thou shouldst be proud, there is nothing thou shouldst ascribe to thyself: for the God of heaven hath given all these things to thee, and all things in which the children of men dwell, and the beasts of the field and the birds of heaven he hath given into thy hand, and hath set all things under thy dominion. That thou presidest over the beasts of the field, that thou presidest over the birds of heaven, that thou canst lord it over the appetites of the flesh and over the thoughts of the mind, that thou art able to work good things and to meditate on useful ones: this thou hast not of thy own virtue, but of the divine gift. For all things in which the children of men dwell, and which obey reason and right counsels, come to be of his gift; by his co-working they come forth into act, they turn out well, they prosper. Whatever therefore thou canst do usefully, 1285Athou canst do of him and through him, who is incapable of nothing, because he can do all things. And so all thy possibility comes of his gift, who hath set all these things under thy dominion. Thou therefore, he says, art the head of gold, who, grace still accompanying thee, canst both correct evils and practise good things. Press down thy pride, therefore, lest thou lose grace as well: for God resisteth the proud, but giveth grace to the humble. And we know that pride is the beginning of all sin. And he that committeth sin is the servant of sin. What then? Will there not now be another kingdom, when sin has already begun to hold dominion and thou to render service, than there was then, when thou couldst press sin down, so that it did not reign in thy mortal body? I think therefore that when thou hast begun through pride to lose grace, 1285Bwithout which thou wilt be able to do nothing, and to lose thyself; I think, I say, that another kingdom, less than thou, will now rise up, according to what is written: Thou, he says, art the head of gold, and after thee shall rise another kingdom, less than thou. But since every arrogant man glories in himself, while boasting glories in another's favour, it can rightly be said in some respect of the head of gold, that is, of the arrogant man, that he is still himself, in that he has not yet deserted himself, he who has set his glory in himself. But he withdraws from himself when he places his glory in another. Consider then how much less a thing it is to glory in the hissing of another's mouth and in false praises, than in goods of one's own, and often true ones, though received from another. Rightly therefore is it said: Thou art the head of gold, 1285Cand after thee shall rise another kingdom, less than thou; and there is added, and another third kingdom of bronze, which shall rule over all the earth. Bronze, in that it is exceedingly durable, rightly denotes obstinacy, as we said above. And so a man then has a belly and thighs as it were of bronze, when he clings obstinately even to those things at which he ought rather to blush. See then how aptly it seems to agree with this kingdom that it is said to rule over all the earth. For how does that not rule over all the earth which extends its tyranny even to the commanding of shameful things? And the fourth kingdom, he says, shall be as iron. As iron breaketh in pieces and subdueth all things, so shall it break in pieces all these, and shall crush them. If we ought to understand savagery in the iron, how, 1285DI ask, can a kingdom of this sort break in pieces and crush the other kingdoms? Without doubt, however shameless the mind may be, yet the glory which it was wont to have in itself through arrogance, or in another through boasting, it altogether (nor is this a wonder) disturbs and confounds. For who, amid so many and so open madnesses of his own savagery, would either believe himself holy, or be confident that this could be believed of him by another? But those violent laws of savagery are wont so to crush and confound the kingdom of obstinacy, that in the time of its fury the mind can persevere in no purpose (however right, however honourable). But whatever in the time of tranquillity the mind deliberates and disposes concerning peace or patience, even with a vow or an oath, in the time of its disturbance an oncoming fury 1286Ascatters and dissolves. Those things which we have thus far said concerning the exposition of this vision we have handled according to a twofold consideration, after the measure of our understanding. But the rest which follow run together, for both readings, into the same sense, and do not require a twofold exposition, as do those that have gone before. For what is savagery but cruelty? So too impatience: what else is it than a pitiable frailty? Thus under diverse names we ought to understand the same thing.

CAPUT XXXIII. Quomodo mens in semetipsam scinditur, laceratur, confunditur, cum ad corruptionis extrema pervenitur.

Nunc ergo sequentia consideremus: Porro, inquit, 1286Bquia vidisti et pedum, et digitorum partem testeam figuli, et partem ferream, regnum divisum erit, quod tamen de plantario ferri orietur (Dan. I). Sed si per pedem necessitas, per ferrum intelligitur crudelitas, quid sibi vult quod pedum pars quaedam ferrea esse describitur? Nunquidnam quisque erga proprias necessitates durus, et immitis seu etiam inflexibilis invenitur? Scimus quia avarus non implebitur pecunia, et qui amat divitias fructus non capiet ex eis, et tamen laborare non cessat, nec satiantur oculi divitiis, nec recogitat, dicens: Cui laboro, et fraudo animam meam bonis? Nonne tibi videtur incedere ferrea planta qui cunctis diebus vitae suae comedit in tenebris, et in curis multis, et in aerumna atque 1286Cin tristitia? Vere, ut scriptum est, avaro nihil est scelestius (Eccle. X), qui nec proximo compatitur, nec sui miseretur. Quid jam miramur ejusmodi hominem habere tibias ferreas, cum jam habet plantas? Quid jam conquerimur quod immitis est erga proximos, qui jam obduratus est erga usus proprios? Qui sibi nequam est, cui bonus erit?

Notate tamen quod qui ejusmodi est, prius in tibiis ferreis obduratur erga proximum suum. Postmodum etiam in ferreis pedibus obduratur erga semetipsum. Vide ergo quisquis haec legis, ne forte post amorem studiumque sapientiae revertaris ad amorem lucrumque pecuniae, hoc est aureum caput in ferreos pedes deflectere. Viscera avari crudelia: pone nunc ante oculos tuos, et considera quoslibet 1286Davaros. Vide, quaeso, quam sint solliciti, quam laboriosi, quam parci, quam austeri sibi. Quid tamen saepe idem facere consueverunt, vel adhuc quoque facturi sunt, si ab alieno gratis nutriantur, vestiantur, protegantur? Videbis utique eos mollibus vestiri velle, crapula et ebrietate madere, somno et otio torpore, irasci et indignari cum sibi videant aliquid de assuetis deesse. Nunquidnam tibi non videtur ejusmodi pedibus ex parte ferreis et ex parte testeis incedere, cum videas unum eumdemque in suis tam parcum, in alienis tam prodigum esse, modo austeritati, modo luxui deservire? Porro, inquit, quod vidisti pedum et digitorum partem testeam figuli, et partem ferream, regnum divisum erit, quod tamen de plantario ferri orietur. 1287APes in quinque digitos dividitur, et necessitatis conditio procul dubio in quinque partitur, cum ad omnes corporis sensus dilatatur. Sed cum sensuum oblectamenta studiose quaerimus vel caute declinamus, quid aliud quam duobus pedibus hinc inde, a dextris et a sinistris nitimur, et inter prospera et adversa per desideriorum nostrorum iter gradimur? Sed saepe videas hos uni, illos alii sensui vehementius indulgere, et propter unius alicujus sensus delicias, aliorum sensuum molestias libenter sustinere. Quam multi multo tempore in agnoque labore acquirunt quod sub unius diei, vel horae crapula vel ebrietate expendunt? Quot timores, quot honores, quot algores hostis, noctis et frigoris adulteri sponte tolerant, ut ad momentanea foedae voluptatis blandimenta 1287Bpertingant? Nonne hujusmodi omnes pedum vel digitorum partem testeam, partem ferream habent, qui in uno tam patientes et pene insensibiles, in altero tam impatientes existunt? Porro quia vidisti, inquit, pedum et digitorum partem testeam figuli, et partem ferream, regnum divisum erit. Vere divisum erit quod hujusmodi fuerit. Nam ut aliqua in unum ex pluribus exempli causa ponamus, cum unus idemque saepe sit avarus, et gulosus; quanta, quaeso, rixa vel contentio sit frequenter in corde illius, cum unum eumdemque nummum saepe gula expendere, avaritia jubeat reservare. In hunc itaque modum multoties levi ex causa regnum in seipsum dividitur, perturbatur atque confunditur, donec funditus desoletur. Omne 1287Cenim regnum in seipsum divisum desolabitur, et domus supra domum cadet.

Notandum sane quod de hoc regno dicitur, quod tamen, inquit, de plantario ferri orietur. Cum enim praevaricatrix mens primo ad saecularia desideria revertitur, ad tolerandos mundi labores animus obduratur, et tunc temporis, secundum sententiam Job, esse sub sentibus delicias computat (Job XXII), dum ad desideriorum suorum finem qualicunque modo pertingat. O quam felices essent, si ea quae sustinent pro amore pecuniae, vel alicujus adipiscendae saecularis pompae vel gloriae, patienter sustinerent pro amore justitiae! Sed qui tot molestias, tot injurias, tot contumelias, saepe tam patienter 1287Dtolerant pro lucro cupiditatis, nec unum vel leve correctionis verbum aequanimiter audiunt pro amore virtutis. Hoc esse puto quod sequitur, cum statim subinfertur: Secundum quod vidisti ferrum mistum testae ex luto, et digitos ex parte ferreos, et ex parte fictiles, ex parte erit solidum, et ex parte erit contritum. Fortes enim et patientes, ut dictum est, ejusmodi homines sunt in ea parte qua eorum labor posset militare virtuti. Quod autem dicit ex testa, ex luto quid aliud est hujusmodi testa nisi foeda consuetudo? Quasi enim lutum in testam duratur quando foeda actio in consuetudinem ducitur, sed ex foedae consuetudinis obduratione homo ad impatientiam dejicitur, et testae more leviter nunc per iracundiam frangitur, nunc 1288Ain concupiscentiam resolvitur. Sed, sicut de avaris ostendimus, videntur multi suis voluptatibus libenter tunc satisfacere, cum hoc possunt sine dispendio pecuniae, cum autem sine lucri damno non possunt, vehementer contraire. Nonne tibi videntur isti ferrum, et testam ex luto pariter commiscere? Quia autem vidisti, inquit, ferrum mistum testae ex luto, commiscebuntur quidem humano semini, sed non adhaerebunt sibi, sicut ferrum non potest misceri testae. Quae sunt humana semina nisi carnalis prudentiae consilia? Docet nos prudentia carnis propter usui necessaria pecuniam quaerere. Docet nos eadem multa de vitae necessariis detrahere, et pecuniae parcere, ne videatur in necessarios usus deficere. Ecce quomodo adhaereat humano semini cum videatur 1288Bunum quasi ex consilio alterum requirere, non tamen permiscentur, quia unum alteri videtur saepe contradicere. Aliud imperat amor divitiarum, atque aliud amor deliciarum; aliud aestus voluptatis, aliud quaestus cupiditatis. Sed cum in hunc modum diversa vitia hominem per diversa rapiunt, vel ad contraria impellunt miserabiliter affligunt, nec die nec nocte quiescere sinunt, et modo mirabili atque miserabili qui ejusmodi sunt damnationis suae tempora praeveniunt, dum interna animi anxietate, vel exteriori afflictione erga semetipsos desaeviunt, et nisi superna eos clementia respiciat, per temporalia tormenta ad aeterna vadunt.

CHAPTER XXXIII. How the mind is cleft against itself, torn and confounded, when the extremities of corruption are reached.

Now therefore let us consider what follows: Moreover, he says, 1286Bwhereas thou sawest both of the feet and of the toes a part of the potter's clay and a part of iron, the kingdom shall be divided, which nevertheless shall rise out of the plantation of iron (Dan. I). But if by the foot necessity is understood, and by iron cruelty, what does it mean that a certain part of the feet is described as being of iron? Is anyone found hard and harsh, or even inflexible, towards his own necessities? We know that the covetous man shall not be filled with money, and he that loveth riches shall reap no fruit from them; and yet he does not cease to labour, nor are his eyes satisfied with riches, nor does he consider, saying: For whom do I labour, and defraud my soul of good things? Does he not seem to thee to walk upon an iron sole, who all the days of his life eateth in darkness, and in many cares, and in misery and 1286Cin sorrow? Truly, as it is written, there is nothing more wicked than the covetous man (Eccle. X), who neither has compassion on his neighbour nor pities himself. Why do we now wonder that a man of this sort has legs of iron, when he already has the soles? Why do we now complain that he is harsh towards his neighbours, who is already hardened towards his own uses? He that is evil to himself, to whom will he be good?

Note however that he who is of this sort is first hardened in iron legs towards his neighbour. Afterwards he is hardened also in iron feet towards himself. See therefore, whoever thou art that readest these things, that thou do not perchance, after the love and zeal of wisdom, turn back to the love and gain of money; this is to bend the head of gold down into feet of iron. The bowels of the covetous are cruel: set now before thine eyes and consider any 1286Dcovetous men thou wilt. See, I pray thee, how anxious they are, how laborious, how sparing, how austere towards themselves. Yet what have these same men often been wont to do, or what will they yet do, if at another's cost they are fed, clothed, and sheltered free of charge? Thou wilt surely see them wishing to be clothed in soft raiment, to be soaked with surfeiting and drunkenness, to grow numb torpore with sleep and idleness, to be angry and indignant when they see something of their accustomed comforts wanting to them. Does such a man not seem to thee to walk upon feet in part of iron and in part of clay, when thou seest one and the same man so sparing in his own goods, so prodigal in another's, serving now austerity, now luxury? Moreover, he says, in that thou sawest of the feet and of the toes a part of the potter's clay and a part of iron, the kingdom shall be divided, which nevertheless shall rise out of the plantation of iron. 1287AThe foot is divided into five toes, and the condition of necessity is beyond doubt parted into five, when it is extended to all the senses of the body. But when we studiously seek the delights of the senses or warily shun them, what else do we do than lean upon two feet, on this side and on that, on the right hand and on the left, and walk between prosperity and adversity along the road of our desires? But thou mayest often see these men indulge one sense more vehemently and those another, and for the delights of some one sense willingly endure the troubles of the other senses. How many over a long time acquire in agnoque labour what they spend in the surfeiting or drunkenness of a single day or hour? How many fears, how many honours, how many chills, of the enemy, of the night, and of the cold, do adulterers willingly endure, that they may attain to the momentary blandishments 1287Bof foul pleasure? Have not all such men a part of the feet or of the toes of clay and a part of iron, who in the one thing are so patient and almost insensible, and in the other so impatient? Moreover, whereas thou sawest, he says, of the feet and of the toes a part of the potter's clay and a part of iron, the kingdom shall be divided. Truly divided will that be which is of this sort. For, to set down by way of example some few things out of many in one man: since one and the same man is often both covetous and gluttonous, how great, I ask, is the quarrel and contention that is frequently in his heart, when gluttony bids him spend, and avarice bids him keep back, one and the same coin. In this way, then, many times upon a slight occasion the kingdom is divided against itself, disturbed and confounded, until it is utterly laid desolate. For every 1287Ckingdom divided against itself shall be brought to desolation, and house upon house shall fall.

It is surely to be noted what is said of this kingdom, which nevertheless, he says, shall rise out of the plantation of iron. For when the transgressing mind first turns back to worldly desires, the spirit is hardened for the enduring of the world's labours, and at that time, according to the saying of Job, it counts it a delight to be under the briers (Job XXII), so long as it may in any manner attain the end of its desires. O how happy they would be, if the things they endure for the love of money, or for the gaining of some worldly pomp or glory, they endured patiently for the love of justice! But they who so patiently bear so many troubles, so many injuries, so many contumelies 1287Dfor the gain of covetousness, do not hear with an even mind so much as one light word of correction for the love of virtue. This I take to be what follows, when it is at once subjoined: According as thou sawest the iron mingled with clay of mire, and the toes in part of iron and in part of earthenware, in part it shall be solid, and in part it shall be broken. For men of this sort are strong and patient, as has been said, in that very part in which their labour might have served virtue as a soldier. But as to what he says, of clay, of mire: what else is clay of this sort than foul habit? For mire is hardened as it were into clay when a foul action is drawn into a habit; but out of the hardening of foul habit a man is cast down into impatience, and after the manner of clay is lightly broken, now by wrath, now 1288Adissolved into concupiscence. But, as we have shown concerning the covetous, many are seen to satisfy their own pleasures gladly when they can do so without expense of money; but when they cannot do so without loss of gain, to go vehemently against them. Do not these men seem to thee to mingle iron and clay of mire alike? And whereas thou sawest, he says, the iron mingled with clay of mire, they shall indeed be mingled to human seed, but they shall not cleave one to another, even as iron cannot be mingled with clay. [Vulg. humano semine, ablative, "mingled with human seed"; Migne's dative governs the gloss that follows] What are human seeds, but the counsels of carnal prudence? The prudence of the flesh teaches us to seek money for the things necessary for use. The same teaches us to withdraw much from the necessaries of life, and to spare money, lest it seem to fail for necessary uses. See how it cleaves to human seed, since 1288Bthe one seems as it were by counsel to require the other; yet they are not mingled together, because the one often seems to contradict the other. The love of riches commands one thing, and the love of delights another; the heat of pleasure one thing, the gain of covetousness another. But when in this manner diverse vices hurry a man in diverse directions, or drive him to contrary things, they wretchedly afflict him, they suffer him to rest neither by day nor by night; and in a manner as wonderful as it is wretched, they who are of this sort anticipate the times of their own damnation, while by the inward anxiety of the spirit, or by outward affliction, they rage savagely against themselves; and unless the clemency from above look upon them, they go through temporal torments to eternal ones.

CAPUT XXXIV. Quomodo divinitus promovetur homo quandoque de profundo iniquitatis ad summum fastigium charitatis.

  • 1288C

Sed divina miseratio saepe venit in adjutorium salutis quod, quantum in ipsis est, cumulus est damnationis, secundum quod scriptum est, ubi abundavit iniquitas, superabundavit et gratia (Matth. XXIV). Nam saepe superna gratia dum hujusmodi homines inter calamitatum suarum angustias visitat, hanc eamdem infelicitatis suae immanitatem eorum oculis objicit, et in ejus consideratione ad poenitentiae lamenta compungit, et eductas de lacu miseriae, et de luto faecis, quibusdam profectuum gradibus de profundo iniquitatis provehit ad summum fastigium charitatis. Nonne hoc est quod Scriptura loquitur, 1288Dubi consequenter adjungitur: In diebus regnorum illorum suscitabit Deus coeli regnum quod in aeternum non dissipabitur, et regnum ejus populo alteri non dabitur. Comminuet, et consumet universa regna haec, et ipsum stabit in aeternum, secundum quod vidisti, quod de monte abscissus est lapis sine manibus, et comminuit testam, et ferrum, et aes, et argentum, et aurum (Dan. II). In diebus itaque regnorum illorum sub tempore tantae malitiae, et tantae miseriae datur abundantia gratiae, et suscitatur regnum charitatis quod nunquam potest perire. Qui enim manet in dilectione, manebit in aeternum. Itaque hoc regnum in aeternum non dissipabitur. Charitas enim omnia suffert, omnia credit, omnia sperat, omnia sustinet (I Cor. XIII). Et regnum ejus populo alteri non dabitur, 1289Ahic est enim fons ille (ut ait Augustinus) de quo non communicat alienus: Comminuet, inquit, et consumet universa regna haec. Vultis autem nosse quomodo charitas universa regna haec comminuit atque consumit: Charitas, ait Apostolus, patiens est, benigna est, charitas non aemulatur, non agit perperam, non inflatur, non est ambitiosa, non quaerit quae sua sunt, non irritatur, non cogitat malum, non gaudet super iniquitate, congaudet autem veritati. Adhuc additur de hoc regno, quia ipsum stabit in aeternum, et Charitas, ait Apostolus, nunquam excidit, secundum quod vidisti, inquit, quod de monte abscissus est lapis sine manibus, et comminuit testam, et ferrum, et aes, et argentum, et aurum. Scimus quia regnum Dei a timore inchoatur, sed in charitate 1289Bconsummatur. Per lapidem itaque timoris dejicitur opus confusionis, et fundatur regnum charitatis. Sed lapis sine manibus abscinditur per quem charitatis regnum fundatur: timoris enim incussio, charitatisque inspiratio fit in Spiritu sancto non opere humano: Charitas, inquit, Dei diffusa est in cordibus nostris, per Spiritum sanctum qui datus est nobis (Rom. V). Comminuet, inquit, atque consumet universa regna haec, secundum quod vidisti, quod lapis abscissus est sine manibus, et contrivit testam, et ferrum, et aes, et argentum, et aurum. Destructio adversantium regnorum incipitur a timore, perficitur in charitate. Per timorem eradicantur a consensu, per charitatem evelluntur ab effectu. Comminuit 1289Cergo testeam fragilitatem charitas, quae patiens est, comminuit ferreum rigorem charitas, quae benigna est. Quid, quaeso, est in quo simulationis aes comminuitur, nisi charitas quae non aemulatur? Quid vero est quod comminuit aeream pertinaciam, nisi charitas quae non agit perperam? Si in argento jactantia, in auro intelligitur arrogantia, quid eis tam contrarium quam charitas quae non inflatur, nec est ambitiosa, nec quaerens quae sunt sua? Si aut in argento illud opus intelligitur quod fit ex sola deliberatione, non autem ex dilectione, non fit tam ex dilectione quam ex veritate, quid tam consentaneum charitati quae congaudet veritati? Si in aureo capite optimi operis defectus reprehenditur, eo quod ad debiti finis talum non 1289Dperducitur, quid melius hoc opus arguit vel corrigit quam perfecta charitas quae nunquam excidit? O felix charitatis regnum quod manet in aeternum! O felix regnum quod nunquam dissipabitur, quando populo alteri in aeternum dabitur! Felix regnum in quo patientia durat, benignitas perseverat! Felix regnum, in quo nemo aemulatur, felix utique in quo non agitur perperam, nemo inflatur, felix vere in quo nemo ambitiosus quaerit quae sua sunt; nemo uritur! Forte in hoc nobis aliquid innuit, quod cum superius in enumeratione contritionis prius ferrum, postea testam posuerit, hoc in loco sane prius testam, postea autem ferrum ponit. Sed scimus quia in aliis timor, in aliis autem principatur pudor. Nam alii quidem sunt magis timorati quam verecundi, 1290Aalii vero magis verecundi quam timorati. Qui igitur timidiores sunt facilius compunguntur ex consideratione propriae crudelitatis quam voluptatis. Qui vero verecundiores familiarius ad poenitentiam emolliuntur ex recordatione foedae voluptatis quam iniquitatis. Si igitur per ferrum crudelitas, per testam recte voluptas intelligitur, quid mirum si alias, alio et alio ordine ferrum et testa comminuta leguntur, cum pro qualitate animorum; vel morum in aliis et aliis intimae compunctionis et verae contritionis ordo vel occasio varietur?

CHAPTER XXXIV. How a man is sometimes advanced by God from the depth of iniquity to the highest summit of charity.

  • 1288C

But the divine mercy often comes to the help of salvation in that which, so far as lies in the men themselves, is a heaping up of damnation, according to what is written: where iniquity abounded, grace also did more abound (Matth. XXIV). [Vulg. Rom. 5:20 reads delictum, "offence", where Migne's noun is that of Matt. 24:12, to which his own note points] For often, when the grace from above visits men of this sort amid the straits of their calamities, it sets this very enormity of their own unhappiness before their eyes, and in the consideration of it pricks them to the lamentations of penitence; and having drawn them out of the pit of misery and out of the mire of dregs, by certain steps of advance it carries them from the depth of iniquity to the highest summit of charity. Is not this what Scripture speaks, 1288Dwhere it is consequently added: In the days of those kingdoms the God of heaven shall raise up a kingdom which shall never be destroyed, and his kingdom shall not be given to another people. It shall break in pieces and shall consume all these kingdoms, and itself shall stand for ever, according as thou sawest that a stone was cut out of a mountain without hands, and it broke in pieces the clay, and the iron, and the bronze, and the silver, and the gold (Dan. II). [Vulg. non tradetur, "shall not be delivered over"] And so in the days of those kingdoms, in the time of so great malice and so great misery, an abundance of grace is given, and there is raised up a kingdom of charity which can never perish. For he that abideth in love shall abide for ever. And so this kingdom shall never be destroyed. Charity indeed beareth all things, believeth all things, hopeth all things, endureth all things (I Cor. XIII). And his kingdom shall not be given to another people, 1289Afor this is that fountain (as Augustine says) whereof a stranger doth not partake: It shall break in pieces, he says, and shall consume all these kingdoms. But would you know how charity breaks in pieces and consumes all these kingdoms: Charity, says the Apostle, is patient, is kind; charity envieth not, dealeth not perversely, is not puffed up, is not ambitious, seeketh not her own, is not provoked to anger, thinketh no evil, rejoiceth not upon iniquity, but rejoiceth with the truth. Further it is added of this kingdom, that itself shall stand for ever; and Charity, says the Apostle, never falleth away, according as thou sawest, he says, that a stone was cut out of a mountain without hands, and it broke in pieces the clay, and the iron, and the bronze, and the silver, and the gold. We know that the kingdom of God is begun from fear, but is consummated 1289Bin charity. And so by the stone of fear the work of confusion is cast down, and the kingdom of charity is founded. But the stone by which the kingdom of charity is founded is cut out without hands: for the striking in of fear and the inspiration of charity comes to be in the Holy Spirit, not by human work: The charity, he says, of God is poured forth in our hearts, by the Holy Spirit who is given to us (Rom. V). It shall break in pieces, he says, and shall consume all these kingdoms, according as thou sawest that a stone was cut out without hands, and it crushed the clay, and the iron, and the bronze, and the silver, and the gold. The destruction of the opposing kingdoms is begun from fear and perfected in charity. Through fear they are rooted out of consent, through charity they are plucked out of effect. Charity therefore, which is patient, breaks 1289Cin pieces the fragility of clay; charity, which is kind, breaks in pieces the rigidity of iron. What, I ask, is that in which the bronze of simulation is broken in pieces, but charity which envieth not? And what is it that breaks in pieces the obstinacy of bronze, but charity which dealeth not perversely? If in the silver boasting is understood and in the gold arrogance, what is so contrary to them as charity, which is not puffed up, nor is ambitious, nor seeketh the things that are her own? If either in the silver that work is understood which is done out of deliberation alone and not out of love, it is not done so much out of love as out of truth: what is so agreeable to charity, which rejoiceth with the truth? If in the head of gold the failure of the best work is reproved, in that it is not 1289Dbrought through to the heel of its due end, what better convicts or corrects this work than perfect charity, which never falleth away? O happy kingdom of charity, which abideth for ever! O happy kingdom which shall never be destroyed, since to another people it shall for ever be given [dabitur; read non dabitur, "shall not be given"]! Happy kingdom in which patience endures and kindness perseveres! Happy kingdom, in which no one envies; happy indeed, in which there is no perverse dealing, no one is puffed up; truly happy, in which no ambitious man seeks the things that are his own; no one is burned! Perhaps he hints something to us in this, that whereas above, in the enumeration of the crushing, he set first the iron and afterwards the clay, in this place he plainly sets first the clay and afterwards the iron. But we know that in some men fear rules, and in others shame. For some indeed are more fearful than modest, 1290Abut others more modest than fearful. Those therefore who are the more timid are the more easily pricked by the consideration of their own cruelty than of their own pleasure. But those who are the more modest are the more readily softened to penitence by the recollection of foul pleasure than of iniquity. If therefore by the iron cruelty is understood, and by the clay pleasure rightly so, what wonder if elsewhere the iron and the clay are read as broken in pieces in one order and another, since according to the quality of spirits; or of manners, the order or the occasion of inward compunction and true contrition varies in one man and another?

CAPUT XXXV. Quae sit nimis sera, vel quae sit utilis et certa prudentia.

Prosequitur adhuc Daniel, et addit: Deus magnus, 1290Binquit, ostendit regi quae futura sunt postea (Dan. II). Deus, inquit, magnus, forte in ejus cogitatione et dilectione creverat, cui tantam mysteriorum profunditatem revelaverat, ut ejus magnitudinem non immerito prae caeteris magnificare debeat. Deus magnus ostendit regi, non cuilibet homini, sed regi ista Dominus ostendit, ut regia libertate et auctoritate illud suum regendum atque disponendum agnoscat, qui ad futurorum praenotitiam et ejusmodi gratiam anhelat. Deus magnus ostendit regi quae futura sunt postea. O quanta Dei pietate agitur, ut cum adhuc futura est, dum adhuc praecaveri potest, sua homini ruina reveletur, ut tanta sollicitudine praevideat, et agnoscat quid timere, quid cavere, cui 1290Cpericulo repugnare debeat. Tarda quidem et nimis sera providentia est, cum jam imminens periculum vitari non potest. Deus magnus ostendit regi quae futura sunt postea. Felix ille qui imminentem ruinam praevidere potest dum adhuc rex est, et passionibus suis dominari et instans adhuc periculum declinare potest! Videamus autem quid post haec omnia subjungitur, cum dicitur: Et verum est somnium, et fidelis interpretatio. Multa saepe divinorum mysteriorum profunda insitae nobis rationis vivacitate penetramus, et nonnulla de futuris prudentiae oculo antequam veniant cauta sollicitudine praevidemus. Sed in his saepe constantia titubat, et quid per certitudinis securitatem inconcusse teneat haud plene comprehendit. Cogitationes enim mortalium 1290Dtimidae, et incertae providentiae nostrae. Econtra autem, quod in lumine Dei cernimus, quod ex ejus revelatione cognoscimus, tanta certitudine et fiducia tenemus, ut nullo super hoc ambiguitatis ancipiti pulsari possimus. Hoc est quod Psalmista figuraliter exprimit, cum dicit: Prae fulgore in conspectu ejus nubes transierunt (Psal. XVII), Domini respicere, est illuminare. Cum enim sit lux vera, more utique solis eo ipso quo respicit, illustrat, accendit et illuminat. Ad divinum itaque respectum tanto saepe divini luminis splendore replemur, ut in eo quod divinitus revelatur omnis mox ambiguitatis vel haesitationis obnubilatio de medio tollatur. Nubes itaque transeunt, cum illae quae animum obnubilare consueverant dubitationes recedunt. Nubes transire 1291Aest veritatis perspicaciam, et intelligentiae serenum sine ulla percunctatione tenere. Ejusmodi certitudinem, certitudinisque securitatem in sententiae suae assertione habere se docuit qui fiducialiter asseruit. Et verum est somnium, et fidelis interpretatio ejus. Sed quid ad haec rex fecerit, Scriptura subjungit.

CHAPTER XXXV. What prudence is far too late, and what is useful and sure.

Daniel still goes on, and adds: The great God, 1290Bhe says, hath shown the king what shall be hereafter (Dan. II). [Vulg. ventura, "what shall come to pass"] God, he says, is great: perhaps he had grown great in the thought and the love of the man to whom he had revealed so great a depth of mysteries, so that not undeservedly he ought to magnify his greatness above all others. The great God hath shown the king: not to any man whatsoever, but to a king the Lord hath shown these things, so that he who pants after the foreknowledge of things to come and after grace of this sort may recognize, with royal liberty and authority, that this realm of his is to be ruled and ordered. The great God hath shown the king what shall be hereafter. O with how great mercy of God is it done, that while it is still to come, while it can still be guarded against, a man's own ruin is revealed to him, that with so great solicitude he may foresee and recognize what he ought to fear, what to beware of, what 1290Cperil he ought to fight against. Slow indeed, and far too late, is that foresight when the peril already imminent cannot be avoided. The great God hath shown the king what shall be hereafter. Happy is he who can foresee the imminent ruin while he is still king, and can still lord it over his passions and turn aside the pressing peril! But let us see what is subjoined after all these things, when it is said: And the dream is true, and the interpretation faithful. We often penetrate many deep things of the divine mysteries by the liveliness of the reason implanted in us, and some things concerning what is to come we foresee with the eye of prudence, in wary solicitude, before they arrive. But in these things constancy often totters, and it does not fully grasp what it might hold unshaken through the security of certitude. For the thoughts of mortal men are fearful, 1290Dand our foresights uncertain. But on the contrary, what we discern in the light of God, what we know from his revelation, we hold with such certitude and confidence that we can be shaken by no doubtful ambiguity concerning it. This is what the Psalmist figuratively expresses when he says: At the brightness that was before him the clouds passed away (Psal. XVII), for the Lord to look upon is to illuminate. For since he is the true light, after the manner of the sun, by that very act by which he looks he lights up, kindles, and illuminates. And so at the divine regard we are often filled with so great a splendour of the divine light, that in that which is divinely revealed every clouding of ambiguity or hesitation is straightway taken out of the midst. And so the clouds pass away, when those doubts which were wont to becloud the spirit withdraw. For the clouds to pass away 1291Ais to hold the clear-sightedness of truth and the serenity of understanding without any questioning. He who confidently asserted it showed that he had certitude of this sort, and the security of certitude, in the assertion of his own judgment. And the dream is true, and the interpretation thereof faithful. But what the king did at these things, Scripture subjoins.

CAPUT XXXVI. Quod humiliatio instabilis, et indiscreta sit quidem reprehensione digna.

Tunc rex Nabuchodonosor cecidit in faciem suam et Danielem adoravit, et hostias, et incensum praecepit ut sacrificarent ei (Dan. II). Multum, nec mirum, seipsum humiliavit, et merito humiliari debuit postquam tale tantumque sibi periculum imminere cognovit. 1291BSed in hoc eum quis non jure reprehendat, quod illum qui soli Deo debetur honorem homini mortali exhibeat? Sed forte illum, quia verum templum esse deprehendit, tali honore dignum judicavit. Sed ad aedificationem nostram oportet nos ista diligenter discutere, vel quod in nobis juxta horum similitudinem actitari soleat subtiliter inquirere: Cecidit, inquit, in faciem suam: per faciem homo cognoscitur, et ea pars in homine caeteris decentior vel dignior habetur. Quid itaque aliud est in faciem cadere quam illud etiam quod in se praecipuum, vel optimum est alta desideratione despicere, et illud utique unde aliis innotescere, et velut venerandus apparere consueverat, tanquam infirmum et pene 1291Cvel penitus nihilum reputare? Attende quomodo in faciem suam cecidit, quomodo seipsum humiliavit, vel humiliari nos voluit, qui omnes justitias nostras panno mulieris menstruatae comparavit. Sed nec valde magnum, nec admodum mirum si eo nos tempore abjicimus, quo periculum nostrum imminere jam cernimus. Tunc, inquit, rex cecidit in faciem suam. Tunc cecidit cum, Daniele docente, periculum suum agnovit. Sed sciendum quod illa humiliatio, quae sine perseverantia est, imminenti periculo repugnare non potest.

Illud quoque notandum quod saepe quo se quis inconsultius et immoderatius abjicit, eo ad insaniorem et enormiorem postmodum superbiam erumpit. Attende in rege humiliationem instabilem et indiscretam, 1291Det postmodum superbiam plus quam humanam. Nam qui prius Danielem adoravit, idem ipse postmodum statuam suam adorare praecepit.

CHAPTER XXXVI. That an unstable and indiscreet humbling is indeed worthy of reproof.

Then king Nabuchodonosor fell upon his face and adored Daniel, and he commanded that they should sacrifice victims and incense to him (Dan. II). Greatly, and no wonder, did he humble himself, and deservedly ought he to have been humbled after he knew that so great a peril hung over him. 1291BBut who would not justly reprove him in this, that he rendered to a mortal man the honour which is owed to God alone? But perhaps, because he perceived him to be a true temple, he judged him worthy of such honour. But for our own edification we must diligently sift these things, and subtly inquire what is wont to be enacted in us after their likeness: He fell, it says, upon his face: by the face a man is known, and that part in a man is held more comely and more worthy than the rest. What else therefore is it to fall upon one's face, than even to despise, with a lofty longing, that which is chief or best in oneself, and to reckon that very thing whereby one was wont to become known to others and to appear as it were venerable, as something weak and almost 1291Cor utterly nothing? Consider how he fell upon his face, how he humbled himself, or rather how he would have us humbled, who compared all our justices to the cloth of a menstruous woman. But it is neither a very great thing nor much of a wonder if we cast ourselves down at that time in which we already discern that our peril hangs over us. Then, it says, the king fell upon his face. Then he fell, when, at Daniel's teaching, he recognized his own peril. But it must be known that that humbling which is without perseverance cannot fight against the imminent peril.

This also is to be noted, that often the more inconsiderately and immoderately a man casts himself down, the more insanely and enormously does he afterwards break out into pride. Consider in the king a humbling unstable and indiscreet, 1291Dand afterwards a pride more than human. For he who first adored Daniel, that same man afterwards commanded his own statue to be adored.

CAPUT XXXVII. Quod in nulla nostra virtute debeamus confidere vel gloriari velle.

Hostias, inquit, et incensum praecepit ut sacrificarent ei. Animalia pro Daniele occidere, vel incensum cremare, et ejusmodi quaelibet in ejus usus vel utilitatem expendere omnino non esset culpabile. Culpabile autem haec illi sacrificare, et homini mortali divinum honorem expendere. In hostiae immolatione, brutorum et irrationabilium caro mactatur, in incensi concrematione suavis odor latius spargitur Quasi igitur viva, sed bruta caro mactatur, 1292Aquando quilibet irrationabilis appetitus vel affectus in nobis carnis contritione mortificatur, et tunc velut incensi odorem latius spargimus, quando cujuscunque virtutis opinionem dignis laudum praeconiis dilatamus. Ex his itaque quae in nostris appetitibus vel affectibus adhuc bestialiter vivunt pro Daniele nostro mactare, et ejusmodi mortificationem in devotionis nostrae usum et utilitatem exercere absque ulla contradictione non erit inutile. Cum autem Danielis officium multiplici laude commendamus, et de virtute devotionis, bonae opinionis fragrantiam sermonis nostri commendatione circumquaque diffundimus, bonum opus quidem facimus, si in his omnibus Danielis nostri non tam gloriam quam utilitatem quaerimus. Et, ut apertius dicam 1292Bquid sentiam, bonum est utique ob devotionis gratiam et orationis instantiam vitam nostram restringere, bonum est virtutem orationis commendare et laudibus efferre, si tamen ab hoc fiat ut nostra devotio in melius excrescat et ferventior exsurgat, non ut gloriosior appareat. Quam multi sunt qui cum per orationis instantiam, aliquam se obtinuisse gratiam credunt, multum jam de sua devotione vel sanctitate praesumentes magna et ab aliis de ea aestimari volunt. Hoc est quod Nabuchodonosor postquam per Danielem ad voti sui desiderium pervenit, tam inepto quam vano eum honore cumulavit. Hoc autem quod circa hanc de qua loquimur virtutem reprehendimus, in qualibet virtute, vel 1292Cgratia cavere debemus. Audi aperte, diligenter adverte, memoriter retine si in aliqua gratia, in aliqua industria, si in aliqua virtute tua confidis, praesumis, gloriaris, divinum quidem et satis improbe honorem ei impendis, ne dicam Deum tibi facis. Qui gloriatur, in Domino glorietur. Maledictus homo qui confidit in homine, ergo qui confidit in se vel aliqua sua virtute: Gloriam meam, inquit, alteri non dabo, nec laudem meam sculptili (Isa XLII).

CHAPTER XXXVII. That we ought to trust in no virtue of our own, nor to wish to glory in it.

Victims, it says, and incense he commanded that they should sacrifice to him. To kill animals for Daniel, or to burn incense, and to expend anything whatever of that kind for his use or profit, would not be blameworthy at all. But it is blameworthy to sacrifice these things to him, and to expend divine honour upon a mortal man. In the immolation of a victim the flesh of brute and irrational creatures is slain; in the burning of incense a sweet odour is spread more widely As therefore living but brute flesh is slain, 1292Awhen any irrational appetite or affection in us is mortified by the crushing of the flesh; and then we spread abroad more widely as it were the odour of incense, when we enlarge the repute of any virtue with worthy heralding of praises. And so to slay for our own Daniel some of those things which still live bestially in our appetites or affections, and to practise a mortification of that sort for the use and profit of our devotion, will without any contradiction not be unprofitable. But when we commend Daniel's office with manifold praise, and concerning the virtue of devotion spread abroad on every side the fragrance of good repute by the commendation of our speech, we do indeed a good work, if in all these things we seek not so much the glory of our Daniel as his profit. And, that I may say more openly 1292Bwhat I think, it is surely good to restrain our life for the sake of the grace of devotion and of instancy in prayer; it is good to commend the virtue of prayer and to extol it with praises, provided that it be done to this end, that our devotion may grow to the better and rise up more fervent, not that it may appear more glorious. How many there are who, when they believe that they have obtained some grace through instancy in prayer, already presuming much upon their own devotion or holiness, wish great things to be thought of it by others also. This is what Nabuchodonosor did, who, after he had come through Daniel to the desire of his vow, heaped upon him an honour as foolish as it was vain. But what we reprove concerning this virtue of which we speak, we ought to beware of in any virtue or 1292Cgrace whatever. Hear it openly, mark it diligently, hold it in memory: if in any grace, in any effort, if in any virtue of thine own thou trustest, presumest, gloriest, thou art indeed bestowing divine honour upon it, and shamelessly enough; not to say that thou art making a God for thyself. He that glorieth, let him glory in the Lord. Cursed be the man that trusteth in man; therefore also he who trusteth in himself or in any virtue of his own: My glory, he says, I will not give to another, nor my praise to a graven thing (Isa XLII). [Vulg. et laudem meam sculptilibus, "and my praise to graven images", plural]

CAPUT XXXVIII. Quam multi quaerunt virtutum scientiam, morumque doctrinam ad ostentationem, non ad utilitatem.

Quam multi gloriantur, et gloriosi in oculis hominum esse volunt, non dicam de eo quod virtutes vel sanctitatem habeant. Sed quod de virtutibus 1292Dquidem prudenter disserere, et diserte disputare sciant. O quanto satius, o quanto utilius tibi esset aurum scientiae et argentum eloquentiae non habere, quam inde tibi idolum facere! Simulacra gentium argentum et aurum, opera manuum hominum. Virtutum modum, et formam solam per intelligentiam et doctrinam depingere, et memoria retinere, earum autem effectu carere, quid aliud est quam quaedam simulacra corde gestare? Scientia sanctitatis sine intentione bona quid aliud est quam imago sine vita? Scientia sola sine sanctitatis effectu, et bonitatis affectu, quid aliud est quam simulacrum vanum sine motu, et sensu? Os, inquit, habent et non loquentur, oculos habent, et non videbunt; aures habent et non audient, nares habent et non odorabunt. 1293AManus habent, et non palpabunt; pedes habent et non ambulabunt; non clamabunt in gutture suo (Psal. CXIII). Os, ut omnes novimus, est instrumentum loquendi; oculi, instrumentum videndi; aures, instrumentum audiendi, et in hunc modum debet et de aliis intelligi. Quid itaque est os, oculos, aures, vel talium quaelibet habere, et ea in nullos usus exercere, nisi officiorum instrumenta habere, et instrumentorum officiis carere? Legis quia corde creditur ad justitiam, ore autem confessio fit ad salutem (Rom. X). Ad os itaque pertinet confessio, ad oculos circumspectio, ad aures obauditio, ad nares discretio, ad manus operatio, ad pedes promotio, ad guttur supplicatio. Ecce illa tua scientia inanis optime fortassis novit quae sit virtus confessionis. 1293BNovit quia omnia in confessione lavantur. Novit fortassis quomodo confiteri debeat, nec tamen confitetur. Os igitur habet et non loquitur. Novit fortassis quomodo vitam suam circumspicere debeat, eam tamen quomodo oportet circumspicere dissimulat. Videndi quodammodo instrumentum habes, sed videndi officio cares. Nosti forte quae sit virtus obedientiae, et qualis debeat esse, nec tamen vis obedire. Hoc est aures habere, et non audire. Per scientiam discernendi instrumentum fortassis habes spiritualis odoramenti. Sed dum in moribus discernendi nullum studium impendis, de inutili instrumento inaniter gloriaris. Scis quomodo te oporteat in bono opere exercere, nec tamen vis fructum boni operis per experientiam capere, hoc est manus habere, 1293Cet non palpare. Quid enim est manibus palpare, nisi operum fructus experiendo probare? Profectuum pedes per scientiam accepisti, si quomodo in anteriora extendere oporteat didicisti. Sed pedes habens minime incedis, si ad profectum non tendis. Supplicandi doctrinam accepisti, et non vis supplicare, hoc est guttur habere, et nolle clamare.

CHAPTER XXXVIII. How many seek the knowledge of the virtues and the teaching of morals for display, not for profit.

How many glory, and wish to be glorious in the eyes of men: I will not say over the fact that they have virtues or holiness, but over the fact that they know how to discourse prudently 1292Dabout the virtues and to dispute eloquently. O how much better, O how much more profitable it would be for thee not to have the gold of knowledge and the silver of eloquence, than to make thyself an idol out of them! The idols of the nations are silver and gold, the works of the hands of men. To paint out the measure and the mere form of the virtues by understanding and teaching, and to hold them in memory, but to lack their effect: what else is it than to carry certain idols in the heart? The knowledge of holiness without a good intention: what else is it than an image without life? Knowledge alone, without the effect of holiness and the affection of goodness: what else is it than a vain idol without motion and without sense? A mouth, he says, they have, and they shall not speak; eyes they have, and they shall not see; ears they have, and they shall not hear; nostrils they have, and they shall not smell. 1293AHands they have, and they shall not feel; feet they have, and they shall not walk; neither shall they cry out in their throat (Psal. CXIII). The mouth, as we all know, is the instrument of speaking; the eyes, the instrument of seeing; the ears, the instrument of hearing; and in this manner it ought to be understood of the rest also. What therefore is it to have a mouth, eyes, ears, or any such things, and to exercise them for no uses at all, but to have the instruments of the offices and to lack the offices of the instruments? Thou readest that with the heart we believe unto justice, but with the mouth confession is made unto salvation (Rom. X). And so to the mouth belongs confession; to the eyes, circumspection; to the ears, obedient hearing; to the nostrils, discretion; to the hands, working; to the feet, advance; to the throat, supplication. Behold, that empty knowledge of thine perhaps knows exceedingly well what the virtue of confession is. 1293BIt knows that all things are washed away in confession. It knows perhaps how it ought to confess, and yet it does not confess. It has a mouth therefore, and speaks not. It knows perhaps how it ought to look around upon its own life, yet it dissembles to look around upon it as it should. Thou hast in a manner the instrument of seeing, but thou lackest the office of seeing. Thou knowest perhaps what the virtue of obedience is, and of what kind it ought to be, and yet thou wilt not obey. This is to have ears, and not to hear. Through knowledge thou hast perhaps the instrument of discerning spiritual fragrance. But while thou spendest no zeal upon discernment in matters of conduct, thou gloriest emptily in a useless instrument. Thou knowest how thou oughtest to exercise thyself in good work, and yet thou wilt not take the fruit of good work by experience: this is to have hands, 1293Cand not to feel. For what is it to feel with the hands, but to prove the fruits of works by experiencing them? Thou hast received through knowledge the feet of advances, if thou hast learned how one ought to stretch forward to the things that are before. But having feet thou dost not walk at all, if thou tendest not to advance. Thou hast received the teaching of supplication, and thou wilt not supplicate: this is to have a throat, and to be unwilling to cry out.

CAPUT XXXIX. Quod in cujuscunque virtutis exsecutione septem ista oporteat observare.
