Si autem diligenter attendimus, septem ista in cujuscunque virtutis exsecutione observare debemus. Primum illud quod ad os pertinet, anteacta mala damnare, et accusare. Secundum quod ad 1293Doculos pertinet, quid agendum sit diligenter investigare, et per investigationem cognoscere. Tertium quod ad obauditionem pertinet, invento consilio consentiendo acquiescere, et obtemperare velle. Quartum quod quasi varium sit, agendo bono insidiantia mala caute deprehendere, et prudenter discernere. Quintum quod etiam quasi ad manus spectat, deliberatum bonum opere explere. Sextum quasi quadam promotione pedum, ex bono incoepto semper ad meliora tendere. Sed quia ad nullum horum per nosmetipsos sufficientes sumus, ad omnia haec divinum auxilium implorare debemus. Si autem haec novimus, nec tamen opere exercemus, quid aliud in acquirendis, et excolendis scientiis otiosis, et inutilibus, quam vana et inania simulacra facimus, 1294Avel colimus, dum sola virtutum notitia contenti sumus? Vide quam sit perversum atque damnabile, spiritualem doctrinam ad solam ostentationem non autem ad aedificationem quaerere. Carnis prudentia est ista profecto, et quae prorsus inimica sit Deo? Quid, quaeso, prodest, imo quantum obest cum magno labore, et summo studio quaerere, et investigare, et anxie scire velle, quae nullo modo velis opere exercere? Nam luce clarius constat, quia servus sciens voluntatem domini sui, et non faciens quin potius agens indigna, plagis vapulabit multis. O quale, et quam inutile consilium, consilia vitae ad hoc solum quaerere, ut habeas quo possis caeteris sapientior apparere, et magistri nomen obtinere! Quam acuta prudentia, quam astuta providentia 1294Billis consiliis exquirendis vehementer insistere, quibus adeptis, nullo modo velis acquiescere! Saepe autem hujusmodi mentibus justo Dei judicio accidere videmus, ut quales prius sunt spiritales providentiae ad spiritualia, tales postmodum fiant carnales providentiae ad carnalia. Qualia, inquam, spiritualia consilia prius existant ad coelestia et divina, talia post modicum carnalia consilia inveniantur ad saecularia et humana. Quemadmodum enim in spiritualibus et animae lucris prius vident quae salubriter facere possint, et tamen facere nolint; sit postmodum in carnibus commodis et temporalibis lucris videant quae utiliter facienda sint, et tamen concupiscentia victi vel socordia devincti, omnino facere non possint. Solent autem hoc ipsum quibusdam 1294Cetiam utiliter accidere, ita ut ex eo ipso jam incipiant vitia sua detestari et abhorrere, et super hoc ipso divinum auxilium implorare, cum videant nec inter summa pericula seipsos posse a suis excessibus temperare, unde est illa prophetica imprecatio, seu postulatio: Similes illis fiant qui faciunt ea, et omnes qui confidunt in eis (Psal. CXIII). Sed ut ad id redeamus unde digressi sumus, hinc saltem colligamus quam ineptum sit et inane de solis inanibus virtutum scientiis, et quibusdam quasi ipsarum simulacris gloriari velle, cum apud Deum detestabile sit hominem de aliqua sua virtute praesumere. Ecce Nabuchodonosor reprehendimus, et merito reprehendere debemus quod Danielem, quamvis 1294Dhominem fidelem, divino honore donari praecepisset, quanto magis si ejus imagini idipsum exhiberi voluisset.

CHAPTER XXXIX. That in the carrying out of any virtue these seven things ought to be observed.

But if we attend diligently, we ought to observe these seven things in the carrying out of any virtue. The first is that which belongs to the mouth: to condemn and accuse the evils done before. The second, which belongs to the 1293Deyes: to investigate diligently what is to be done, and through investigation to know it. The third, which belongs to obedient hearing: when counsel has been found, to rest in it by consenting, and to be willing to comply. The fourth, which is as it were a various thing: warily to detect the evils that lie in wait for the good that is to be done, and prudently to discern them. The fifth, which likewise looks as it were to the hands: to fulfil in work the good that has been deliberated. The sixth, as it were by a certain advance of the feet: from a good beginning always to tend to better things. But because we are of ourselves sufficient for none of these, we ought to implore the divine help for them all. But if we know these things and yet do not practise them in work, what else, in acquiring and cultivating idle and useless kinds of knowledge, do we make 1294Aor worship than vain and empty idols, while we are content with the mere acquaintance with the virtues? See how perverse and damnable a thing it is to seek spiritual teaching for display alone, and not for edification. This is assuredly the prudence of the flesh, and one that is utterly an enemy to God? What, I ask, does it profit; nay, how much does it hurt, to seek with great labour and the utmost zeal, and to investigate, and anxiously to wish to know, the things which thou art in no way willing to practise in work? For it stands clearer than the light that the servant who knows his lord's will and does it not, but rather does things unworthy, shall be beaten with many stripes. O what a counsel, and how useless, to seek the counsels of life for this alone, that thou mayest have wherewith thou canst appear wiser than the rest, and obtain the name of master! How sharp a prudence, how cunning a foresight, 1294Bto press vehemently after the searching out of those counsels in which, once obtained, thou art in no way willing to rest! But we often see it happen to minds of this sort, by the just judgment of God, that such as their spiritual foresights were before towards spiritual things, such afterwards do their carnal foresights become towards carnal things. Of what kind, I say, their spiritual counsels stood before towards heavenly and divine things, of that kind after a little are their carnal counsels found to be towards worldly and human things. For just as in spiritual things and in the gains of the soul they first see what they might healthfully do, and yet are unwilling to do it; may it be [sit; read sic, "so"] that afterwards in advantageous fleshes and in temporal temporalibis gains they see what ought usefully to be done, and yet, conquered by concupiscence or bound fast by sloth, are wholly unable to do it. But this very thing is wont to happen to some men 1294Ceven profitably, so that from that very fact they now begin to detest and abhor their own vices, and over this very thing to implore the divine help, when they see that not even amid the greatest perils can they restrain themselves from their own excesses; whence comes that prophetic imprecation, or petition: Let them that make them become like unto them, and all such as trust in them (Psal. CXIII). But that we may return to that from which we digressed, let us at least gather from this how foolish and empty a thing it is to wish to glory in the mere empty knowledges of the virtues, and in certain idols as it were of the virtues themselves, since with God it is detestable that a man should presume upon any virtue of his own. Behold, we reprove Nabuchodonosor, and deservedly ought we to reprove him, in that he had commanded Daniel, though 1294Da faithful man, to be presented with divine honour; how much the more if he had wished the same thing to be rendered to Daniel's image.

CAPUT XL. Quod saepe desiderium corrumpitur unde intelligentia illuminatur.

Sed videamus inter haec quam vera de Deo senserit vel dixerit, qui tam inania fieri voluit. Loquens ergo rex ait Danieli: Vere Deus vester Deus deorum est, et Dominus regum, revelans mysteria, quoniam potuisti aperire sacramentum hoc. (Dan. II). Ecce quam sane, et de divinis sentit, et tamem idololatriae simile quod per opus exercuit. Sic sic sub uno eodemque tempore et Moyses in monte ad summa et divina contemplanda sublimatur, et 1295Aplebs sibi commissa ad idololatriae scelus, et vituli culturam in valle dejicitur, et fit utique saepe in uno eodemque animo, modo mirabili atque miserando, ut unde in summis intelligentia humana ad divina illuminatur, inde minus desideriorum turba corrumpatur. Quis enim nesciat quomodo magnitudo revelationum mentem incautam in superbiam elevat, cum Apostolus ipse de seipso dicat: Ne magnitudo revelationum extollat me, datus est mihi stimulus carnis meae angelus Satanae, qui me colaphizet (I Cor. XII). Saepe etenim quidam adhuc inter ipsa divinarum revelationum spectacula constituti, apud semetipsos tacita mente superbiunt, jam magna de se aestimantes, magni ab aliis aestimari dignum ducunt, jam quomodo et aliis innotescere 1295Bvaleant sagaci sollicitudine perquirunt, jam inanis favoris auram captant, et illam quam apud Deum habere consueverant gloriam veram, in vanam commutant. De qualibus Psalmista: Et mutaverunt gloriam in similitudinem vituli comedentis fenum (Psal. CV). Cum enim scriptum sit: Omnis caro fenum, et omnis gloria ejus quasi flos feni (Isa. XL). Quid aliud erit vitulus fenum comedens, nisi humani favoris appetitus, carnalem et temporalem gloriam ambiens? Quid igitur aliud erit vitulum animal quidem petulcum et brutum colere, quam in gloriae temporalis ambitione irrationabili appetitui deservire, et in temporali et inani gloria gloriam suam constituere? Et Nabuchodonosor quidem, 1295Cquem tamen juste reprehendimus, tolerabilius aliquid fecit, qui hominem utique animal, videlicet rationale, divino honore donavit. Nam procul dubio irrationabilius erit irrationabile, quam rationabile animal colere. Alii confidunt et gloriantur in propria virtute, alii autem de sola virtutis aestimatione. Illi de eo quod habent vel se credunt habere, isti de eo quod aestimantur ab aliis esse. Illi de testimonio conscientiae, non quasi apud Deum, isti de testimonio famae quasi apud proximum tantum. Illud itaque est velut animal irrationabile, istud velut animal rationale. Felix qui gloriatur de conscientiae testimonio, sic tamen quasi in Domino. Alioquin ingratum valde gloriari de munere, sed non in muneris auctore. Quanto melius, quanto rectius 1295Ddictus rex Deum quam Danielem adoraret, et ei sacrificaret, qui eum quidem (quamvis Danielis officio) docuerat quod quaerebat.

CHAPTER XL. That desire is often corrupted from the very source by which the understanding is illuminated.

But let us see amid these things how truly he thought and spoke concerning God, who willed such empty things to be done. The king therefore, speaking, says to Daniel: Verily your God is the God of gods, and the Lord of kings, revealing mysteries, since thou couldst open this sacrament. (Dan. II). Behold how soundly he thinks even concerning divine things, and yet tamem what he practised in deed was like to idolatry. Thus, thus, at one and the same time both Moses on the mountain is lifted up to the contemplation of the highest and divine things, and 1295Athe people committed to him is cast down in the valley to the crime of idolatry and to the worship of the calf; and often indeed it comes to pass in one and the same spirit, in a manner as wonderful as it is pitiable, that from the very source by which human understanding is illuminated on high toward divine things, from that same source the crowd of desires is the less corrupted. For who does not know how the greatness of revelations lifts an incautious mind into pride, since the Apostle himself says of himself: Lest the greatness of the revelations should exalt me, there was given me a sting of my flesh, an angel of Satan, to buffet me (I Cor. XII). For often certain men, while still set amid the very sights of divine revelations, grow proud within themselves with a silent mind; already thinking great things of themselves, they count it worthy to be esteemed great by others; already they search out with shrewd solicitude how they may become known 1295Bto others also; already they catch at the breeze of empty favour, and change into a vain glory that true glory which they were wont to have with God. Of such the Psalmist says: And they changed the glory into the likeness of a calf that eateth hay (Psal. CV). Since indeed it is written: All flesh is hay, and all the glory thereof as the flower of hay (Isa. XL). [Vulg. flos agri, "the flower of the field"] What else will the calf that eateth hay be, but the appetite for human favour, courting a carnal and temporal glory? What else therefore will it be to worship a calf, an animal wanton indeed and brute, than in the courting of temporal glory to serve an irrational appetite, and to set one's own glory in a temporal and empty glory? And Nabuchodonosor indeed, 1295Cwhom nevertheless we justly reprove, did something more tolerable, in that he presented with divine honour a man, an animal to be sure, but a rational one. For beyond doubt it will be more irrational to worship an irrational animal than a rational one. Some trust and glory in their own virtue, but others in the mere estimation of virtue. Those, over what they have or believe themselves to have; these, over what they are esteemed by others to be. Those, over the testimony of conscience, yet not as before God; these, over the testimony of report, as before their neighbour only. And so the former is as it were an irrational animal, the latter as it were a rational animal. Happy is he who glories over the testimony of conscience, yet so as in the Lord. Otherwise it is a very thankless thing to glory over the gift, but not in the giver of the gift. How much better, how much more rightly 1295Dwould the said king have adored God rather than Daniel, and sacrificed to him who had indeed taught him (though by Daniel's office) what he was seeking.

CAPUT XLI. Quod alia ex divina revelatione cognoscimus, alia ex ratione colligimus.

Sed quia reprehendenda, ut jam diximus, fecit, videamus quam recta de Deo senserit in eo, quod dixit: Vere Deus vester, Deus deorum est, et Dominus regum. Cum potestas regia sit principalis et summa, quid est regem hoc dicere, nisi ex intimo animae sensu hoc fortiter immobiliterque tenere? Quid est hoc Danielem a rege audire, nisi intimum animae sensum, veram animi devotionem non debere, nec posse latere? Vel in eo fortassis quod alia Daniel 1296Aa Deo discit, alia etiam a rege audivit sacra Scriptura mystice innuit, quia in devotionis nostrae usum alia sunt quae ex divina revelatione discimus, alia quae ex insita nobis rationis vivacitate perpendimus. Quod autem de Deo loquens vester et noster dicit, respectum gratiae, non conditionem naturae attendit. Speciali enim quodammodo illorum proprie erat, qui ex gratiae ipsius munere, et meritorum praerogativa singularem, prae caeteris apud ipsum familiaritatis locum, et confidentiae ausum obtinuerant. Et absque dubio devotio est, quae Deum et propitium reddit, et familiarem efficit. Deus, inquit, deorum, et Dominus regum. Nam etsi sint qui dicantur dii, sive in coelo, sive in terra, secundum illud: Ego dixi: Dii estis (Psal. LXXXI), nuncupative 1296Bquidem sic dici possunt: nam quod Deus naturaliter et substantialiter est, omnino esse non possunt. Est ergo Deus deorum, et Dominus regum. Quia, testante Apostolo: Non est potestas nisi a Deo (Rom. XIII). Nam quod alii aliis praesunt, quod viri virtutum vitiis et passionibus suis dominari possunt, ex summi Regis sane munere accipiunt. Ait ergo: Vere Deus vester, Deus deorum est, et Dominus regum, revelans mysteria, unde autem haec colligat, atque conjiciat, statim adnectit, cum dicit: Quia potuisti aperire sacramentum hoc. Ex eo enim, quod sibi secreta cordis sui, ex eo item quod sibi arcana consilii divini, unum de praeterito, alterum de futuro, sub uno eodemque tempore revelavit, 1296Cmira ingenii vivacitate, summae divinitatis revelationem perpendit. Ex eo autem, quod de sui cordis secreto audivit, et verum probavit, verum et illud esse collegit, quod divinitatis consilio vel proposito interim per experientiam probare non potuit. Prudenter igitur animadvertit summam sapientiam esse, quae Danieli potuit in tam tenebroso humani cordis abysso alienae conscientiae secreta aperire. Nam, secundum Salomonis sententiam, solus Deus est, qui novit cor omnium filiorum hominum. Sagaciter nihilominus deprehendit summam potestatem esse quae Danieli potuit futurorum, atque gerendorum ordinem et dispositionem pro beneplacito revelare. Quis enim alius quam Deus divinum consilium ei manifestare potuit? Juxta illud, 1296Dquod Isaias dixit: Quis novit sensum Domini, aut quis consiliarius ejus fuit? (Isai. XL.) Teste namque Apostolo, sicut nemo novit quae sunt hominis nisi spiritus hominis, qui in ipso est, sic nemo novit quae sunt Dei, nisi Spiritus Dei. Recte ergo dicitur: Vere Deus vester, Deus deorum est, et Dominus regum, revelans mysteria, quia potuisti aperire sacramentum hoc.

CHAPTER XLI. That some things we know from divine revelation, and others we gather from reason.

But since he did things to be reproved, as we have already said, let us see how rightly he thought concerning God in that which he said: Verily your God is the God of gods, and the Lord of kings. Since the royal power is chief and highest, what is it for the king to say this, but from the inmost sense of the soul to hold it firmly and unmovably? What is it for Daniel to hear this from the king, but that the inmost sense of the soul neither ought nor is able to lie hidden from the true devotion of the spirit? Or perhaps in this, that some things Daniel 1296Alearns from God and others he heard also from the king, Holy Scripture mystically hints that, for the use of our devotion, some things are those which we learn from divine revelation, others those which we weigh out from the liveliness of reason implanted in us. But in that, speaking of God, he says "your" and "our", he attends to the regard of grace, not to the condition of nature. For in a certain special way he belonged properly to those who, out of the gift of his own grace and by the prerogative of merits, had obtained with him a singular place of familiarity beyond the rest, and the boldness of confidence. And without doubt it is devotion which both renders God propitious and makes him familiar. God, he says, of gods, and Lord of kings. For although there be some that are called gods, whether in heaven or on earth, according to that word: I have said: You are gods (Psal. LXXXI), by name 1296Bindeed they can be so called: for what God is naturally and substantially, that they can in no wise be. He is therefore the God of gods, and the Lord of kings. Because, as the Apostle testifies: There is no power but from God (Rom. XIII). For that some are set over others, that men of virtues are able to have dominion over their own vices and passions, they receive assuredly from the gift of the highest King. He says therefore: Verily your God is the God of gods, and the Lord of kings, revealing mysteries; but whence he gathers and conjectures these things he at once subjoins, when he says: Since thou couldst open this sacrament. For from this, that he revealed to him the secrets of his own heart, and from this likewise, that he revealed to him the hidden things of the divine counsel, the one concerning the past, the other concerning the future, at one and the same time, 1296Che weighed, with a wonderful liveliness of wit, the revelation of the highest Godhead. And from this, that he heard concerning the secret of his own heart and proved it true, he gathered that that also was true which meanwhile he could not prove by experience concerning the counsel or the purpose of the Godhead. Prudently therefore he observed that it was the highest wisdom which was able to open to Daniel, in so dark an abyss of the human heart, the secrets of another's conscience. For, according to Solomon's sentence, God alone it is who knows the heart of all the sons of men. Shrewdly none the less he perceived that it was the highest power which was able to reveal to Daniel, according to its good pleasure, the order and disposition of things to come and of things to be done. For who else than God could manifest to him the divine counsel? According to that word 1296Dwhich Isaiah said: Who hath known the sense of the Lord, or who hath been his counsellor? (Isai. XL.) [Vulg. Isa. XL:13 opens Quis adjuvit spiritum Domini, "Who hath helped the spirit of the Lord"; the wording printed here is that of Rom. XI:34, Quis enim cognovit sensum Domini] For, as the Apostle witnesses, just as no one knows the things that are of man save the spirit of man which is in him, so no one knows the things that are of God, save the Spirit of God. Rightly therefore is it said: Verily your God is the God of gods, and the Lord of kings, revealing mysteries, since thou couldst open this sacrament.

CAPUT XLII. Quod optimum sit devotionem nostram eousque proficere, quod nulla occupatio possit ejus officium interrumpere.

Tunc rex Danielem in sublime extulit, et munera magna, et multa dedit ei. Si per Danielem devotionem recte intelligimus, quid aliud erit Danielem in 1297Asublime attollere quam devotionis officium inter caetera quaelibet officia principale et praecipuum reputare, et quasi summo loco habere? Fieri autem solet postquam mens quanta sit virtus verae devotionis per experientiam ejus probat, ut studiis vehementius intendat et ejus provectibus diligenter insistat, investigando quaerat, quaerendo inveniat, unde in idipsum ad meliora proficiat. Quid aliud igitur quam munera magna et multa devotionis nostrae Daniel accipit, quando ex alacriter impenso studio, quasi quaedam intelligentiae talenta et prudentiae lucra facile invenit, unde ad majora crescere, et ad plurima dilatari possit? Et constituit eum, inquit, principem super omnes provincias Babylonis, et praefectum magistratuum super cunctos sapientes Babylonis. 1297BO utinam devotio nostra, eousque proficiat, quo cunctis principibus Babylonis superior, cunctis prudentibus Babyloniae sapientior appareat, et omni eo quod in mundo placet, vel pretiosum videtur ex mentis nostrae judicio, pretiosior emineat, et ex arbitrii decreto superius lucrum obtineat! Hoc erit, ut arbitror, Danielem a rege principatus, et magisterii arcem accipere. Utinam contingat, tandem aliquando nostram devotionem id perfectionis attingere, quo nulla quantumlibet vehemens affectio, nulla occupatio, nulla sollicitudo, nulla suggestio, vel suasio possit in nobis ejus studium, non dicam dissipare, sed nec quidem interrumpere, et illud immobile Apostolicum impleatur: Sine intermissione 1297Corate! (I Thess. V.) Utinam caetera quaelibet officia ita disponantur, vel ordinentur, ut devotionis nostrae usibus et utilitatibus militare, seu etiam subservire probentur!

CHAPTER XLII. That it is best that our devotion advance so far that no occupation can interrupt its office.

Then the king raised Daniel on high, and gave him great and many gifts. If by Daniel we rightly understand devotion, what else will it be to lift Daniel on 1297Ahigh than to reckon the office of devotion, among all other offices whatsoever, as chief and principal, and to hold it as it were in the highest place? And it is wont to come to pass, after the mind proves by experience how great the virtue of true devotion is, that it stretches out more vehemently toward its pursuits and diligently presses upon its advancements, that by investigating it may seek, by seeking may find, whence it may advance in the selfsame thing toward better things. What else therefore than great and many gifts does the Daniel of our devotion receive, when out of zeal eagerly bestowed he easily finds, as it were, certain talents of understanding and gains of prudence, whereby he may grow toward greater things and be enlarged to very many? And he set him, he says, prince over all the provinces of Babylon, and chief of the magistrates over all the wise men of Babylon. 1297BO would that our devotion might advance so far that it should appear higher than all the princes of Babylon, wiser than all the prudent of Babylonia, and stand out more precious, by the judgment of our mind, than everything that pleases in the world or seems precious, and by the decree of the will obtain the higher gain! This will be, as I judge, for Daniel to receive from the king the citadel of princedom and of mastery. Would that it might at length at some time befall our devotion to attain that measure of perfection wherein no affection, however vehement, no occupation, no solicitude, no suggestion or persuasion could in us, I will not say scatter, but not even interrupt its zeal, and that unmoved apostolic word be fulfilled: Without ceasing 1297Cpray! (I Thess. V.) Would that all other offices whatsoever were so disposed or ordered that they might be proved to serve as soldiers, or even to be subservient, to the uses and advantages of our devotion!

CAPUT XLIII. Quod etiam in necessitatis nostrae usibus nihil improvide, nihil inconsulte agere debeamus.

Daniel autem postulavit a rege, et constituit super opera provinciae Babylonis, Sidrac, Misach et Abdenago (Dan. II). Petenti Danieli, rex absque dubio consentit, quando mentis arbitrium facile acceptat, et recipit quidquid ei conscientia sub tempore intimae devotionis suggerit. Sed quae, quaeso, dicta est illa Babylonis provincia, vel quae illius provinciae opera? Certe circumadjacentem Babylonis provinciam 1297DDeus fecit: nam muros utique, et aedificia humana industria explevit. Si ergo Babylon recte confusio interpretatur, si ejus quaelibet confusioni subjacere videntur, quomodo, quaeso, ad Babylonem pertinere, vel confusioni subesse potuit, quod Deus fecit et instituit? Scimus autem, quia Deus hominem multiplici necessitati subjecit: nam comedendi bibendi, et quorumlibet ejusmodi necessitatem ex ipso conditionis suae jure accepit. Sed si in primo illo conditionis suae statu perstitisset, ista homo nullo modo erubesceret, quia sine omni peccato 1298Aexercere potuisset. Nunc autem eadem ipsa necessitatis officia juste erubescimus, quia corruptionis nostrae vitio devicti, sine culpa exercere non valemus. Recte ergo Babylonicae possessioni deputatur lata illa, et vere multum dilatata infirmitatis nostrae corruptela, quae confusioni subjacere non dubitatur. In hujusmodi itaque provincia, et necessitatis nostrae officio, vel opera, erit omnino perutile praedictos illos viros praefecisse, et sub eorum dispositione omnia redegisse. Sicut superius jam diximus, per istos tres viros, circumspectionem, discretionem, deliberationem intelligere debemus. Debemus itaque non solum in studiis et exercitiis spiritualibus, verum etiam in negotiis saecularibus et necessitatis usibus nihil incircumspecte, nihil 1298Bindiscrete, nihil improvide facere. Et hoc erit Sidrach, Misach et Abdenago, super omnia opera provinciae Babylonis constituisse. Itaque in his etiam, in quibus humanae necessitati consulimus, vel communi infirmitati deseruimus, nihil sine modestia, nihil sine mensura, nihil sine intentione bona, nihil praeter ordinem, nil praeter rationem agamus, et quale Daniel magisterium in Babylonica provincia habere nos voluit, obtinemus. Denique secundum Sapientis sententiam, omnia fac cum consilio, et tale magisterium habes, quale tibi propheta designat ex mysterio. Omnino namque opus est circumspectione, discretione, si volumus nihil improvide, nihil inconsulte agere.

CHAPTER XLIII. That even in the uses of our necessity we ought to do nothing improvidently, nothing inconsiderately.

But Daniel requested of the king, and he appointed over the works of the province of Babylon, Sidrac, Misach and Abdenago (Dan. II). The king without doubt consents to Daniel asking, when the will of the mind readily accepts and receives whatever conscience suggests to it in the time of inmost devotion. But what, I ask, is that province of Babylon that is spoken of, or what are the works of that province? Certainly God made the province lying round about Babylon: 1297Dfor the walls indeed and the buildings human effort completed. If therefore Babylon is rightly interpreted confusion, if whatsoever things are hers seem to lie subject to confusion, how, I ask, could that which God made and instituted belong to Babylon, or be under confusion? But we know that God has subjected man to a manifold necessity: for the necessity of eating, of drinking, and of any things of that kind, he received from the very right of his own condition. But if he had persisted in that first state of his condition, man would in no way blush at these things, because he could have practised them without any sin. 1298ABut now we justly blush at those same offices of necessity, because, overcome by the vice of our corruption, we have not strength to practise them without fault. Rightly therefore is that broad, and truly much widened, corruption of our infirmity assigned to the Babylonian possession, which is not doubted to lie subject to confusion. In a province of this kind, therefore, and in the office or the works of our necessity, it will be altogether most useful to have set those aforesaid men in charge, and to have brought all things under their disposition. As we have already said above, by those three men we ought to understand circumspection, discretion, deliberation. We ought therefore, not only in spiritual pursuits and exercises, but also in secular business and in the uses of necessity, to do nothing incircumspectly, nothing 1298Bindiscreetly, nothing improvidently. And this will be to have appointed Sidrach, Misach and Abdenago over all the works of the province of Babylon. And so in these things also, in which we take counsel for human necessity, or serve the common infirmity, let us do nothing without modesty, nothing without measure, nothing without good intention, nothing beside order, nothing beside reason, and we obtain such mastery as Daniel willed us to have in the Babylonian province. Finally, according to the sentence of the Wise Man, do all things with counsel, and thou hast such mastery as the prophet designates for thee out of the mystery. For there is altogether need of circumspection and of discretion, if we wish to do nothing improvidently, nothing inconsiderately.

CAPUT XLIV. Quod quidquid animum per cogitationem pulsat, mentis devotionem excitare debeat.

  • 1298C

Ad ultimum autem in conclusione sermonis infertur: Ipse autem Daniel erat in foribus regis (Dan. II). Scimus autem, quia perfores aditus ad interiora patet. Daniel denique, quasi in regis foribus observat, quando devotio nostra circa quaslibet suggestiones caute invigilat, et contra quaslibet maligni machinationes in ipso tentationis aditu oratione repugnat, ne incautam mentem ulla infestatio, vel violentia opprimat, vel fraude circumveniat. Felix animi status, et omni studio expetendus, quando omne quod animum per cogitationem pulsat, et statim absque ulla mora mentis devotionem excitat. Debet itaque devotio 1298Dmentis omnem arbitrii consensum studiose praevenire, et oratione praemunire. Et hoc erit Danielem regis foribus praesidere. Ad omnem suggestionem, ad omnem emergentem cogitationem petamus a Domino ne nos inducat in tentationem, et habeamus Danielem civitatis nostrae foribus praesidentem. Audi quid secundum haec Sapiens admoneat, cum dicat: Omni tempore benedic Deum, et pete ab eo ut vias tuas dirigat (Tob. IV). Ad veram autem vitam vias nostras dirigat, qui sine fine vivit, et regnat. Amen.

CHAPTER XLIV. That whatever strikes the spirit through thought ought to arouse the devotion of the mind.

  • 1298C

But at the last, in the conclusion of the discourse, it is brought in: But Daniel himself was in the doors of the king (Dan. II). Now we know that through the doors perfores the entrance to the inner parts lies open. Daniel, finally, keeps watch as it were in the doors of the king, when our devotion warily keeps vigil over all suggestions whatsoever, and in the very entrance of temptation fights back with prayer against all the machinations of the evil one, lest any infestation or violence oppress the incautious mind, or circumvent it by fraud. Happy the state of the spirit, and to be sought after with all zeal, when everything that strikes the spirit through thought also at once, without any delay, arouses the devotion of the mind. The devotion 1298Dof the mind ought therefore studiously to forestall every consent of the will, and to fortify it beforehand with prayer. And this will be for Daniel to preside over the doors of the king. At every suggestion, at every emerging thought, let us ask of the Lord that he lead us not into temptation, and let us have Daniel presiding over the doors of our city. Hear what the Wise Man admonishes according to these things, when he says: At all times bless God, and ask of him that he direct thy ways (Tob. IV). And may he direct our ways to the true life, who without end lives and reigns. Amen.

LIBER SECUNDUS.

BOOK TWO.

CAPUT PRIMUM. De conscientiae securitate, vel profectuum innovatione.

  • 1299A

Ego Nabuchodonosor quietus eram in domo mea, et florens in palatio meo (Dan. IV). Quid per domum intelligimus, nisi conscientiam, et quid per palatium, nisi conscientiae securitatem, securitatisque fiduciam? Nam et palatium domus est, quaelibet tamen domus palatium dici non potest. Palatium namque est domus quaedam fortis, sublimis atque regalis. Si per domum conscientiam debemus intelligere, recte per palatium intelligitur securitas conscientiae. In domo itaque sua quietus sedet, quem sua conscientia non remordet. Quietam conscientiam facit praeteritorum 1299Bmalorum condigna satisfactio, et instantium malorum cauta providaque declinatio. Quietus itaque in domo manet, quem conscientia sua, nec de praeterita, nec de praesenti culpa remordet. Quietus in domo erat, qui veraciter dicebat: Neque enim reprehendit me cor meum in omni vita mea (Job XXVII). Quietus in domo sua exstitit, qui veraciter dicere potuit: Nihil enim mihi conscius sum (I Cor. IV). Quietus sane tunc temporis in domo erat, et in palatio suo florebat, cum dicebat: Gloria nostra, haec est testimonium conscientiae nostrae (II Cor. I). Quia in flore fructus speratur, recte per florem certa futurorum bonorum exspectatio figuratur. Quia flos est inchoatio futurorum fructuum, recte nihilominus 1299Cper florem intelligitur innovatio profectuum. In flore ergo figuratur vel certa praemiorum exspectatio, vel nova meritorum promotio. In palatio itaque suo ille veraciter floret, qui sub bonae conscientiae suae testimonio coronam gloriae securus exspectat. Audi in domo sua quiescentem, et in palatio suo florentem: Ego autem in justitia apparebo conspectui tuo; satiabor, cum apparuerit gloria tua (Psal. XVI). Quod dicit in justitia apparebo, pertinet ad domus quietem. Quod dicit satiabor cum apparuerit gloria tua, pertinet ad efflorationem. In palatio suo nihilominus florebat, qui cum magnae securitatis conscientia dicebat: Bonum certamen certavi, cursum consummavi, fidem servavi, de reliquo reposita est mihi corona justitiae, quam reddet mihi Dominus 1299Din illa die justus judex (II Tim. VI). Nonne et ille tibi in palatio suo florere videtur, cujus sensus de die in diem renovatur? In palatio suo sedet, floret, qui inter magna opera sua quae libenter facit, adhuc majora et meliora in corde proponit.

CHAPTER ONE. Of the security of conscience, or the renewal of advancements.

  • 1299A

I Nabuchodonosor was at rest in my house, and flourishing in my palace (Dan. IV). What do we understand by the house, but conscience, and what by the palace, but the security of conscience and the confidence of security? For a palace also is a house, yet not any house whatsoever can be called a palace. For a palace is a certain house that is strong, lofty and royal. If by the house we ought to understand conscience, rightly by the palace is understood the security of conscience. And so he sits at rest in his own house whom his own conscience does not bite back. A quiet conscience is made by a worthy satisfaction for past 1299Bevils, and by a wary and provident turning aside from present evils. And so he remains at rest in the house whom his own conscience bites back neither for past nor for present fault. He was at rest in the house who truly said: For neither hath my heart reproved me in all my life (Job XXVII). He stood at rest in his own house who could truly say: For I am conscious to myself of nothing (I Cor. IV). He was assuredly at rest in the house at that time, and was flourishing in his own palace, when he said: Our glory, this is the testimony of our conscience (II Cor. I). Because in the flower fruit is hoped for, rightly by the flower is figured the certain expectation of the goods to come. Because the flower is the beginning of the fruits to come, rightly none the less 1299Cby the flower is understood the renewal of advancements. In the flower therefore is figured either the certain expectation of rewards, or the new promotion of merits. And so he truly flourishes in his own palace who, under the testimony of his own good conscience, securely awaits the crown of glory. Hear one resting in his own house and flourishing in his own palace: But I shall appear before thy sight in justice; I shall be satisfied when thy glory shall appear (Psal. XVI). That he says, I shall appear in justice, pertains to the rest of the house. That he says, I shall be satisfied when thy glory shall appear, pertains to the flowering. He was none the less flourishing in his own palace who with the conscience of a great security said: I have fought the good fight, I have finished the course, I have kept the faith; for the rest there is laid up for me a crown of justice, which the Lord 1299Dwill render to me in that day, the just judge (II Tim. VI). Does not he also seem to thee to flourish in his own palace, whose sense is renewed from day to day? He sits and flourishes in his own palace who, amid his own great works which he gladly does, purposes in his heart yet greater and better ones.

CAPUT II. De intellectualis sensus illuminatione, vel quomodo contemplationis nostrae spectacula, menti soleant utilem timorem incutere.

Somnium vidi, inquit, quod perterruit me, et cogitationes 1300Ameae in stratu meo, et visiones capitis mei conturbaverunt me (Dan. IV). Prius dormimus quam somnium videamus. Quia per somnium corporis sensus corporeus sopitur, recte per somnium mentis alienatio intelligitur, per quam exteriorum omnium memoria funditus intercipitur. Somnium autem videre, est in divinae contemplationis arcanum mente transire. Dormit itaque et somnium videt qui per mentis excessum in sublimium contemplationem ascendit: Somnium, inquit, vidi, quod perterruit me. Hujusmodi somniorum alia deterrent, alia demulcent, alia contristant, alia laetificant. Laetificat sane (nec mirum) contemplatio aeternorum gaudiorum, terret vero speculatio divinorum judiciorum. Quis enim digne exprimere possit, quam 1300Blaetum, quam jucundum sua eum visio sive revelatio facit, cui Deus per Spiritum suum revelavit, quod oculus non vidit, nec auris audivit, nec in corde hominis ascendit? Sed quomodo eum econtra sua visio terret, qui mentis sublevatione per contemplationem videt, quam miro, quam occulto Dei judicio quod et de carcere catenisque interdum quis egrediatur ad regnum, et alius natus in regno inopia consummetur? Quem, quaeso, divinorum judiciorum abyssus non terreat, cum divino lumine tactus mentis intelligentia perspiciat, nec velocium esse cursum, nec fortium bellum, nec sapientium panem, nec doctorum divitias, nec artificum gratiam, sed tempus, casumque in omnibus? Viderat 1300Cutique Propheta, quia judicia Domini abyssus multa et perterruerant eum visiones capitis sui, cum diceret Domino: A judiciis enim tuis timui (Psal. CXVIII). Solent tamen hujusmodi visiones perterrere, non tam eo tempore quo per excessum mentis videntur, quam tunc temporis cum post excussum somnum ad memoriam revocantur, quando subtili examinatione secum tacita mente retractantur. Hinc est illud quod sequitur, cum dicitur: Et cogitationes meae in stratu meo, et visiones capitis mei conturbaverunt me. Ex duobus siquidem se asserit conturbari, ex cogitationibus suis, et visionibus capitis sui. Cum enim mens ex divina revelatione mirandum, imo et formidandum aliquid considerat, et postmodum ad se rediens secumque retractans seipsam 1300Dforti examinatione dijudicat, ne forte digna sit, cui tale aliquid contingat ex propriis nimirum cogitationibus secum agitur, ut sua eam visio perterreat. Cogitationes, inquit, meae in stratu meo. Quid hic intelligitur per stratum, nisi intimae quietis otium, visio vero capitis, illuminatio sensus intellectualis. Et visiones, inquit, capitis mei conturbaverunt me. Cogitationes suae itaque in stratu suo, et visiones capitis sui unumquemque tunc perterrent, terrendoque conturbant, cum ex eo quod secum 1301Apost divinae revelationis arcanum meditationis suae negotioso otio pertractat, ad salubrem sollicitudinem, utilemque formidinem evigilat. Cum enim quis ex divina revelatione cognoscat quomodo soleat alius de bono in melius proficere, alius de malo in deteriora vergere, vel quomodo contingat alios de summis bonis in infima mala corruere, vel de infimis malis ad virtutum summa ascendere; quid, quaeso, mirum, si sua eum visio cogat formidare? Nescit namque homo finem suum; sed, sicut pisces capiuntur hamo, et aves laqueo comprehenduntur, sic capiuntur homines in tempore malo, cum eis extemplo supervenerit (Eccle. IX).

CHAPTER II. Of the illumination of the intellectual sense, or how the sights of our contemplation are wont to strike a useful fear into the mind.

I saw a dream, he says, which terrified me, and my thoughts 1300Aupon my bed, and the visions of my head troubled me (Dan. IV). We sleep before we see a dream. Because through the dream the bodily sense of the body is lulled, rightly by the dream is understood that alienation of the mind through which the memory of all outward things is utterly cut off. But to see a dream is to pass over in mind into the secret place of divine contemplation. And so he sleeps and sees a dream who through excess of mind ascends into the contemplation of sublime things: I saw a dream, he says, which terrified me. Of dreams of this kind, some deter, others soothe, others sadden, others gladden. The contemplation of eternal joys assuredly gladdens (nor is it a wonder), but the beholding of the divine judgments terrifies. For who could worthily express how 1300Bglad, how joyful his own vision or revelation makes him to whom God has revealed through his Spirit that which eye hath not seen, nor ear heard, nor hath ascended into the heart of man? But how, on the contrary, does his own vision terrify him who by the lifting up of the mind sees through contemplation by how wonderful, by how hidden a judgment of God it is that one man sometimes goes forth even from prison and from chains unto a kingdom, and another born in a kingdom is consumed in want? Whom, I ask, would the abyss of the divine judgments not terrify, when, touched by the divine light, the understanding of the mind perceives clearly that the course is not the swift men's, nor the war the strong men's, nor bread the wise men's, nor riches the learned men's, nor favour the craftsmen's, but time and chance in all things? The Prophet had 1300Ccertainly seen that the judgments of the Lord are a great abyss, and the visions of his head had terrified him, when he said to the Lord: For I have feared thy judgments (Psal. CXVIII). Yet visions of this kind are wont to terrify, not so much at that time in which they are seen through excess of mind, as at that time when, after sleep has been shaken off, they are recalled to memory, when with subtle examination they are gone over again with oneself in a silent mind. Hence is that which follows, when it is said: And my thoughts upon my bed, and the visions of my head troubled me. For indeed he asserts that he is troubled by two things: by his own thoughts, and by the visions of his own head. For when the mind considers out of divine revelation something to be wondered at, nay rather to be dreaded, and afterwards, returning to itself and going over it with itself, judges itself 1300Dwith a strong examination, lest perchance it be worthy that some such thing should befall it, it is dealt with by its very own thoughts, so that its own vision terrifies it. My thoughts, he says, upon my bed. What is here understood by the bed, but the leisure of inmost quiet; and the vision of the head, but the illumination of the intellectual sense. And the visions, he says, of my head troubled me. And so his own thoughts upon his own bed, and the visions of his own head, then terrify each man and by terrifying trouble him, when out of that which, after the secret of divine revelation, he handles with himself 1301Ain the busy leisure of his own meditation, he wakes up unto a wholesome solicitude and a useful dread. For when a man knows out of divine revelation how one is wont to advance from good to better, another to sink from evil into worse things, or how it befalls that some fall down from the highest goods into the lowest evils, or climb up from the lowest evils to the heights of the virtues; what wonder, I ask, if his own vision compels him to dread? Man knoweth not indeed his own end; but, as fishes are taken with the hook, and birds are caught with the snare, so are men taken in the evil time, when it shall suddenly come upon them (Eccle. IX).

CAPUT III. Quod alia sit gratia per quam cognoscimus judicia divina, alia per quam discernimus merita propria.

  • 1301B

Vide autem quam sit stupendum, quamve formidandum quod saepe mens divino lumine irradiata, per contemplationem videat quid tali homini, qualis ipse est, digne debeat contingere, et tamen talem tunc temporis esse nesciat, cui tale judicium ex divina sententia merito incumbat. Nonne Nabuchodonosor regi hoc idem contingit, qui divinam sententiam de arboris abscissione ex Dei revelatione cognovit, verumtamen ostensae arboris similitudinem ad seipsum trahere ignoravit? Vidit quid tali homini ex divina sententia incumberet, qui ejusmodi arboris imaginem, vel similitudinem in seipso gestaret, 1301Cet tamen quod talis ipse exstiterit, quia per seipsum non potuit, per alium cognovit. Vide ergo quia alia est gratia, per quam cognoscimus judicia divina, et alia illa per quam discernimus merita propria. Illa namque contemplando, ista invenimus meditando. Illud itaque posse accepimus ex gratia contemplationis, istud vero ex studio meditationis et gratia discretionis. Et saepe unus idemque homo, utramque simul gratiam accipit quamvis sub uno eodemque tempore simul utramque exercere non possit. Quasi itaque alius alium consulit, quando quod per unum eumdemque mentis statum plene non potuit, ex alio statu alioque tempore discit, quod ad plenam sui cognitionem interim sufficere possit. Hinc est quod Nabuchodonosor per Danielem 1301Deruditur de his quae ipse in sua visione dubitare videtur. Mirumque in modum dum rex prophetae suam visionem narrat, ignorans docet alium, quod ab eo discat, et Daniel ab interrogante et ignorante discit quid discentem docere debeat. Sic utique, sic saepe mens prudenter inscia de contemplationis lumine reportat, quod postmodum per meditationem inveniat, pleniusque cognoscat. Accipitque meditatio de praecognita revelationis luce, unde nodos dubitationis dissolvat. Sicut superius jam diximus, per regem liberum arbitrium, per Danielem devotionem intelligimus. Ad ejusmodi itaque regem illa contemplatio pertinet, quae mentis arbitrio subjacet, quando eam animus jam ad manum habet, et velut ex potestate exercet. Sic et illa meditatio 1302Aad Danielem videtur pertinere, quam ex devotionis studio religiosa mens circa morum suorum circumspectionem solet exercere. Designatus ergo rex apud Danielem quasi somniorum suorum interpretationem quaerit, et invenit, quando animus, quae inter liberos contemplationis suae volatus prospexit, postmodum meditando, et circumspiciendo in moribus suis legit, et altius intelligit. Sed videamus modo quam instanter Nabuchodonosor quaesivit quod nescivit.

CHAPTER III. That the grace by which we know the divine judgments is one, and that by which we discern our own merits another.

  • 1301B

But see how astonishing a thing it is, and how dreadful, that often the mind, irradiated by the divine light, sees through contemplation what ought worthily to befall such a man as it is itself, and yet knows not that it is at that time such a one, upon whom by the divine sentence such a judgment deservedly presses. Did not this same thing befall king Nabuchodonosor, who knew from God's revelation the divine sentence concerning the cutting down of the tree, and yet knew not how to draw to himself the likeness of the tree that was shown? He saw what by the divine sentence would press upon such a man as should bear in himself the image or the likeness of a tree of that kind, 1301Cand yet that he himself stood forth such a one, because he could not through himself, he knew through another. See therefore that the grace is one by which we know the divine judgments, and that other by which we discern our own merits. For those we find by contemplating, these by meditating. And so that power we have received from the grace of contemplation, but this from the zeal of meditation and the grace of discretion. And often one and the same man receives both graces together, although he cannot exercise both together at one and the same time. And so it is as though one consulted another, when what he could not fully do through one and the same state of mind, he learns from another state and at another time, which meanwhile may suffice for the full knowledge of himself. Hence it is that Nabuchodonosor is instructed through Daniel 1301Dconcerning those things about which he himself seems to doubt in his own vision. And in a wonderful manner, while the king narrates his own vision to the prophet, not knowing it he teaches another what he may learn from him, and Daniel learns from the questioner who does not know, what he ought to teach the learner. Thus indeed, thus often the mind, prudently unknowing, brings back from the light of contemplation that which afterwards it may find through meditation, and may more fully know. And meditation receives from the foreknown light of revelation that whereby it may loose the knots of doubt. As we have already said above, by the king we understand free will, by Daniel devotion. And so to a king of this kind belongs that contemplation which lies subject to the will of the mind, when the spirit already has it at hand, and exercises it as it were out of its own power. So also that meditation 1302Aseems to belong to Daniel which, out of the zeal of devotion, a religious mind is wont to exercise about the circumspection of its own morals. The king thus designated therefore seeks, as it were, the interpretation of his own dreams from Daniel, and finds it, when the spirit afterwards, by meditating and by looking about, reads in its own morals the things which it descried amid the free flights of its own contemplation, and understands them more deeply. But let us now see how urgently Nabuchodonosor sought what he knew not.

CAPUT IV. Quomodo oporteat saecularia studia deponere, et ad spiritualium investigationem convertere.

Inter haec autem, quos capitis visiones et subsequentes cogitationes conturbant, discant regis Nabuchodonosor 1302Bexemplo quid facere debeant: Et per me, inquit, propositum est decretum, ut introducerentur in conspectu meo cuncti sapientes Babylonis, et ut solutionem somnii indicarent mihi (Dan. IV). Ecce quam diligenter, ecce quam instanter quaerit quod nescit, nisi prius desistit, donec somnii sui solutionem inveniat quam quaerit. Hinc est, quod Job de semetipso dicit: Causam quam nesciebam, diligenter investigabam (Job XXIX). Decretum proponere, est aliquid firmiter statuere, et quod omnino non liceat temerare. Vide ergo quam vehementer fieri voluit, super quo decretum proponit. Ad sapientes Babylonis pertinet sapientia hujus mundi, quae stultitia est apud Deum. Ad sapientes 1302CBabylonis pertinet prudentia carnis, quae inimica est Deo. Sapientes itaque Babylonis sunt peracuta scrutinia mundanae philosophiae. Sapientes Babylonis sunt peracuta consilia carnis prudentiae. Quid itaque erit ejusmodi sapientes introducere, et ad interiora revocare, nisi peracuta illa investigationis nostrae scrutinia, quae solebamus saecularibus negotiis impendere, ad spiritualia studia convertere? Quasi enim exterius sunt, quae sensu exteriori percepi possunt. Illa autem velut intus sunt qua: nisi interiori sensu omnino comprehendi non possunt. Intus ergo spiritualia, foris corporalia. Sapientes itaque Babylonis introducimus quando saecularia, et quaelibet exteriora studia ad spiritualem intelligentiam et spiritualium investigationem 1302Dconvertimus. Sapientes Babylonis introducere, est exteriorum curam postponere, et solis spiritualibus exercitiis studium impendere. Sapientes suos in intimis tenebat, qui dicebat: Cogitavi dies antiquos, et annos aeternos in mente habui. Meditatus sum nocte cum corde meo, et exercitabar, et scopebam spiritum meum (Psal. LXXVI).

Notandum autem quod sapientes nostri, modo intus, modo foris sunt, et tamen in conspectu nostro non sunt. Quasi in arbitrii nostri conspectu habetur, quod per intentionem, et ex deliberatione agitur. Saepe autem contingit ex multa studiorum nostrorum consuetudine animum ad inolita scrutinia incidenter recurrere, et antequam advertat quid intendat, multam ibi moram invectere, hoc est sapientes 1303Asuos ante conspectum suum non habere. Sed ille absque dubio sapientes suos ante conspectum suum sistere facit, qui studia sua ab omni evagatione restringit, et juxta deliberationis suae propositum solis utilibus, et necessariis impendit. Sapientes suos mens quasi in conspectu suo tenet, et observat, quando circa studiosas meditationes suas tanta cura invigilat, ut investigationis suae pedem nusquam divertere liceat, nisi quo eas ex deliberatione direxerat, intantum ut sagacitatis suae industriam, nulla perscrutationum suarum evagatio vel ad modicum praeterfugere queat.

CHAPTER IV. How we ought to lay aside secular pursuits, and to turn them to the investigation of spiritual things.

But amid these things, let those whom the visions of the head and the thoughts that follow trouble learn from the example of king Nabuchodonosor 1302Bwhat they ought to do: And by me, he says, a decree was set forth, that all the wise men of Babylon should be brought in before me, and that they should show me the solution of the dream (Dan. IV). Behold how diligently, behold how urgently he seeks what he knows not, unless [nisi; read nec, "nor does he first desist"] he first desists, until he finds the solution of his dream which he seeks. Hence it is that Job says of himself: The cause which I knew not, I diligently investigated (Job XXIX). [Vulg. diligentissime, "most diligently"] To set forth a decree is to establish something firmly, and such as it may in no wise be lawful to violate. See therefore how vehemently he willed that to be done concerning which he sets forth a decree. To the wise men of Babylon belongs the wisdom of this world, which is foolishness with God. To the wise men 1302Cof Babylon belongs the prudence of the flesh, which is an enemy to God. And so the wise men of Babylon are the exceedingly sharp searchings of worldly philosophy. The wise men of Babylon are the exceedingly sharp counsels of the prudence of the flesh. What therefore will it be to bring in wise men of this kind, and to call them back to the inner parts, but to turn to spiritual pursuits those exceedingly sharp searchings of our investigation which we were wont to bestow upon secular business? For those things are as it were outside which can be perceived by the outward sense. But those things are as it were within which cannot at all be comprehended save by the inward sense. Within, therefore, are spiritual things; without, bodily things. And so we bring in the wise men of Babylon when we turn secular pursuits, and all outward pursuits whatsoever, to spiritual understanding and to the investigation 1302Dof spiritual things. To bring in the wise men of Babylon is to postpone the care of outward things, and to bestow zeal upon spiritual exercises alone. He was keeping his own wise men in the inmost parts who said: I thought upon the days of old, and I had the eternal years in mind. I meditated in the night with my own heart, and I was exercised, and I swept my spirit (Psal. LXXVI).

But it is to be noted that our wise men are now within, now without, and yet are not in our sight. That is held as it were in the sight of our will which is done through intention and out of deliberation. But it often befalls, out of the great accustomedness of our pursuits, that the spirit runs back incidentally to its ingrained searchings, and, before it notices what it intends, weaves in invectere much delay there; that is, not to have its own wise men 1303Abefore its own sight. But he without doubt makes his own wise men stand before his own sight who restrains his own pursuits from all wandering, and, according to the purpose of his own deliberation, bestows them upon useful and necessary things alone. The mind holds and watches its own wise men as it were in its own sight, when it keeps vigil with so great care over its own zealous meditations that it may nowhere be allowed to turn aside the foot of its investigation, save whither it had directed them out of deliberation, insomuch that no wandering of its own searchings can escape past the effort of its own shrewdness even for a little.

CAPUT V. Quomodo insistere oporteat, qui ad divinorum mysteriorum enodationem anhelat.

1303BSed videamus modo cur sapientes suos in unum cogat, vel ad interiora compellat, ut solutionem, inquit, somnii indicarent mihi. Sicut in alterius visionis expositione jam diximus, aliud est divina judicia oculo contemplationis, et quasi per somnium cornere, et aliud eorumdem rationem perpendere, et qua aequitate, vel utilitate proveniant discretionis suae vivacitate dijudicare. Illud ad somnii visionem, istud pertinet ad somnii interpretationem. Somnium ergo quodammodo videmus, quando occulta Dei judicia in contemplationem et admirationem adducimus. Somnii nostri interpretationem quaerimus, quando post contemplationis nostrae spectacula eorum, quae per mentis sublevationem vidimus rationem, 1303Cmodum et ordinem investigamus. Nonne ad somnii visionem pertinet stupenda illa, quae in Scripturis legimus divinorum judiciorum magnalia in considerationem adducere, et eorum admirationi diutius et diligentius inhaerere? Nonne ad somnii interpretationem pertinet mysticam eorum intelligentiam quaerere, et per expositionem explanare posse? Nonne ad hujusmodi somnia anhelabat, qui in oratione dicebat: Illumina oculos meos, et considerabo mirabilia de lege tua (Psal. CXVIII). Quam autem pro magno haberet in ejusmodi somniorum interpretationem divinitus gratiam accipere, potestis ex illa ejus sententia perpendere. Cum enim de Domino praemiserit: Qui annuntiat verbum suum Jacob, justitias et judicia sua Israel, statim adnectit: 1303DNon fecit taliter omni nationi, et judicia sua non manifestavit eis (Psal. CXLVII). Veluti somnium jam viderat quae ex contemplationis suae luce deprehenderat, quod universa aeque eveniant justo et impio, bono et malo, mundo et immundo, immolanti victimas et sacrificia contemnenti. Somnii interpretationem quaesierat, sed necdum invenerat, cum alio loco dicebat: Ecce hoc inveni, dixit Ecclesiastes, unum et alterum, ut invenirem rationem, quam adhuc quaerit anima mea, et non inveni (Eccle. VII). Oportet ergo sapientes suos in unum congregare, et ad interiora revocare, qui cupit ad ejusmodi, quae jam determinavimus somniorum interpretationem gratiam obtinere. Tunc ingrediebantur arioli, magi, Chaldaei et aruspices. Tunc, inquit, 1304Aingrediebantur. Quid est tunc, nisi post decretum, post deliberationis propositum? Multi enim saepe multa proponunt, minime tamen faciunt, quia quam facile aliquid statuunt, tam facile rursum statuta rejiciunt.

Primo itaque observandum nobis est, ut utilia statuamus. Secundo autem, ut utiliter statuta firmiter teneamus. Attende quam firmiter statuit, quod optimus ille deliberator, optimum esse perpendit: Juravi, inquit, et statui custodire judicia justitiae tuae (Psal. CXXVIII). Audisti de voti institutione, audi et de voti exsecutione: Vota mea reddam in conspectu omnis populi ejus (Psal. CXV). Tunc ingrediebantur arioli, magi, Chaldaei et aruspices. Superius generaliter de Babylonis sapientibus proposuit, 1304Bhic quosdam specialiter expressit. Arioli, inquit, magi, Chaldaei et aruspices. Sed quia veraciter constat hujusmodi sapientes non vere sapientes esse, nusquam pene Scriptura sacra dicit eos sapientes nisi cum determinatione, sed modo Babylonis, modo dicit eos rex sapientes regni sui, vel aliquid hujusmodi. Si igitur per ariolos, magos, Chaldaeos et aruspices, studia prava, studia frivola, inepta et vana intelligimus, vide quam viriliter, vide quam salubriter ejusmodi studia ad spiritualia exercitia convertimus. Sapientes itaque Babylonis ad interiora compellimus, cum curam et sollicitudinem quam rebus supervacuis impendere solebamus, solis spiritualibus impendimus. Sapientes Babylonis velut 1304Cin somniorum interpretatione occupamus, quando studiis supervacuis omnino sepositis, in divinorum mysteriorum, seu judiciorum enodatione singulariter insistimus.

CHAPTER V. How he ought to press on who pants after the unravelling of the divine mysteries.

1303BBut let us now see why he gathers his own wise men into one, or drives them to the inner parts: that they should show me, he says, the solution of the dream. As we have already said in the exposition of the other vision, it is one thing to discern cornere the divine judgments with the eye of contemplation and as it were through a dream, and another to weigh the reason of the same, and by the liveliness of one's own discretion to judge with what equity or with what usefulness they come to pass. That belongs to the seeing of the dream, this to the interpretation of the dream. We therefore see a dream in a certain manner, when we bring the hidden judgments of God into contemplation and admiration. We seek the interpretation of our dream when, after the sights of our contemplation, we investigate the reason, 1303Cthe manner and the order of those things which we have seen through the lifting up of the mind. Does it not belong to the seeing of the dream to bring into consideration those astonishing great works of the divine judgments which we read in the Scriptures, and to cleave longer and more diligently to the admiration of them? Does it not belong to the interpretation of the dream to seek their mystical understanding, and to be able to explain it through exposition? Was he not panting after dreams of this kind who said in prayer: Enlighten mine eyes, and I will consider the wondrous things of thy law (Psal. CXVIII). [Vulg. Ps. CXVIII:18 Revela oculos meos, "Uncover mine eyes"; this work prints Revela at 1253A and at 1262C] But how great a thing he would count it to receive grace divinely for the interpretation of dreams of this kind, you can weigh from that saying of his. For when he has premised concerning the Lord: Who declareth his word unto Jacob, his justices and his judgments unto Israel, he at once subjoins: 1303DHe hath not done in like manner to every nation, and his judgments he hath not made manifest unto them (Psal. CXLVII). He had already seen as a dream the things which he had caught out of the light of his own contemplation: that all things happen alike to the just and to the wicked, to the good and to the evil, to the clean and to the unclean, to him that immolates victims and to him that despises sacrifices. He had sought the interpretation of the dream, but had not yet found it, when in another place he said: Behold this I have found, said Ecclesiastes, one and another, that I might find out the reason which my soul still seeketh, and I have not found it (Eccle. VII). He must therefore gather his own wise men into one, and call them back to the inner parts, who desires to obtain grace for the interpretation of dreams of that kind which we have already determined. Then came in the soothsayers, the magicians, the Chaldeans and the diviners. Then, he says, 1304Athey came in. What is then, but after the decree, after the purpose of deliberation? For many often set forth many things, yet by no means do them, because as easily as they establish something, so easily do they again reject what was established.

First therefore it is to be observed by us that we establish useful things. But secondly, that we firmly hold what has been usefully established. Mark how firmly he established that which that best deliberator weighed to be best: I have sworn, he says, and I have determined to keep the judgments of thy justice (Psal. CXXVIII). Thou hast heard of the establishing of the vow; hear also of the performing of the vow: I will render my vows in the sight of all his people (Psal. CXV). [Vulg. Ps. CXV:14 Vota mea Domino reddam coram omni populo ejus, "I will pay my vows to the Lord before all his people"] Then came in the soothsayers, the magicians, the Chaldeans and the diviners. Above he set forth generally concerning the wise men of Babylon; 1304Bhere he expressed certain of them specially. The soothsayers, he says, the magicians, the Chaldeans and the diviners. But because it truly stands that wise men of this kind are not truly wise, Holy Scripture almost nowhere calls them wise save with a determination, but now of Babylon, now the king calls them the wise men of his own kingdom, or something of this sort. If therefore by the soothsayers, the magicians, the Chaldeans and the diviners we understand depraved pursuits, frivolous, foolish and empty pursuits, see how manfully, see how wholesomely we turn pursuits of this kind to spiritual exercises. And so we drive the wise men of Babylon to the inner parts, when the care and the solicitude which we were wont to bestow upon superfluous things we bestow upon spiritual things alone. We occupy the wise men of Babylon as it were 1304Cin the interpretation of dreams, when, superfluous pursuits being wholly set aside, we press singularly upon the unravelling of the divine mysteries or judgments.

CAPUT VI. Quam sit inane mysteriorum profunda rimari, sine magisterio Spiritus sancti.

Sed sapientibus introductis, adhuc rex addit, et dicit: Et somnium narravi in conspectu eorum, et solutionem ejus non indicaverunt mihi (Dan. IV). Quid, quaeso, est somnia nostra in conspectu sapientum nostrorum narrare, nisi post contemplationem contemplationis nostrae spectacula ad memoriam revocare, sagaci meditatione retractare, studiosis cogitationibus discutere, et ex earum rerum collatione quas novimus 1304Dquaestionum nostrarum nodos dissolvere, et juxta assuetum perscrutationum nostrarum modum spiritualium rerum investigationem velle formare? Nonne in conspectu sapientum nostrorum somnia nostra narramus et solutionem quaerimus, quando ea quae de humanis scimus, et ea quae de divinis scire cupimus, mutua collatione conferimus, quando juxta doctum traditarum disciplinarum modum et ordinem divinorum mysteriorum, vel judiciorum perplexa discutere et penetrare praesumimus? Sed quidquid in divinis secretis de humana industria praesumitur, frustra attentatur, et quandiu in hunc modum quaeritur, in vanum laboratur. Juxta quod de hoc somnio legitur, et solutionem ejus non indicaverunt mihi. Somniorum sane divinitus ostensorum 1305Asolutionem sapientes nostri minime inveniunt, quia scientiarum nostrarum disciplinae, vel quaelibet traditiones humanae, ad divinorum secretorum discussionem et investigationem sufficere non possunt. Quis enim altitudinem illam consilii divini de suo sensu rimari praesumat, cum Domini prophetam clamantem et reclamantem audiat: Quis cognovit sensum Domini, aut quis consiliarius ejus fuit? (Isai. XL). Quis profunditatem illam mysteriorum, et Scripturarum divinarum de sua penetrare sagacitate confidat, cum Petrum apostolum legat, quomodo pariter omnes ab ejusmodi praesumptione compescat? Hoc, inquit, primum intelligentes, quia prophetia Scripturae propria interpretatione non fit (II Petr. I). Absque dubio nunquam proprie Scripturarum 1305Bsacrarum interpretatio fit, sine ejus magisterio, qui eas inspiravit.

CHAPTER VI. How empty a thing it is to search into the deep things of the mysteries without the mastery of the Holy Spirit.

But when the wise men had been brought in, the king still adds and says: And I told the dream in their sight, and they showed me not the solution thereof (Dan. IV). What, I ask, is it to tell our dreams in the sight of our wise men, but, after contemplation, to recall to memory the sights of our contemplation, to go over them with shrewd meditation, to discuss them with zealous thoughts, and out of the comparison of those things which we know 1304Dto loose the knots of our questions, and to wish to shape the investigation of spiritual things according to the accustomed manner of our searchings? Do we not tell our dreams in the sight of our wise men and seek the solution, when we bring together in a mutual comparison the things which we know of human matters and the things which we desire to know of divine ones, when, according to the learned manner and order of the disciplines handed down, we presume to discuss and to penetrate the perplexed things of the divine mysteries or judgments? But whatsoever in the divine secrets is presumed out of human effort is attempted in vain, and so long as it is sought in this manner, the labour is spent for nothing. According to that which is read of this dream: and they showed me not the solution thereof. Assuredly our wise men by no means find the solution 1305Aof dreams divinely shown, because the disciplines of our learning, or any human traditions whatsoever, cannot suffice for the discussion and the investigation of the divine secrets. For who would presume to search out that height of the divine counsel out of his own sense, when he hears the Lord's prophet crying and crying again: Who hath known the sense of the Lord, or who hath been his counsellor? (Isai. XL). [Vulg. Isa. XL:13 opens Quis adjuvit spiritum Domini, "Who hath helped the spirit of the Lord"; the wording printed here is that of Rom. XI:34, and this work prints the same verse with Quis novit at 1296D] Who would trust to penetrate that depth of the mysteries and of the divine Scriptures out of his own shrewdness, when he reads the apostle Peter, how he equally restrains all men from presumption of this kind? This, he says, first understanding, that prophecy of Scripture is not made by private interpretation (II Petr. I). [Vulg. quod omnis prophetia Scripturae, "that no prophecy of Scripture"] Without doubt the interpretation of the holy Scriptures 1305Bis never properly made without the mastery of him who inspired them.

CAPUT VII. Quod ad mysteriorum enodationem melius proficimus devotione, quam investigatione, et quod verae devotionis sit omnia discutere, ut possit omnia dijudicare.

Si itaque ad mysticam divinarum Scripturarum intelligentiam, si ad divinorum judiciorum scientiam erudiri volumus, melius in hoc ipsum orando quam investigando proficimus, altius devota compunctione quam profunda perscrutatione illuminamur. Unde est quod ad ea, quae praemisimus, statim subnectitur: Donec collega ingressus est in conspectu meo Daniel, cui nomen Balthassar secundum 1305Cnomen Dei mei, qui habet spiritum sanctorum in semetipso (Dan. IV). Ecce spiritum deorum sanctorum habere dicitur, qui ad somnii interpretationem idoneus invenitur. In eo sane, quod dii isti, dii sancti dicuntur, illi profecto excluduntur, de quibus in psalmo canitur: Omnes dii gentium, daemonia (Psal. CXIII). Sed profecto qui hoc loco designantur. Spiritum sanctum habere creduntur; quia, si Spiritum sanctum non haberent, dii sancti non essent. De horum numero deorum esse deprehenditur, ad quem voce Dominica dicitur: Ecce constitui te deum Pharaonis (Exod. VII). Ejusmodi itaque deos Psalmista expressit, cum alio loco dixit: Ego dixi: Dii estis, et filii Excelsi omnes (Psal. LXXXI). Ejusmodi 1305Ddeorum spiritum omnis sanctus accipit, ut sanctus esse possit. Omnis enim nostra justificatio, omnis nostra sanctificatio, absque ulla dubitatione est a spiritu isto. Sine hoc spiritu nescimus quod oremus, quomodo oportet, ipsum enim esse, docente Apostolo, didicimus qui interpellat pro nobis gemitibus inenarrabilibus (Rom. VIII). Ut ergo devotio nostra possit vera esse, oportet hunc spiritum habere. Si igitur per Danielem vera devotio intelligitur, recte de eo per Nabuchodonosor dicitur: Qui habet spiritum deorum sanctorum in semetipso. Sed cum superius expresserit quosdam sapientes, et quales veraciter constat, vere sapientes non esse, mirum, quomodo dicatur Daniel hoc loco eorum collega esse. Nisi enim cum eis aliquid commune habeat, 1306Anescio quomodo eorum collega existat. Illud autem scio quod spiritus ille quem Daniel secundum Scripturae testimonium acceperat, spirituales efficiat. Spiritualis autem dijudicat omnia, et ipse a nemine judicatur. Spiritus enim solet omnia scrutari, etiam profunda Dei. Si spiritualis dijudicat omnia, si spiritus scrutatur omnia, ergo eadem ipsa quae mundana philosophia; ergo eadem ipsa quae carnalis prudentia. Si omnia, ergo et ipsa quae quaelibet perversa doctrina. Solet namque qui spiritualis est omnia discutere, ut possit omnia judicare. Audi denique quomodo spiritualis ille omnia in sapientia sua tentaverit, ut omnia dijudicare possit: Dedi, inquit, cor meum, ut scirem prudentiam atque doctrinam, erroresque et stultitiam (Eccle. I). Vides 1306Bquomodo iste rimatur, quomodo scrutatur, non solum bonos mores, verum etiam errores, non solum prudentiam, verum etiam stultitiam. Verae itaque devotionis est tam bona quam mala in contemplationem adducere, omnia investigare, omnia discutere, ut subsequenter et possit omnia dijudicare. Habet itaque vera devotio ex investigatione et perscrutatione cum Babylonis sapientibus aliquid commune, ut merito dicatur ex eo eorum collega esse. Differunt tamen ex intentione. Nam vera devotio doctrinas vanas atque perversas non ad hoc investigat, ut eis inhaereat, vel in eis ullam fiduciam ponat, sed ut eas dijudicando convincat, confutet atque condemnet. Daniel itaque noster, et mundi sapientes per investigationem eadem via currunt, quamvis 1306Cper intentionem ad eumdem exitum non pertingunt. Recte ergo Daniel eorum collega dicitur, quibus in studiorum suorum discursibus, nonnulla ex parte communicare videtur.

CHAPTER VII. That toward the unravelling of the mysteries we advance better by devotion than by investigation, and that it belongs to true devotion to discuss all things, that it may be able to judge all things.

If therefore we wish to be instructed unto the mystical understanding of the divine Scriptures, unto the learning of the divine judgments, we advance better toward this same thing by praying than by investigating, and we are illuminated more deeply by devout compunction than by profound searching. Whence it is that to those things which we have premised there is at once subjoined: Until my colleague Daniel came in before me, whose name is Balthassar according to 1305Cthe name of my God, who hath the spirit of the holy ones in himself (Dan. IV). [Vulg. Dan. IV:5 has deorum before sanctorum, "the spirit of the holy gods"; Richard restores the word in both of his own re-quotations below] Behold, he is said to have the spirit of the holy gods who is found fit for the interpretation of the dream. In this indeed, that these gods are called holy gods, those are assuredly excluded of whom it is sung in the psalm: All the gods of the Gentiles are devils (Psal. CXIII). But assuredly they who are designated in this place. They are believed to have the Holy Spirit; because, if they had not the Holy Spirit, they would not be holy gods. He is perceived to be of the number of these gods to whom by the Lord's voice it is said: Behold I have appointed thee the god of Pharao (Exod. VII). And so the Psalmist expressed gods of this kind, when in another place he said: I have said: You are gods, and all of you sons of the Most High (Psal. LXXXI). Every 1305Dholy man receives the spirit of gods of this kind, that he may be able to be holy. For all our justification, all our sanctification, is without any doubt from that spirit. Without this spirit we know not what we should pray, as we ought; for we have learned, by the Apostle's teaching, that it is he who maketh intercession for us with unspeakable groanings (Rom. VIII). [Vulg. Rom. VIII:26 reads postulat, "asketh"; interpellat, "maketh intercession", is the verb of Rom. VIII:34] That our devotion therefore may be able to be true, it must have this spirit. If therefore by Daniel true devotion is understood, rightly is it said of him through Nabuchodonosor: Who hath the spirit of the holy gods in himself. But since he expressed above certain wise men, and such as it truly stands are not truly wise, it is a wonder how Daniel is in this place said to be their colleague. For unless he has something in common with them, 1306AI know not how he should stand as their colleague. But this I know, that that spirit which Daniel, according to the testimony of Scripture, had received, makes men spiritual. But the spiritual man judgeth all things, and he himself is judged of no man. For the Spirit is wont to search all things, even the deep things of God. If the spiritual man judgeth all things, if the spirit searcheth all things, then those same things also which worldly philosophy searches; then those same things also which carnal prudence searches. If all things, then those also which any perverse doctrine whatsoever searches. For he who is spiritual is wont to discuss all things, that he may be able to judge all things. Hear finally how that spiritual man tried all things in his own wisdom, that he might be able to judge all things: I gave, he says, my heart, that I might know prudence and doctrine, and errors and folly (Eccle. I). Thou seest 1306Bhow this man searches out, how he searches, not only good morals, but also errors, not only prudence, but also folly. And so it belongs to true devotion to bring both good things and evil into contemplation, to investigate all things, to discuss all things, that thereafter it may also be able to judge all things. And so true devotion has, out of investigation and searching, something in common with the wise men of Babylon, so that it is deservedly said on that account to be their colleague. Yet they differ in intention. For true devotion investigates empty and perverse doctrines not for this, that it may cleave to them or place any confidence in them, but that by judging it may convict, confute and condemn them. And so our Daniel and the wise men of the world run by the same way through investigation, although 1306Cthrough intention they do not reach the same issue. Rightly therefore is Daniel called their colleague, with whom in the courses of his own pursuits he seems in some part to share.

CAPUT VIII. Quibus devotionis studiis insistere debeat, qui occultorum revelationem divinitus exspectat.

Donec collega ingressus est in conspectu meo Daniel. Perpendis ex his, ut arbitror, quo Daniel iste modo ingreditur de exterioribus ad interiora, modo egreditur de interioribus ad exteriora. Exterius est, quando devotio nostra meditando, dijudicando, orando, sollicita est de rebus exterioribus. Interius est, quando eisdem exercitiis vel 1306Dstudiis occupata est, et sollicita pro rebus interioribus. Necesse enim habet providere bona coram Deo, et coram hominibus.
