Coram hominibus in sermone et opere, coram Deo in cogitatione et voluntate. Facta itaque et dicta nostra ad judicium revocare, et pro his Domino supplicare, hoc est Danielem exterius esse. Cogitationes, affectiones, voluntates, intentiones alta perscrutatione rimari, et pro ejusmodi in oratione compungi, hoc est Danielem intus morari. Vel hoc est Danielem exterius esse, tam interiora quam exteriora nostra secundum humanum judicium pensare, et hoc est Danielem interius esse, tam ista quam illa, secundum judicium divinum aestimare. Quando inter bona etiam opera quae agimus, quando inter utilia 1307Aquae dicimus, subtili examinatione pensamus, quid de nobis homines sentirent, quid sentire deberent, si nostras intentiones, si sordidas cogitationes, si pravas affectiones viderent; quando, inquam, hoc agimus, quasi Danielem exterius tenemus. Quando autem studiosa discussione quaeritur, qualiter opera, vel dicta nostra in conspectu Dei judicentur, etiam tunc cum secundum judicium humanum bona videntur, quando hujusmodi studio, cum magna devotione insistitur, Daniel noster intus invenitur. Quid itaque est Danielem ad interiora intrare, nisi ex intima devotione in his quae discutienda sunt, vel quaerenda tam de exterioribus quam de interioribus, ad divinum judicium recurrere? Et fit saepe, dum sapientes nostri in somniorum 1307Binterpretatione deficiunt, dum studiosae meditationes, dum intimae perscrutationes divinorum judiciorum, arcana divinorum mysteriorum profunda penetrare praesumunt, nec possunt; fit, inquam saepe ut mens post hujusmodi defectum, ex ipso suo defectu admonita, ad semetipsam redeat, infirmitatem suam diligentius attendat, et sine Dei adjutorio quod nihil possit plenius agnoscat. Incipit tunc ex infirmitatis suae consideratione gemere, suspirare, praesumptiones suas arguere, et de propriis studiis prius inaniter praesumpta jam de sola Domini revelatione sperare, et devotionis studio penitus insistere. Et hoc est Danielem post sapientes intrare.

CHAPTER VIII. With what pursuits of devotion he ought to press on who awaits from God the revelation of hidden things.

Until my colleague Daniel came in before me. Thou weighest from these things, as I judge, how this Daniel now enters from outward things to inward, now goes forth from inward things to outward. He is outside when our devotion, by meditating, by judging, by praying, is solicitous concerning outward things. He is within when it is occupied with the same exercises or 1306Dpursuits, and solicitous for inward things. For it must needs provide good things before God and before men. Before men in speech and in work, before God in thought and in will. And so to call our deeds and our words back to judgment, and to make supplication to the Lord for them, this is for Daniel to be outside. To search out thoughts, affections, wills, intentions with deep searching, and to be pricked with compunction in prayer for such things, this is for Daniel to abide within. Or this is for Daniel to be outside: to weigh both our inward and our outward things according to human judgment; and this is for Daniel to be within: to esteem both these and those according to the divine judgment. When, even amid the good works which we do, when amid the useful things 1307Awhich we say, we weigh with subtle examination what men would think of us, what they ought to think, if they saw our intentions, if they saw our sordid thoughts, if they saw our depraved affections; when, I say, we do this, we hold Daniel as it were outside. But when it is sought with zealous discussion how our works or our words are judged in the sight of God, even then when according to human judgment they seem good, when such a pursuit is pressed with great devotion, our Daniel is found within. What therefore is it for Daniel to enter to the inner parts, but out of inmost devotion, in those things which are to be discussed or to be sought concerning outward things as well as inward, to have recourse to the divine judgment? And it often comes to pass, while our wise men fail in the interpretation 1307Bof dreams, while zealous meditations, while inmost searchings presume to penetrate the hidden things of the divine judgments, the deep things of the divine mysteries, and cannot; it comes to pass, I say, often, that the mind, after a failure of this kind, admonished by that very failure of its own, returns to itself, attends more diligently to its own infirmity, and more fully acknowledges that without God's help it can do nothing. Then it begins, out of the consideration of its own infirmity, to groan, to sigh, to reprove its own presumptions, and now to hope from the Lord's revelation alone for the things it had before emptily presumed upon out of its own pursuits, and to press wholly upon the zeal of devotion. And this is for Daniel to enter after the wise men.

CAPUT IX. Quanta virtus verae devotionis sit, quae Filium Dei facit, quae animum humanum Deo conjungit.

  • 1307C

Donec collega, inquit, ingressus est in conspectu meo Daniel, cujus nomen Balthassar. Balthassar capillus capitis interpretatur. Recte ergo Daniel Balthassar dicitur, quia mens nostra ex vera devotione adornatur, ex vera devotione ad omnem profunditatem subtiliatur. Sed in hoc loco cum Daniel Balthassar dicitur, aliquid adhuc latere adjungitur, unde, ut arbitror, ad profundiorem intelligentiam provocamur. Daniel, inquit, cujus nomen Balthassar, secundum nomen Dei mei. Illud autem hoc loco praemonere jam volumus, quod multa Scripturarum loca ineptum aliquid vel perversum sonant juxta historiam, 1307Dquae tamen mystice discussa, rectum aliquid loquuntur juxta spiritalem intelligentiam. Nam quod Daniel a rege, secundum nomen Dei sui Balthassar dicitur, quid aliud quam idololatriam juxta litteram sonare videtur? Sed sicut beatus Gregorius dicit: « Saepe res quaelibet per historiam virtus est, per significationem culpa, sicut aliquando res gesta in facto causa damnationis est, in scripto autem, prophetia virtutis. » Nam cum de regis David culpa, dum de Uriae innocentia tractaret, dum per adulterantem David Christum, per Uriam innocentem populum Judaicum interpretari voluisset, quid, inquit, per effectum istum David scelestius, quid Uria mundius dici potest? Sed rursus per mysterium, quid David sacratius, quid Uria infidelius invenitur, quando et ille per 1308Avitae culpam prophetiae signat innocentiam, et iste per vitae innocentiam in prophetia exprimit culpam? Virtus namque sacri eloquii sic transacta narrat, ut ventura exprimat. Sic in facto rem approbat, ut ei in mysterio contradicat. Sic gesta damnat, ut haec mystice gerenda persuadeat. Sic et hoc loco sane, quod juxta historiam est reprehensibile, juxta mysterium potest commendabile aliquid exprimere. Cui nomen, inquit, Balthassar secundum nomen Dei mei. Si per regiam personam, regium recte intelligimus animum, quem, quaeso, dicit Deum suum nisi Christum? Nam, quamvis Pater, et Filius, et Spiritus sanctus veraciter sit unus idemque Deus, Christum tamen, secundum quemdam dicendi modum, recte religiosus quisque suum esse dicit, cujus nomine 1308Binsignitum, cujus gratia praeventum se meminit, cujus denique personae agnoscit familiarius inhaerere per Incarnationis mysterium ex communione naturae. Si vero Balthassar capillus capitis interpretatur, videamus modo quomodo hoc nomen Christo congruere videatur, vel quomodo secundum hoc Dei sui nomen, mysticus Daniel merito nuncupetur. Scimus autem, quia caput viri Christus, caput vero Christi, Deus. Scimus absque dubio quia Trinitas Deus, principium est omnium creaturarum; scimus nihilominus quia persona Patris principium est aliarum in Trinitate personarum. Et quamvis secundum alium et alium modum dicatur principium hic et ibi, utrobique tamen tam congruenter 1308Cquam veraciter potest principium dici. Quia igitur persona Patris, principium omnium est, jure ex capite designari potest. Si igitur Dei digitus, per similitudinem dicitur Spiritus sanctus, cur per similitudinem, similiter Christus non dicatur hujus capitis capillus? Capillus de capite oritur, et Christus de Patre generatur. Dominus dixit ad me: Filius meus es tu, ego hodie genui te (Hebr. I). Caput capillo ornatur, et Pater per Christum clarificatur: Pater, inquit, clarifica Filium tuum, ut Filius tuus clarificet te (Joan. XVII). Vides certe, quam juste Christus, Balthassar, hoc est capillus capitis, dicitur, quem verum Dei Patris Filium catholica fides veraciter confitetur. Nonne et nos secundum hoc nomen accipimus, quando a Christo, 1308Det per Christum obtinemus, ut merito filii Dei dici debeamus? Fratres, ait Joannes apostolus, filii Dei sumus, et nondum apparet quid erimus (I Joan. III). Hinc est quod ei qui voluntarie genuit nos verbo virtutis suae in quotidiana oratione dicimus: Pater noster qui es in coelis (Matth. VI). Si igitur Christus convenienter satis Balthassar dici potest, quia summi Patris Filius est, cur non et verus Christianus secundum dictam nominis rationem Balthassar dicatur, qui secundum adoptionis gratiam inter ejusdem Dei Patris filios deputatur? Non tamen omnis Christianus hoc nomine est, vel honore dignus. Sola devotio est quae eos discernit, sola devotio filios Dei facit. Si itaque devotio in Daniele intelligitur, vide quam recte Balthassar per effectum dicitur. Per quem 1309Aquilibet in filiorum libertatem adoptatur. Quid autem aliud est devotio, nisi fervida mentis in Deum dilectio? Charitas autem Dei diffusa est in cordibus nostris per Spiritum sanctum, qui datus est nobis (Rom. V). Recte ergo devotio capillus capitis dicitur, quae quasi de summo omnium capite profluere videtur, dum de summi Patris inspiratione procreatur. Recte Balthassar dicitur, qui in modum capilli summo omnium capiti inhaerere videtur. Perpendis, ut arbitror, ex his quanta sit virtus verae devotionis, quae filium Dei facit, quae animum humanum Deo conjungit, et cum eo quasi unum spiritum efficit. Qui enim adhaeret Deo, unus spiritus est (I Cor. VI).

CHAPTER IX. How great the virtue of true devotion is, which makes a Son of God, which joins the human spirit to God.

  • 1307C

Until my colleague, he says, Daniel came in before me, whose name is Balthassar. Balthassar is interpreted hair of the head. Rightly therefore is Daniel called Balthassar, because our mind is adorned out of true devotion, and out of true devotion is made subtle unto every depth. But in this place, when Daniel is called Balthassar, something is added that still lies hidden, whereby, as I judge, we are called forth to a deeper understanding. Daniel, he says, whose name is Balthassar, according to the name of my God. But this we now wish to forewarn in this place, that many places of the Scriptures sound something foolish or perverse according to the history, 1307Dwhich nevertheless, when mystically discussed, speak something right according to the spiritual understanding. For that Daniel is called Balthassar by the king according to the name of his god, what else does it seem to sound, according to the letter, than idolatry? But as blessed Gregory says: « Often any thing whatsoever is, through the history, a virtue, and through the signification a fault, just as sometimes a deed done is, in the fact, a cause of damnation, but in the writing, a prophecy of virtue. » For when he was treating of king David's fault, while he was treating of Urias's innocence, when he had wished to interpret Christ by the adulterous David, and by the innocent Urias the Jewish people: what, he says, through this effect can be said more wicked than David, what more clean than Urias? But again, through the mystery, what is found more sacred than David, what more faithless than Urias, when the one by 1308Athe fault of his life signifies the innocence of prophecy, and the other by the innocence of his life expresses in prophecy a fault? For the virtue of the sacred discourse so narrates things past that it may express things to come. So it approves a thing in the fact, that it may contradict it in the mystery. So it condemns things done, that it may persuade that these same things are to be done mystically. So also in this place assuredly, that which according to the history is reprehensible can, according to the mystery, express something commendable. Whose name, he says, is Balthassar according to the name of my God. If by the royal person we rightly understand the royal spirit, whom, I ask, does he call his God but Christ? For although the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit is truly one and the same God, yet every religious man rightly says, according to a certain manner of speaking, that Christ is his own: by whose name 1308Bhe remembers that he was marked, by whose grace he was prevented, and to whose person, finally, he acknowledges that he cleaves more familiarly through the mystery of the Incarnation, out of the communion of nature. But if Balthassar is interpreted hair of the head, let us now see how this name may seem to agree with Christ, or how according to this name of his God the mystical Daniel is deservedly named. But we know that the head of the man is Christ, and the head of Christ, God. We know without doubt that God the Trinity is the beginning of all creatures; we know none the less that the person of the Father is the beginning of the other persons in the Trinity. And although it is called a beginning here and there according to one manner and another, yet in both places it can be called a beginning as fittingly 1308Cas truly. Since therefore the person of the Father is the beginning of all things, he can by right be designated by the head. If therefore the Holy Spirit is called by a likeness the finger of God, why should not Christ likewise be called by a likeness the hair of this head? The hair arises from the head, and Christ is begotten of the Father. The Lord said to me: Thou art my Son, this day have I begotten thee (Hebr. I). The head is adorned with hair, and the Father is glorified through Christ: Father, he says, glorify thy Son, that thy Son may glorify thee (Joan. XVII). Thou seest certainly how justly Christ is called Balthassar, that is, the hair of the head, whom the Catholic faith truly confesses to be the true Son of God the Father. Do not we also receive this name, when from Christ 1308Dand through Christ we obtain that we ought deservedly to be called sons of God? Brethren, says John the apostle, we are the sons of God, and it doth not yet appear what we shall be (I Joan. III). [Vulg. I John III:2 opens Carissimi, nunc, "Dearly beloved, now"; and it reads apparuit, "hath appeared", for the present apparet] Hence it is that to him who of his own will begot us by the word of his power we say in our daily prayer: Our Father who art in heaven (Matth. VI). If therefore Christ can fittingly enough be called Balthassar, because he is the Son of the highest Father, why should not the true Christian also be called Balthassar according to the said reason of the name, who according to the grace of adoption is reckoned among the sons of the same God the Father? Yet not every Christian is worthy of this name or of this honour. Devotion alone it is that distinguishes them; devotion alone makes sons of God. If therefore devotion is understood in Daniel, see how rightly he is called Balthassar in effect. Through whom 1309Aany man whatsoever is adopted into the liberty of the sons. But what else is devotion, but the fervent love of the mind toward God? But the charity of God is poured forth in our hearts by the Holy Spirit, who is given to us (Rom. V). Rightly therefore is devotion called the hair of the head, which seems as it were to flow forth from the highest head of all, while it is begotten out of the inspiration of the highest Father. Rightly is he called Balthassar who after the manner of hair seems to cleave to the highest head of all. Thou weighest, as I judge, from these things how great is the virtue of true devotion, which makes a son of God, which joins the human spirit to God, and makes it with him as it were one spirit. He who indeed cleaveth to God, is one spirit (I Cor. VI). [Vulg. I Cor. VI:17 reads Domino, "to the Lord", where the plate prints Deo]

CAPUT X Quod maxime sub tempore orandi quibusdam solet intellectualis sensus aperiri.

  • 1309B

Ingresso Daniele, rex coram ipso somnium loquitur, sicut verbis subsequentibus ostenditur: Et somnium, inquit, coram ipso locutus sum (Dan. IV). Somnium ejusmodi coram Daniele loqui, est mysteriorum quae divinitus erudiri cupimus, sub tempore orandi memoria tangi, et inter orandum divinae revelationis desiderio affici. Nam quemadmodum experti recognoscunt illi qui frequentibus relevationibus divinitus relevari laetificarique meruerunt, sub tempore orandi maxime solent illuminantis gratiae lumen accipere, et ex ejus inspiratione mysteriorum profunda penetrare. Ex intima namque compunctione 1309Canimus lavatur, mundatur, et procul dubio quanto purior redditur, tanto facilius, tanto profundius illuminatur. Hinc est, quod Balaam de semetipso testatur: Dixit auditor sermonum Dei, qui visionem omnipotentis Dei intuitus est, qui cadit, et sic aperientur oculi ejus (Num. XXIV). Quid est quod dicitur, quod cadendo oculi aperiuntur, nisi quod inter orandum saepe intellectualis sensus aperitur. Solemus nos sane in terram toto corpore saepe prosternere, cum volumus attentius orare. Quantum ergo ad expositionem litterae spectare videtur, oculi ei quasi cadendo aperiuntur, qui lumen revelantis gratiae accipit, dum se in orationem prosternit. Si autem eadem littera mystice discutitur, in eamdem sententiam concurrere videtur. Quid enim est cadere, 1309Dnisi semetipsum ex peccatorum suorum recordatione humiliare, et ex vera contritione cordis rigorem deponere? Sed animus saepe, dum semetipsum in hunc modum dejicit, in majori gratia resurgit, quia lumen oculorum suorum cadendo recuperat, quos male erectus clausos tenebat. Possumus tamen dicere, quod hoc sit absque dubio cadere, blandienti culpae consentire. Scimus autem quia nonnulli sunt, qui ex multitudine revelationum suarum se attollunt. Permittuntur autem qui ejusmodi sunt ex digno Dei judicio cadere, ut desinant superbire. Dum igitur in culpam labuntur, confunduntur, compunguntur, compuncti purgantur, purgati illuminantur. Sed si illuminati iterum superbiunt, superbiendo lumen quod receperant, amittunt. Illuminati 1310Aitaque et elati iterum atque iterum dejiciuntur, lapsi et humiliati iterum et iterum illuminantur. Hanc cadendi resurgendique vicissitudinem saepe alternant, et hos alternandi modos multi diu, multumque frequentant. Multi autem hanc alternandi consuetudinem pessimo tandem fine concludunt, de quibus constat quod spiritualium charismatum gratiam non tam ad suam quam ad aliorum utilitatem acceperunt. Horum absque dubio Balaam typum ex suis operibus gessit, horum proprietatem in suis verbis expressit, cum dicit: Dixit auditor sermonum Dei, qui visionem Omnipotentis videt, qui cadit, et sic aperiuntur oculi ejus.

CHAPTER X That most of all in the time of praying the intellectual sense is wont to be opened to certain men.

  • 1309B

Daniel having entered, the king speaks the dream before him, as is shown by the words that follow: And the dream, he says, I spoke before him (Dan. IV). To speak a dream of this kind before Daniel is, in the time of praying, to be touched by the memory of the mysteries concerning which we desire to be divinely instructed, and while praying to be affected with the desire of divine revelation. For as those recognize by experience who have deserved to be divinely relieved and gladdened by frequent relievings, in the time of praying most of all are they wont to receive the light of illuminating grace, and out of its inspiration to penetrate the deep things of the mysteries. For out of inmost compunction 1309Cthe spirit is washed and cleansed, and beyond doubt the purer it is rendered, the more easily, the more deeply is it illuminated. Hence it is that Balaam testifies of himself: He hath said, the hearer of the words of God, who hath beheld the vision of God almighty, who falleth, and so his eyes shall be opened (Num. XXIV). [Vulg. Num. XXIV:4 has no Dei after omnipotentis, and reads aperiuntur, "are opened", for the future aperientur] What is it that is said, that by falling the eyes are opened, but that while praying the intellectual sense is often opened. We are indeed wont often to prostrate ourselves upon the earth with the whole body, when we wish to pray more attentively. So far therefore as it seems to look to the exposition of the letter, his eyes are opened as it were by falling who receives the light of revealing grace while he prostrates himself in prayer. But if the same letter is mystically discussed, it seems to run together into the same meaning. For what is it to fall, 1309Dbut to humble oneself out of the remembrance of one's own sins, and out of true contrition to lay aside the rigidity of the heart? But the spirit often, while it casts itself down in this manner, rises again in a greater grace, because by falling it recovers the light of its own eyes, which, being ill upraised, it was holding closed. Yet we can say that this without doubt is to fall: to consent to a flattering fault. But we know that there are some who lift themselves up out of the multitude of their own revelations. But those who are of this kind are permitted, by the worthy judgment of God, to fall, that they may cease to be proud. While therefore they slip into fault, they are confounded, they are pricked with compunction; being pricked they are purged, being purged they are illuminated. But if, being illuminated, they are again proud, by being proud they lose the light which they had received. And so, being illuminated 1310Aand lifted up, they are cast down again and again; having fallen and been humbled, they are illuminated again and again. This vicissitude of falling and of rising again they often alternate, and many long and much frequent these manners of alternating. But many at length conclude this custom of alternating with a most evil end, of whom it stands that they received the grace of spiritual charisms not so much for their own advantage as for that of others. Of these without doubt Balaam bore the type by his own works, and expressed their property in his own words, when he says: He hath said, the hearer of the words of God, who seeth the vision of the Almighty, who falleth, and so his eyes are opened. [Vulg. Num. XXIV:4 has the perfect, "hath beheld", where this second printing prints the present videt, "seeth"; the printing at 1309C keeps the perfect]

CAPUT XI. Quod saeculares scientiae debeant spiritualibus studiis subservire, et verae devotionis lucris militare.

  • 1310B

Sed ut ad id redeamus unde digressi sumus, illud pro certo tenemus quod multi saepe sub orationis suae tempore lumen revelantis gratiae suscipiunt, et quod ad ejusmodi gratiam multi ex virtute devotionis proficiunt. Hinc est quod rex a Daniele somnii sui interpretationem cum omni fiducia exigit, cum dicit: Balthassar princeps ariolorum quem ego scio quod spiritum deorum sanctorum habeas in te, et omne sacramentum non est impossibile tibi. Visiones somniorum meorum quas vidi et solutionem eorum narra (Dan. IV). In quibus verbis illud primo notandum, quod dicitur hoc loco princeps ariolorum, qui superius dictus est collega eorum, nec eorum tantum, 1310Csed aliorum Babyloniae sapientum. Inter saeculares utique scientias quasdam scimus utiles et usui necessarias, quasdam vero (qualis ars ariolandi est) omnino perversas. Si igitur has quae absque dubio sunt perversae, potest vera devotio ad aliquem virtutis usum convertere, et in eis quemdam spiritalis militiae principatum coercere, quemadmodum hic ex arte ariolandi possumus colligere, quid tunc de his sentiendum est quae possunt esse utiles et bonae? Nam, ut caetera taceam, nonne tunc Daniel principatur, nonne quaelibet perversa scientia devotioni subservire cogitur, quando ex ejus consideratione humanus error plenius agnoscitur, quando ex ejus retractatione animus humiliatur, confunditur, compungitur, et ad devotionis 1310Dstudia instigatur? Nonne perversa quaelibet scientia devotioni subservit, quando animus a semetipso tempora exigit quae in hujusmodi studiis ineptis perdere consuevit, quando ex comparatione colligit quantum spiritualibus studiis invigilare debeat, qui tantis ineptiis quandoque se insudasse retractat? Sic itaque Daniel dictorum sapientum collega intelligatur, ut in eis quemdam spiritalis militiae principatum aequitatis jure tenere credatur. Debent ergo quaelibet saeculares scientiae spiritualibus studiis subservire, et nil quod verae devotionis lucris non militet in suis exercitiis praesumere. Quando ergo saecularibus scientiis importune insistimus, quando studiorum nostrorum importunitate devotionis nostrae exercitia interrumpimus, eum profecto qui principari 1311Adebet, Danielem videlicet, servire compellimus. Sed attendendum est quam sit malum bonis etiam scientiis abuti, et earum abusione meliora studia praepediri, quando ex devotionis officio perversa quaelibet scientia debet, et solet in bonis usus deflecti.

CHAPTER XI. That the secular kinds of learning ought to be subservient to spiritual studies, and to serve as soldiers for the gains of true devotion.

  • 1310B

But that we may return to that from which we digressed, we hold this for certain: that many often, in the time of their prayer, receive the light of revealing grace, and that many advance to grace of this kind out of the virtue of devotion. Hence it is that the king with all confidence demands of Daniel the interpretation of his dream, when he says: Balthassar, prince of the soothsayers, whom I know that thou hast the spirit of the holy gods in thee, and every sacrament is not impossible to thee. Tell the visions of my dreams which I saw, and the solution of them (Dan. IV). [Vulg. quoniam ego scio, "forasmuch as I know"; the relative printed here leaves the clause without a governing verb] In these words this first is to be noted, that he is here called prince of the soothsayers who above was called their colleague, and not theirs only, 1310Cbut of the other wise men of Babylon also. Among the secular kinds of learning we assuredly know some to be useful and necessary for use, but others (such as the art of soothsaying is) altogether perverse. If therefore true devotion can turn those which are without doubt perverse to some use of virtue, and can constrain within them a certain princedom of the spiritual soldiery, even as here we can gather from the art of soothsaying, what then is to be thought of those which can be useful and good? For, to say nothing of the rest, does not Daniel then hold the princedom, is not any perverse learning whatsoever compelled to be subservient to devotion, when from the consideration of it human error is more fully recognized, when from the going over of it again the spirit is humbled, confounded, pricked with compunction, and stirred up to the 1310Dzeals of devotion? Does not any perverse learning whatsoever serve devotion, when the spirit exacts of itself the times which it was wont to waste in useless studies of this kind, when from the comparison it gathers how much he ought to watch over spiritual studies who goes over again in his mind that he once sweated at follies so great? And so let Daniel be understood as the colleague of the said wise men in such wise, that he be believed to hold among them, by the right of equity, a certain princedom of the spiritual soldiery. All secular kinds of learning, therefore, ought to be subservient to spiritual studies, and to presume nothing in their exercises which does not serve as a soldier for the gains of true devotion. When therefore we press importunately upon the secular kinds of learning, when by the importunity of our studies we interrupt the exercises of our devotion, we assuredly compel him who ought to hold the princedom, 1311ADaniel namely, to serve. But it must be attended to how great an evil it is to abuse even good kinds of learning, and by the abuse of them to have better studies hindered, when out of the office of devotion any perverse learning whatsoever ought, and is wont, to be bent to good uses.

CAPUT XII. Quod sit utile summae devotioni summam curam impendere.

Jure autem devotio bonis studiis antefertur, jure vero malis principatur, jure Danieli inter omnes sapientes Babylonis primatus conceditur, si veraciter constat quod per Nabuchodonosor dicitur: Scio quod spiritum deorum sanctorum habeas in te, et omne sacramentum non est impossibile tibi. Nonne 1311Bhuic sententiae concordare videtur, si recte pensetur quod ex ore Veritatis auditur: Amen dico vobis, quidquid orantes petieritis, credite quia accipietis, et fiet vobis (Marc. VI). Vera autem et plena devotio non est quae attestationi Veritatis credere non potest. Si autem potest credere, potest petendo et credendo obtinere. Fit itaque summae devotioni nullum sacramentum impossibile, si obtineri potest ex fide. Recte ergo animi arbitrium ex virtute devotionis obtinere nititur, quod ei impossibile non esse Veritatis testimonio comprobatur. Visiones, inquit, somniorum meorum quas vidi, et solutiones eorum narra. Optimum ergo studii genus est summae devotioni summam curam impendere, eo quod nullum sacramentum juxta demonstratam rationem 1311Csit ei impossibile. Juxta regis itaque Nabuchodonosor exemplum, Danielem nostrum quibuslibet sapientibus nostris praeferamus, omnia studia, omnia exercitia nostra verae devotionis lucris subservire cogamus, et tunc procul dubio non frustra attentabimus quidquid de divinis mysteriis cum Dei adjutorio ex ejus officio utiliter praesumimus. Sed nunc visionis ordinem prosequamur, spiritalisque intelligentiae sensum, saltem secundum moralem aedificationem majoribus ingeniis majora relinquentes perscrutemur.

CHAPTER XII. That it is useful to bestow the highest care upon the highest devotion.

Now rightly is devotion set before good studies, rightly does it hold the princedom over evil ones, rightly is the primacy among all the wise men of Babylon granted to Daniel, if that stands truly which is said through Nabuchodonosor: I know that thou hast the spirit of the holy gods in thee, and every sacrament is not impossible to thee. Does not 1311Bthis seem to agree with that sentence, if it be rightly weighed, which is heard from the mouth of Truth: Amen I say to you, whatsoever you shall have asked in praying, believe that you shall receive, and it shall be done unto you (Marc. VI). [Vulg. Mark 11:24 Propterea dico vobis, omnia quaecumque orantes petitis … et evenient vobis, "Therefore I say to you, all things whatsoever you ask … and they shall come unto you"] But that devotion is not true and full which cannot believe the attestation of Truth. But if it can believe, it can by asking and believing obtain. And so to the highest devotion no sacrament becomes impossible, if it can be obtained out of faith. Rightly therefore does the judgment of the spirit strive to obtain, out of the virtue of devotion, that which is proved by the testimony of Truth not to be impossible to it. Tell, he says, the visions of my dreams which I saw, and the solutions of them. [Vulg. and the lemma at 1310B print the singular, solutionem] The best kind of zeal, therefore, is to bestow the highest care upon the highest devotion, in that no sacrament, according to the reason demonstrated, 1311Cis impossible to it. According to the example, then, of king Nabuchodonosor, let us set our Daniel before any wise men of ours whatsoever; let us compel all our studies, all our exercises, to be subservient to the gains of true devotion; and then beyond doubt we shall not attempt in vain whatsoever we usefully presume, with God's help, concerning the divine mysteries out of his office. But now let us pursue the order of the vision, and let us search out the sense of the spiritual understanding, at least according to moral edification, leaving greater things to greater wits.

CAPUT XIII. De contemplandi modo, vel qualitate, et quod aliter videntur res in suo esse, atque aliter in sola ratione, vel summa veritate.

1311DVisio, inquit, capitis mei in cubili meo. Videbam et ecce arbor in medio terrae et altitudo ejus nimia. Magna arbor, et fortis, et proceritas ejus contingens coelum, aspectus illius erat usque ad terminos universae terrae. Folia ejus pulcherrima, et fructus ejus nimius, et esca universorum in ea. Subter eam habitabant animalia et bestiae, et in ramis ejus conversabantur volucres coeli, et ex ea vescebatur omnis caro (Dan. IV). Ut ex superioribus pater, visio capitis est intelligentia mentis. Cubile autem tranquillitas cordis otiumque studiosae quietis. Visio capitis in cubili divinitus percipitur, quando inter studii sui secreta otia ad veritatis contemplationem mens humana divina aspiratione sublevatur. Videbam, inquit, et 1312Aecce arbor in medio terrae. Aliud est videre res ipsas in suo esse, atque aliud est eas videre in sola ratione, vel summa veritate. Aliud est eas videre cuique in propria essentia, et longe aliud videre eas in sola sapientia. Aliud est videre rerum substantias in suis generibus et speciebus, atque aliud videre singulas in suis individuis. Istud est sensus seu imaginationis, illud vero primum est rationis et contemplationis. Cum itaque mens ad universalem quamdam rerum considerationem rapitur, et ad universitatis pulchritudinem speculandam divinitus sublevatur, et in hujusmodi contemplatione contemplationisque dulcedine delectabiliter figitur, mirabiliterque reficitur, videt saepe in divino lumine quot, vel cujusmodi rerum genera oporteat esse, 1312Binconcussumque tenet nihil horum omnium quae summae dispositionis ordo, universitatisque pulchritudo exigit, penitus posse deesse. Verbi gratia, videt oportere esse bonos similiter et malos, et in bonis alios aliis meliores, alios optimos; sic et in malis, alios aliis pejores, alios pessimos. Videt nihilominus, quia oportet alios malos esse, et malos perseverare, alios malos esse, et malitiam in perpetuum deserere; alios autem ad tempus deserere, et iterum ad malitiam redire. Sic e diverso, videt alios bonos esse et bonos perseverare, alios irrecuperabiliter cadere, alios post lapsum ad bonitatem redire. De hoc sexto rerum genere esse videtur quem Nabuchodonosor in Dei lumine contemplatur qui ei per similitudinem ostenditur, quem etiam postquam 1312Cad seipsum rediit secum mirabatur, seu etiam miserebatur. Videbat universitatis pulchritudinem talem exigere, talemque omnino deesse non potuisse, et tamen nescivit seipsum talem esse, vel talis hominis imaginem in seipso gestare, qui de tanta sublimitate subito caderet, et in imis diu jaceret, tandem tamen ad pristinum statum rediret. Hoc est quod arbor magna prius in altum erigitur, postmodum abscinditur, et tamen radices ejus in terra reservantur unde iterum reparetur. Videamus ergo et nos, et discamus ab ipso qualis sit arbor quam ipse videt, vel quomodo cecidit, vel cadere potuit quae tanta vel talis fuit.

CHAPTER XIII. Of the manner, or the quality, of contemplating; and that things are seen otherwise in their own being, and otherwise in reason alone, or in the highest truth.

1311DThe vision, he says, of my head in my bed. I was seeing, and behold a tree in the midst of the earth, and the height thereof excessive. A great tree, and strong, and the tallness thereof reaching unto heaven; the sight of it was even unto the ends of all the earth. The leaves thereof most beautiful, and the fruit thereof excessive, and in it the food of all. Under it dwelt the animals and the beasts, and in the branches thereof abode the birds of heaven, and of it all flesh did eat (Dan. IV). As is plain from the things above, the vision of the head is the understanding of the mind. The bed, moreover, is the tranquillity of the heart and the leisure of a zealous quiet. The vision of the head in the bed is divinely perceived when, amid the secret leisures of its zeal, the human mind is lifted up by divine inbreathing to the contemplation of truth. I was seeing, he says, and 1312Abehold a tree in the midst of the earth. It is one thing to see the things themselves in their own being, and another to see them in reason alone, or in the highest truth. It is one thing to see each of them in its own proper essence, and far another to see them in wisdom alone. It is one thing to see the substances of things in their genera and species, and another to see them singly in their individuals. The latter belongs to sense or to imagination, but that first one belongs to reason and to contemplation. When therefore the mind is caught up to a certain universal consideration of things, and is divinely lifted up to behold the beauty of the universe, and is delightfully fixed and wonderfully refreshed in a contemplation of this kind and in the sweetness of contemplation, it often sees in the divine light how many and of what sort the kinds of things must needs be, 1312Band holds it unshaken that nothing of all those which the order of the highest disposition and the beauty of the universe require can be utterly lacking. For example, it sees that there must needs be good men and likewise evil, and among the good some better than others, and some best of all; and so among the evil, some worse than others, and some worst of all. It sees none the less that some must needs be evil and persevere in evil, others be evil and forsake malice for ever, and others forsake it for a time and return again to malice. So, conversely, it sees that some are good and persevere in good, that others fall irrecoverably, that others after a lapse return to goodness. Of this sixth kind of things he seems to be whom Nabuchodonosor contemplates in the light of God, who is shown to him by a likeness, at whom also, after 1312Che had returned to himself, he wondered within himself, or even had pity. He was seeing that the beauty of the universe required such a one, and that such a one could by no means be lacking; and yet he knew not that he himself was such a one, or that he bore in himself the image of such a man as should fall suddenly from so great a sublimity, and lie long in the lowest places, and yet at last return to his former state. This is what it is that the great tree is first raised up on high, is afterward cut away, and yet its roots are kept in the earth, whence it may be repaired again. Let us therefore see, we also, and let us learn from him what kind of tree it is that he sees, or how it fell, or how that could fall which was so great and of such a kind.

CAPUT XIV. De magnitudine gratiae mentis postmodum casurae, et in tanta plenitudine gratiae, quid soleat esse causa ruinae.

  • 1312D

Videbam, inquit, et ecce arbor magna in medio terrae et altitudo ejus nimia. Quid intelligemus per magnitudinem arboris, nisi immensum robur virtutis? Nonne et magna illa arbor fuit quae plenitudinem gratiae, et fortitudinis accepit? Stephanus, inquit, plenus gratia, et fortitudine (Act. VI). Ecce arbor magna in medio terrae, et altitudo ejus nimia. Ergo haec arbor magna fuit, alta fuit, et in medio terrae fuit. Ad multa et magna se dilatabat per virtutem, ad alta se erigebat per subtilitatem, et in intimis stabat per intentionem. Ad fortia se extendebat, ad summa se erigebat, et in intimis fixa manebat. 1313AIn magnis fuit per exercitium, in summis per studium, in intimis per desiderium. Quia ergo fortia agebat, magna arbor erat; quia intima bona desiderabat, in medio terrae erat; quia de sublimibus subtiliter sentiebat, altitudo ejus magna erat. Magna, inquit, arbor et fortis, et proceritas ejus contingens coelum. Ecce arbor magna, arbor robusta, arbor procera. Magna ad bona agenda, fortis ad mala toleranda, procera ad sublimia contemplanda. Magna in actionibus, fortis in tentationibus, sublimis in investigationibus. Attende quam alta erat cujus proceritas etiam coelum tangebat. Proceritas ejus coelum tangebat, quia investigationis ejus sublimitas coelestia penetrabat. Proceritas itaque ejus alta fuit, et altitudo ejus procera fuit. Vere magna altitudo 1313Bfuit quae se summis, et coelestibus immersit. Elige itaque in hujus consideratione arboris quod amplius mireris, magnitudinem, fortitudinem seu proceritatem. Magna, inquit, arbor et fortis, et proceritas ejus contingens coelum, Magna arbor fuit, quae veraciter dicere potuit: Ubique et in omnibus instructus sum. Scio humiliari, scio et abundare, et penuriam pati. Omnia possum in eo qui me confortat (Philipp. IV). Fortis arbor fuit quae fidenter dicere potuit: Non timebo quid faciat mihi homo. Si consistant adversum me castra, non timebit cor meum (Psal. LV). Si exsurgat adversum me praelium in hoc ego sperabo (Psal. XXVI). Prae caeteris arboribus procera fuit, quae dicere de se non dubitavit: Et praecessi sapientia omnes qui fuerunt ante me in Hierusalem, et 1313Cmens mea contemplata est multa sapienter et didicit (Eccle. I). Proceritas ejus coelum tangebat, qui veraciter dicere poterat: Sapientiam loquimur inter perfectos, sapientiam vero non hujus saeculi nec principum hujus saeculi qui destruuntur. Sed loquimur Dei sapientiam in mysterio, quae abscondita est, quam praedestinavit ante saecula, quam nemo principum hujus saeculi cognovit (II Cor. XXIX). Sed vide quisquis haec audis, vel legis, si forte altitudo tua est magna, vide, inquam, et diligenter attende ne forte sit et nimia. Hoc fortassis in hac descripta arbore reprehensibile fuit, hoc fortassis succisionis causa exstitit quod ejus altitudo nimia fuit. Et altitudo, inquit, ejus nimia. Forte Nabuchodonosor ambulabat in magnis et mirabilibus 1313Dsuper se, et hoc ei fortassis exstitit causa ruinae. Fortassis Nabuchodonosor alta sapiebat, et idcirco altitudo ejus nimia erat: Noli, inquit Apostolus, altum sapere, sed time (Rom. XI). Sicut enim qui mel multum comedit, non est ei bonum, sic qui scrutator est majestatis, opprimetur a gloria. Altiora igitur te ne quaesieris, ne forte proceritas tua sit alta nimis. Scrutator majestatis opprimitur a gloria, et sententiam succisionis meretur arbor cujus altitudo est nimia. Nonne ejusmodi arbores altae fuerunt, et juste succidi meruerunt, quae cum cognovissent Deum non sicut Deum, glorificaverunt, aut gratias egerunt: Sed evanuerunt in cogitationibus suis, et obscuratum est insipiens cor eorum (Rom. I).

CHAPTER XIV. Of the greatness of grace in a mind that is afterward to fall, and what is wont to be the cause of ruin amid so great a fullness of grace.

  • 1312D

I was seeing, he says, and behold a great tree in the midst of the earth, and the height thereof excessive. [Vulg. Dan. 4:7, and the lemma printed at 1311D, have ecce arbor in medio terrae, without "great"] What shall we understand by the greatness of the tree, but the immense strength of virtue? Was not that also a great tree which received the fullness of grace and of fortitude? Stephen, he says, full of grace and fortitude (Act. VI). Behold a great tree in the midst of the earth, and the height thereof excessive. This tree, therefore, was great, was high, and was in the midst of the earth. It spread itself out to many and great things by virtue, it raised itself to high things by subtlety, and it stood in the inmost things by intention. It stretched itself out to strong things, it raised itself to the highest things, and it remained fixed in the inmost things. 1313AIt was in great things by exercise, in the highest by zeal, in the inmost by desire. Because therefore it did strong things, it was a great tree; because it desired inmost goods, it was in the midst of the earth; because it thought subtly of sublime things, its height was great. A great tree, he says, and strong, and the tallness thereof reaching unto heaven. Behold a great tree, a robust tree, a tall tree. Great for doing good things, strong for enduring evil things, tall for contemplating sublime things. Great in actions, strong in temptations, sublime in investigations. Consider how high it was, whose tallness even touched heaven. Its tallness touched heaven, because the sublimity of its investigation penetrated the heavenly things. And so its tallness was high, and its height was tall. Truly it was a great height 1313Bwhich plunged itself into the highest and heavenly things. Choose therefore, in the consideration of this tree, what thou mayest wonder at the more: its greatness, its strength, or its tallness. A great tree, he says, and strong, and the tallness thereof reaching unto heaven, A great tree it was, which could truly say: Everywhere and in all things I am instructed. I know how to be humbled, I know also how to abound, and to suffer want. I can do all things in him who strengtheneth me (Philipp. IV). [Vulg. Phil. 4:12 Scio et humiliari, scio et abundare (ubique et in omnibus institutus sum) et satiari, et esurire, et abundare, et penuriam pati: the received text has institutus, "taught," for instructus, and sets the "everywhere and in all things" clause after the two verbs, not before them] A strong tree it was, which could confidently say: I will not fear what man can do unto me. If armies in camp should stand together against me, my heart shall not fear (Psal. LV). If a battle should rise up against me, in this will I hope (Psal. XXVI). Beyond the other trees it was tall, which doubted not to say of itself: And I went beyond in wisdom all that were before me in Hierusalem, and 1313Cmy mind hath contemplated many things wisely, and hath learned (Eccle. I). [Vulg. Eccl. 1:16 et didici, "and I have learned," in the first person; Migne's third person makes "my mind" the subject] Its tallness touched heaven, who could truly say: We speak wisdom among the perfect, yet not the wisdom of this world, nor of the princes of this world that are brought to nought. But we speak the wisdom of God in a mystery, which is hidden, which he predestined before the worlds, which none of the princes of this world hath known (II Cor. XXIX). But see, whosoever thou art that hearest or readest these things, if perchance thy height is great; see, I say, and diligently attend, lest perchance it be also excessive. This perhaps was blameworthy in the tree here described, this perhaps stood as the cause of its cutting down, that its height was excessive. And the height thereof, he says, excessive. Perhaps Nabuchodonosor was walking in great things and in things wonderful 1313Dabove himself, and this perhaps stood to him as the cause of his ruin. Perhaps Nabuchodonosor was minded of high things, and therefore his height was excessive: Be not, says the Apostle, high-minded, but fear (Rom. XI). For as for him who eats much honey it is not good for him, so he who is a searcher of majesty shall be overwhelmed by glory. Seek not therefore the things that are higher than thee, lest perchance thy tallness be too high. The searcher of majesty is overwhelmed by glory, and that tree deserves the sentence of cutting down whose height is excessive. Were not trees of this kind high, and did they not justly deserve to be cut down, which, when they had known God, glorified him not as God, or gave thanks: But they became vain in their thoughts, and their foolish heart was darkened (Rom. I).

CAPUT XV. De auctoritatis magnitudine mentis postmodum ruiturae.

  • 1314A

Sed audiamus quid adhuc de hac arbore dicitur in eo quod sequitur: Aspectus illius erat usque ad terminos universae terrae. Aspectus ejusmodi arboris usque ad terminos universae terrae porrigitur, quando nominis ejus dignitatem qui ejusmodi est, morum similitudine propinqui et morum dissimilitudine longinqui pariter verentur, auctoritatesque ejus tam a bonis diligitur, quam a malis timetur. Nonne illius aspectus usque ad terminos universae terrae prominebat, de quo illius sententiae veritas constabat: Christi bonus odor sumus Deo in his qui salvi fiunt, et in his qui pereunt. Aliis quidem odor mortis in 1314Bmortem, aliis autem odor vitae in vitam (II Cor. II). Nonne et illarum arborum aspectus usque ad terminos universae terrae dilatabatur, de quibus veraciter dicitur: In omnem terram exivit sonus eorum, et in fines orbis terrae verba eorum (Psal. LXXXI). Sed et secundum alium quemdam sensum arboris nostrae aspectus in nobismetipsis usque ad terminos universae terrae, patet, cum jam unumquemque nostrum propriae virtutis experientia, nec in magnis, nec in minimis latet. Si enim per arborem virtutem, per aspectum arboris notitiam virtutis accipimus, cum jam quid in singulis rebus possumus multo studio, multo exercitio didicerimus, quid aliud quam arboris nostrae aspectum usque ad terminos universae terrae propalavimus? Nonne arboris suae 1314Caspectum usque ad terminos universae terrae propalaverat, et virtutis suae experientiam per omnia jam coeperat, qui de seipso illud quod et superius jam posuimus veraciter asseverat. Ego enim didici in quibus sufficiens sim. Scio humiliari, scio et abundare ubique, et in omnibus instructus sum (Philipp. VI).

CHAPTER XV. Of the greatness of authority in a mind that is afterward to fall into ruin.

  • 1314A

But let us hear what is further said of this tree in that which follows: The sight of it was even unto the ends of all the earth. The sight of a tree of this kind reaches unto the ends of all the earth, when the dignity of his name who is of this kind is alike revered by those near him through likeness of morals and by those far off through unlikeness of morals, and his authorities are alike loved by the good and feared by the evil. Did not his sight stand forth even unto the ends of all the earth, of whom the truth of that sentence stood firm: We are the good odour of Christ unto God in them that are saved, and in them that perish. To some indeed the odour of death unto 1314Bdeath, but to others the odour of life unto life (II Cor. II). Was not the sight of those trees also spread abroad even unto the ends of all the earth, of which it is truly said: Their sound hath gone forth into all the earth, and their words unto the ends of the world (Psal. LXXXI). But according to a certain other sense also the sight of our tree in our own selves lies open even unto the ends of all the earth, when now the experience of his own virtue is hidden from no one of us, neither in great things nor in the least. For if by the tree we take virtue, and by the sight of the tree the cognizance of virtue, when we have now learned with much zeal and much exercise what we can do in each several matter, what else have we done than publish the sight of our tree even unto the ends of all the earth? Had he not published the sight of his tree 1314Ceven unto the ends of all the earth, and had he not already begun the experience of his own virtue through all things, who truly affirms of himself that which we have already set down above. For I have learned in what things I am sufficient. I know how to be humbled, I know also how to abound everywhere, and in all things I am instructed (Philipp. VI). [Vulg. Phil. 4:12 Scio et humiliari, scio et abundare (ubique et in omnibus institutus sum); this second printing also reverses the order of the two members against the printing at 1313B]

CAPUT XVI. De multitudine scientiae mentis ruiturae, et in tanta scientiae plenitudine quid esse soleat causa ruinae.

Adhuc additur de hac arbore: Folia ejus pulcherrima, et fructus ejus nimius, et esca universorum in ea. In foliis verba, in fructu scientia, in esca doctrina. Foliis fructus vestitur, et verbis compositis 1314Dsententia ornatur: Os enim sapientis ornat scientiam (Prov. XV). Fructus bonus sapit, et pascit, et scientia spiritalis spiritalem esuriem per sacram admonitionem suaviter reficit. Folia itaque pulcherrima, sunt verba diserta, fructus copiosus, sensus eruditus, multiplex intellectus. Esca universorum est plena eruditio omnium, discreta admonitio singulorum. Folia verborum pulchra, ut arbitror, illa arbor habebat, et bonorum sensuum, utiliumque consiliorum fructibus abundabat quae veraciter dicere potuit: Qui me audiebant exspectabant sententiam et intenti tacebant ad consilium meum. Verbis meis addere nihil audebant, et super illos stillabat eloquium meum (Job XXIX). Fructum copiosum illa arbor habuit, quae in veritate dicere potuit: Mihi autem 1315Adedit Deus dicere ex sententia, ipse enim mihi dedit horum quae sunt scientiam veram, ut sciam dispositionem orbis terrarum, et virtutes elementorum; initium et consummationem, et medietatem temporum; vicissitudinum permutationes, et consummationes temporum; morum mutationes, et divisiones temporum; anni cursus, et stellarum dispositiones; naturas animalium, et iras bestiarum; vim ventorum, et cogitationes hominum; differentias virgultorum, et virtutes radicum; et quaecunque sunt absconsa, et improvisa, didici (Sap. VII). Esca universorum nonne in illo fuit qui in hoc ipsum se debitorem cognovit, cum dixit: Graecis ac barbaris, sapientibus et insipientibus debitor sum (Rom. I). Nonne esca universorum in ipso est qui doctrina sua omnes aedificare 1315Bpotest? Nonne escam universorum in seipso habet, qui per spiritum consilii qui menti ejus praesidet, et per gratiam discretionis qua pollet, quacunque tentatione circumventos, quacunque tribulatione oppressos, salubri consilio instruere, et blanda admonitione erigere sufficienter valet? Nonne similiter et ille quantum ad semetipsum attinet, omnium escam in seipso invenit, qui quaelibet sancta desideria nutrire, et ad quaelibet bona opera animum suum inflammare propriis meditationibus et spiritualibus theoriis novit, quoties, et quantum voluit? Sed, sicut superius innuimus de arboris altitudine, videamus ne forte et hoc in ea sit reprehensibile quod dicitur fructum nimium habere. Non enim 1315Cdicitur quod fructus ejus multus, vel fructus ejus copiosus, sed fructus ejus, inquit, nimius, fructum nimium arguit qui dicit, in rebus supervacuis noli scrutari multipliciter. Aliud est importune quaerere impossibilia, quamvis forte sint utilia, atque aliud curiose investigare inutilia, quamvis et ipsa sint possibilia. Illud pertinet ad altitudinis nimietatem, istud pertinet ad fructuum superfluitatem. Praesumptio incomprehensibilium prohibetur, cum dicitur: Altiora te ne quaesieris, et fortiora te ne scrutatus fueris, sed quae praecepit tibi Deus, etc. (Eccle. III.) Curiositas supervacuarum rerum reprehenditur in eo quod dicitur, in rebus supervacuis noli scrutari multipliciter, et in pluribus operibus ejus ne fueris curiosus. Vultis audire de arboribus 1315Dquae ejusmodi sunt, quae praesumptionis et curiositatis audaciam temperare nolunt, quomodo saepe sententiam succisionis incurrunt: Multos enim, inquit, supplantavit suspicio illorum; et in vanitate detinuit sensum illorum (Eccli. III).

CHAPTER XVI. Of the multitude of learning in a mind that is to fall into ruin, and what is wont to be the cause of ruin amid so great a fullness of learning.

There is further added concerning this tree: The leaves thereof most beautiful, and the fruit thereof excessive, and in it the food of all. In the leaves, words; in the fruit, learning; in the food, teaching. By the leaves the fruit is clothed, and by well-composed words 1314Dthe sentence is adorned: For the mouth of the wise adorneth learning (Prov. XV). [Vulg. Prov. 15:2 Lingua sapientium, "the tongue of the wise"] Good fruit has savour and feeds; and spiritual learning sweetly refreshes spiritual hunger by holy admonition. And so the most beautiful leaves are eloquent words; the copious fruit is an instructed sense, a manifold intellect. The food of all is the full instruction of all, the discreet admonition of each. Beautiful leaves of words, as I judge, that tree had, and it abounded in the fruits of good senses and of useful counsels, which could truly say: They that heard me waited for my sentence, and being attentive held their peace at my counsel. To my words they durst add nothing, and my speech dropped upon them (Job XXIX). Copious fruit that tree had, which could say in truth: But to me 1315AGod hath given to speak according to my mind, for he himself hath given me the true learning of those things which are: that I may know the disposition of the whole world, and the virtues of the elements; the beginning and the ending, and the midst of the times; the alterations of their courses, and the endings of the times; the changes of manners, and the divisions of times; the courses of the year, and the dispositions of the stars; the natures of animals, and the rages of beasts; the force of winds, and the thoughts of men; the differences of shrubs, and the virtues of roots; and whatsoever things are hidden and unforeseen, I have learned (Sap. VII). Was not the food of all in him who acknowledged himself a debtor unto this very thing, when he said: To the Greeks and to the barbarians, to the wise and to the unwise, I am a debtor (Rom. I). Is not the food of all in him who by his teaching can edify 1315Ball? Has he not the food of all in himself, who by the spirit of counsel which presides over his mind, and by the grace of discretion in which he is strong, is sufficiently able to instruct with wholesome counsel and to raise up with kindly admonition those beset by any temptation whatsoever, those oppressed by any tribulation whatsoever? Does not he likewise, so far as pertains to himself, find the food of all in himself, who knows how by his own meditations and spiritual theories to nourish any holy desires whatsoever, and to inflame his own spirit to any good works whatsoever, as often and as much as he has willed? But, as we hinted above concerning the height of the tree, let us see whether perchance this also be blameworthy in it, that it is said to have excessive fruit. For it is not 1315Csaid that its fruit was much, or its fruit copious, but its fruit, he says, excessive. He reproves excessive fruit who says, In superfluous matters search not manifoldly. It is one thing importunately to seek things impossible, though perchance they be useful, and another curiously to investigate things useless, though these also be possible. The former pertains to the excess of height, the latter pertains to the superfluity of fruits. The presumption of things incomprehensible is forbidden when it is said: Seek not the things that are too high for thee, and search not out things stronger than thee, but the things which God hath commanded thee, etc. (Eccle. III.) The curiosity of superfluous things is reproved in that it is said, In superfluous matters search not manifoldly, and in many of his works be not curious. Will you hear of trees 1315Dwhich are of this kind, which will not temper the boldness of presumption and of curiosity, how often they incur the sentence of cutting down: For the suspicion of them, he says, hath supplanted many, and hath held their sense in vanity (Eccli. III).

CAPUT XVII. De rigore disciplinae quem solet ruitura mens saepe erga alios exercere.

Audivimus ex praecedentibus cujus fuerit scientiae qui designatur ex hac arbore, audiamus adhuc ex sequentibus cujus fuerit disciplinae. Audivimus quomodo per doctrinam potuit vel indoctos erudire, vel inconsultos instruere, audiamus quomodo per disciplinam soleat et carnales premere, et spiritales sublimare. 1316ASubter eam habitabant animalia, et bestiae, et in ramis ejus conversabantur volucres coeli, et ex ea vescebatur omnis caro. Per animalia et bestias, intellige homines animales, mentes carnales et saeculares; per volucres accipe spiritales, bonorum studiorum, et sanctorum desideriorum alis ad coelestia volantes. Duo autem genera carnalium solemus videre. Unum quidem quod servit concupiscentiae, alterum quod cedit iracundiae. Per animalia itaque intelligimus mentes voluptati deditas; per bestias, mentes iracundas. Si per bestias nonnisi fera animalia congrue intelligere possumus, relinquitur ut nomine animalium sola hic mansueta intelligere debeamus. Recte ergo voluptuosae mentes designantur per animalia quae solo ventri deserviunt. 1316BRecte nihilominus mentes iracundae designantur per bestias quae dente et ungue desaeviunt. De talibus ergo recte dicitur: Subter eam habitabant animalia et bestiae, eo quod hujusmodi mentes per concupiscentiam vel iracundiam dissolutae, juste quidem premi debeant rigore disciplinae. Recte in ramis, et quasi in sublimioribus conversabantur volucres coeli, eo quod spiritales viri et coelestibus desideriis accensi merito debeant et in auctoritatem erigi, et digno honore sublimari: Subter eam, inquit, habitabant animalia, et bestiae, et in ramis ejus conversabantur volucres coeli. Ergo bestiales in imis coercebat, spiritales vero in alta sublevabat. Discant praelati nostri, discant hic quid facere debeant; 1316Cdiscant, inquam, indisciplinatos mores premere, bonos attollere et honorare. Quid, quaeso, hoc genus monstri est, quod nostri temporis arbores, ursos, et leones, vel alias hujusmodi quaslibet bestiales mentes portant in ramis; volucres vero coeli, et quaelibet volatilia pennata contra conditionis jura, Creatorisque instituta premunt in imis? Quoties ego ipse vidi, vidensque ingemui impium superexaltatum et elevatum super cedros Libani. Quid, quaeso, est quod facitis, qui ejusmodi bruta et bestialia corda in sublime attollitis, et velut in aere suspenditis? Si vere eos diligitis, cur eis non parcitis? Quid, quaeso, eis aliud quam ruinam paratis, dum eos ad alta suspenditis et quasi in ramis portatis? Quid aliud quisque suo sublimatori, vel potius 1316Dsubversori dicturus est in fine in ipso confinio ruinae, nisi quia elevans allisisti me? Conversentur volucres coeli in ramis, locumque teneant in summis quos sublevat penna sanctitatis. Bestiae sub ramis locum teneant in imis, et qui pronos vultus habent per appetitum carnis, districtionem sentiant piae severitatis et justae humiliationis. Subter eam habitabant animalia et bestiae, et in ramis ejus conversabantur volucres coeli, et ex ea vescebatur omnis caro. Ex illa sane omnis caro vescitur, cujus virtutum exemplis omnes aedificantur, cujus consolationibus omnes foventur, cujus beneficiis omnes roborantur et doctrina reficiuntur. O qualis arbor, o quantae sublimitatis, cui convenit tantae praeconium laudis!

CHAPTER XVII. Of the rigor of discipline which a mind that is to fall into ruin is often wont to exercise toward others.

We have heard from what precedes of what learning he was who is designated by this tree; let us hear yet from what follows of what discipline he was. We have heard how by teaching he could either instruct the untaught or inform the ill-advised; let us hear how by discipline he is wont both to press down the carnal and to raise up the spiritual. 1316AUnder it dwelt the animals and the beasts, and in the branches thereof abode the birds of heaven, and of it all flesh did eat. By the animals and the beasts understand animal men, carnal and secular minds; by the birds take the spiritual, flying to heavenly things on the wings of good zeals and of holy desires. Now we are wont to see two kinds of carnal men. One indeed which serves concupiscence, the other which yields to wrathfulness. And so by the animals we understand minds given over to pleasure; by the beasts, wrathful minds. If by beasts we can fitly understand none but wild animals, it remains that under the name of animals we ought here to understand tame ones only. Rightly therefore are voluptuous minds designated by the animals, which serve the belly alone. 1316BRightly none the less are wrathful minds designated by the beasts, which rage with tooth and claw. Of such, therefore, it is rightly said: Under it dwelt the animals and the beasts, in that minds of this kind, dissolute through concupiscence or wrathfulness, ought indeed justly to be pressed down by the rigor of discipline. Rightly did the birds of heaven abide in the branches, and as it were in the higher places, in that spiritual men, kindled with heavenly desires, ought deservedly both to be raised into authority and to be exalted with worthy honor: Under it, he says, dwelt the animals and the beasts, and in the branches thereof abode the birds of heaven. Therefore it confined the bestial in the lowest places, but lifted the spiritual into the high places. Let our prelates learn, let them learn here what they ought to do; 1316Clet them learn, I say, to press down undisciplined morals, to lift up and to honor the good. What, I ask, is this kind of monstrous thing, that the trees of our time bear in their branches bears and lions, or any other bestial minds whatsoever of this sort, but press down in the lowest places the birds of heaven and any feathered flying creatures whatsoever, against the laws of their condition and the ordinances of the Creator? How often have I myself seen, and seeing have groaned over, the wicked man exalted above measure and lifted up above the cedars of Lebanon. What, I ask, is it that you do, who lift up on high brute and bestial hearts of this kind, and suspend them as it were in the air? If you truly love them, why do you not spare them? What, I ask, do you prepare for them but ruin, while you suspend them on high and bear them as it were in your branches? What else shall each man say to him who exalted him, or rather 1316Dto him who subverted him, at the end, in the very border of ruin, but that lifting me up thou hast dashed me down? Let the birds of heaven abide in the branches, and let those whom the wing of sanctity lifts up hold their place in the highest. Let the beasts hold their place beneath the branches in the lowest, and let those who have downward-bent faces through the appetite of the flesh feel the strictness of a devout severity and of a just humiliation. Under it dwelt the animals and the beasts, and in the branches thereof abode the birds of heaven, and of it all flesh did eat. Of that tree assuredly does all flesh eat, by whose examples of virtues all are edified, by whose consolations all are cherished, by whose benefits all are strengthened and are refreshed with teaching. O what a tree, O of how great a sublimity, to which so great a proclamation of praise belongs!

CAPUT XVIII. De rigore disciplinae quem solet ruitura mens apud seipsam exercere.

  • 1317A

Sed et hoc ipsum quod de hac arbore dicitur in nobis ipsis implemus, si carnales affectiones sub rigore disciplinae restringimus, et cogitationes nostras in morum circumspectione occupamus, et per spiritales meditationes omnes sensus nostros in bonis desideriis nutrimus. Quid enim per volatilia intelligimus nisi subtiles cogitationes et quid per quadrupedia et terrestria, nisi carnales affectiones? Per animalia, illa desideria quae surgunt, praeter rationem; per bestias, illa quae nituntur contra rationem; praeter rationem vana et frivola; contra rationem perversa et noxia. Per arborem, ut dictum 1317Best, accipimus robur fortitudinis, et recte per ramos arboris opera intelligimus et exercitia virtutis. Et ex hujusmodi ramis meritorum bonorum fructus tempore suo colligimus, secundum quod per Prophetam audivimus: Labores manuum tuarum manducabis, beatus es, et bene tibi erit (Psal. CXXVII). Ex hujusmodi ramis salubrium consiliorum subtiliumque intellectuum fructus prodeunt, cum per multa experimenta virtutum exercitia hominem ad discretionis et scientiae perfectionem erudiunt. Multa enim quae per investigationem non possumus, per experientiam plenius et certius addiscimus, hinc illud Psalmistae: A mandatis tuis intellexi (Psal. CXVIII). A mandatis intelligit qui ex eo 1317Cquod agit ad majorem intelligentiam proficit. Hinc est quod alias scribitur: In intellectibus manuum suarum deduxit eos (Psal. LXVII). In intellectibus manuum suarum alium deducit, qui ad viam veritatis quam per operum experimenta didicit certis profectuum gradibus dirigit. Parva et parum certa est illa scientia, quam non adjuvat et confirmat rerum experientia: hinc est quod per Sapientem dicitur: Qui non est expertus, pauca recognoscit (Eccle. XXXIV). Non solum autem ex bonis quae agimus, verum etiam ex malis quae sustinemus ad discretionem erudimur, et ex ipsis tentationibus prudentiores efficimur. Hoc nos illa vox Sapientis admonet, quae dicit: Qui non est tentatus, quid scit? (Ibid.) Vir in multis expertus cogitavit multa, et qui 1317Dmulta didicit, enarrabit intellectum. Ex ramis itaque arboris fructus colligitur, quia ex multo virtutum exercitio scientia multiplicabitur. Et quo quisque perfectior atque probatior, eo uberiores fructus gignit, delectabiliusque reficit. Perfectorum autem, ait Apostolus, est solidus cibus eorum qui pro consuetudine exercitatos habent sensus ad discretionem boni et mali (Hebr. V). Boni sane et bonae arboris rami qui talem fructum ferunt, cibumque animarum gignunt, qui volatilia coeli suscipiunt, et sublimes cogitatus ad alta suspendunt, qui bestias protegunt, et carnis impetus deorsum premunt. Puto quia et apostolus sub hujusmodi ramis animalia deorsum premebat, cum diceret: Castigo corpus meum, et in servitutem redigo (I Cor. IX). Qui autem 1318Avoluerit volucres coeli hujusmodi arboris ramis assuescere, sit in lege Domini voluntas ejus, et in lege ejus meditetur die ac nocte.

Caveamus ergo quarumdam avium more nidum nobis in terra facere, vel in aquosis degere, ramosque arboris deserere, pudeatque nos curis saecularibus, carnisque desideriis assiduarum importunitate cogitationum inhaerere: Quae sursum sunt inquit Apostolus, sapite, non quae super terram (Coloss. III). Debent itaque aves nostrae, pro tempore ad coelestia elevari, et in aeternorum contemplatione suspendi, et iterum post sublimes volatus ad arboris suae ramos reverti, ibique immorari, imo et conversari. Quid enim aliud est volucres coeli ad ramos suae arboris semper redire, et ibi inhabitare, 1318Bnisi cogitationes cordis sui ad morum, operumque suorum considerationem frequenter revocare, et eorum circumspectioni crebrius insistere, et vehementius incumbere? Hinc est quod per Salomonem dicitur: Oritur sol, et occidit, et ad locum suum revertitur (Eccle. I), intelligentiae enim sol post ortum suum, et sublimis contemplationis ascensum iterum ad locum suum revertitur, et ad sui circumspectionem oculus cordis revocatur, ut iterum ad altiora sublevetur. Cor enim quod ad sui conservationem obdormit, cito contemplationis gratiam quam acceperat amittit. Recte ergo de hac arbore dicitur, et in ramis ejus conversabantur volucres coeli, eo quod circumspectiones mentis praecipue invigilare debeant circa custodiam sui. Ecce jam 1318Caudivimus quid de hujusmodi arbore sentire debeamus. Nemo tamen putet me credere Nabuchodonosor regem talem fuisse qualem me audit ex mystica significatione describere: neque enim in sua persona talis fuit, sed ex sua persona talem virum praefiguravit, et in visa arbore talem designavit. Sed mysticam interim descriptionem prosequamur, et inveniemus fortassis in ea quod juste miremur, nec solum quod admirari debeamus, imo digne defleamus, et merito de nobis timere debeamus.

CHAPTER XVIII. Of the rigor of discipline which a mind that is to fall into ruin is wont to exercise upon itself.

  • 1317A

But this very thing also which is said of this tree we fulfil in our own selves, if we restrain carnal affections under the rigor of discipline, and occupy our thoughts in the circumspection of our morals, and through spiritual meditations nourish all our senses in good desires. For what do we understand by the flying creatures but subtle thoughts, and what by the fourfooted and earthly creatures but carnal affections? By the animals, those desires which arise beside reason; by the beasts, those which strive against reason: beside reason, the vain and frivolous; against reason, the perverse and hurtful. By the tree, as has been 1317Bsaid, we take the strength of fortitude, and rightly by the branches of the tree we understand the works and the exercises of virtue. And from branches of this kind we gather in their own season the fruits of good merits, according as we have heard through the Prophet: Thou shalt eat the labours of thy hands, blessed art thou, and it shall be well with thee (Psal. CXXVII). From branches of this kind come forth the fruits of wholesome counsels and of subtle understandings, when through many trials the exercises of the virtues instruct a man unto the perfection of discretion and of learning. For many things which we cannot attain by investigation we learn more fully and more certainly by experience; hence that saying of the Psalmist: By thy commandments I have understood (Psal. CXVIII). He understands from the commandments who, from that 1317Cwhich he does, advances to a greater understanding. Hence it is that elsewhere it is written: In the understandings of his hands he led them (Psal. LXVII). He leads another in the understandings of his hands who directs him by sure steps of advancement to the way of truth which he has learned through the trials of works. Small and of little certainty is that learning which the experience of things does not help and confirm: hence it is that it is said through the Wise Man: He that is not experienced knoweth few things (Eccle. XXXIV). But not only from the good things which we do, but also from the evils which we endure, are we instructed unto discretion, and from the very temptations we are made more prudent. Of this that voice of the Wise Man admonishes us, which says: He that hath not been tempted, what doth he know? (Ibid.) [Vulg. Sir. 34:11 Qui tentatus non est, qualia scit?, "what manner of things doth he know?"] A man experienced in many things hath thought many things, and he who 1317Dhath learned many things shall declare understanding. [Vulg. Sir. 34:9 cogitabit, future, "shall think many things"] And so from the branches of the tree fruit is gathered, because from much exercise of the virtues learning shall be multiplied. And the more perfect and the more approved each man is, the more abundant fruits he begets, and the more delightfully he refreshes. But for the perfect, says the Apostle, is solid food, for them who by custom have their senses exercised unto the discerning of good and evil (Hebr. V). Good indeed, and branches of a good tree, are those which bear such fruit and beget the food of souls; which receive the flying creatures of heaven and suspend sublime thoughts on high; which cover the beasts and press down the assaults of the flesh. I think that the Apostle too was pressing down the animals beneath branches of this kind, when he said: I chastise my body, and bring it into subjection (I Cor. IX). But he who 1318Awould accustom the birds of heaven to the branches of a tree of this kind, let his will be in the law of the Lord, and in his law let him meditate day and night.

Let us beware, therefore, of making our nest in the earth after the manner of certain birds, or of living in watery places, and of forsaking the branches of the tree; and let us be ashamed to cleave to worldly cares and to the desires of the flesh by the importunity of unremitting thoughts: Mind the things that are above, says the Apostle, not the things that are upon the earth (Coloss. III). And so our birds ought for a season to be lifted up to heavenly things, and to be suspended in the contemplation of eternal things, and again, after their sublime flights, to return to the branches of their own tree, and there to tarry, nay even to dwell. For what else is it for the birds of heaven always to return to the branches of their own tree, and to inhabit there, 1318Bbut for the thoughts of one's own heart frequently to be called back to the consideration of one's own morals and works, and to press the more often upon the circumspection of them, and to lean upon it the more vehemently? Hence it is that it is said through Solomon: The sun riseth and setteth, and returneth to his own place (Eccle. I); for the sun of the understanding, after its rising and after the ascent of sublime contemplation, returns again to its own place, and the eye of the heart is called back to the circumspection of itself, that it may again be lifted up to higher things. For the heart which sleeps as to the keeping of itself quickly loses the grace of contemplation which it had received. Rightly therefore is it said of this tree, and in the branches thereof abode the birds of heaven, in that the circumspections of the mind ought chiefly to be watchful about the custody of themselves. Behold, we have now 1318Cheard what we ought to think concerning a tree of this kind. Yet let no one suppose that I believe king Nabuchodonosor to have been such as he hears me describe out of the mystical signification: for neither was he such in his own person, but out of his own person he prefigured such a man, and in the tree he saw he designated such a one. But let us meanwhile pursue the mystical description, and we shall find perhaps in it what we may justly wonder at; and not only what we ought to admire, but rather what we may worthily bewail, and what we ought deservedly to fear concerning ourselves.

CAPUT XIX. Quod in quolibet virtutum culmine oporteat semper morum ruinam timere.

Audiamus ergo quid adhuc de hac arbore dicat, et quid quisque timere debeat discat et ingemiscat: 1318DVidebam in visione capitis mei super stratum meum, et ecce vigil, et sanctus de coelo descendit, et clamavit fortiter, et ait sic: Succidite arborem et praecidite ramos ejus; excutite folia ejus et dispergite fructum ejus. Fugiant bestiae quae subter eam sunt, et volucres de ramis ejus (Dan. IV). Vae filiis Adae, vae conditioni humanae. Quid, quaeso, quantumcunque profecerit, quantumcunque ascenderit de reliquo poterit securus esse si tantus vir tamque perfectus et quasi sub unius horae momento potuit de tanta sublimitate cadere? Recolamus ergo qualis superius ex mystica arbore describitur qui hic praecidi jubetur, et tunc melius agnoscimus in qualicunque virtutis fastigio constitutis morum ruina 1319Aquam juste timetur. In magnitudine arboris miramur modum fortitudinis; in altitudine ejus profunditatem subtilitatis; in aspectus dilatatione, magnitudinem auctoritatis. In pulchritudine foliorum facundiam sermonis; in nimietate fructuum, multitudinem eruditionis; in esca universorum, doctrinam aedificationis; in subjectione bestiarum, rigorem severitatis; in cohabitatione volucrum, providentiam circumspectionis. Quis ergo, nisi insanus, in tanto mutabilitatis ancipiti sibi securitatem promittit, si vere aliquando talis fuit, qui postea succisus cecidit? Vere, ut Scriptura dicit: Beatus homo qui semper est pavidus. Nescit enim quid futura pariat dies (Prov. XXVIII).

CHAPTER XIX. That upon any summit of the virtues whatsoever one ought always to fear the ruin of morals.

Let us hear therefore what he further says of this tree, and let each man learn and groan at what he ought to fear: 1318DI was seeing in the vision of my head upon my bed, and behold a watcher, and a holy one came down from heaven, and cried out mightily, and said thus: Cut down the tree and cut off its branches; shake off its leaves and scatter its fruit. Let the beasts flee which are under it, and the birds from its branches (Dan. IV). [Vulg. Dan. 4:11 dispergite fructus ejus, plural, "scatter its fruits"; the exposition at 1321C–D follows the singular printed here] Woe to the sons of Adam, woe to the human condition. What, I ask, how much soever he shall have advanced, how much soever he shall have ascended, will he be able to be secure for the rest, if so great a man and one so perfect could fall from so great a sublimity, and that as it were within the moment of a single hour? Let us call to mind, therefore, of what sort he is described above out of the mystical tree who here is bidden to be cut off, and then we shall the better recognize how justly the ruin of morals 1319Ais feared by those who are set upon any height of virtue whatsoever.
