In the greatness of the tree we wonder at the measure of fortitude; in its height, at the profundity of subtlety; in the spreading of its sight, at the greatness of authority. In the beauty of its leaves, at the eloquence of speech; in the excess of its fruits, at the multitude of instruction; in the food of all, at the teaching of edification; in the subjection of the beasts, at the rigor of severity; in the cohabitation of the birds, at the providence of circumspection. Who therefore, unless he be mad, promises himself security amid so great a hazard of mutability, if he was truly once such a one who afterward was cut down and fell? Truly, as the Scripture says: Blessed is the man who is always fearful. For he knoweth not what the day to come may bring forth (Prov. XXVIII). [Vulg. Prov. 27:1 ignoras enim quid superventura pariat dies, "for thou knowest not what the day to come may bring forth"; the second clause is not part of Prov. 28:14, to which the note points]

CAPUT XX. Quod quidquid circa nos agitur, Dei dispositione et angelica administratione dispensatur.

  • 1319B

Nemo tamen existimet Deum humana non curare, nemo audeat temere ejus judicia reprehendere, cum videat vel audiat aliquid praeter existimationem, vel contra rationem, ut sibi videtur, accidisse. Ecce de coelo descendisse et sanctus fuisse dicitur a quo succisionis sententia datur seu dispensatur. Ecce, inquit, vigil sanctus de coelo descendit. Cum igitur audias hujus operis ministrum de coelo venire, intellige Dei dispositione id agi, et cum sanctum legas, crede eum quod justum est juste exsequi. Ecce vigil et sanctus de coelo descendit. Circa hominum curam tam boni angeli quam mali perpetuam sollicitudinem impendunt, et nec nocte nec die ab hac 1319Csollicitudine se vel ad modicum subtrahunt, sed mali ad impugnandum, boni ad protegendum invigilant, ita ut utrique recte satis vigiles dici queant. Sed quod bonus angelus sit qui hujusmodi sententiam, vel e coelo cognovit vel in terra disposuit: satis ostenditur ex eo quod sanctus et de coelo descendisse perhibetur. O quam recte hoc nomen bonis angelis convenit quibus somnus erroris vel oblivionis circa nostrae salutis negotia nunquam subrepit! O celsitudo angelicae pietatis! Qui securi sunt de seipsis, adhuc sollicitudinem gerunt pro nobis. De hac angelorum circa nostram salutem vigilia, vigilique custodia est illa apostolica sententia: Nonne omnes administratorii spiritus sunt in ministerium 1319Dmissi propter eos qui haereditatem capiunt salutis? (Hebr. I.) Quid itaque aliud est ejusmodi vigiles de coelo descendere, nisi salutis nostrae ministros terrena curare, et secundum quod in summis divinitus discunt, et imis humana disponere? Recte ergo dicitur: Ecce vigil, et sanctus de coelo descendit, et clamavit fortiter; et ait sic: Praecidite arborem. Alia itaque sunt quae boni angeli per seipsos faciunt, alia quae fieri permittunt nulla omnino sine eorum dispositione uspiam fiunt. Bona itaque fiunt eorum cooperatione, mala autem eorum permissione. Bona ergo per bonos angelos, mala administrantur per malos. Quoniam erga rectitudinis statum subvertere malum esse cognovit, bonus angelus arborem praecidere noluit; verumtamen quia hoc unius meritum, 1320Avel universitatis bonum exigere cognovit, fieri permisit. Quod enim dicit, praecidite arborem, permissionem intellige non autem praeceptionem. Neque enim in hoc est spiritibus malignis obedientia injuncta, sed licentia indulta. Hoc siquidem eos tandem aliquando facere permisit, quod eos diu, multumque desiderasse non ignoravit. Hinc est quod a Domino Judae traditori dicitur: Quod facis fac citius (Joan. XIII). Non enim ei in hoc praeceptum injungitur, sed agendi facultas conceditur.

Notum autem est omnibus quod magnae vocis clamore uti solemus, quoties longe positis aliquid innotescere volumus. Quid autem ab invicem distat longius quam spiritus angelicus, spiritusque malignus, non tam loci longinquitate quam voluntatis 1320Bdiversitate? Hinc est quod prodigus ille filius qui omnem substantiam suam vivendo luxuriose cum meretricibus dissipasse legitur, in regionem longinquam abiisse in Evangelio perhibetur (Luc. XV), eo quod a patris summi beneplacito volontatis depravatione elongatus alienatur. Secundum hunc itaque modum multum utique sunt inter se longinqui tam varia, tam contraria sorte distincti atque disjuncti angelus confirmatus in bono, angelus confirmatus in malo, angelus deputatus gloriae, et angelus addictus gehennae. Recte ergo vigil ille et sanctus fortiter clamasse perhibetur, ut ex eo aperte daretur intelligi cujusmodi angeli et a bono angelo quam remoti esse viderentur, quibus vigilum sententia coelitus dictata non sine forti clamore 1320Cintimatur. Est tamen et inter bonos quosdam angelos magna distantia non contrariae voluntatis, sed supereminentis dignitatis divinitus decenter ordinata differentia. Unde nec incongruum videtur si inter eos aliquis clamor describitur, per quem dignitatis eorum differentia denotetur. Recte ergo angelus qui hic malignis spiritibus tentandi licentiam concessisse introducitur, non solum clamasse, sed et fortiter clamasse perhibetur. Ecce, inquit, vigil, et sanctus de coelo descendit et clamavit fortiter, et sic ait: Succidite arborem.

CHAPTER XX. That whatsoever is done concerning us is dispensed by God's disposition and by angelic administration.

  • 1319B

Yet let no one think that God has no care for human things, let no one dare rashly to reprehend his judgments, when he sees or hears that something has happened beside expectation, or against reason, as it seems to him. Behold, he by whom the sentence of cutting down is given or dispensed is said to have come down from heaven and to have been holy. Behold, he says, a holy watcher came down from heaven. [Vulg. Dan. 4:10 and the lemma at 1318D read vigil, et sanctus, two subjects, "a watcher, and a holy one"] When therefore thou hearest that the minister of this work comes from heaven, understand that this is done by God's disposition; and when thou readest that he is holy, believe that he justly executes that which is just. Behold, a watcher and a holy one came down from heaven. About the care of men both the good angels and the evil expend a perpetual solicitude, and neither by night nor by day do they withdraw themselves even for a little from this 1319Csolicitude; but the evil are watchful for assailing, the good for protecting, so that both may rightly enough be called watchers. But that it is a good angel who either learned this sentence from heaven or disposed it on earth is sufficiently shown from this, that he is reported to be holy and to have come down from heaven. O how rightly does this name befit the good angels, upon whom the sleep of error or of forgetfulness never creeps as touching the business of our salvation! O height of angelic devotion! They who are secure concerning themselves yet bear solicitude for us. Of this watch of the angels about our salvation, and of their watchful custody, is that apostolic sentence: Are they not all ministering spirits, sent in ministry 1319Dfor them who receive the inheritance of salvation? (Hebr. I.) [Vulg. Heb. 1:14 qui haereditatem capient salutis, future, "who shall receive the inheritance of salvation"; the received text also runs Nonne omnes sunt administratorii spiritus] What else, therefore, is it for watchers of this kind to come down from heaven, but for the ministers of our salvation to have a care of earthly things, and, according to what they divinely learn in the highest things, to dispose human affairs also in the lowest? Rightly therefore is it said: Behold, a watcher and a holy one came down from heaven, and cried out mightily; and said thus: Cut off the tree. [Vulg. Dan. 4:11 and the lemma at 1318D read Succidite arborem, "Cut down the tree"] And so some things there are which the good angels do by themselves, others which they permit to be done, nothing at all is anywhere done without their disposition. Good things therefore are done by their cooperation, but evil things by their permission. Good things therefore are administered through the good angels, evil things through the evil. Since he knew that to overthrow, as regards the state of rectitude, was an evil, the good angel was unwilling to cut off the tree; nevertheless, because he knew that the merit of this one man, 1320Aor the good of the universe, required it, he permitted it to be done. For in that he says, Cut off the tree, understand a permission and not a precept. For herein obedience is not enjoined upon the malignant spirits, but licence is granted. This at length he permitted them at some time to do, which he was not ignorant that they had long and much desired. Hence it is that it is said by the Lord to Judas the betrayer: That which thou dost, do quickly (Joan. XIII). For herein a precept is not enjoined upon him, but the faculty of acting is conceded.

Now it is known to all that we are wont to use the outcry of a great voice as often as we would make something known to those set far off. But what stands farther apart one from another than an angelic spirit and a malignant spirit, not so much by the distance of place as by the 1320Bdiversity of will? Hence it is that that prodigal son, who is read to have wasted all his substance in living riotously with harlots, is reported in the Gospel to have gone away into a far country (Luc. XV), in that, made distant by the depravation of his will, he is estranged from the good pleasure of the supreme Father. According to this manner, therefore, they are assuredly far distant from one another, distinguished and disjoined by a lot so various and so contrary: an angel confirmed in good, an angel confirmed in evil; an angel deputed to glory, and an angel consigned to Gehenna. Rightly therefore is that watcher and holy one reported to have cried out mightily, that from this it might be openly given to understand of what kind those angels were, and how far removed from a good angel they might be seen to be, to whom a sentence of the watchers, dictated from heaven, is made known not without a mighty outcry. 1320CYet there is among certain of the good angels also a great distance, a difference not of contrary will but of supereminent dignity, becomingly ordained of God. Whence neither does it seem incongruous if some outcry is described among them, whereby the difference of their dignity may be denoted. Rightly therefore is the angel who is here brought in as having conceded to the malignant spirits the licence of tempting reported not only to have cried out, but to have cried out mightily. Behold, he says, a watcher, and a holy one came down from heaven and cried out mightily, and thus said: Cut down the tree.

CAPUT XXI. Quod nihil in nos possunt spiritus maligni nisi permissione Dei.

1320DDuo sunt quae ex praesenti possumus patenter colligere. Primum quod non possunt nos maligni spiritus, nisi quantum permittuntur, tentare. Secundum quia, si juxta omnem virtutem suam ad nostram impugnationem relaxarentur, nemo posset eorum impetum tolerare. Qui enim arborem succidere possunt, et volunt, et tamen non nisi permissi succidunt, patet profecto quia sine Dei permissione nil in nostra insectatione praesumunt. Sed quid in hominibus praesumerent sine Dei permissione qui, attestante Evangelio, nec gregem porcorum sine divina licentia ausi sunt invadere? (Matth. VIII; Marc. V; Luc. VIII.) Tantum itaque in electorum impugnatione eis licentiae relaxatur, quantum utilitas 1321Aeorum exigit, vel justitia meretur. Quae enim humana virtus eorum impetum posset sustinere, si talem tantamque arborem, accepta divinitus licentia, statim potuerunt evertere? Sed consolatur nos in hoc apostolus Paulus: Fidelis, inquit, est Dominus, qui non sinit vos tentari supra id quod potestis, sed faciet etiam cum tentatione proventum, ut possitis sustinere (I Cor. X). Nisi ergo arbor ista nimis alta fuisset et de sua virtute intumesceret, nimisque praesumeret, sententiam succisionis divinitus non acciperet. Attende modo grandem arborem, et de fortitudinis suae abundantia nimis praesumentem: Ego dixi, inquit, in abundantia mea, non movebor in aeternum (Psal. XXIX). Sed statim ex subtractione gratiae supervenientique tentatione didicit 1321Bquid, vel quantum de seipsa potuit: Avertisti, inquit, faciem tuam a me, et factus sum conturbatus (ibid.). Arbor itaque succiditur, quando constantiae virtus tentationibus paulatim attenuatur, tandemque funditus tollitur. Nonne arboris illius robur succisum fuit quae dixit: Dereliquit me virtus mea, et lumen oculorum meorum et ipsum non est mecum (Psal. XXXVII). Nonne virtus ejus subtracta arborque succisa ad ima ruit, quando ad unius concupiscentiae respectum in adulterium, et de adulterio in homicidium cecidit?

CHAPTER XXI. That the malignant spirits can do nothing against us save by God's permission.

1320DThere are two things which from the present passage we can plainly gather. The first, that the malignant spirits cannot tempt us save so far as they are permitted. The second, that if they were let loose for our assailing according to all their power, no one could endure their onset. For since they who can and will cut down the tree yet do not cut it down unless permitted, it plainly appears that without God's permission they presume nothing in their persecution of us. But what would they presume upon men without God's permission, who, as the Gospel attests, dared not invade even the herd of swine without divine licence? (Matth. VIII; Marc. V; Luc. VIII.) So much licence, therefore, is let loose to them in the assailing of the elect as their profit 1321Arequires, or as justice deserves. For what human virtue could sustain their onset, if, having received licence from God, they were able straightway to overthrow a tree so great and of such a kind? But in this the apostle Paul consoles us: Faithful, he says, is the Lord, who suffereth you not to be tempted above that which you are able, but will make also with the temptation issue, that you may be able to sustain it (I Cor. X). [Vulg. 1 Cor. 10:13 Fidelis autem Deus est, qui non patietur vos tentari, "God is faithful, who will not suffer you to be tempted"] Unless therefore that tree had been too high, and had swollen concerning its own virtue, and presumed too much, it would not have received from God the sentence of cutting down. Consider now a great tree, and one presuming too much upon the abundance of its own fortitude: I said, he says, in my abundance, I shall never be moved (Psal. XXIX). But straightway, from the withdrawal of grace and the temptation that came upon it, it learned 1321Bwhat, or how much, it could of itself: Thou hast turned away, he says, thy face from me, and I became troubled (ibid.). And so the tree is cut down when the virtue of constancy is little by little worn away by temptations, and at last is utterly taken away. Was not the strength of that tree cut down which said: My virtue hath forsaken me, and the light of my eyes, even that is not with me (Psal. XXXVII). Was not its virtue withdrawn, and the tree, being cut down, fallen to the lowest, when at the regard of a single concupiscence it fell into adultery, and from adultery into homicide?

CAPUT XXII. Quomodo, vel quo ordine ad subtractionem gratiae soleat mens humana corruere.

1321CSuccisa arbore etiam rami praeciduntur; quia, virtute animi deficiente, bona opera paulatim subtrahuntur. Arbor succiditur et ramus praeciditur, quando virtus in occulto, et virtutis opus deficit in aperto. Prius igitur subtrahitur virtus animi, postmodum etiam tollitur facultas, et occasio bene agendi, et succiso robore arboris ramisque praecisis, folia excutiuntur, fructusque dispergitur; quia, deficiente virtutum proposito virtutumque exercitio, prius gratia bene loquendi tollitur, tandem etiam gratia recte intelligendi subtrahitur. Recte quidem postquam deficit studium bonae operationis, subtrahitur facundia sermonis, quia scandalizare magis quam aedificare audientes potest omnis castigatio vel exhortatio depravati praedicatoris, cum ei loquenti 1321Dquivis tacite respondeat: Cur ergo haec ipse non facis? Peccatori autem dixit Deus: Quare tu enarras justitias meas et assumis testamentum meum per os tuum? Foliorum gratia excutitur, quando ex infami suspicione prius habita, loquendi fiducia vel auctoritas amittitur. Fructus dispergitur, quando ejus doctrina contemnitur, quae quamvis per sensum, seu consilium utilis forte videatur, propter tamen pravum vitae exemplum facile conculcatur. Fructus dispergitur quando importuna variarum concupiscentiarum incursione studium meditationis dissipatur, et subtilitas investigandi, intelligendique facilitas cogitationum evagatione confunditur. Ecce quam facile, ecce quo ordine mens ab alto dejicitur quae meritis exigentibus a gratia divina 1322Adeseritur, quae malignis exactoribus ex divina permissione exponitur. Recte ergo de hac arbore dicitur: Excutite folia ejus, et dispergite fructum ejus.

CHAPTER XXII. How, or in what order, the human mind is wont to fall to ruin upon the withdrawal of grace.

1321CWhen the tree is cut down, the branches also are cut off; because, as the virtue of the spirit fails, good works are little by little withdrawn. The tree is cut down and the branch is cut off, when virtue fails in secret and the work of virtue in the open. First therefore the virtue of the spirit is withdrawn, afterward the faculty also and the occasion of doing well is taken away; and, the strength of the tree being cut down and the branches cut off, the leaves are shaken off and the fruit is scattered; because, as the purpose of the virtues and the exercise of the virtues fail, first the grace of speaking well is taken away, and at last the grace of understanding rightly also is withdrawn. Rightly indeed, after the zeal of good working fails, is the eloquence of speech withdrawn, because every chastisement or exhortation of a depraved preacher can scandalize his hearers rather than edify them, since to him as he speaks 1321Danyone may silently answer: Why then dost thou not thyself do these things? But to the sinner God hath said: Why dost thou declare my justices, and take my testament in thy mouth? The grace of the leaves is shaken off when, from an infamous suspicion first entertained, the confidence or the authority of speaking is lost. The fruit is scattered when his teaching is despised, which, although through its sense or its counsel it may perchance seem useful, yet on account of the depraved example of his life is easily trodden underfoot. The fruit is scattered when, by the importunate incursion of various concupiscences, the zeal of meditation is dissipated, and the subtlety of investigating and the facility of understanding are confounded by the wandering of thoughts. Behold how easily, behold in what order that mind is cast down from on high which, its merits requiring it, is deserted by divine grace, 1322Awhich by divine permission is exposed to the malignant exactors. Rightly therefore is it said of this tree: Shake off its leaves, and scatter its fruit.

CAPUT XXIII. Quomodo mens post foedam dejectionem sui soleat postmodum et ab aliis contemni, et in semetipsam resolvi.

Sed cum coeperit homo virtutum ramos, consiliique fructum amittere, et tam vitae honestatem quam doctrinae auctoritatem omnino non habere, quis jam ei vel carnalium obedire, vel spiritualium per familiaritatem dignetur inhaerere? Recte ergo adjungitur quod adhuc de hac arbore dicitur: Fugiant bestiae quae subter eam sunt, et volucres de ramis ejus. Bestiae siquidem quae prius subter eam 1322Berant diffugiunt, quando subjecti ipsius ejus obedientiam detrectant, et carnales quique magisterium ejus contemnunt. Volucres coeli de ramis ejus fugere, est spirituales quosque viros vitae illius infamiam horrere, consortiaque declinare. Nonne illius magnae arboris cui, secundum Dominicum testimonium, non erat similis super terram, eo quod vere esset vir magnus inter omnes orientales, et inter praecipuos primus; nonne, inquam, magnae illius arboris umbracula juniores abominabantur, et jam subter eam esse bestiae dedignabantur cum de se veraciter protestabatur: Nunc autem derident me juniores tempore quorum non dignabar patres ponere cum canibus gregis mei. Quorum virtus manuum erat 1322Cmihi pro nihilo, et vita ipsa putabantur indigni (Job XXX). Quid, quaeso, majores faciebant si juniores, et tam viles eum deridebant, eo quod arbor tanta esset ex Domini permissione succisa, et a planta pedis usque ad verticem ulcere pessimo percussa? Quam magna et illa arbor erat cui Dominus testimonium perhibebat, quod hominem secundum cor suum invenerat. Nonne conversabantur in ramis ejus volucres coeli, cum veraciter de se protestetur: Quia innocentes, et recti adhaeserunt mihi? (Psal. XXIV.) Nonne et tunc temporis de ramis ejus aufugerant cum iterum in oratione proclamabat: Longe fecisti notos meos a me, posuerant me abominationem sibi (Psal. LXXXVII); et iterum: Elongasti a me amicum, et proximum, et notos meos a miseria (ibid.). 1322DSed tunc apud quemlibet virtutis virum impletur quod scribitur de hac arbore, ut, ligni robore succiso, fugiant subter habitantes bestiae, cum vigore animi fracto tentationibusque exhausto, jam non valet mens carnales impetus cohibere, vel quaelibet irrationabilia desideria sub rationis magisterio coercere. Avium coeli cohabitationem amittimus, cum sublimium investigationum jucunda spectacula, tot phantasmatum occursione turbati, exercere non valemus. Ramis suis inhabitantes volucres arbor amittit, cum meditationes suas circa assuetam morum suorum circumspectionem exercere jam nequit. Contubernia talium volucrum nos amisisse saepe dolemus, cum cogitationes nostras ab inepta evagatione restringere non valemus, ita ut unusquisque 1323Anostrum illud beati Job recte apud se dicat, cum suas aves non sine suo dolore evolasse jam videat: Cogitationes meae dissipatae sunt, torquentes cor meum (Job VII).

CHAPTER XXIII. How the mind, after a foul casting down of itself, is wont afterwards both to be despised by others, and to be dissolved into itself.

But when a man has begun to lose the branches of the virtues and the fruit of counsel, and to have not at all either uprightness of life or the authority of teaching, who now among carnal men would deign to obey him, or who among spiritual men to cleave to him through familiarity? Rightly, therefore, is that added which is still said of this tree: Let the beasts flee which are under it, and the birds from its branches. For the beasts which before were under it 1322Bflee away, when his own subjects refuse obedience to him, and all carnal men despise his mastery. For the birds of heaven to flee from its branches is for all spiritual men to shudder at the infamy of that life, and to shun its fellowships. Did not the younger men abominate the shady coverts of that great tree to which, according to the Lord's testimony, there was none like upon the earth, in that he was truly a great man among all the men of the east, and the first among the chief; did not, I say, the younger men abominate the shady coverts of that great tree, and did not the beasts now disdain to be under it, when he truly protested of himself: But now they that are younger in time deride me, whose fathers I did not deign to set with the dogs of my flock. The strength of whose hands was 1322Cto me as nothing, and they were thought unworthy of life itself (Job XXX). What, I ask, were the elders doing, if the younger, and men so vile, derided him, in that so great a tree had been cut down by the Lord's permission, and smitten with a most grievous ulcer from the sole of the foot even to the crown? How great also was that tree to which the Lord bore testimony, that he had found a man according to his own heart. Did not the birds of heaven dwell in its branches, when he truly protests of himself: that the innocent and the upright have cleaved to me? (Psal. XXIV.) [Vulg. Ps. 24:21 begins Innocentes et recti adhaeserunt mihi; the quia Migne prints inside the quotation is the conjunction binding it to protestetur] Had they not also at that time fled away from its branches, when again in prayer he cried out: Thou hast put my acquaintance far from me, they had made me an abomination to themselves (Psal. LXXXVII) [Vulg. posuerunt, perfect, "they have made"; Migne prints the pluperfect]; and again: Thou hast removed far from me friend and neighbour, and my acquaintance because of misery (ibid.). 1322DBut then in any man of virtue is fulfilled what is written of this tree, that, the strength of the wood being cut down, the beasts dwelling beneath flee away, when, the vigour of the spirit being broken and drained by temptations, the mind is no longer strong enough to check carnal onsets, or to coerce any irrational desires under the mastery of reason. We lose the dwelling together of the birds of heaven, when, disturbed by the onrush of so many phantasms, we are not strong enough to practise the pleasant spectacles of sublime investigations. The tree loses the birds that dwell in its branches, when it can no longer practise its meditations about the accustomed circumspection of its own morals. We often grieve that we have lost the companionship of such birds, when we are not strong enough to restrain our thoughts from unfitting wandering, so that each one 1323Aof us may rightly say within himself that word of blessed Job, when he now sees his own birds to have flown away, not without grief of his own: My thoughts are dissipated, tormenting my heart (Job VII).

CAPUT XXIV. Quod turpiter lapsos, et a sublimi dejectos divina miseratio saepe et ad poenitentiam reservat, et ad correctionem exspectat.

Illud autem quam pium, et quanta admiratione dignum quod divina miseratio nec tales homines saepe funditus deserit, nec post talem lapsum tantamque ruinam penitus destituit, sed adhuc tales saepe ad correctionem exspectat, et ad poenitentiam reservat. Vae tibi quisquis talem te esse deprehendis, si divitias bonitatis ejus, et patientiae, et longanimitatis 1323Bcontemnis. An ignoras quoniam benignitas Dei ad poenitentiam te adducit, qui spei tuae radices nondum praecidit, sed in judiciorum suorum arcano, et quasi in sinu terrae abscondit? Audiamus ergo quomodo illa divinae pietatis altitudo severitatis suae sententiam temperat, cum post tantae dissipationis vastitatem ad nostram consolationem aliquid adhuc de hac arbore adjungat: Verumtamen, inquit, germen radicum ejus in terra sinite. Hoc est quod sine divinae benignitatis admiratione nec possumus, nec debemus transire, qui jubet germen radicum ejus in terra sinere, unde possit iterum reparari, et ad pristinam plenitudinem reformari. Verumtamen, inquit, germen radicum ejus in terra sinite. Tunc arbor quasi radicitus avellitur, quando 1323Cpost perpetrata mala etiam in desperationem praecipitatur. Quasi enim radices suas arbor amittit, quando omnem correctionis spem perdit, et in desperationem cadit. Arbor illa radicitus avulsa erat, et sine reparationis germine corruendo remanserat, quae ad Dominum loquens, et de venia desperans dicebat: Major est iniquitas mea quam ut veniam merear (Gen. IV). Quid enim aliud sunt germen, vel radix hujusmodi arboris, nisi propositum correctionis et spes divinae propitiationis? Omne enim opus bonum ex bono proposito germinat ut in effectum crumpat et ad bonae consuetudinis robur convalescat, et frustra virtutis arbor ad alia nititur, quae in spe divinae propitiationis non radicatur: Non 1323Denim volentis neque currentis, sed miserentis est Dei (Rom. IX). Quid itaque aliud est hujusmodi arbori germen radicum post ruinam remanere, nisi quemlibet post peccati lapsum poenitentiae propositum habere et poenitentiae fructum de divina pietate sperare, in tantum ut sentiens de Domino in bonitate audeat, et dicat: Non in aeternum irascetur, neque in aeternum comminabitur? (Psal. CII.) Cum enim iratus fuerit, misericordiae recordabitur (Habac. III). Quam multos quotidie videmus qui inter flagitia quae assidue committunt, spem et propositum resipiscendi non amittunt, et non solum peccata dimittere, imo etiam omnia quae mundi sunt relinquere, et ad ordinem, et religionem venire proponunt. Quid aliud isti quam boni germinis radices retinent? Unde 1324Acum tempus miserendi pro divino beneplacito advenerit, ad bonum studium pullulant. Alii autem, cum de conversione admonentur, nunquam se ad ordinem vel religionem venire etiam cum juramento affirmant, et cum de peccatis corripiuntur, se a suis voluptatibus non posse cohibere cum sacramento asseverant. Qui ergo ejusmodi sunt qui correctionis spem, propositumque perdiderunt, nonne cum virtutis arbore etiam radicum germen amiserunt? Quo contra dicitur de hac arbore: Verumtamen germen radicum ejus in terra sinite.

CHAPTER XXIV. That the divine mercy often both reserves unto repentance those who have basely fallen and been cast down from on high, and waits for their correction.

But how merciful a thing is this, and how worthy of great wonder, that the divine mercy often neither utterly forsakes such men, nor after such a fall and so great a ruin wholly abandons them, but still often waits for such men unto correction, and reserves them unto repentance. Woe to thee, whosoever thou art that dost catch thyself to be such a one, if thou despisest the riches of his goodness, and of his patience, and of his longsuffering. 1323BOr knowest thou not that the kindness of God leadeth thee to repentance, who has not yet cut away the roots of thy hope, but has hidden them in the secret place of his judgments, and as it were in the bosom of the earth? Let us hear, therefore, how that height of the divine mercy tempers the sentence of its own severity, when after the waste of so great a scattering it still adds something concerning this tree for our consolation: Nevertheless, he says, leave the shoot of its roots in the earth. This is that which we neither can nor ought to pass by without wonder at the divine kindness, in him who bids the shoot of its roots be left in the earth, whence it may again be repaired, and reformed to its former fullness. Nevertheless, he says, leave the shoot of its roots in the earth. Then is the tree as it were plucked up by the roots, when 1323Cafter evils have been perpetrated it is hurled headlong even into despair. For the tree loses as it were its own roots, when it loses all hope of correction, and falls into despair. That tree had been plucked up by the roots, and had remained in its falling without any shoot of repair, which, speaking to the Lord and despairing of pardon, said: My iniquity is greater than that I may deserve pardon (Gen. IV). For what else are the shoot, or the root, of a tree of this kind, but the purpose of correction and the hope of the divine propitiation? For every good work germinates out of a good purpose, that it may crumpat into effect and grow strong unto the sturdiness of good habit; and in vain does the tree of virtue strive after other things, which is not rooted in the hope of the divine propitiation: For 1323Dit is not of him that willeth, nor of him that runneth, but of God that sheweth mercy (Rom. IX). What else, then, is it for the shoot of the roots to remain to a tree of this kind after its ruin, than for anyone after the fall of sin to have a purpose of repentance and to hope for the fruit of repentance from the divine mercy, so far that, feeling concerning the Lord in goodness, he may dare, and say: He will not be angry unto eternity, neither will he threaten unto eternity? (Psal. CII.) For when he shall have been angry, he will remember mercy (Habac. III). [Vulg. Ps. 101:9 Non in perpetuum irascetur, "not for ever", and the clause is a statement; chunk 0014 prints the same verse at 1262B with in perpetuum and no question mark] [Vulg. Hab. 3:2 cum iratus fueris, misericordiae recordaberis, second person, "when thou shalt be angry, thou wilt remember mercy"] How many do we daily see who, amid the outrages which they assiduously commit, do not lose the hope and the purpose of coming to their senses, and who purpose not only to put away their sins, nay rather to leave all things that are of the world, and to come to an order and to religion. What else do these retain than the roots of a good shoot? Whence, 1324Awhen the time of showing mercy shall have come according to the divine good pleasure, they sprout forth unto good zeal. But others, when they are admonished concerning conversion, affirm even with an oath that they will never come to an order or to religion, and when they are rebuked concerning their sins, asseverate under solemn oath that they cannot restrain themselves from their own pleasures. They therefore who are of this kind, who have lost the hope and the purpose of correction, have they not, together with the tree of virtue, lost also the shoot of the roots? Against which it is said of this tree: Nevertheless leave the shoot of its roots in the earth.

CAPUT XXV. Ad quantam pravitatem, mens quandoque obduratur quae tamen divinitus ad veniam reservatur.

Mirum sane quomodo ad unum statim flagitium 1324Bmulti saepe in desperationem cadunt. Alii nec post multa, et maxima, et diuturna mala spem, vel propositum resipiscendi amittunt. De quo et illud recte intelligitur quod hic de hac adhuc arbore subinfertur, quia alligetur vinculo ferreo et aereo in herbis quae foris sunt, et rore coeli tingatur, et cum feris pars ejus in herba terrae. Cor ejus ab humano commutetur, et cor ferae detur ei (Dan. IV). Ex his certe, ut arbitror, perpendis quanta mala, sed audi adhuc quam sint diuturna: Et septem, inquit, tempora mutentur super eum. Quid enim intelligitur rectius in vinculo ferreo et aereo, quam perversitatis indomabilis consuetudo? In ferro audacia, in aere pertinacia, in herbis terrae bona infima, bona terrena. Quid enim nisi quaedam herbae terrae sunt bona ista 1324Cterrena, quae nobis sunt cum bestiis communia. De quibus recte dicitur: quae foris sunt. Eo enim ipso foris existunt quo corporalia et carnalia, non autem spiritualia sunt. Interna namque bona sunt spiritualia, exteriora autem sunt corporalia. Illa pertinent ad hominem interiorem, ista ad hominem exteriorem. Illa itaque invisibilia, ista autem visibilia; illa aeterna, ista temporalia: Quae enim videntur, ait Apostolus, temporalia sunt, quae autem non videntur aeterna (II Cor. IV). Illa autem ad mentem, ista vero pertinent ad carnem. Sed omnis caro fenum, et omnis gloria ejus quasi flos feni. Cum ergo depravata mens coeperit ad hujusmodi infima bona seipsam inclinare, et quasi in herba terrae carnalia desideria 1324Dsatiare, et ejusmodi oblectamenta in usum ducere, prava consuetudine paulatim invalescente, fit tandem saepe ut jam appetitum suum ab eorum amore vel usu non possit temperare. Quasi ergo vinculo ferreo in herba terrae mens depravata alligatur, quando in carnalium voluptatum amore ex prava consuetudine obduratur. Quia ferrum caetera omnia metalla domat, recte, ut dictum est, audaciam designat, quae neminem veretur, neminem formidat. Et quoniam aes caeteris omnibus est durabilius, recte per ipsum pertinaciam designari jam diximus. Quasi ergo in ferri, seu etiam aeris rigore prava consuetudo obdurescit, quando depravata mens tam pertinaciter quam audaciter assuetis desideriis insistit. Nonne ferreo et aereo vinculo alligatur quae 1325Anullius unquam terrore, nullius unquam admonitione ab assuetis concupiscentiis a vertitur? Nonne vincula ferrea vel aerea jam sentit, quae nec minis cedit, nec consiliis acquiescit? Constat ergo ejusmodi mentes omnino corrigi non posse, nisi in ejus utique potestate qui quoties voluit contrivit portas aereas, et vectes ferreos confregit. Portis sane procedentium transitum obstruimus, vectibus autem ipsam clausuram munimus atque obfirmamus. Quid itaque per portam accipimus nisi contradictionem, et quid per vectem nisi rebellionem? Et scimus quidem quia non sufficit quibusdam praelatorum suorum monitis vel mandatis contradicere, sed cum non possunt eorum instituta dictis refellere vel avertere, nituntur etiam factis obsistere, et operibus rebellare, 1325Bet quasi contradictionem suarum portas rebellionum vectibus munire. Si ergo, ut superius jam dictum est, per aes pertinacia, per ferrum recte intelligitur audacia, quid aliud erit porta aerea, nisi contradictio pertinax, et quid vectis ferreus, nisi rebellio audax? Quid itaque aliud est Dominum portas aereas et vectes ferreos confringere, quam pertinacem contradictionem et audacem rebellionem interna aspiratione et cordis compunctione coercere? Solius itaque Dei est, ut dictum est, hanc tantam cordis duritiem conterere, et ad poenitentiam et humiliationem emollire. Cum igitur aliquis, juxta praedictum modum, vinculo ferreo et aereo in herbis quae foris sunt alligatur, tantae obdurationis 1325Cet obligationis resolutio de sola Dei misericordia exspectetur.

CHAPTER XXV. To how great depravity the mind is sometimes hardened, which nevertheless is divinely reserved unto pardon.

It is truly a wonder how at a single outrage 1324Bmany often fall at once into despair. Others do not, even after many and very great and long-continued evils, lose the hope or the purpose of coming to their senses. And of this is rightly understood that also which is here still brought in concerning this tree, namely, that it be bound with a bond of iron and of bronze among the grasses which are without, and let it be dipped in the dew of heaven, and let its portion be with the wild beasts in the grass of the earth. Let his heart be changed from man's, and let the heart of a beast be given him (Dan. IV). From these things assuredly, as I judge, thou weighest how great the evils are; but hear yet how long-continued they are: And let seven, he says, times be changed over him. For what is more rightly understood in the bond of iron and of bronze, than the habit of untameable perversity? In the iron, audacity; in the bronze, obstinacy; in the grasses of the earth, the lowest goods, earthly goods. For what are these earthly 1324Cgoods, which are common to us with the beasts, but certain grasses of the earth. Of which it is rightly said: which are without. For by that very thing they exist without, in that they are bodily and carnal, and not spiritual. For the inward goods are spiritual, but the outward are bodily. Those belong to the inner man, these to the outer man. Those, then, are invisible, but these visible; those eternal, these temporal: For the things which are seen, says the Apostle, are temporal, but the things which are not seen, eternal (II Cor. IV). [Vulg. closes quae autem non videntur, aeterna sunt; Migne's printing drops the second sunt] But those belong to the mind, and these to the flesh. But all flesh is hay, and all the glory thereof as the flower of hay. When therefore the depraved mind has begun to incline itself to lowest goods of this kind, and as it were in the grass of the earth to sate its carnal desires, 1324Dand to bring delights of this kind into habitual use, then, as the crooked habit little by little grows strong, it often comes to pass at last that it can no longer temper its own appetite away from the love or the use of them. The depraved mind, therefore, is bound as it were with a bond of iron in the grass of the earth, when out of a crooked habit it is hardened in the love of carnal pleasures. Because iron tames all other metals, it rightly, as has been said, designates audacity, which reveres no one, dreads no one. And since bronze is more durable than all the rest, we have already said that obstinacy is rightly designated by it. The crooked habit, therefore, grows hard as it were in the rigidity of iron, or also of bronze, when the depraved mind presses upon its accustomed desires as obstinately as audaciously. Is not that mind bound with a bond of iron and of bronze which is turned away a vertitur 1325Afrom its accustomed concupiscences by the terror of no man ever, by the admonition of no man ever? Does it not already feel bonds of iron or of bronze, which neither yields to threats, nor acquiesces in counsels? It stands firm, therefore, that minds of this kind can in no wise be corrected, save assuredly in the power of him who, as often as he willed, has ground down the gates of bronze and shattered the bars of iron. With gates, surely, we block the passage of those going forth, but with bars we fortify and make fast the closure itself. What, then, do we understand by the gate but contradiction, and what by the bar but rebellion? And we know indeed that for certain men it is not enough to contradict the admonitions or the mandates of their prelates, but when they cannot refute or turn aside their ordinances by words, they strive also to withstand them by deeds, and to rebel against them by works, 1325Band as it were to fortify the gates of their contradiction with the bars of rebellions. If therefore, as has already been said above, by bronze is rightly understood obstinacy, and by iron audacity, what else will the gate of bronze be, but obstinate contradiction, and what the bar of iron, but audacious rebellion? What else, then, is it for the Lord to shatter the gates of bronze and the bars of iron, than to coerce obstinate contradiction and audacious rebellion by an inward inbreathing and by compunction of heart? It belongs, therefore, to God alone, as has been said, to grind down this so great hardness of heart, and to soften it unto repentance and humiliation. When therefore anyone, according to the aforesaid manner, is bound with a bond of iron and of bronze among the grasses which are without, let the loosing of so great a hardening 1325Cand binding be looked for from the mercy of God alone.

CAPUT XXVI. Quomodo mens, quae moribus depravatur, postmodum ad errores vel fraudes imbuitur.

Sed adhuc ad augmentum mali additur, quod adjungitur: Et rore coeli tingatur. Quid intelligimus per rorem coeli, nisi subtiles et vix intelligibiles suggestiones diaboli? Si enim per terram convenienter satis designatur corpus, cur non aeque per coelum recte designetur spiritus? Corporum itaque comparatione spiritales illae in daemonibus substantiae, coeli dici possunt. Et in Evangelio volucres coeli dicti sunt, non propter justitiam, non propter gloriam, sed propter angelicam spiritualemque naturam. 1325DRore itaque coeli mens depravata intingitur, quando per suggestiones diaboli diversis erroribus imbuitur. Ex concupiscentiarum enim suarum exsultatione, tantam saepe incurrit caligmem errorum, ut dicat malum bonum, et bonum malum, ponens tenebras lucem et lucem tenebras, ponens amarum in dulce et dulce in amarum. Qui igitur in herbis terrae foris alligatur, oportet ut rore tingatur: nam post depravationem morum, etiam sensus depravatur et multis erroribus facile implicatur. Hinc est, quod in eversione Hierusalem, rex prius filios, postea oculos amisit (IV Reg. XXV). Spirituales utique filios perdit, qui virtutum sobolem, quas diu nutrierat, amittit. Sed, filiis amissis, lumen oculorum eruitur, quia saepe ex depravatione voluntatum acumen intelligentiae 1326Aobducitur et obnubilatur. Qui coelestis itaque desideria amittit, qui terrenorum se amore involvit, quem quasi herbarum pastu vinculum cupiditatis astringit, necesse est quandoque errorum caliginem incurrere, et stillantibus velut coeli nubibus, quodam se seductionis rore inficere. Nonne et ille tibi rore coeli intingi videtur, qui spirituum malignorum suggestione ad omnem fraudem instruitur, ut ad eorum similitudinem libenter velit, et facile possit proximos suos decipere et fraudibus circumvenire? Absque dubio enim cum nimius amor terrenorum regnare incipit, paulatim quidem fraudulentiam inducit, ad extremum autem, ad violentiam et rapinam impellit. Unde est quod subjungit, et cum feris pars ejus in herba terrae.

CHAPTER XXVI. How the mind, which is depraved in its morals, is afterwards imbued with errors or deceits.

But there is still added, to the increase of the evil, that which is joined on: And let it be dipped in the dew of heaven. What do we understand by the dew of heaven, but the subtle and scarcely intelligible suggestions of the devil? For if by the earth the body is fittingly enough designated, why should not the spirit be equally rightly designated by heaven? In comparison with bodies, therefore, those spiritual substances in the demons can be called heavens. And in the Gospel they have been called birds of heaven, not on account of justice, not on account of glory, but on account of their angelic and spiritual nature. 1325DThe depraved mind, therefore, is dipped in the dew of heaven, when through the suggestions of the devil it is imbued with divers errors. For out of the exultation of its own concupiscences it often runs into so great a darkness caligmem of errors, that it calls evil good, and good evil, putting darkness light and light darkness, putting bitter for sweet and sweet for bitter. He therefore who is bound without among the grasses of the earth must needs be dipped in the dew: for after the depravation of morals, the sense also is depraved and is easily entangled in many errors. Hence it is that in the overthrow of Jerusalem the king first lost his sons, and afterwards his eyes (IV Reg. XXV). He assuredly loses spiritual sons, who loses the offspring of the virtues which he had long nourished. But when the sons have been lost, the light of the eyes is plucked out, because often from the depravation of the wills the keenness of understanding 1326Ais overdrawn and beclouded. He therefore who loses the desires of the heavenly, who wraps himself in the love of earthly things, whom the bond of cupidity binds fast as it were by a pasturing upon grasses, must needs at some time run into the darkness of errors, and, as the clouds of heaven drop down, infect himself with a certain dew of seduction. Does not he also seem to thee to be dipped in the dew of heaven, who by the suggestion of malignant spirits is trained unto every deceit, so that after their likeness he may willingly will, and easily be able, to deceive his neighbours and to circumvent them by frauds? For without doubt, when an excessive love of earthly things begins to reign, it little by little brings in fraudulence, but at the last it drives on to violence and rapine. Whence it is that he subjoins, and let its portion be with the wild beasts in the grass of the earth.

CAPUT XXVII. Quomodo mens ad omnem crudelitatem quandoque effrenatur, cum tamen divinitus ad poenitentiam reservetur.

  • 1326B

Et mirum quidem, nec tamen minus verum, quod quosdam divina dispositio ad summam crudelitatem effrenari permittit, quos tamen ad poenitentiam reservare disponit, utpote quos ad vitam aeternam praeordinavit. Dicitur ergo de hujusmodi homine: Et cum feris pars ejus in herba terrae. Per feras quae dente et ungue saeviunt (unde et a feritate, feroces dictae sunt) recte satis crudeles quique intelligi possunt. De qualibus Psalmista: Filii hominum, inquit, dentes eorum arma, et sagittae, 1326Cet lingua eorum gladius acutus (Psal. LVI). Per herbam terrae intelligimus oblectamenta carnis moriturae et gloriam transeuntis vitae. Omnis siquidem caro fenum, et omnis gloria ejus quasi flos feni. Partem igitur cum crudelibus, et quasi cum feris in herba terrae habere videntur, qui propter avaritiam et ambitionem ad rapinam et fraudem et ad omnem crudelitatem effrenantur. Quid enim est cum crudelitatis ministris, tabulisque feris partem habere, nisi cum talibus sociis, sicut in morum similitudine, sic et in retributionum debito sortem accipere? Econtra autem Propheta talium consortia habere, vel cum eis partem accipere summopere declinabat, cum dicebat: Odivi Ecclesiam malignantium, et cum impiis non sedebo (Psal. C). Unde et 1326Dalias: Non adhaesit mihi cor pravum (ibid.). Cor ejus ab humano commutetur. Cor animae praevaricatricis et in ima ruentis ab humano commutatur, quando totius humanitatis et pietatis affectus omnino amittitur, quando temporalium amore et ex avaritiae vitio ad opera misericordiae medullitus obduratur, secundum illud. Viscera avari crudelia (Prov. XII). Post tot autem corruptionis gradus, adhuc in augmentum mali et illud additur, quod subinfertur, et cor ferae detur ei. Cor ferae datur et accipitur, quando proximus proximum suum sine alicujus commodi respectu, solo malignitatis instinctu, tam inaniter quam crudeliter prosequitur. Audi quid sentiat Propheta de talibus: Virum sanguinum, inquit, et dolosum abominabitur Dominus (Psal. V).

CHAPTER XXVII. How the mind is sometimes let loose unto every cruelty, though it be nevertheless divinely reserved unto repentance.

  • 1326B

And it is a wonder indeed, yet none the less true, that the divine disposition permits some to be let loose unto the highest cruelty, whom nevertheless it disposes to reserve unto repentance, inasmuch as it has preordained them unto eternal life. It is said, therefore, of a man of this kind: And let his portion be with the wild beasts in the grass of the earth. By the wild beasts, which rage with tooth and claw (whence also from their fierceness they are called ferocious), all cruel men can rightly enough be understood. Of such the Psalmist: The sons of men, he says, their teeth are weapons and arrows, 1326Cand their tongue a sharp sword (Psal. LVI). By the grass of the earth we understand the delights of a flesh that is to die and the glory of a life that passes. For all flesh is hay, and all the glory thereof as the flower of hay. They therefore seem to have a portion with the cruel, and as it were with the wild beasts in the grass of the earth, who for the sake of avarice and ambition are let loose unto rapine and deceit and unto every cruelty. For what is it to have a portion with the ministers of cruelty, and with wild planks, but to receive a lot with such companions in the due of retributions, even as in the likeness of morals? But on the contrary the Prophet utterly shunned to have the fellowships of such men, or to receive a portion with them, when he said: I have hated the church of the malignant, and with the wicked I will not sit (Psal. C). Whence also 1326Delsewhere: The crooked heart hath not cleaved to me (ibid.). Let his heart be changed from man's. The heart of a soul that prevaricates and rushes into the depths is changed from man's, when all affection of humanity and of mercy is utterly lost, when out of the love of temporal things and out of the vice of avarice it is hardened to the marrow against the works of mercy, according to that word. The bowels of the covetous man are cruel (Prov. XII). [Vulg. Prov. 12:10 viscera autem impiorum crudelia, "the bowels of the wicked"; Migne prints avari] But after so many steps of corruption, that also is still added to the increase of the evil which is brought in below, and let the heart of a beast be given him. The heart of a beast is given and received, when a neighbour pursues his neighbour without regard to any advantage of his own, by the mere instinct of malignity, as emptily as cruelly. Hear what the Prophet feels concerning such men: The bloody and deceitful man, he says, the Lord will abhor (Psal. V).

CAPUT XXVIII. Quomodo malum grassatur in his qui semper in deteriora labuntur.

  • 1327A

Attende, obsecro, quomodo malum grassatur. Prius amittuntur bona, postea committuntur et mala. In excussione foliorum, et dispersione fructuum, intellige detrimenta bonorum. In alligatione in herbis et obduratione cordis, intellige incrementa malorum.

Illud autem notandum, quibus gradibus accrescat accumulatio vitiorum, prius in avaritiam dejicitur, tandem autem per multiplices corruptionis gradus ad omnem crudelitatem effrenatur. Primo itaque vinculo ferreo et aereo praevaricatrix anima ligatur, quando cum forti labore et pertinaci sollicitudine, circa terrenorum acquisitionem infatigabiliter occupatur. 1327BRore coeli tingitur, quando maligna suggestione ad fraudem imbuitur, ut ad avaritiae instigationem, quod non potest per industriam, acquirat per fraudulentiam. Fit autem tandem cum feris pars ejus in herbis terrae, quando id quod non potest per industriam, neque per fraudem acquirere cupit, ac satagit per violentiam et rapinam obtinere. Ecce quomodo morbus avaritiae crescit, et malum crudelitatis inducit. Est autem primus gradus saevitiae, quando pro aliqua sui commoditate praesumit, vel sustinet (non tamen sine conscientiae remorsu) in proximum suum saevire. Cor ab humano commutatur, quando nulla compassione movetur, nulla humanitate mollitur, quando exercendis rapinis nullo 1327Cdivini timoris respectu, nulla poenae vel nulla penitus conscientiae stimulatione mordetur. Cor ferae accipit qui gratis etiam saevit, qui sine causa aliqua ad apertam malitiam erumpit. Primus itaque malitiae gradus est, quando sine causa omnino erga proximum suum obduratur. Secundus autem, quando in miseriis proximi nulla compassione tangitur. Tertius et pessimus, quando jam in ejus malis pascitur et delectatur.

CHAPTER XXVIII. How evil goes rioting on in those who ever slip into worse things.

  • 1327A

Attend, I beseech thee, how evil goes rioting on. First goods are lost, afterwards evils also are committed. In the shaking off of the leaves, and the scattering of the fruits, understand the declines of goods. In the binding among the grasses and the hardening of the heart, understand the increases of evils.

But this is to be noted, by what steps the accumulation of vices grows: first one is cast down into avarice, but at last through manifold steps of corruption is let loose unto every cruelty. First, then, the prevaricating soul is bound with a bond of iron and of bronze, when with strong labour and obstinate solicitude it is untiringly occupied about the acquisition of earthly things. 1327BIt is dipped in the dew of heaven, when by a malignant suggestion it is imbued unto deceit, so that at the instigation of avarice it may acquire by fraudulence what it cannot acquire by effort. But at last its portion comes to be with the wild beasts in the grasses of the earth, when that which it can acquire neither by effort nor by deceit, it desires and busies itself to obtain by violence and rapine. Behold how the disease of avarice grows, and brings in the evil of cruelty. Now the first step of savagery is when for some advantage of his own a man presumes, or endures (yet not without remorse of conscience), to rage against his neighbour. The heart is changed from man's, when it is moved by no compassion, softened by no humanity, when in the practising of rapines it is stung by no 1327Cregard of the divine fear, by no goading of punishment or of conscience whatsoever. He receives the heart of a beast who rages even gratuitously, who without any cause breaks out into open malice. The first step of malice, then, is when without any cause at all a man is hardened toward his neighbour. The second, when in his neighbour's miseries he is touched by no compassion. The third and worst, when he now feeds and delights himself in that neighbour's evils.

CAPUT XXIX. Quod saepe contingat mentem omniformiter corrumpi, quam tamen constat ad poenitentiam ex divina miseratione reservari.

Unum autem adhuc, et velut ultimo loco additur in quo severitatis sententia consummari videtur. 1327DEt septem, inquit, tempora mutentur super eum. Mutantur septem tempora, quando variis corruptionis gradibus infirmitatis nostrae qualitas variatur, dum semper in deteriora vertitur. Nonne tunc aliud tempus, et velut priori contrarium incurrimus quando vitio cui prius resistere potuimus, jam omnino non posse praevalere incipimus? Tempus itaque circa quemlibet multipliciter mutatur, quando corruptionis ejus modus multiformiter variatur. Vitiorum siquidem dominatio animum paulatim emollit, et semper magis magisque debilem reddit, et gradatim ad deteriora impellit. Septem autem diebus, ut in commune novimus, totum tempus evolvitur, et idcirco saepe ipso septenario universitas figuratur. Item capitalia vitia septem esse leguntur. Hinc est 1328Aquod in Evangelio legimus immundum spiritum septem spiritus secum introducere, et domum suam quam ad tempus reliquerat inhabitare, quia unum quodlibet vitium saepe cum dominari jam coeperit, solet totum illud detestandum vitiorum collegium pedetentim subintroducere. Hinc econtrario mulier peccatrix a septem daemoniis purgata describitur, quae ab omni vitiorum tyrannide per septiformem gratiam liberatur. Septenaria itaque temporum mutatio, est multiformis, imo universalis morum depravatio. Quasi enim septem super eum tempora mutantur qui omniformiter corrumpitur, et ad omnem vitiorum servitutem tandem aliquando enerviter inclinatur. Sed vide modo quod sit mirabile quod divina miseratio solet quandoque hujusmodi 1328Bhominem ad poenitentiam reservare.

CHAPTER XXIX. That it often happens that the mind is corrupted in every form, which nevertheless is agreed to be reserved unto repentance out of the divine mercy.

But one thing more is added, and as it were in the last place, in which the sentence of severity seems to be consummated. 1327DAnd let seven, he says, times be changed over him. Seven times are changed, when by the various steps of corruption the quality of our infirmity is varied, while it is ever turned unto worse things. Do we not then run into another time, and one as it were contrary to the former, when against a vice which we were before able to resist we now begin to be wholly unable to prevail? The time, then, is manifoldly changed about anyone, when the manner of his corruption is multiformly varied. For the domination of the vices little by little softens the spirit, and renders it ever more and more feeble, and step by step drives it on to worse things. But in seven days, as we commonly know, all time is unrolled, and therefore universality is often figured by that same sevenfold number. Likewise the capital vices are read to be seven. Hence it is 1328Athat in the Gospel we read that the unclean spirit brings in seven spirits with him, and inhabits his house which he had left for a time, because any one vice, when it has already begun to have dominion, is wont step by step to bring in after it that whole detestable college of vices. Hence on the contrary the sinful woman is described as purged of seven demons, who is freed from every tyranny of the vices by the sevenfold grace. The sevenfold change of times, then, is a multiform, nay rather a universal depravation of morals. For seven times are as it were changed over him who is corrupted in every form, and who at last at some time nervelessly bends to every servitude of the vices. But see now how wonderful it is that the divine mercy is sometimes wont to reserve a man of this kind 1328Bunto repentance.

CAPUT XXX. Quomodo soleat multorum utilitati deservire quod quosdam contingat de sublimi corruere.

In sententia vigilum decretum est, et sermo sanctorum, et petitio, donec cognoscant viventes quod dominatur Excelsus in regno hominum, et cuicunque voluerit dabit illud, et humillimum hominem constituet super eo (Dan. IV). Ut superius jam diximus, per vigiles hoc loco sanctos angelos intelligimus. Apostolo autem docente, didicimus quia omnes administratorii spiritus missi sunt propter eos qui haereditatem capiunt salutis. Salutis itaque nostrae cooperatores angeli, et divinae ordinationis 1328Cministri humana disponunt, prout communi hominum utilitati congruere, Deo revelante, cognoscunt. Solet autem saepe multorum utilitati militare quod quosdam contingit ex magno sublimitatis culmine corruere, ut discant caeteri non altum sapere, sed de semetipsis semper humilia sentire, et in quolibet virtutum culmine morum ruinam timere, et veraciter agnoscant quia solus Dominus est qui operatur in nobis velle et perficere pro bona voluntate. Hanc ejusmodi ruinae utilitatem propheticus sermo innuit, dicens: Donec cognoscant viventes quod dominatur Excelsus in regno hominum. Divina itaque dispensatione, cum communis utilitas hoc exigit, magnae auctoritatis viri permittuntur cadere, eo duntaxat tempore 1328Dquo inveniuntur de sua magnitudine superbire. Fit itaque circa eamdem personam unum idemque judicium, tam justum quam pium, quantum ad ipsam quidem justum, pium vero inquantum proficit ad cautelam aliorum. Justum namque valde est, ut qui ex divinis muneribus insolenter intumescit, ex propria ruina discat quam nihil sit, et per semetipsum quam nihil possit. Sermo autem sanctorum idem decretum idcirco esse dicitur, quia quod in lumine Dei futurum a quibusdam praevidetur, in commune confertur. Petitio vero sanctorum est, quia non solum fieri volunt, verum etiam cum desiderio expetunt quod multis utile, et divinae gloriae militare praenoscunt. Erit itaque ejusmodi judicium sanctorum decretum, quia misericorditer justum, 1329Asermo sanctorum, quia veraciter futurum. Petitio, quia juste pium.

CHAPTER XXX. How it is wont to serve the profit of many that some men happen to fall from a height.

In the sentence of the watchers it is decreed, and the word of the holy ones, and the petition, until the living know that the Most High ruleth in the kingdom of men, and to whomsoever he will he shall give it, and he shall set the lowliest man over it (Dan. IV). [Vulg. Dan. 4:14 donec cognoscant viventes quod dominetur Excelsus in regno hominum … constituet super eum; Migne prints the indicative dominatur and super eo] As we have already said above, by the watchers in this place we understand the holy angels. But by the Apostle's teaching we have learned that all ministering spirits are sent for the sake of those who receive the inheritance of salvation. The angels, therefore, fellow-workers of our salvation and ministers of the divine 1328Cordination, dispose human affairs according as they know, by God's revealing, that it agrees with the common profit of men. But it is often wont to serve the profit of many that some men happen to fall from a great summit of loftiness, that the rest may learn not to be high-minded, but always to think humble things concerning themselves, and upon any summit of the virtues to fear the ruin of their morals, and may truly acknowledge that it is the Lord alone who works in us to will and to accomplish according to good will. [Vulg. Phil. 2:13 qui operatur in vobis et velle et perficere, "in you"; Migne prints in nobis and drops the first et] This profit of such a ruin the prophetic word intimates, saying: Until the living know that the Most High ruleth in the kingdom of men. [Vulg. dominetur, subjunctive] By the divine dispensation, therefore, when the common profit demands this, men of great authority are permitted to fall, at that time only 1328Din which they are found to grow proud over their own greatness. And so about one and the same person there comes one and the same judgment, as just as it is merciful: just indeed as regards that person, but merciful inasmuch as it profits toward the wariness of others. For it is exceedingly just that he who insolently swells up over the divine gifts should learn from his own ruin how much he is nothing, and how much he can do nothing by himself. But the same decree is said to be also the word of the holy ones, for this reason, that what is foreseen by certain of them in the light of God as to come is brought into common. But it is the petition of the holy ones, because they not only will it to be done, but even with desire seek after that which they foreknow to be useful to many and to serve the divine glory. A judgment of this kind, then, will be the decree of the holy ones, because it is mercifully just; 1329Athe word of the holy ones, because it is truly to come. A petition, because it is justly merciful.

CAPUT XXXI. Quomodo addiscant quidam ex proprio, quidam ex alieno casu, quod nihil suis viribus attribuere debeant.

Donec cognoscant viventes quod dominetur excelsus in regno hominum. Ecce utilitas, ut cognoscant viventes. Affectus in nobis sane secundum Deum vivunt, dum in bono fortes sunt, et ad virtutum opera convalescunt, dum gratiam cooperantem habent, et in studiis spiritualibus se exercere valent. Tunc procul dubio moriuntur, cum in eis gratia cooperatrix subtrahitur. Subtrahitur autem cum de suis bonis et divinis donis mens improba superbit, et sua sibi merita ascribit. Subtracta gratia, affectus 1329Bboni deficiunt et ad omne bonum languescunt, et sic de viventibus mortui fiunt. Quid enim aliud est virtutum defectus, quam quasi quidam illarum interitus? Qui igitur infirmitatem suam, gratiamque divinam tunc primum attendunt cum jam in bono deficiunt, isti post mortem, et quasi per mortem divinae dominationis, et moderationis magisterium vel beneficium discunt et agnoscunt. Quid igitur est supernae majestatis dominium et principatum in regno hominum viventes agnoscere, nisi unumquemque eo etiam tempore dum adhuc in bonis operibus vivit infirmitatem suam attendere, et nihil sibi, nihil suis meritis ascribere, et inter quaelibet virtutum exercitia humilitatis custodiam non deserere, 1329Csed veraciter et absque simulatione quidquid mali, sibi ipsi, quidquid boni divinae gratiae ascribere? Et solet quidem pietas superna viros etiam virtutum in quibusdam humiliare, et gratiam subtrahere, donec discant inter quaelibet morum insignia nil altum sapere, nihil de propria virtute praesumere. Felices qui alieno casu potius quam proprio, humanae fragilitatis infirma addiscunt, et humilitatis gratiam proficiunt. Sed et cum eis quidem bene agitur qui ex propria ruina ad humilitatis custodiam evigilant, cum tandem aliquando post infirmitatis suae lapsum fortiores resurgant. Utriusque rei typus in regis Nabuchodonosor ruina vel reparatione exprimitur, quia et idem ipse post ruinam ad maiorem gloriam reparatur, et multi per ejus ruinam ad circumspectionis 1329Dcautelam erudiuntur. Donec cognoscant, inquit, viventes quod dominatur Excelsus in regno hominum. Viventes itaque est ejusmodi cognitionem habere, justum quaelibet et coelestem vitam ducentem in bono studio perseverare, et nihil sibi de suis bonis attribuere. Viventes utique ejusmodi cognitionem habent, quando et in bona intentione perdurant, et nihil de sua virtute praesumunt, sed totum quod utiliter possunt, Domino ascribunt, eo quod ipse sit qui solus dominatur in regno hominum quoniam Domini est regnum, et ipse dominabitur gentium. Sane et illud regnum exterius quo homines hominibus praeesse solent, et illud item interius quo vitiis et passionibus suis principari debent ab ipso disponitur, et dispensatur, et utrumque ex ejus 1330Amunere possidetur. Sed illud utique vitare et fugere omnino oportet, sicut magistra veritas proprio exemplo nos docet. Istud autem debemus desiderare, et quaerere, et cum summo desiderio postulare, et cum Propheta supplicare: Da imperium puero tuo (Psal. LXXXV). Nunquidnam, quaeso, animam prophetalem tantae ambitionis credere oportet, ut regnum Israeliticum a Domino peteret, et ab ejusmodi eum petitione nec saltem pudor cohiberet? Sed si non potuit cohibere linguam ne hoc peteret, cur non saltem prohibuit manum ne tale aliquid scriberet? Sed absit de anima tam sancta et anima, prophetali tale aliquid suspicari. Sed tale utique regnum, vel imperium de Domini pietate postulabat, unde salutis suae proventum, vel provectum sperabat. 1330BDa imperium, inquit, puero tuo, et salvum fac filium ancillae tuae. Pete ergo et tu tale imperium, vel regnum a Domino Deo tuo, ut pro hac parte psallere possis: Ego autem constitutus sum rex ab eo (Psal. II). Vera namque satis sententia, cuicunque voluerit dabit illud. Non est enim volentis, neque currentis, sed miserentis Dei. Si enim nihil habes quod non accepisti, quanto magis regnum?

CHAPTER XXXI. How some men learn from their own fall, and some from another's, that they ought to attribute nothing to their own powers.

Until the living know that the most High ruleth in the kingdom of men. Behold the profit: that the living may know. The affections in us surely live according to God, while they are strong in good, and grow strong unto the works of the virtues, while they have cooperating grace, and are able to exercise themselves in spiritual zeals. Then without doubt they die, when the cooperating grace is withdrawn in them. But it is withdrawn when the wicked mind grows proud over its own goods and over the divine gifts, and ascribes its own merits to itself. When grace has been withdrawn, the good affections 1329Bfail and languish toward every good, and thus from living they become dead. For what else is the failure of the virtues, than as it were a certain perishing of them? They therefore who first attend to their own infirmity and to the divine grace only when they are already failing in good, these men learn and acknowledge the mastery, or the benefit, of the divine dominion and moderation after death, and as it were through death. What, then, is it for the living to acknowledge the dominion and princedom of the supernal majesty in the kingdom of men, but for each one, even at that time while he still lives in good works, to attend to his own infirmity, and to ascribe nothing to himself, nothing to his own merits, and amid any exercises whatever of the virtues not to desert the guard of humility, 1329Cbut truly and without simulation to ascribe whatever of evil to himself, whatever of good to the divine grace? And the supernal mercy is indeed wont to humble even men of virtues in certain things, and to withdraw grace, until they learn amid any distinctions of morals to be in nothing high-minded, to presume nothing concerning their own virtue. Happy are they who learn the weak things of human frailty from another's fall rather than from their own, and advance in the grace of humility. But it goes well indeed even with those who from their own ruin wake up to the guard of humility, since at last at some time after the fall of their infirmity they rise again the stronger. The type of both things is expressed in the ruin, or the repair, of king Nabuchodonosor, because he himself also is repaired after his ruin unto greater glory, and many through his ruin are instructed unto the 1329Dwariness of circumspection. Until the living know, he says, that the Most High ruleth in the kingdom of men. It belongs, then, to the living to have knowledge of this kind: for one leading a just life of whatsoever sort, and a heavenly one, to persevere in good zeal, and to attribute nothing to himself out of his own goods. The living assuredly have knowledge of this kind, when they both endure in a good intention, and presume nothing concerning their own virtue, but ascribe to the Lord all that they usefully can, in that he it is who alone ruleth in the kingdom of men, since the kingdom is the Lord's, and he shall rule over the nations. Surely both that outward kingdom by which men are wont to be set over men, and likewise that inward one by which they ought to have princedom over their own vices and passions, are disposed by him and dispensed, and both are possessed out of his 1330Agift. But the former we must assuredly avoid and flee altogether, even as the mistress Truth teaches us by her own example. But the latter we ought to desire, and to seek, and with the highest desire to ask for, and with the Prophet to entreat: Give rule to thy servant (Psal. LXXXV). [Vulg. Ps. 85:16 Da imperium tuum puero tuo, "thy command"; Migne drops tuum] Must we, I ask, believe the prophetic soul to be of so great ambition that he would seek the kingdom of Israel from the Lord, and that not even shame would restrain him from a petition of that kind? But if he could not restrain his tongue from seeking this, why did he not at least forbid his hand to write any such thing? But far be it to suspect any such thing of a soul so holy, and a soul prophetic. But he was assuredly asking of the Lord's mercy such a kingdom, or rule, whence he hoped for the outcome, or the advancement, of his own salvation. 1330BGive rule, he says, to thy servant, and save the son of thy handmaid. [Vulg. Da imperium tuum puero tuo; Migne again drops tuum] Ask thou also, therefore, such rule, or kingdom, from the Lord thy God, that on this behalf thou mayest be able to sing: But I am appointed king by him (Psal. II). For it is a saying true enough: to whomsoever he will he shall give it. For it is not of him that willeth, nor of him that runneth, but of God that sheweth mercy. [Vulg. Rom. 9:16 Igitur non volentis, neque currentis, sed miserentis est Dei; Migne prints enim and moves the copula forward, where chunk 0040 prints the same verse at 1323D as Non enim volentis neque currentis, sed miserentis est Dei] For if thou hast nothing which thou hast not received, how much more a kingdom?

CAPUT XXXII. Quod ex modo humilitatis pendet mensura magnitudinis.

Et humillimum, inquit, hominem constituet super eo. Superbis namque resistit, humilibus autem dat gratiam, et certe quanto humiliorem, tanto sublimiorem. 1330CAbsque dubio si illud internum regnum cogitemus, eris in hoc regno quanto humilior, tanto sublimior, et tanto sine ambiguitate major, quanto in oculis tuis fueris minor. Cuinam, quaeso, dubium esse queat quod in illo exteriori regno multo aliter, seu econtrario saepe contingat? In illo autem interiori regno semper ex mensura humilitatis pendet mensura magnitudinis. Quia humilem in eo, et humillimum constituet super eo. Humilis est qui seipsum apud semetipsum veraciter contemnit, humilior autem qui se contemni etiam ab aliis non refugit. Humillimus vero qui contemptum suum non solum contemnit, sed et admodum concupiscit. Quid itaque, quaeso in illum superbia possit, cui despectus suus in despectum venit? Cum igitur, attestante 1330DScriptura, initium sit omnis peccati superbia (Eccle. X), quid, quaeso, contra illum possit peccatum cui ex victoria superbiae longe est omnis peccati initium? Qui igitur ejusmodi est vide quam fortis et potens in illo regno invenitur, quod contra peccatum statuitur, quod a solo peccato destruitur, vel dissipatur. Ecce jam ex fine visionis manifeste colligimus quod longe superius praelocuti sumus, quia commune bonum et universitatis utilitas exigebat talem hominem, forte qualis designatur in arbore, talem, inquam, hominem qui juxta similitudinem arboris de sublimi quodam statu caderet et iterum resurgeret, ut ex sui ruina, stantibus metum cadendi incuteret, et ex sui reparatione jacentes ad spem resurgendi reformaret.

CHAPTER XXXII. That the measure of greatness hangs upon the manner of humility.

And he shall set, he says, the lowliest man over it. For he resisteth the proud, but to the humble he giveth grace, and assuredly the humbler a man is, the more sublime. 1330CWithout doubt, if we think upon that inward kingdom, thou wilt be in this kingdom the more sublime the humbler thou art, and without ambiguity the greater, the less thou shalt have been in thine own eyes. To whom, I ask, can it be doubtful that in that outward kingdom it often happens far otherwise, or rather contrariwise? But in that inward kingdom the measure of greatness ever hangs upon the measure of humility. Because he will set the humble man in it, and the lowliest over it. He is humble who truly despises himself in his own sight, but he is humbler who does not shrink from being despised also by others. But he is the lowliest who not only despises his own contempt, but even greatly covets it. What, then, I ask, can pride do against him, to whom his own contempt has come into contempt? Since therefore, as 1330DScripture testifies, pride is the beginning of all sin (Eccle. X), what, I ask, can sin do against him, from whom, by the victory over pride, the beginning of all sin is far off? See therefore how strong and how powerful he who is of this kind is found in that kingdom, which is established against sin, which is destroyed or scattered by sin alone. Behold, now from the end of the vision we manifestly gather what we spoke of far above, that the common good and the profit of the universe demanded such a man, perhaps such as is designated in the tree; such a man, I say, who according to the likeness of the tree should fall from a certain sublime state and rise again, so that out of his own ruin he might strike into those who stand a fear of falling, and out of his own repair might reform those who lie prostrate unto the hope of rising again.

CAPUT XXXIII. Quod aliud sit divina judicia per contemplationem cernere, atque aliud eorum rationem perpendere.

  • 1331A

Ecce somnium quod Nabuchodonosor vidit, sed adhuc ejus interpretationem requirit, unde et dicit: Hoc somnium vidi ego rex Nabuchodonosor. Tu ergo Balthassar interpretationem narra festinus, quia omnes sapientes regni mei nequeunt solutionem edicere mihi. Tu autem potes, quia spiritus deorum sanctorum in te est (Dan. IV). Sicut in superioribus jam dixi, aliud est judicia divina perspicaciae vel praevidentiae oculo cernere, atque aliud est eorum rationem discretionis judicio et intelligentiae vivacitate penetrare atque cognoscere. Illud ad somnii visionem, istud pertinet ad somnii interpretationem. 1331BAd somnii visionem pertinet ex divina revelatione cognoscere quid contingere oporteat, ad interpretationem autem somnii pertinet intelligere qualitatem personae cui hoc accidere debeat, causam videlicet perpendere quae ad ejusmodi casum qualemcunque personam impellere soleat. Hoc est quod Daniel investigando invenit, et regi interpretando aperuit, cum dicit: Tu es arbor illa quam vidisti. Quid enim est dicere, tu es, nisi talem te esse perpendo, talem in te causam invenio, unde tale aliquid tibi accidere debere non dubito? Vis autem adhuc apertius nosse quomodo post ostensam divinitus visionem solemus, imo et debemus ejus interpretationem quaerere? Ecce forte quis per contemplationis lucem videt quomodo quorumdam sublimium virorum lapsum, 1331Cet reparationem communis utilitas requirat. Videt quomodo hoc ipsum ad gratiam Dei commendat, quomodo hoc ipsum stantes adhuc ad cautelam informat, quomodo lapsos ad spem reparat, quomodo ex hoc universitatis pulchritudo mirabilior appareat, et certum habet quia fieri oporteat, eo quod omnipotens bonitas commune bonum impedire nolit, nec debeat. Sed iterum mirari incipit quomodo possit summae justitiae convenire, vel quomodo possit justum esse, ut vel propter universitatis utilitatem, permittantur aliqui cadere. Nam, sicut universitatis debitor est qui omnia fecit, sic et singulorum debitor est qui singula instituit. Et, sicut pium est commune bonum non impedire, sic et justum esse 1331Doportet, semper aliquam causam lapsuro inesse propter quam merito debeat imminenti patere ruinae. Qui igitur ejusmodi causam nosse concupiscit, et divinorum judiciorum rationem summopere quaerit, quid aliud quam ostensae visionis interpretationem inquirit? Tunc autem ejusmodi interpretationem quasi a Daniel exigit quando quod per investigationem non potest, praecipuo, et summae devotionis studio a Domino extorquere non desistit. Hoc somnium, inquit, vidi ego Nabuchodonosor. Tu ergo, Balthasar, interpretationem ejus narra festinus. Perpendis, ut arbitror, quia alia rex per seipsum videt, et alia a Daniele extorquet, quia alia sunt quae animo libere et ex facili occurrunt, alia quae sine magno labore, et ingenti sollicitudine sciri non possunt. In illis 1332Aanimus provocatur ut ad majora assurgat, in istis exercetur et humiliatur, ne praesumat. Innuitur autem quod in his quae rex viderat, sine timore non fuerit qui interpretationem cum festinatione requirit. Admonemur autem et nos ejus rei absolutionem cum omni festinatione quaerere, ubi periculi suspicio potest metum incutere.

CHAPTER XXXIII. That it is one thing to discern the divine judgments through contemplation, and another to weigh the reason of them.

  • 1331A
