Behold the dream which Nabuchodonosor saw; but he still requires its interpretation, whence also he says: This dream I, king Nabuchodonosor, saw. Do thou therefore, Balthassar, tell the interpretation in haste, because all the wise men of my kingdom are unable to declare the solution to me. But thou art able, because the spirit of the holy gods is in thee (Dan. IV). [Vulg. Dan. 4:15 Hoc somnium vidi ego Nabuchodonosor rex; Migne inverts the title to rex Nabuchodonosor, and prints nequeunt where the Vulgate has non queunt] As I have already said in what went before, it is one thing to discern the divine judgments with the eye of perspicacity or of foresight, and another to penetrate and know the reason of them by the judgment of discretion and the liveliness of understanding. The former belongs to the vision of the dream, the latter to the interpretation of the dream. 1331BTo the vision of the dream it belongs to know from divine revelation what must come to pass; but to the interpretation of the dream it belongs to understand the quality of the person to whom this must happen, namely, to weigh the cause which is wont to drive a person of whatever kind to a fall of this sort. This is what Daniel found out by investigating, and opened to the king by interpreting, when he says: Thou art that tree which thou sawest. For what is it to say, thou art, but: I weigh thee to be such a one, I find such a cause in thee, whence I doubt not that some such thing must happen to thee? But wilt thou know yet more openly how, after a vision has been divinely shown, we are wont, nay rather ought, to seek its interpretation? Behold, perhaps someone sees through the light of contemplation how the common profit requires the fall 1331Cand the repair of certain sublime men. He sees how this very thing commends the grace of God, how this very thing forms those who still stand unto wariness, how it repairs the fallen unto hope, how from this the beauty of the universe appears more wonderful; and he holds it certain that it must come to pass, in that the omnipotent goodness neither wills nor ought to hinder the common good. But again he begins to wonder how it can agree with the highest justice, or how it can be just, that some should be permitted to fall even for the profit of the universe. For as he who made all things is debtor to the universe, so he who established each several thing is debtor also to each. And as it is merciful not to hinder the common good, so it must also be 1331Djust that there be always in him who is about to fall some cause on account of which he deservedly ought to lie open to the impending ruin. He therefore who covets to know a cause of this kind, and utterly seeks the reason of the divine judgments, what else does he inquire after than the interpretation of the vision that has been shown? But then he demands an interpretation of this kind as it were from Daniel, when what he cannot obtain by investigation he ceases not to wrest from the Lord by a chief zeal, and one of the highest devotion. This dream, he says, I Nabuchodonosor saw. Do thou therefore, Balthasar, tell the interpretation of it in haste. Thou weighest, as I judge, that the king sees some things by himself, and wrests others from Daniel, because some things are such as occur to the spirit freely and easily, others such as cannot be known without great labour and huge solicitude. In the former 1332Athe spirit is provoked to rise up to greater things; in the latter it is exercised and humbled, lest it presume. But it is intimated that in those things which the king had seen he was not without fear, who requires the interpretation with haste. And we too are admonished to seek with all haste the resolution of that matter wherein a suspicion of danger can strike in fear.

CAPUT XXXIV. Quod eorum notitiam saepe merito devotionis acquirimus quae nulla nostra sagacitate, nullo nostro studio penetrare valemus.

Notandum sane quod Daniel Balthassar, hoc est capillus capitis vocatur, ubi ad subtilem et sublimium absolutionem invitatur. In humanis etenim membris quid capite sublimius, quid capillo subtilius? 1332BRecte ergo capillus capitis dicitur, a quo subtilitas sublimis intelligentiae exigitur. Et vera mentis devotio ab effectu recte, ni fallor, capillus capitis dicitur, quia per ipsam absque dubio ad omnem subtilitatem acuitur. Per effectum itaque ipsa est capillus capitis, ipsa subtilitas mentis. Audiamus adhuc quid ad haec subjungat: Quia omnes sapientes regni mei nequeunt solutionem edicere mihi. Qui sunt, quaeso, sapientes illius interioris regni nisi acerrima acumina, et peracuti sensus cordis humani? Attende ergo quae sit virtus devotionis, quam recte dicatur capillus capitis, si per ipsam mens potest quod per omnes regni sui sapientes non potest. Cum igitur mens quod supra suam intelligentiam multiformiter quaerit, quaerendo insistit, insistendo 1332Cdeficit, deficiendo diffidit, quid aliud, quaeso, jam apud se tacita per diffidentiam loquitur nisi hoc quod rex Nabuchodonosor palam profitetur? Quia omnes sapientes regni mei nequeunt solutionem edicere mihi. Sed cum Veritas dicat: Amen dico vobis, quidquid orantes petitis, credite quia accipietis, et fiet vobis (Marc. XI). Si igitur mens credit quod audit, si se ad orationem convertit, si per veram et intimam devotionem se posse obtinere non diffidit quod per investigationem non potuit, quid aliud, quaeso, per spei fiduciam, fideique constantiam apud semetipsum asserit nisi idem idipsum quod rex ad Danielem: Tu autem potes, inquit? Et causam quoque adjungit: Quia spiritus deorum sanctorum in te est. Scimus 1332Dquia in Scripturis sanctis homines sancti dicti sunt dii, et potest eadem ratione et hoc de angelis dici. Quis ergo est istorum deorum spiritus, nisi Spiritus sanctus? Ipse profecto est qui interpellat pro nobis gemitibus inenarrabilibus. Ipse est qui suggerit nobis quid oremus. Ipse qui docet quomodo orare debeamus. Sine isto Spiritu devotio nostra vera esse non potest, quod cum ipso, et per ipsum attentaverit incassum iri non potest: Tu autem, inquit, potes, quia spiritus deorum sanctorum in te est.

Notandum autem quod rex Nabuchodonosor non inutilia et supervacua per Danielis officium quaesivit, sed quae ad declinandi periculi cautelam sciri oportuit. Dabitur autem et nobis quod petimus, si juxta illius exemplum cum fide et ex vera devotione 1333Aquaerimus quod sine periculo ignorare non possumus.

CHAPTER XXXIV. That we often acquire by the merit of devotion the cognizance of those things which by no sagacity of ours, by no zeal of ours, are we able to penetrate.

It is surely to be noted that Daniel is called Balthassar, that is, hair of the head, where he is invited to the subtle resolution of sublime things. For among human members, what is more sublime than the head, what more subtle than the hair? 1332BRightly, therefore, is he called hair of the head, from whom the subtlety of a sublime understanding is demanded. And the true devotion of the mind is rightly called, from its effect, unless I am mistaken, the hair of the head, because through it the mind is without doubt sharpened unto every subtlety. Through its effect, then, it is itself the hair of the head, it is itself the subtlety of the mind. Let us hear yet what he subjoins to these things: Because all the wise men of my kingdom are unable to declare the solution to me. Who are, I ask, the wise men of that inward kingdom, but the keenest points and the most piercing senses of the human heart? Attend, therefore, what the virtue of devotion is, how rightly it is called the hair of the head, if through it the mind can do what it cannot do through all the wise men of its own kingdom. When therefore the mind seeks in manifold ways what is above its own understanding, and by seeking presses on, by pressing on 1332Cfails, by failing distrusts, what else, I ask, does it now say within itself, silent through distrust, but this which king Nabuchodonosor openly professes? Because all the wise men of my kingdom are unable to declare the solution to me. But since Truth says: Amen I say to you, whatsoever you ask when praying, believe that you shall receive, and it shall be done unto you (Marc. XI). [Vulg. Mark 11:24 Propterea dico vobis, omnia quaecumque orantes petitis, credite quia accipietis, et evenient vobis; chunk 0035 prints this same verse at 1311B with petieritis where Migne prints petitis here] If therefore the mind believes what it hears, if it turns itself to prayer, if through true and inmost devotion it does not distrust that it can obtain what it could not obtain by investigation, what else, I ask, does it assert within itself, through the confidence of hope and the constancy of faith, but that very same thing which the king said to Daniel: But thou art able, he says? And he adds the cause also: Because the spirit of the holy gods is in thee. We know 1332Dthat in the Holy Scriptures holy men have been called gods, and by the same reason this can be said of the angels also. Who, then, is the spirit of those gods, but the Holy Spirit? He assuredly it is who intercedes for us with unspeakable groanings. He it is who suggests to us what we should pray. He who teaches how we ought to pray. Without this Spirit our devotion cannot be true; and what it shall have attempted with him and through him cannot go for nothing: But thou, he says, art able, because the spirit of the holy gods is in thee.

But it is to be noted that king Nabuchodonosor did not seek useless and superfluous things through Daniel's office, but such things as it behoved to be known for the wariness of avoiding danger. And that which we ask shall be given to us also, if after his example we seek with faith and out of true devotion 1333Athat which we cannot without danger be ignorant of.

CAPUT XXXV. Quod animus dum ex multa consideratione infirmitatis suae humiliatur, saepe ad sublimem intelligentiam divinitus illuminatur.

Tunc Daniel, cujus nomen Balthasar, coepit intra semetipsum tacitus cogitare quasi hora una, et cogitationes ejus conturbabant eum (Dan. IV). Puto quia ad Danielem pertineat, et quia verae devotionis officium existat ad semetipsum redire, exteriorum oblivisci et interiora cogitare, eo maxime tempore cum debet propriae, vel alienae saluti consulere. Aliud est tempus investigandi quae sunt supra se, atque aliud est tempus discutiendi quae sunt intra 1333Bse. Omnia enim tempus habent. Tempus itaque est contemplandi magnalia divina, et est tempus considerandi infirma nostra. Istud est tempus ridendi. Nam et ad vesperum demorabitur fletus, et ad matutinum laetitia. Cur non sit tempus ridendi in matutino divinae revelationis et contemplatione aeterni luminis? Cur non sit tempus flendi in vespertino horrendae visionis, in considerandis humanae conscientiae tenebris? Vespertinum tempus est quando ante contemplantis oculos lumen revelantis gratiae emergit et in sui admirationem convertit, et saepe extra semetipsum per mentis excelsum rapit: Exitus, inquit, matutini, et vespere delectabis (Psal. LXIV). Qui sunt isti matutini exitus, nisi ad veri solis irradiationem humanae mentis excessus? Dicit 1333Cergo quia delectant exitus matutini, cur non et vespera, vel introitus vespertini? Nam mane solemus ab interioribus ad exteriora exire, et vespere ab exterioribus ad interiora redire. Quis autem digne miremur quam sit dulce, quamve delectabile, fervente jam charitate, et quasi sub aestivo tempore ad matutinam lucem radiantis divinitatis se a somno excutere, et extra humanae mentis angustias in auram libertatis erumpere, et in latum illum divinorum spectaculorum campum mentis alienatione transire? Delectant itaque exitus matutini, contristant autem introitus vespertini, eo procul dubio tempore cum animus revertitur ad considerationem sui: Ad meipsum, inquit, anima mea conturbata est 1333D(Psal. XLI). Sed in horrore visionis nocturnae, in consideratione horridae conscientiae unum est certum levamen animi, memoria videlicet Dei sui, et hoc est quod additur ibi: Ad meipsum, anima mea conturbata est, propterea memor ero tui. Hoc est quod alias dicit: Memor fui Dei, et delectatus sum (Psal. LXXVI). Et hoc in loco: Exitus matutini, et vespere delectabis. Tu, inquit, delectabis, non ipsa hora, non ipsi introitus vespertini temporis. Nam quae, quaeso, poterit esse delectatio in consideranda tenebrosa illa humani cordis abysso? Pravum siquidem est cor hominis, et inscrutabile, et quis cognoscet illud? Hoc est quod Daniel, dum diu cogitat, apud semetipsum cogitationes suae conturbant eum. Sed qui infirma sua libenter considerat, diligenter retractat, 1334Aet se in eorum consideratione humiliat, dum illud studiose discutit quod in seipso per semetipsum deprehendere potuit, saepe et ad id divinitus illuminatur quod per seipsum scire non potuit. Hoc est quod Daniel, dum apud se diu tacitus meditatur, ad praenoscendam regiae potestatis ruinam ejusdemque ruinae causam divinitus eruditur.

CHAPTER XXXV. That the spirit, while it is humbled by much consideration of its own weakness, is often divinely enlightened unto a sublime understanding.

Then Daniel, whose name is Balthasar, began to think silently within himself for about one hour, and his thoughts troubled him (Dan. IV). I think that it belongs to Daniel, and that it is the office of true devotion, to return to oneself, to forget outward things and to think upon inward ones, and this chiefly at that time when one must take counsel for his own salvation or another's. One is the time for investigating the things that are above oneself, and another the time for examining the things that are within 1333Boneself. For all things have their time. There is therefore a time for contemplating the mighty works of God, and there is a time for considering our own weaknesses. The latter is the time of laughing. For in the evening weeping shall abide, and in the morning gladness. Why should there not be a time of laughing in the morning of divine revelation and in the contemplation of the eternal light? Why should there not be a time of weeping in the evening of a dreadful vision, in considering the darkness of the human conscience? It is evening time when before the eyes of the contemplative the light of revealing grace comes forth and turns him into admiration of itself, and often snatches him beyond himself through the height of the mind: The goings out, he says, of the morning, and at evening thou shalt delight (Psal. LXIV). What are those goings out of the morning, but the excesses of the human mind toward the irradiation of the true sun? He says 1333Ctherefore that the goings out of the morning delight; why not the evening also, or the goings in of the evening? For in the morning we are wont to go out from inward things to outward, and in the evening to return from outward things to inward. But who may we worthily wonder how sweet it is, or how delightful, when charity is now fervent and as it were under the summer season, to shake oneself out of sleep at the morning light of the radiant Godhead, and to break out beyond the straits of the human mind into the air of liberty, and to pass over by an alienation of the mind into that broad field of the divine spectacles? And so the goings out of the morning delight, but the goings in of the evening sadden, at that time without doubt when the spirit turns back to the consideration of itself: Unto myself, he says, my soul is troubled 1333D(Psal. XLI). But in the horror of the nightly vision, in the consideration of a dreadful conscience, there is one sure relief of the spirit, namely the memory of one's God; and this is what is added there: Unto myself my soul is troubled, therefore will I be mindful of thee. This is what he says elsewhere: I was mindful of God, and was delighted (Psal. LXXVI). And in this place: The goings out of the morning, and at evening thou shalt delight. Thou, he says, shalt delight; not the hour itself, not the goings in themselves of the evening time. For what delight, I ask, could there be in considering that dark abyss of the human heart? For the heart of man is perverse and unsearchable, and who shall know it? This is why, with Daniel, while he thinks long, his own thoughts trouble him within himself. But he who willingly considers his own weaknesses, diligently goes over them again, 1334Aand humbles himself in the consideration of them, while he zealously examines that which he was able to detect in himself by himself, is often enlightened by God even unto that which he could not know by himself. This is why Daniel, while he meditates long and silently within himself, is divinely instructed to foreknow the ruin of the royal power and the cause of that same ruin.

CAPUT XXXVI. De vera mentis devotione, quomodo debeat semetipsam discutere.

Debet autem non mediocriter et quasi in transitu interiora sua meditari, qui vult ad profundam et plenam sui cognitionem erudiri, qui suae vel alienae saluti necessaria optat sibi revelari. Sic Daniel quasi hora una apud semetipsum cogitavit, et 1334Bcausam quam quaesivit obtinuit. Investigationis hora tunc demum debito fine concluditur, quando jam invenitur quod quaeritur. Et nos itaque, articulo necessitatis urgente, studeamus orare, quaerere, pulsare quantum necessitas cogit, vel opportunitas sinit et studii nostri continuatio instantem horam complevit. Sed si per Danielem devotionem oportet intelligere, forte hoc erit Danielem intra semetipsum cogitare, devotionem nostram semetipsam discutere, semetipsam examinare, quam fervens, quam frequens, quam supplex sit apud semetipsam inquirere. Apud semetipsum cogitat, cum studii et exercitii sui qualitatem, modum et intentionem pensat. Cogitat apud semetipsum dum diligenter inquirit si hoc ipsum quod bene intendit non de 1334Csuis viribus, sed de sola pietate Domini praesumit. Devotio itaque nostra saepe cum hoc secum agit, arrogantiae vitium quod in se prius latebat deprehendit. Sed absque dubio vitium arrogantiae cum coeperit menti praevalere, semper solet esse causa ruinae. Cum igitur hoc vitium ad eam enormitatem devotio excrevisse invenerit, futuram animi ruinam imminere colligit, et pertimescit. Scit enim quod ante ruinam exaltabitur cor, et quia Deus superbis resistit.

CHAPTER XXXVI. On the true devotion of the mind, how it ought to examine itself.

But he ought to meditate upon his inward things not slightly and as it were in passing, who wishes to be instructed unto a deep and full knowledge of himself, who desires that the things necessary for his own salvation or another's be revealed to him. So Daniel thought within himself for about one hour, and 1334Bobtained the cause which he sought. The hour of investigation is then at last closed with its due end, when that which is sought is now found. And we too, therefore, when the moment of necessity presses, let us be earnest to pray, to seek, to knock, as much as necessity compels, or opportunity allows and the continuance of our zeal has filled up the present hour. But if by Daniel devotion is to be understood, perhaps this will be for Daniel to think within himself: for our devotion to examine itself, to try itself, to inquire within itself how fervent, how frequent, how suppliant it is. It thinks within itself when it weighs the quality, the measure and the intention of its own zeal and exercise. It thinks within itself while it diligently inquires whether this very thing which it rightly intends it presumes not upon 1334Cits own powers, but upon the piety of the Lord alone. And so our devotion, when it often deals with this within itself, detects the vice of arrogance which before lay hidden within it. But without doubt the vice of arrogance, when it has begun to prevail over the mind, is always wont to be the cause of ruin. When therefore devotion has found that this vice has grown to such enormity, it gathers that a coming ruin of the spirit is at hand, and greatly fears it. For it knows that before ruin the heart shall be exalted, and that God resisteth the proud.

CAPUT XXXVII. Quomodo devotio quandoque totum interioris hominis statum deprehendat, et tam praesentem perspiciat, quam futurum provideat.

1334DRespondens rex ait: Balthasar, somnium, et interpretationes ejus non conturbent te (Dan. IV). Quod Danielem rex turbari prohibuit, quid aliud est nisi quod mens seipsam deludit, et spe inani saepe utilem timorem tollit? Sed ecce Daniel facit quod, imminente periculo, a nobis fieri voluit, et quod verae devotionis est, se ad orationem convertit Respondit Balthasar, et dixit: Domine mi, somnium his quae te oderunt, et interpretatio ejus hostibus tuis sit. Maligni spiritus sunt qui odio inexstinguibili in nos exarserunt. Vitia veri animae hostes sunt, quae eam ad aeternum interitum impellunt. Horum ruinam imprecari docemur in eo quod dicitur: Somnium his qui te oderunt, et interpretatio ejus hostibus tuis sit. Cum igitur mens spiritu superbiae, et 1335Aejusmodi vitiorum incentoribus impugnatur, cum arrogantia, et vana gloria opprimitur, debet ad orationem confugere, et juxta Danielis exemplum eorum ruinam expostulare. Sed cum animi devotio contra arrogantiam et vanam gloriam per orationem praevalere non possit, saepe ex eo ipso altius intelligit, certiusque colligit quod animi tumor, nisi per foedam et erubescendam ipsius dejectionem sanari nequiverit. Et hoc est quod Daniel per mysterium loquitur, cum dicit: Ejicient te ab hominibus, et cum bestiis ferisque erit habitatio tua. Audiamus nunc quid dicat, et ex eo colligamus quomodo totum saepe interioris hominis statum devotio deprehendat, et tam praesentem describat, quam futurum provideat. Dicat ergo, jam imminente ruina, quid 1335Banimus interim sit per gratiam, qualis futurus sit per culpam, novissime autem addat ruinae ipsius, vel reparationis suae causam. Hunc, ni fallor, ordinem in verbis Danielis ad regem invenies, si recte examines: Arborem, inquit, quam vidisti sub limem atque robustam, cujus altitudo pertingit ad coelum, et aspectus illius in omnem terram, et rami ejus pulcherrimi, et fructus ejus nimius, et esca omnium in ea, subter eam habitantes bestiae agri, et in ramis ejus commorantes aves coeli; tu es rex, qui magnificatus es, et invaluisti; et magnitudo tua crevit, et pervenit usque ad coelum, et potestas tua in terminos universae terrae. Quomodo in his verbis describatur quidam magnae perfectionis status, superius 1335Cjam diximus, et hic replicare non debemus.

Nota autem illud quod dicitur: Arborem quam vidisti, tu es. Si per ejusmodi arborem, magnum quoddam admirandae virtutis robur oportet intelligere, si per visionem divinitus ostensam contemplationis revelationem debemus accipere, quid, quaeso, erit dicere, tu es arbor quam vidisti, nisi quia talem gratiam accepisti, qualem prius per contemplationem vidisti, et magnae perfectionis virum qualem esse oporteret per contemplationis gratiam intellexisti, talis, et tantus ex divino munere esse meruisti. In arbore igitur mystice describitur gratia divinitus accepta, sequentibus autem verbis exprimitur arrogantis animae superveniens ruina: Quod autem, inquit, vidit rex vigilem, et sanctum 1335Ddescendere de coelo, et dicere: Succidite arborem, et dissipate illam, attamen germen radicum ejus in terra dimittite, et vinciatur ferro et aere in herbis foris, et rore coeli conspergatur, et cum feris sit pabulum ejus, donec septem tempora commutentur super eum. Haec est interpretatio sententiae Altissimi, quae pervenit super dominum meum regem. Ejicient te ab hominibus, et cum bestiis, ferisque erit habitatio tua, et fenum ut bos comedes, et rore coeli infunderis. Septem quoque tempora mutabuntur super te. Quomodo in his verbis animae de sublimi quodam statu in ima ruentis typus exprimitur, in superioribus habetur.

Nota quod hic quoque quemadmodum superius prius sit divinitus per contemplationem ostensum, postea autem opere explendum. Quod autem vidit 1336Arex hoc pertinet ad contemplationem, quod in sequentibus additur, ejicient te ab hominibus, hoc pertinet ad visionis exsecutionem, ac si dicat: Secundum hoc quod per contemplationem vidisti talem ruinam de tam sublimi statu posse et solere contingere, simile periculum tibi video imminere, quia in te invenio causam ruinae. Ruinae autem causa innuitur in eo quod novissime infertur: Donec scias quod dominetur Excelsus super regnum hominum, et cuicunque voluerit det illud. Quod dicit donec scias, notat quod hoc ipsum prout oportuit nondum agnoverat. Sequentibus autem verbis exprimitur qualis tandem aliquando futurus sit per gratiam, et cur, vel quomodo reparandus sit post ruinam. Quod autem praecepit, inquit, ut relinqueretur germen radicum 1336Bejus, id est arboris: regnum tuum tibi manebit postquam cognoveris potestatem esse coelestem. Haec autem omnia per Danielem audire, est per devotionis nostrae studium, arrogantiae nostrae enormitatem deprehendere, quae semper solet esse causa ruinae, et causa ruinae inventa atque perspecta, violentissimum cadendi metum incurrere, spem tamen firmissimam resipiscendi, et resurgendi inconcusse retinere. Quomodo vero haec omnia debeant plenius intelligi, ex superiori expositione facile potest agnosci.

CHAPTER XXXVII. How devotion sometimes detects the whole state of the inner man, and as much discerns it present as it foresees it future.

1334DThe king answering said: Balthasar, let not the dream and the interpretations thereof trouble thee (Dan. IV) [Vulg. interpretatio ejus, singular, "the interpretation thereof"; the next lemma prints the singular]. That the king forbade Daniel to be troubled, what else is it but that the mind deludes itself, and by an empty hope often takes away a profitable fear? But behold, Daniel does what he willed to be done by us when danger is at hand, and what belongs to true devotion: he turns himself to prayer Balthasar answered and said: My lord, the dream be to them which hate thee, and the interpretation thereof be to thine enemies. [Vulg. his qui te oderunt, masculine, "to them that hate thee", and without sit; the re-quotation three lines below prints the masculine] They are malignant spirits who have blazed out against us with an inextinguishable hatred. The vices are the true enemies of the soul, which drive it to eternal destruction. We are taught to imprecate their ruin in that which is said: The dream be to them who hate thee, and the interpretation thereof to thine enemies. When therefore the mind is assailed by the spirit of pride and 1335Aby such kindlers of vices, when it is oppressed by arrogance and vainglory, it ought to flee to prayer, and after Daniel's example to demand their ruin. But when the devotion of the spirit cannot prevail by prayer against arrogance and vainglory, often from that very thing it understands more deeply, and gathers more surely, that the swelling of the spirit could not be healed save by a foul and shameful casting down of itself. And this is what Daniel speaks through a mystery, when he says: They shall cast thee out from among men, and thy dwelling shall be with beasts and wild beasts. Let us now hear what he says, and from it let us gather how devotion often detects the whole state of the inner man, and as much describes it present as it foresees it future. Let it say, then, when ruin is now at hand, what 1335Bthe spirit meanwhile is by grace, what manner of thing it will be by fault, and let it add last of all the cause of that ruin, or of its own repair. This order, unless I am mistaken, thou wilt find in Daniel's words to the king, if thou examine rightly: The tree, he says, which thou sawest sub limem and strong, whose height reacheth unto heaven, and the sight thereof unto all the earth, and its branches most fair, and its fruit exceeding great, and food for all in it, the beasts of the field dwelling under it, and the birds of the air abiding in its branches: thou art the king, who art magnified, and hast grown strong; and thy greatness hath increased, and hath reached unto heaven, and thy power unto the ends of all the earth. How in these words a certain state of great perfection is described, we have already said above, 1335Cand we ought not to repeat it here.

But note that which is said: The tree which thou sawest, thou art. If by such a tree we must understand a certain great strength of admirable virtue, if by the vision divinely shown we ought to take the revelation of contemplation, what, I ask, will it be to say, thou art the tree which thou sawest, but that thou hast received such grace as thou first sawest through contemplation; and that, having understood through the grace of contemplation what manner of man a man of great perfection ought to be, thou hast deserved of the divine gift to be such and so great a one. In the tree, therefore, is mystically described grace received from God; but in the words that follow is expressed the ruin that comes upon an arrogant soul: But whereas, he says, the king saw a watcher and a holy one 1335Dcome down from heaven, and say: Cut down the tree, and scatter it, yet leave the shoot of its roots in the earth, and let it be bound with iron and bronze among the grass without, and let it be sprinkled with the dew of heaven, and let its fodder be with the wild beasts, until seven times be changed over it. This is the interpretation of the sentence of the Most High, which is come upon my lord the king. They shall cast thee out from among men, and thy dwelling shall be with beasts and wild beasts, and thou shalt eat hay like an ox, and shalt be drenched with the dew of heaven. Seven times also shall be changed over thee. How in these words is expressed the type of a soul falling from a certain lofty state into the depths, is held in what has gone before.

Note that here also, just as above, a thing is first shown by God through contemplation, and afterward to be fulfilled in deed. That the 1336Aking saw belongs to contemplation; that which is added in what follows, they shall cast thee out from among men, belongs to the carrying out of the vision, as if he should say: According to this, that thou hast seen through contemplation that such a ruin from so lofty a state can and is wont to happen, I see a like danger threatening thee, because in thee I find the cause of ruin. But the cause of ruin is hinted in that which is brought in last: Until thou know that the Most High ruleth over the kingdom of men, and giveth it to whomsoever he will. In that he says until thou know, he marks that he had not yet acknowledged this very thing as he ought. But in the words that follow is expressed what manner of man he will at length one day be by grace, and why, or how, he is to be repaired after his ruin. But whereas he commanded, he says, that the shoot of its roots 1336Bshould be left, that is, of the tree: thy kingdom shall remain to thee after thou shalt have known that the power is heavenly. But to hear all these things through Daniel is, through the zeal of our devotion, to detect the enormity of our own arrogance, which is always wont to be the cause of ruin; and, the cause of ruin found and looked through, to incur a most violent fear of falling, yet to hold unshaken a most firm hope of coming to oneself again and of rising again. But how all these things ought to be more fully understood can easily be known from the exposition above.

CAPUT XXXVIII. Quomodo imminenti periculo repugnare oporteat, vel quantum eleemosyna valeat, si simplici intentione fiat.

1336CImminenti periculo jam cognito, et in medium prolato, novissimo Daniel loco adjungit quid sub tantae necessitatis articulo fieri, vel quomodo instanti periculo repugnari oporteat: Quamobrem, inquit, rex, consilium meum placeat tibi, et peccata tua eleemosynis redime, et iniquitates tuas misericordiis pauperum; forsitan ignoscet Deus dilectis tuis (Dan. IV). Quid, quaeso, consultius, quidve commodius quam, urgente periculo, ad divinam protectionem confugere, ejusque propitiationem quaerere cui nihil est impossibile? Justum est autem valde, ut qui in sua necessitate suffragium a suo expetit superiori, opem quam valet, et oportet suo exhibeat inferiori, ut qui sibi misereri desiderat, 1336Dmisericordiam libenter impendat, ut fiat quod scriptum est: Beati misericordes, quoniam ipsi misericordiam consequentur (Matth. V). Recte ergo dicitur peccata tua eleemosynis redime, et iniquitates tuas misericordiis pauperum. Forte in hac gemina clausula est repetita sententia, ut idem sit redimere iniquitates atque peccata. Possumus tamen in peccatis intelligere praevaricationes operis, in iniquitatibus vero pravitates voluntatis. Monet namque illud nos psalmi aliquam in his distinctionem debere, vel posse fieri: Tu remisisti, inquit, impietatem peccati mei (Psal. XXXI). In eleemosynis nihilominus recte intelligitur pietatis actus, in misericordiis pietatis affectus. Et vide quam recte expietur opus opere, actus ex affectione. Peccata tua, inquit, eleemosynis 1337Aredime, et iniquitates tuas misericordiis pauperum. Pauperes facit indulgentia bonorum exteriorum, multo magis autem pauperes reddit inopia bonorum interiorum. Pauper est, qui discretione caret, qui consilium non habet, qui virtutum divitiis caret. Sed ubi paupertas est, ibi et eleemosyna esse potest. Et quanto paupertas est verior, tanto eleemosyna major. Magna eleemosyna suo cibo reficere carnem quandoque morituram, major autem sui generis alimento saginare animam in aeternum victuram. Eleemosyna nudum vestire, major autem indisciplinatum morum disciplina ornare. Optimum eleemosynae genus corripere inquietos, consolari pusillanimes, patientem esse ad omnes. Misericordia magna alienae afflictioni compati, major autem pro 1337Balienis erratibus compungi; praeclarum genus misericordiae, delinquentes deflere, poenitentibus indulgere, aliena delicta propriis lacrymis delere. In his misericordiae operibus nos exercitare debemus, qui exteriores divitias non habemus. Forsitan, inquit, ignoscet delictis tuis. Quid est quod forsitan dicit, et quasi sub dubitatione ponit, nisi, quod nec in his misericordiae operibus potest animus plenam fiduciam habere, quando superbiae et inanis gloriae tabe corruptus, non potest haec pura et simplici intentione exercere?

CHAPTER XXXVIII. How one ought to fight back against an imminent danger, and how much alms avails, if it be done with a simple intention.

1336CThe imminent danger being now known and brought into the midst, Daniel adds in the last place what ought to be done under the moment of so great a necessity, or how the pressing danger ought to be resisted: Wherefore, he says, O king, let my counsel be pleasing to thee, and redeem thou thy sins with alms, and thine iniquities with mercies to the poor; perhaps God will pardon thy beloved ones (Dan. IV) [Vulg. delictis tuis, "thy sins"; Migne prints dilectis here and delictis at 1337B below]. What, I ask, is more prudent, or what more profitable, than, when danger presses, to flee to the divine protection, and to seek the propitiation of him to whom nothing is impossible? But it is very just that he who in his own necessity seeks support from his superior should show to his inferior the aid he can and ought, so that he who desires mercy for himself may 1336Dwillingly spend mercy, that what is written may come to pass: Blessed are the merciful, for they shall obtain mercy (Matth. V). Rightly therefore is it said, redeem thy sins with alms, and thine iniquities with mercies to the poor. Perhaps in this twofold clause the sentence is repeated, so that to redeem iniquities and sins is one and the same. Yet we can understand in sins the transgressions of work, and in iniquities the perversities of the will. For that word of the psalm admonishes us that some distinction in these ought, or can, be made: Thou hast forgiven, he says, the impiety of my sin (Psal. XXXI). In alms nonetheless the act of piety is rightly understood, in mercies the affection of piety. And see how rightly a work is expiated by a work, an act by an affection. Redeem thy sins, he says, with alms, 1337Aand thine iniquities with mercies to the poor. Indulgence in outward goods makes men poor, but far more does the want of inward goods render them poor. He is poor who lacks discretion, who has no counsel, who lacks the riches of virtues. But where poverty is, there alms too can be. And by how much the poverty is truer, by so much is the alms greater. It is a great alms to refresh with one's own food flesh that will one day die, but a greater to fatten with the nourishment of its own kind a soul that will live forever. It is an alms to clothe the naked, but a greater to adorn the undisciplined with the discipline of morals. The best kind of alms is to rebuke the restless, to comfort the fainthearted, to be patient toward all. It is a great mercy to suffer together with another's affliction, but a greater to be pricked with compunction for 1337Banother's straying; a noble kind of mercy, to weep for the delinquent, to pardon the penitent, to blot out another's offenses with one's own tears. In these works of mercy we ought to exercise ourselves, who have no outward riches. Perhaps, he says, he will pardon thy offenses. What is it that he says perhaps, and sets it as it were under a doubt, but that not even in these works of mercy can the spirit have full confidence, when, corrupted by the wasting of pride and vainglory, it cannot practice them with a pure and simple intention?

CAPUT XXXIX. Quam patienter, vel misericorditer correptionem nostram Dei longanimitas exspectat, vel quomodo perversa mens ejus patientia abuti soleat.

1337COmnia haec venerunt super Nabuchodonosor regem (Dan. IV). Omnia, inquit, venerunt super regem, et regem talem. Omnia venerunt, omnia venire possunt super regium animum, et qualem regia persona designat per mysterium: Post finem mensium duodecim in aula Babylonis deambulabat. Respondit rex, et ait: Nonne haec est Babylon magna, quam ego aedificavi in domum regni, in robore fortitudinis meae, et in gloria decoris mei? Quis, quaeso, divinae pietatatis viscera digne pensare sufficiat, quod contemplum suum non statim vindicat, sed correptionis nostrae satisfactionem exspectat, et propitiationis suae tempora prolongat. Post finem, inquit, mensium duodecim, in duodenario senarius duplicatur, et in 1337Dsenario propter sui perfectionem, perfectio figuratur. Quid itaque est, quod divina moderatio in exspectationis suae mora senarium duplicat, nisi quod ultionis suae dilationem, secundum justitiae et misericordiae suae plenitudinem dispensat? Parum namque ei est ut nostram correptionem exspectet quantum justitia exigit, nisi et illud superaddat quod misericordia requirit, ut exspectatio sua sit tam justa quam pia, et tam aequitate perfecta quam pietate plena. Sed multi Dei benignitate abutuntur, et unde corrigi debuerant, deteriorantur. Ecce Nabuchodonosor dum satisfaciendi tempora divinitus data accipit, unde se corrigere debuit, altius intumuit. In aula Babylonis deambulabat. Forte palatium et firmissimum habitaculum humilitas vera et custos 1338Avirtutum. Sed pertinet ad Hierusalem hujusmodi habitaculum: Hierusalem, inquit, quae aedificatur ut civitas, cujus participatio ejus in idipsum (Psal. CXXI). Sed, sicut ad Hierusalem pertinet humilitas vera, sic ad Babylonem humilitas ficta. Quid enim aliud ficta humilitas quam ignominiam parat, quid aliud quam confusionem exspectat? Aula igitur Babylonica humilitas ficta. Aula Babylonis sine humilitate disciplina humiliationis. In aula Babylonis non sedet, non jacet, sed deambulat quem sua conscientia dum remordet inquietat. In aula Babylonica deambulat quem de fieta humilitate sua conscientia accusando fatigat. Est autem deambulare, non in unam partem ire, sed passim huc illucque vagari. Ille ergo deambulat, quem conscientiae stimulus undique exagitat.

CHAPTER XXXIX. How patiently, or mercifully, the long-suffering of God awaits our correction, or how a perverse mind is wont to abuse his patience.

1337CAll these things came upon king Nabuchodonosor (Dan. IV). All things, he says, came upon the king, and upon such a king. All things came, all things can come upon a kingly spirit, and upon such a one as the royal person designates through a mystery: After the end of twelve months he was walking about in the hall of Babylon. The king answered and said: Is not this the great Babylon, which I have built to be the house of the kingdom, in the strength of my fortitude, and in the glory of my comeliness? Who, I ask, may suffice worthily to weigh the bowels of the divine pietatatis, in that he does not at once avenge his own contemplum, but awaits the satisfaction of our correction, and prolongs the times of his propitiation. After the end, he says, of twelve months: in the number twelve the number six is doubled, and in 1337Dsix, because of its own perfection, perfection is figured. What then is it, that the divine moderation doubles the six in the delay of its waiting, but that it dispenses the postponement of its vengeance according to the fullness of its justice and of its mercy? For it is a small thing to him to await our correction as much as justice demands, unless he add over and above that which mercy requires, so that his waiting may be as just as it is pious, and as perfect in equity as it is full of piety. But many abuse the kindness of God, and are made worse by that whereby they ought to have been corrected. Behold, while Nabuchodonosor receives the times of making satisfaction given him by God, he swelled the higher by that whereby he ought to have corrected himself. He was walking about in the hall of Babylon. Perhaps true humility, the guardian 1338Aof virtues, is a palace and a most firm dwelling. But such a dwelling belongs to Jerusalem: Jerusalem, he says, which is built as a city, whose participation thereof is in itself (Psal. CXXI). But as true humility belongs to Jerusalem, so feigned humility to Babylon. For what else does feigned humility prepare but disgrace, what else does it look for but confusion? The hall of Babylon, therefore, is feigned humility. The hall of Babylon is a discipline of humiliation without humility. In the hall of Babylon he does not sit, does not lie down, but walks about, whom his own conscience, while it gnaws him, disquiets. He walks about in the hall of Babylon whom his own conscience wearies by accusing him concerning his fieta humility. But to walk about is not to go in one direction, but to wander here and there at random. He therefore walks about whom the goad of conscience drives on from every side.

CAPUT XL. De typo arrogantis animae, vel quomodo soleat semetipsam magnificare.

  • 1338B

Responditque rex, et ait: Quae, quaeso, est ista responsio, ubi nulla praecedit interrogatio? An forte alienae cogitationi respondebat, et forsitan cogitationi quae nulla erat, sed esse credebat? Forte eorum cogitationi respondebat quos suam magnitudinem et auctoritatem admirari aestimabat. Habet autem hoc proprium arrogans animus, ut celsitudinem suam omnes admirari credat, quam ipse omni hora apud semetipsum magnificat: unde fit ut et apud alios in verba jactantiae fiducialiter prorumpat, et idem quod Nabuchodonosor, quamvis aliis verbis, exprimat. 1338CConsideremus modo quam alte intumuit qui in ea quae superius posuimus verba prorupit. Nonne haec est Babylon magna quam ego aedificavi? Ecce ostentatio. In domum regni; ecce ambitio. In robore fortitudinis meae; ecce arrogantia. Et in gloria decoris mei; ecce vana gloria. Haec est Babylon magna quam ego aedificavi. Idem dicimus sonare Babylonem quod confusionem. Animae superbienti et dupliciter ingredienti est Babylon aedificare, ad omnem se humilitatem inclinare, et quasi pro religionis obtentu ad omnem se utilitatem et abjectionem exponere, et ad quaelibet in quibus solent homines erubescere, qualis est vilis habitus, humilis gestus, tenuis cibus, voluntaria servitus. Dicat ergo: Haec est Babylon magna, 1338Dhumiliatio profunda, haec est dejectio voluntaria, haec est confusio votiva, quam ego aedificavi, quam observare decrevi. Sed quo fine, qua utilitate? In domum, inquit, regni. Nonne tibi aedificare videtur quasi in domum qui agit ad obtinendam auctoritatem, et artem magisterii, ut talis tantusque omnibus appareat qui digne debeat promoveri, aliisque praeferri et dignitate seu etiam potestate sublimari? In robore, inquit, fortitudinis meae. Ecce quanta arrogantia sibi ipsi attribuerit, quod totum pendet ex gratia divina. Non dicit in munere miserationis divinae, sed in robore fortitudinis meae. O quam aliter veritas ipsa et sensit, et docuit cum dicit: Sine me nihil potestis facere (Joan. XV). Sed quod se mens improba efficere aestimat per fortitudinem suam, 1339Aconsequenter retorquet ad gloriam propriam: In robore, inquit, fortitudinis meae, et in gloria decoris mei. O quam aliter hujusmodi gloriator agit, quam Apostolus praecepit, quam aliter, quam magistra veritas instituit: Qui gloriatur, ait, in Domino glorietur (I Cor. II); et illa: Sic luceat lux vestra coram hominibus, ut videant opera vestra bona, et glorificent Patrem vestrum qui in coelis est (Matth. V). Sed iste in gloria, inquit, decoris mei.

CHAPTER XL. On the type of an arrogant soul, or how it is wont to magnify itself.

  • 1338B

And the king answered and said: What, I ask, is this answering, where no questioning goes before? Was he perhaps answering another's thought, and perhaps a thought which was not at all, but which he believed to be? Perhaps he was answering the thought of those whom he reckoned to be admiring his greatness and authority. But an arrogant spirit has this as its own, that it believes all men to admire its loftiness, which it magnifies within itself at every hour: whence it comes about that before others too it confidently breaks out into words of boasting, and expresses the same thing as Nabuchodonosor, though in other words. 1338CLet us now consider how high he swelled who broke out into those words which we set down above. Is not this the great Babylon which I have built? Behold ostentation. To be the house of the kingdom; behold ambition. In the strength of my fortitude; behold arrogance. And in the glory of my comeliness; behold vainglory. This is the great Babylon which I have built. We say that Babylon sounds the same as confusion. For a soul that is proud and enters in doubly, to build Babylon is to bend itself to every humility, and, as it were under the pretext of religion, to expose itself to every service and abjection, and to whatsoever things men are wont to blush at, such as mean apparel, lowly bearing, thin food, voluntary servitude. Let him say then: This is the great Babylon, 1338Da deep humiliation; this is a voluntary casting down, this is a confusion taken by vow, which I have built, which I have resolved to observe. But to what end, to what profit? To be the house, he says, of the kingdom. Does he not seem to thee to build as it were for a house, who acts to obtain authority and the art of mastery, that he may appear to all such and so great a man as ought worthily to be promoted, and preferred to others, and exalted in dignity or even in power? In the strength, he says, of my fortitude. Behold with how great arrogance he has attributed to himself that which wholly hangs upon divine grace. He does not say, in the gift of the divine compassion, but, in the strength of my fortitude. O how otherwise the Truth itself both thought and taught, when it says: Without me you can do nothing (Joan. XV). But what the wicked mind reckons that it accomplishes by its own fortitude, it consequently twists back 1339Ato its own glory: In the strength, he says, of my fortitude, and in the glory of my comeliness. O how otherwise such a boaster acts than the Apostle enjoined, how otherwise than the Truth our mistress appointed: He that glorieth, he says, let him glory in the Lord (I Cor. II); and that other word: So let your light shine before men, that they may see your good works, and glorify your Father who is in heaven (Matth. V). But this man says, in the glory of my comeliness.

CAPUT XLI. Quam subito, saepe, et quam profunde labuntur, qui patientia Dei abutuntur.

Cum esset adhuc sermo in ore regis, vox de coelo ruit: Tibi dicitur Nabuchodonosor rex: Regnum transiit 1339Ba te (Dan. IV). Vox ruit, cum adhuc sermo esset in ore, sub ipso videlicet adhuc gloriandi tempore. Saepe namque impudens animus, dum gloriatur in immensum, inter ipsum gloriandum, remordente conscientia, subito consternatur, et divino quodam nutu subitaneo horrore concutitur, et cadendi metu in non evadendi desperationem praecipitatur. Vox de coelo ruit, quando quod aure cordis percipit ab intimis animae sensibus venit. Velut terra audit, quidquid per sensum corporeum haurit. De coelo venit, quod per Spiritum agnoscit. E coelo discit, quod intellectualiter comprehendit. Tibi dicitur Nabuchodonosor: tibi tam inaniter superbienti, tibi tam inaniter glorianti. Regnum transiit a te, et ab hominibus te ejicient et cum bestiis atque feris erit habitatio 1339Ctua. Regnum ab uno quolibet transit, quando gratiam divinitus sibi collatam amittit, per quam desideriis, et passionibus suis dominari consuevit. Ab hominibus te ejicient. Quantum te supra homines extulisti per arrogantiam, tantum infra homines dejicieris per culpam, et cum bestiis et feris erit habitatio tua. Ab hominibus ejectus ad bestias et feras descendit et degit, qui rationales mores deserens, de caetero irrationabiliter vivit, ut bestialiter degens et feraliter agens cum bestialiter viventibus sit ad omnem carnalitatem resolutus, et cum feraliter agentibus ad omnem crudelitatem effrenatus. Addit adhuc: Fenum quasi bos comedes. Bos cum fenum accipit pro summis deliciis ducit, et nil lautius 1339Drequirit. Cum sit ergo omnis caro fenum, et omnis gloria ejus quasi flos feni, fenum quasi bos comedit, qui omne gaudium suum in temporali gloria et carnalibus oblectamentis ponit, et spirituales delicias omnino, nec per studium, nec per desiderium requirit. Ecce in quam profundum labitur, qui patientia Dei abutitur. Ecce ad quem exitum venit, qui resipiscendi tempora in vanum accepit.

CHAPTER XLI. How suddenly, often, and how deeply they fall who abuse the patience of God.

While the word was yet in the king's mouth, a voice rushed down from heaven: To thee it is said, king Nabuchodonosor: The kingdom has passed 1339Bfrom thee (Dan. IV). The voice rushed down while the word was yet in his mouth, that is, still under the very time of his glorying. For often a shameless spirit, while it glories beyond measure, in the very act of glorying, its conscience gnawing it, is suddenly dismayed, and by a certain divine nod is shaken with a sudden horror, and by the fear of falling is hurled headlong into the despair of not escaping. A voice rushes down from heaven when that which he perceives with the ear of the heart comes from the inmost senses of the soul. He hears as it were the earth, whatever he draws in through bodily sense. That comes from heaven which he acknowledges through the Spirit. He learns from heaven that which he comprehends intellectually. To thee it is said, Nabuchodonosor: to thee so emptily proud, to thee so emptily glorying. The kingdom has passed from thee, and they shall cast thee out from among men, and thy dwelling 1339Cshall be with beasts and with wild beasts. The kingdom passes from any man whatsoever when he loses the grace divinely conferred upon him, by which he was wont to rule over his own desires and passions. They shall cast thee out from among men. By as much as thou hast raised thyself above men through arrogance, by so much shalt thou be cast down beneath men through fault, and thy dwelling shall be with beasts and wild beasts. Cast out from among men, he goes down to beasts and wild beasts and lives there, who, forsaking rational manners, lives thenceforth irrationally, so that, living like a beast and acting like a wild beast, he is with those who live like beasts dissolved into every carnality, and with those who act like wild beasts unbridled into every cruelty. He adds further: Thou shalt eat hay like an ox. The ox, when it receives hay, counts it for the highest delights, and seeks nothing 1339Dmore sumptuous. Since therefore all flesh is hay, and all its glory as the flower of hay, he eats hay like an ox who places all his joy in temporal glory and in carnal delights, and seeks spiritual delights not at all, neither by zeal nor by desire. Behold into how deep a place he falls who abuses the patience of God. Behold to what end he comes who has received in vain the times for coming to himself again.

CAPUT XLII. Quod una, eademque sententia per contemplationem praenoscitur, per timorem intentatur, per desperationem confirmatur.

Et septem tempora mutabuntur super te, donec scias, quod dominetur Excelsus in regno hominum, et cuicunque voluerit det illud (Dan. IV). Ista jam 1340Asuperius exposuimus, et eadem iterum replicare non debemus.

Illud sane notandum quod unum atque idem saepius repetitur, et una et eadem divinae severitatis sententia tertio replicatur. Et ut tibi propriis differentiis distinguam, primo animus per contemplationem, quasi per visionem agnoscit quid evenire soleat; secundo, per devotionem et quasi per Danielis officium invenit quid de seipso timere jam debeat, novissime ad subtractionem gratiae desperatione jam fractus, manifeste deprehendit quid jam evitare non valet. Una itaque eademque sententia per contemplationem praenoscitur, per timorem intentatur, per desperationem confirmatur.

CHAPTER XLII. That one and the same sentence is foreknown through contemplation, threatened through fear, confirmed through despair.

And seven times shall be changed over thee, until thou know that the Most High ruleth in the kingdom of men, and giveth it to whomsoever he will (Dan. IV). These things we have already 1340Aexpounded above, and we ought not to repeat the same again.

This surely is to be noted, that one and the same thing is repeated rather often, and one and the same sentence of the divine severity is rehearsed a third time. And that I may distinguish it for thee by its own differences: first the spirit, through contemplation and as it were through a vision, acknowledges what is wont to happen; second, through devotion and as it were through Daniel's office, it finds what it must now fear concerning itself; last, broken already by despair at the withdrawal of grace, it plainly detects what it is now unable to avoid. And so one and the same sentence is foreknown through contemplation, threatened through fear, confirmed through despair.

CAPUT XLIII. Quod sine divina protectione nullus valeat suis passionibus, vel malignis suggestionibus resistere.

  • 1340B

Eadem hora sermo completus est super Nabuchodonosor, ex hominibus abjectus est (Dan. IV). Quid est eadem hora, nisi statim ad subtractionem gratiae tentationibus cedere, et tentantibus malignis spiritibus non posse resistere? Eadem hora, non quod concepta nequitia statim in actum prodeat, cum peccandi occasionem non semper ad manum habeat: sed mens dejicitur, et cadit ex quo peccato consentit. Dejicitur, et cadit ex quo occasionem quaerit. Ex hoc loco, ut arbitror, possumus patenter colligere, quod nullus sine divina protectione posset, vel passionibus suis, vel suggestionibus malignis, 1340Cvel ad modicum resistere. Eadem itaque hora ab hominibus abjectus est, et fenum ut bos comedit, et rore coeli corpus ejus infectum est. Haec omnia jam superius exposuimus. Sed additur adhuc quod in superioribus non legimus: Donec capilli ejus in similitudinem aquilarum crescerent, et ungues quasi ungues avium.

CHAPTER XLIII. That without divine protection no one is able to resist his own passions, or malignant suggestions.

  • 1340B

In the same hour the word was fulfilled upon Nabuchodonosor, he was cast out from among men (Dan. IV). What is in the same hour, but at once, upon the withdrawal of grace, to yield to temptations, and to be unable to resist the malignant spirits that tempt? In the same hour: not that the wickedness conceived goes forth at once into act, since it does not always have the occasion of sinning at hand; but the mind is cast down, and falls, from the moment it consents to sin. It is cast down, and falls, from the moment it seeks an occasion. From this place, as I judge, we can plainly gather that without divine protection no one could resist his own passions, or malignant suggestions, 1340Ceven for a little. And so in the same hour he was cast out from among men, and he ate hay like an ox, and his body was stained with the dew of heaven. All these things we have already expounded above. But something is added further which we did not read in what went before: Until his hairs grew after the likeness of eagles', and his claws like the claws of birds. [Vulg. et ungues eius quasi avium, "and his nails as birds'"; Migne sets ungues twice and drops ejus, which the exposition at 1341B restores]

CAPUT XLIV. De quanto virtutum culmine contingat in ima vitiorum corruere.

Sunt autem capilli subtiles et longi, et recte in eis profunda et peracuta consilia possunt intelligi. Corpus vero aquilarum multa tegitur densitate plumarum. Solent autem aquilae caeteris cunctis animantibus videre perspicacius et volare sublimius. 1340DCapillos itaque quasi in similitudinem aquilarum habet, qui multa prudentia, qui plurima astutia callet, qui in fraudibus inveniendis, qui in dolis nectendis nullo jam segnior, nullo invenitur inferior. Rapaces autem aves, ut omnes novimus, unguibus desaeviunt, omnes vero aves in commune lacerant et laedunt quod unguibus apprehendunt. Quid itaque est ungues velut avium habere, quam quoscunque laedere quibus possis praevalere. Sed si sub unguium typo crudelitatis officium recte intelligitur, nonne id ipsum superius sub alia figura exprimitur, ubi dicitur: Et cum feris erit habitatio tua. Sed ferae aves non aucupant, nec habent omnino quid avibus faciant. Qui igitur saeculares et carnales quosque et quasi terrae incubantes libenter opprimunt, 1341Aquid aliud quam ferarum more in animalia desaeviunt? Nonne recte animalia dicendi sunt quae soli ventri deserviunt, quorum Deus venter est, et gloria in confusione? Qui autem spiritales viros persequuntur quid aliud quam avium more avibus insidiantur, et vetut unguibus debacchantur? Nonne tibi aves coeli videntur esse qui veraciter possunt dicere: Nostra autem conversatio in coelis est? Nonne coeli aves dicendi sunt qui se in alta contemplationis penna suspendunt? In priori igitur sententia habemus quomodo infesti sunt carnalibus; in hac autem habemus quomodo tandem aliquando penitus non parcunt vel ipsis spiritualibus. Dicat ergo de hujusmodi homine, dicat: Donec capilli ejus in similitudinem aquilarum crescerent, et ungues 1341Bejus quasi ungues avium, ut sit tam promptus ad circumveniendum etiam circumspectos, quam absque dubio est pronus ad opprimendum viros etiam virtute conspicuos. Hic efficax ad fallendum, hic procax et impudens ad persequendum: Os tuum, inquit Psalmista, abundavit malitia, et lingua tua concinnabat dolos (Psal. XLIX). Ecce quomodo fallacem arguit. Audi, et quomodo procaces confundit: Captabunt in animam justi, et sanguinem innocentem condemnabunt (Psal. XCIII). Nonne in coeli volatilia, et velut unguibus desaeviunt, qui justi animam captant, et innocentem condemnant? Vel per aves daemones possunt intelligi, sicut habemus ex lectione Evangelii (Luc. VIII). Quid igitur erit ungues ad 1341Cejusmodi avium similitudinem habere, nisi bonos quoslibet solo malignitatis instinctu infestare? Ecce jam habemus de quam alto perfectionis culmine contingat virum virtutum in ima vitiorum corruere, ut discat homo in qualicunque sanctitatis celsitudine, semper morum ruinam timere. Recole qualem superius ex mystica arbore descripsit, simul et attende ad quem exitum tandem aliquando venit. Et de arbore quidem legis, quia in ramis ejus conversabantur volucres coeli, et ex ea vescebatur omnis caro. Erat itaque bonis ad protectionem, omnibus autem ad consolationem. Quia volucres coeli conversabantur in ea, et omnis caro vescebatur ex ea. Ecce idem ipse post omnia, dente et ungue in omnes saevit, quia et pars ejus cum feris exstitit et 1341Dungues ut avium accepit.

CHAPTER XLIV. From how great a summit of virtues it may happen that a man falls into the depths of vices.

Now hairs are fine and long, and in them deep and exceedingly sharp counsels can rightly be understood. But the body of eagles is covered with a great thickness of feathers. And eagles are wont to see more keenly and to fly more loftily than all other living creatures. 1340DHe therefore has hairs as it were after the likeness of eagles' who is skilled in much prudence, in exceeding great cunning, who in finding out frauds, in weaving deceits, is found now slower than none, inferior to none. But birds of prey, as we all know, rage with their claws; and all birds in common tear and hurt what they seize with their claws. What then is it to have claws like birds', but to hurt whomsoever thou canst prevail against. But if under the type of claws the office of cruelty is rightly understood, is not this very thing expressed above under another figure, where it is said: And thy dwelling shall be with wild beasts. But wild beasts do not go fowling, nor have they anything at all that they may do to birds. Those, therefore, who willingly oppress all worldly and carnal men, and such as brood as it were upon the earth, 1341Awhat else do they do but rage against animals after the manner of wild beasts? Are not they rightly to be called animals who serve the belly alone, whose God is the belly, and whose glory is in their confusion? But those who persecute spiritual men, what else do they do but lie in wait for birds after the manner of birds, and vetut with their claws rave? Do they not seem to thee to be birds of heaven who can truly say: But our conversation is in heaven? Are not they to be called birds of heaven who hang themselves aloft on the wing of contemplation? In the former sentence, therefore, we have how they are hostile to carnal men; but in this we have how at length one day they utterly do not spare even spiritual men themselves. Let him say then of such a man, let him say: Until his hairs grew after the likeness of eagles', and his claws 1341Blike the claws of birds, so that he is as ready to circumvent even the circumspect as he is without doubt prone to crush men even conspicuous in virtue. This man is effective in deceiving, this man forward and shameless in persecuting: Thy mouth, says the Psalmist, hath abounded with malice, and thy tongue framed deceits (Psal. XLIX). Behold how he convicts the deceiver. Hear also how he confounds the forward: They shall hunt after the soul of the just, and shall condemn innocent blood (Psal. XCIII). Do they not rage against the fowls of heaven, and as it were with claws, who hunt after the soul of the just, and condemn the innocent? Or by birds demons can be understood, as we have from the reading of the Gospel (Luc. VIII). What then will it be to have claws after 1341Cthe likeness of such birds, but to harass any good men whatever by the mere instinct of malignity? Behold, we now have from how high a summit of perfection it may happen that a man of virtues falls into the depths of vices, that a man may learn, in whatever loftiness of sanctity, always to fear the ruin of morals. Call to mind what manner of man he described above from the mystical tree, and at the same time mark to what end he came at length. And of the tree indeed thou readest that the birds of heaven dwelt in its branches, and that all flesh fed upon it. And so it was for the protection of the good, and for the consolation of all. Because the birds of heaven dwelt in it, and all flesh fed upon it. Behold, this same man after all things rages against all with tooth and claw, because his portion also was with the wild beasts, and 1341Dhe received claws like birds'.

CAPUT XLV. Quomodo interim etiam ad majorem gloriam quandoque resurgit qui prius de sublimi cecidit.

Laetificat valde quod sequitur cum post ista subjungitur: Igitur post finem dierum ego Nabuchodonosor oculos meos ad coelum levavi, et sensus meus redditus est mihi, et Altissimo benedixi, et viventem in sempiternum laudavi, et glorificavi, quia potestas ejus potestas sempiterna, et regnum ejus in generatione et generationem. Et omnes habitatores terrae apud eum in nihilum reputati sunt (Dan. IV). Ecce iterum resurgit, ecce ad priorem, imo ad potiorem potius gloriam redit, qui de tam sublimi in tam profundum ruit. Quis non miretur, quis non obstupescat? 1342AQuis divinae pietatis viscera digne pensare sufficiat? Quis, quaeso, quantumlibet peccator de divina miseratione non praesumat, et ad spem veniae non reviviscat? Post finem, inquit, dierum levavi oculos meos ad coelum. Finis dierum, completio divinorum judiciorum. Si lumen est justitia, vel judicia humana, cur non multo magis judicia divina? Et educet, inquit, quasi lumen justitiam tuam, et judicium tuum tanquam meridiem (Psal. XXXVII). Si dies (imo meridies) potest esse judicium tuum, quanto magis judicium divinum? Judicia Dei dies sunt, quia in lumine veritatis, quia in luce rectitudinis fiunt. Siquidem judicia Domini vera justificata in semetipsa. Ejusmodi dierum numerus super quemlibet afflictum finitur, quando divinorum 1342Bjudiciorum severitas in ejus percussione completur? Talium dierum finis completur, quando divinae severitatis animadversio in misericordiam commutatur. Horum dierum finem in sua Nabuchodonosor attritione accepit, quando eum jam divina miseratio respexit. Nam si non respiceret, nec ipse quidem oculos suos ad coelum levaret. Oculos ad coelum levare, est coelestia et aeterna cogitare, et cura et sollicitudine saecularium semota, sola spiritualia prae oculis habere. Coelum respicere, et regionem lucis prae oculis habere, et in illud aeternitatis lumen intelligentiae oculos tota intentione figere. Illic oculos jam fixerat, qui dicebat: Cogitavi dies antiquos, et annos aeternos in mente habui 1342C(Psal. LXXVIII). Sed ecce qui oculos in coelum levavit, sensum quem amiserat, recepit. Attende ergo quam sit utile oculos in hunc modum ad coelum levare. Ego, inquit Nabuchodonosor, oculos meos ad coelum levavi, et sensus meus redditus est mihi. Sensus itaque carnis, suus est animalis. Sensus vero mentis, suus est hominis. Sensus animalis quo percipit candidum et nigrum, sensus vero suus est hominis qui discernit inter bonum et malum, verum et falsum. Hunc sensum cum Nabuchodonosor perdiderunt, vel nunquam habuerunt, qui dicunt bonum malum et malum bonum, ponentes amarum dulce et dulce amarum, ponentes tenebras lucem et lucem tenebras. Hunc sensum ex magna parte recipiunt qui judicant inter diem et 1342Ddiem, plenius autem qui judicant omnem diem. Alius, inquit Apostolus, judicat inter diem et diem, alius autem judicat omnem diem (Rom. XIV). Suum itaque sensum recuperat, qui ad discretionis judicium quandoque evigilat. Non autem dicitur sensus suus datus, sed redditus; unde et constat quod prius sit habitus, sed amissus. Sensus itaque amissus cuique tunc redditur, quando illa quam habere consueverat discretionis in eo gratia reparatur. Sensum autem recipit dum oculos levat, quia animus dum aeterna et coelestia cogitat, ad discretionis judicium evigilat. Dum enim coelestibus vehementer intendit, videt quam nihil sit quidquid terrenum, et transitorium amavit. Qui igitur ad gratiam discretionis anhelat coelestibus intendat, ut lumen oculorum 1343Asuorum recipiat, et cum receperit Domino suo proclamet, et dicat: Quid enim mihi est in coelo, et a te quid volui super terram? Sensum autem suum Nabuchodonosor quam non in vanum acceperit subsequentibus verbis ostendit.

CHAPTER XLV. How meanwhile he who before fell from on high sometimes rises again even to a greater glory.

What follows gladdens greatly, when after these things it is subjoined: Therefore after the end of the days I Nabuchodonosor lifted up my eyes to heaven, and my sense was restored to me, and I blessed the Most High, and I praised and glorified him that liveth for ever; for his power is an everlasting power, and his kingdom in generation and unto generation. And all the inhabitants of the earth are reputed as nothing before him (Dan. IV). Behold, he rises again; behold, he returns to his former glory, nay rather to a better, who fell from so lofty a place into so deep a one. Who would not wonder, who would not be astonished? 1342AWho may suffice worthily to weigh the bowels of the divine piety? Who, I ask, however great a sinner, would not presume upon the divine compassion, and revive unto the hope of pardon? After the end, he says, of the days I lifted up my eyes to heaven. The end of the days is the completion of the divine judgments. If justice, or human judgments, is a light, why not much more the divine judgments? And he shall bring forth, he says, thy justice as the light, and thy judgment as the noonday (Psal. XXXVII). If thy judgment can be a day (nay rather a noonday), how much more the divine judgment? The judgments of God are days, because they are made in the light of truth, in the light of rectitude. For indeed the judgments of the Lord are true, justified in themselves. The number of such days is ended over any afflicted man when the severity of the divine 1342Bjudgments is completed in the striking of him? The end of such days is completed when the punishment of the divine severity is changed into mercy. Nabuchodonosor received the end of these days in his own crushing, when the divine compassion now looked upon him. For if it had not looked upon him, neither indeed would he have lifted his own eyes to heaven. To lift the eyes to heaven is to think upon heavenly and eternal things, and, the care and solicitude of worldly things put away, to have spiritual things alone before the eyes. To look back to heaven, and to have the region of light before the eyes, and to fix the eyes of understanding with the whole intention upon that light of eternity. He had already fixed his eyes there who said: I thought upon the days of old, and I had the eternal years in mind 1342C(Psal. LXXVIII). But behold, he who lifted his eyes to heaven received back the sense which he had lost. Mark therefore how profitable it is to lift the eyes to heaven in this manner. I, says Nabuchodonosor, lifted up my eyes to heaven, and my sense was restored to me. And so the sense of the flesh is the animal's own. But the sense of the mind is man's own. The animal's sense is that by which it perceives white and black; but man's own sense is that which discerns between good and evil, true and false. This sense, along with Nabuchodonosor, they have lost, or never had, who call good evil and evil good, putting bitter sweet and sweet bitter, putting darkness light and light darkness. This sense they receive in great part who judge between day and 1342Dday, but more fully they who judge every day. One, says the Apostle, judgeth between day and day, but another judgeth every day (Rom. XIV) [Vulg. alius iudicat diem plus inter diem, "one esteemeth one day above another"; the exposition here and above turns on Migne's inter diem et diem]. And so he recovers his own sense who at some time awakens to the judgment of discretion. But his sense is not said to be given, but restored; whence also it is plain that it was first had, but lost. And so the lost sense is then restored to a man when that grace of discretion which he had been wont to have is repaired in him. But he receives his sense while he lifts his eyes, because the spirit, while it thinks upon eternal and heavenly things, awakens to the judgment of discretion. For while it strains vehemently toward heavenly things, it sees how nothing is whatever earthly and transitory thing it loved. He therefore who pants after the grace of discretion, let him strain toward heavenly things, that he may receive the light of his own 1343Aeyes, and, when he has received it, may cry out to his Lord and say: For what have I in heaven, and besides thee what have I desired upon earth? But how not in vain Nabuchodonosor received his own sense, he shows in the words that follow.

CAPUT XLVI. Quomodo solum Deum glorificat, qui se ipsum tam impudenter magnificabat.

Et Altissimo, inquit, benedixi. Ecce qui seipsum superius tam impudenter magnificabat, modo eum solum glorificat, qui, secundum quod Apostolus ait, est super omnia benedictus in saecula: Et viventem in sempiternum laudavi, et glorificavi (Rom. IX). Viventem in sempiternum, vita utique quae est ipse, cui non est aliud esse quam vivere, nec vivere quam 1343Besse. Quia potestas ejus potestas sempiterna, quia quod semel potest, semper potest qui omnipotens est. Et regnum ejus in generatione et generationem, quia ei ex tempore nihil accedit, nihil recedit, apud quem non est transmutatio, nec vicissitudinis obumbratio. Et omnes habitatores terrae apud eum in nihilum reputati sunt. Habitatores terrae, amatores terrenorum intellige, qui nesciunt quae sursum sunt quaerere, nec possunt cum Apostolo dicere: Nostra autem conversatio in coelis est (Philipp. III). Vere autem in nihilum reputantur, qui perdiderunt verum esse quibus profecto melius esset omnino non esse quam sic esse, nisi quis putet melius esse semper miserum esse, quam omnino non esse. Juxta voluntatem enim suam facit tam in virtutibus coeli quam in 1343Chabitatoribus terrae, et non est qui resistat manui ejus, et dicat ei: Quare fecisti? Eidem sententiae congruit quod Psalmista dicit: Omnia quaecunque voluit fecit in coelo et in terra, in mari, et in omnibus abyssis (Psal. CXXXIV). Cum in sequentibus idem Nabuchodonosor in Dei laude dicat, quia omnia opera ejus vera, et viae ejus judicia: quid est quod hic eum dicit omnia agere pro voluntate sua? Certe si omnia opera ejus vera sunt, omnia fiunt in veritate; certe si omnes viae ejus judicia sunt, omnia fiunt in aequitate, quomodo ergo pro voluntate? An forte fieri secundum aequitatem est fieri secundum voluntatem, et eo ipso quo fit in aequitate, eo ipso, inquam, fit pro ejus voluntate? Sed si idem est 1343Domnino fieri secundum aequitatem, et fieri secundum ejus voluntatem, ergo idem est ejus voluntas quod aequitas, et aequitas quod voluntas. Et non est qui resistat manui ejus, et dicat, quare fecisti? Quis ei resistat? quis contradicat? cum omnia tam potenter, quam sapienter agat, cum nemo sit qui possit vel omnipotentiam cohibere, vel summam sapientiam arguere.

CHAPTER XLVI. How he glorifies God alone, who was so shamelessly magnifying himself.

And I blessed, he says, the Most High. Behold, he who above so shamelessly magnified himself now glorifies him alone who, according as the Apostle says, is over all things blessed forever: And I praised and glorified him that liveth for ever (Rom. IX). Him that liveth for ever: that life, surely, which is himself, for whom to be is nothing other than to live, nor to live 1343Bthan to be. For his power is an everlasting power, because what he can do once, he who is almighty can always do. And his kingdom in generation and unto generation, because to him nothing accrues from time, nothing departs, with whom there is no change, nor shadow of alteration. And all the inhabitants of the earth are reputed as nothing before him. By the inhabitants of the earth understand the lovers of earthly things, who know not how to seek the things that are above, nor can say with the Apostle: But our conversation is in heaven (Philipp. III). But they are truly reputed as nothing who have lost true being, for whom assuredly it were better not to be at all than to be thus, unless someone think it better to be always wretched than not to be at all. For he does according to his own will as much among the powers of heaven as among 1343Cthe inhabitants of the earth, and there is none that can resist his hand and say to him: Why hast thou done it? To the same sentence agrees what the Psalmist says: Whatsoever he would he hath done, in heaven and in earth, in the sea, and in all the deeps (Psal. CXXXIV). Since in what follows the same Nabuchodonosor says in the praise of God that all his works are true and his ways judgments: what is it that here he says that he does all things according to his own will? Surely if all his works are true, all things are done in truth; surely if all his ways are judgments, all things are done in equity: how then according to will? Or is it perhaps that to be done according to equity is to be done according to will, and by the very fact that it is done in equity, by that very fact, I say, it is done according to his will? But if it is 1343Daltogether the same to be done according to equity and to be done according to his will, then his will is the same as equity, and equity the same as will. And there is none that can resist his hand and say, Why hast thou done it? Who should resist him? who gainsay him? since he does all things as powerfully as wisely, since there is no one who could either restrain omnipotence or convict the highest wisdom.

CAPUT XLVII. Quid agat superba gloriatio, vel quid efficiat devota glorificatio.

In ipso tempore sensus meus reversus est ad me, et ad honorem regni mei decoremque perveni, et figura mea reversa est ad me (Dan. IV). In ipso, inquit, tempore. In ipso quo Deum benedixi, in ipso quo 1344Alaudavi, atque glorificavi. Certe si dicta superius bene recolimus, ibi cecidit, cum superbe satis magnificaret seipsum. Ecce idem ipse resurgit cum devote glorificat Deum, et Dominum suum. Certe vides quid agat superba gloriatio, certe vides quid efficiat devota glorificatio. Superba gloriatio meretur dejectionem, devota glorificatio exaltationem. Ecce cum jam non in semetipso, sed in Domino gloriatur, sensus ejus ad semetipsum revertitur. Quod superius dixit sensum sibi redditum, hoc modo dicit sensum ad se reversum; vel forte sensus cujusque ad semetipsum revertitur, quando discretionis oculus ad sui considerationem revocatur. Sensus ad se revertitur, quando ad meritorum suorum considerationem et cognitionem divinitus 1344Billuminatur. Dum igitur animus humanus Deum suum glorificat, sensus ejus ad semetipsum redit quia dum ad illud divinitatis lumen oculos aperit, et vehementer intendit, infirmitatis suae tenebras plenius agnoscit: Accedite ad eum, inquit, et illuminamini (Psal. XXXIII). Quid mirum si accedendo ad lumen illuminamur? quid mirum si ex lumine lumen accenditur? Et in lumine, inquit, tuo videbimus lumen (Psal. XXXV). Lumen videtur in lumine, quando ex cognitione et operatione claritatis divinae plenius illuminatur animus ad cognitionem infirmitatis humanae. Solet tamen haec gemina cognitio alterna sibi incrementa ministrare, et utraque profecto ex alterutra crescere. Nam et ex cognitione Dei proficit animus ad cogitationem sui, et eodem utique 1344Cmodo ex multa cognitione sui crescit ad cognitionem Dei. Sed ecce dum sensus qui amissus fuerat redit, regnum suum qui perdiderat recepit. Procul dubio nisi discretionis gratia in eo reparetur, nunquam ad plenam virtutis integritatem animus perducitur.

CHAPTER XLVII. What proud glorying does, or what devout glorification effects.

At that very time my sense returned to me, and I came unto the honor of my kingdom and unto my comeliness, and my figure returned to me (Dan. IV). At that very, he says, time. At that very time in which I blessed God, in that very one in which I 1344Apraised and glorified him. Surely, if we well recall the things said above, he fell there, when he was magnifying himself proudly enough. Behold, this same man rises again when he devoutly glorifies God, and his Lord. Surely thou seest what proud glorying does, surely thou seest what devout glorification effects. Proud glorying deserves a casting down, devout glorification an exaltation.
