Behold, when he now glories not in himself but in the Lord, his sense returns to himself. What above he called his sense restored to him, he now calls his sense returned to himself; or perhaps a man's sense returns to itself when the eye of discretion is called back to the consideration of itself. The sense returns to itself when it is divinely 1344Benlightened unto the consideration and knowledge of its own merits. While therefore the human spirit glorifies its God, its sense returns to itself; because while it opens its eyes to that light of the Godhead, and strains vehemently toward it, it more fully acknowledges the darkness of its own weakness: Come ye to him, he says, and be enlightened (Psal. XXXIII). What wonder if by drawing near to the light we are enlightened? what wonder if from light a light is kindled? And in thy light, he says, we shall see light (Psal. XXXV). Light is seen in light when, from the knowledge and working of the divine brightness, the spirit is more fully enlightened unto the knowledge of human weakness. Yet this twofold knowledge is wont to minister alternate increases to itself, and each assuredly to grow out of the other. For from the knowledge of God the spirit advances to the thought of itself, and in the same way 1344Cfrom much knowledge of itself it grows to the knowledge of God. But behold, while the sense which had been lost returns, he received back his kingdom which he had lost. Without doubt, unless the grace of discretion be repaired in him, the spirit is never brought through to the full integrity of virtue.

CAPUT XLVIII. Quomodo per discretionem judicii, vel circumspectionem sui solet morum disciplina reformari.

Ad honorem, inquit, regni mei decoremque perveni. Constat profecto liberum hominis arbitrium regnum divinitus accipere atque tenere, quando jam potest malos cordis motus a malo cohibere. Hoc autem regnum tunc efficitur honorabile, quando ex 1344DDei munere jam potest bonos cordis motus in bono exercere. Idem autem ipsum condigno decore superducitur, quando in eo quidquid boni agitur cum summo moderamine et magna discretione disponitur. Regnum itaque accipit, et obtinet, qui rebelles animi motus fortiter domat, viriliterque coercet. Et tunc temporis qui ejusmodi est, judicabit in nationibus, implebit ruinas, conquassabit capita in terra multorum. Habet autem et honorabile tunc regnum, quando jam efficitur Dominus exercituum in tanta multitudine tam innumerabilium virtutum, et habens sub se milites dicit huic vade, et vadit, et alii veni, et venit, et servo suo, fac hoc, et facit. Decoratur hoc regnum inde, vel maxime cum modeste se agens moderatur omnia cum mansuetudine, 1345Aet tranquillitate: Fili, inquit, in mansuetudine fac opera tua, et super hominum gloriam diligeris (Eccle. XIII). Regni itaque honor, facere potenter, regni decor, facere sapienter, ita ut in illo regno suo qui possessor est regni attingat a fine usque ad finem fortiter, et disponat omnia suaviter. Et figura mea reversa est ad me. Figura est qualitas extrinseca, exterior videlicet corporis forma. Corporalis autem disciplina pertinet ad qualitatem extrinsecam, sicut compositio mentis ad qualitatem intrinsecam. In figura igitur quae constat in corporalibus lineamentis, recte intelligitur disciplina corporis, forma religionis, habitus et compositio exterioris hominis. Figura itaque revertitur, quando juxta pristinam consuetudinem, amissa ad tempus religionis forma 1345Breformatur. Sensu reverso, figura revertitur, quia per discretionem judicii, et circumspectionem sui morum disciplina, vel nova statuitur, vel antiqua innovatur.

CHAPTER XLVIII. How through the discretion of judgment, or through circumspection of oneself, the discipline of morals is wont to be reformed.

I came, he says, unto the honor of my kingdom and unto my comeliness. It is assuredly plain that man's free will receives and holds a kingdom from God, when it can now restrain the evil motions of the heart from evil. But this kingdom is then made honorable when, by 1344DGod's gift, it can now exercise the good motions of the heart in good. And that same kingdom is overlaid with a worthy comeliness when whatever good is done in it is ordered with the highest moderation and with great discretion. And so he receives and obtains a kingdom who bravely tames and manfully restrains the rebellious motions of the spirit. And at that time he who is such shall judge among the nations, shall fill up ruins, shall crush the heads in the land of many. And he has then an honorable kingdom too, when he is now made Lord of hosts in so great a multitude of virtues so innumerable, and, having soldiers under him, says to this one, Go, and he goes, and to another, Come, and he comes, and to his servant, Do this, and he does it. This kingdom is made comely thereby, and most of all when, bearing himself modestly, he moderates all things with meekness 1345Aand tranquillity: Son, he says, do thy works in meekness, and thou shalt be loved above the glory of men (Eccle. XIII) [Vulg. in mansuetudine opera tua perfice, "perfect thy works in meekness"]. And so the honor of the kingdom is to act powerfully, the comeliness of the kingdom to act wisely, so that in that his kingdom he who is the possessor of the kingdom may reach from end to end mightily and order all things sweetly. And my figure returned to me. Figure is an outward quality, namely the exterior form of the body. But bodily discipline belongs to the outward quality, as the composure of the mind to the inward quality. In the figure, therefore, which consists in bodily lineaments, there is rightly understood the discipline of the body, the form of religion, the habit and composure of the outer man. And so the figure returns when, according to the former custom, the form of religion lost for a time 1345Bis reformed. The sense having returned, the figure returns, because through the discretion of judgment and through circumspection of oneself the discipline of morals is either newly established or the old renewed.

CAPUT XLIX. Quomodo debeat dejecta mens semetipsam discutere, et ad pristinum fervorem innovare.

Adhuc Nabuchodonosor addit et dicit: Et optimates mei, et magistratus mei requisierunt me, et in regno meo restitutus sum, et magnificentia amplior addita est mihi (Dan. IV). Ecce a quibus quaeritur, et in regnum restituitur. Et optimates mei et magistratus mei. Quid est optimum in homine nisi intentiones bonae? Qui vero debent magisterium exercere, 1345Cvel illud interius regnum disponere, nisi deliberationes discretae? In optimatibus ergo mentis intentiones. In magistratibus vero intelligimus mentis deliberationes. Quid vero est hujusmodi optimates et magistratus, amissum regni caput quaerere, et in suum locum restituere, nisi cum summa intentione, et deliberatione investigare, quomodo post ruinam vel sero possit amissum animi vigorem ad primam integritatem reformare? Debet autem anima aliquando diligenter quaerere, qualis ejusmodi fuerit, quam constanter egerit, quantum perfectionis obtinuerit. Debet et consequenter deliberare quomodo in statum pristinum restitui possit, et hoc erit, ut arbitror, regiam illam animi dignitatem per intentionem quaerere, et in suum gradum restituere 1345Dvelle. Hanc regiam dignitatem in alieno corde quaerebant, et in suum locum restitui cupiebant, quia dejectam putabant, qui dicebant: Ubi est timor tuus, et fortitudo tua, patientia tua, et perfectio viarum? (Job IV.) Non solum autem illi qui in malum corruunt, verum etiam illi qui a bono tepescunt debent semetipsos discutere, et pristinum illum religionis fervorem in semetipsis innovare. Hinc est quod ad angelum Ephesi in Apocalypsi scribitur: Habeo adversum te aliquid quod primam charitatem reliquisti. Memento unde excideris, et age poenitentiam, et prima opera fac (Apoc. II).

Notandum tamen sane, quod rex iste a suis dicitur requisitus, non autem ab eisdem restitutus. Sed quis, quaeso, eum restituit, nisi ad quem oculos levavit, 1346Abenedixit, glorificavit? Nam et si forte in nostra jam potestate sit, mentis nostrae statum, qualis modo sit, vel aliquando fuerit, per deliberationem quaerere, non tamen et aeque in nostra potestate est virtutis nostrae detrimenta, pro arbitrio reparare. Sed si studiose exsequimur, quod jam posse ex divino munere accepimus, forte et illud superaddetur, quod nondum potuimus. Et in regno meo, inquit, restitutus sum, et magnificentia amplior addita est mihi.

CHAPTER XLIX. How a cast-down mind ought to examine itself, and to renew itself unto its former fervour.

Nabuchodonosor still adds and says: And my nobles, and my magistrates sought me, and I was restored unto my kingdom, and a greater magnificence was added to me (Dan. IV). Behold by whom he is sought, and into his kingdom restored. And my nobles and my magistrates. What is best in a man but good intentions? And who ought to exercise mastery, 1345Cor to dispose that inward kingdom, but discreet deliberations? In the nobles, therefore, the mind's intentions. But in the magistrates we understand the mind's deliberations. And what is it for nobles and magistrates of this sort to seek the lost head of the kingdom, and to restore him unto his own place, but with the utmost intention and deliberation to investigate how, after the ruin, even late, one may be able to reform the lost vigour of the spirit unto its first integrity? But the soul ought sometimes diligently to inquire what sort of thing of this kind it was, how constantly it acted, how much of perfection it obtained. And it ought consequently to deliberate how it may be restored unto its former state; and this will be, as I judge, to seek that royal dignity of the spirit by intention, and to be willing to restore it 1345Dunto its own step. They were seeking this royal dignity in another's heart, and desiring it to be restored unto its own place, because they thought it cast down, who said: Where is thy fear, and thy fortitude, thy patience, and the perfection of ways? (Job IV.) [Vulg. timor tuus, fortitudo tua … perfectio viarum tuarum, "the perfection of thy ways"] But not only they who fall headlong into evil, but also they who grow lukewarm from good, ought to examine themselves, and to renew in themselves that former fervour of religion. Hence it is that it is written to the angel of Ephesus in the Apocalypse: I have somewhat against thee, that thou hast left the first charity. Remember from whence thou art fallen, and do penance, and do the first works (Apoc. II). [Vulg. Sed habeo adversum te, quod caritatem tuam primam reliquisti. Memor esto itaque unde excideris, "But I have against thee, that thou hast left thy first charity. Be mindful therefore"]

Yet it is soundly to be noted that this king is said to have been sought by his own men, but not to have been restored by the same. But who, I ask, restored him, save he unto whom he lifted up his eyes, 1346Awhom he blessed, whom he glorified? For even if it now perhaps lies within our power to seek by deliberation the state of our mind, of what sort it now is, or once was, yet it does not equally lie within our power to repair at will the declines of our virtue. But if we zealously carry out that which we have already received of the divine gift the power to do, perhaps that also will be added over which we have not yet been able to do. And I was restored, he says, unto my kingdom, and a greater magnificence was added to me.

CAPUT L. Quomodo ex virtute humilitatis animus in bono roboratur, et ad boni perseverantiam solidatur.

Magnificentia amplior post ruinam, ad plenam reparationem additur, quia ex ipso lapsu suo, homo 1346Bsaepe ad veram humilitatem cruditur. Et tunc veraciter magnificentia ampliatur, quando inter virtutum magnalia, virtus etiam humilitatis immobiliter custoditur. Ex virtute vero humilitatis, animi profectio et in bono roboratur, et ad boni perseverantiam solidatur. Constat itaque magnificentiam tunc veraciter ampliari quando, ad consummationem boni, datur etiam gratia perseverandi. Amplificatio itaque magnificentiae, est virtus perseverantiae. Amplificatio magnitudinis, ex virtute humilitatis adjuncta, divinitus gratia confirmationis. Hanc magnificentiae amplificationem Propheta postulabat, cum psallendo orabat: Redde mihi laetitiam salutaris tui, et spiritu principali confirma me (Psal. L). Hujusmodi quotidie amplificationem petimus, 1346Ccum dicimus: Confirma Deus hoc quod operatus es in nobis (Psal. LXVII). Perpendis ex his, ut arbitror, quia multi ad hoc, Deo dispensante, cadunt, ut fortiores resurgant; et qui prius ante lapsum habuerunt solummodo gratiam adjuvantem, post ruinae reparationem, accipiunt simul et gratiam confirmantem. Vere, secundum quod scriptum est: Dominus mortificat, et vivificat, deducit ad inferos, et reducit (I Reg. II). Vere ipse est qui humiliat et sublevat, ipse qui percutit ut sanet (Job V).

CHAPTER L. How from the virtue of humility the spirit is strengthened in good, and made solid unto perseverance in good.

A greater magnificence is added after the ruin, unto full reparation, because out of his very fall a man 1346Bis often instructed cruditur unto true humility. And then is magnificence truly enlarged, when among the great things of the virtues the virtue of humility also is unmovably kept. But from the virtue of humility the spirit's advance is both strengthened in good, and made solid unto perseverance in good. It stands, therefore, that magnificence is then truly enlarged, when unto the consummation of the good the grace of persevering is given also. The amplification of magnificence, therefore, is the virtue of perseverance. The amplification of greatness, from the virtue of humility adjoined, is divinely the grace of confirmation. This amplification of magnificence the Prophet was asking, when in psalmody he prayed: Restore unto me the joy of thy salvation, and strengthen me with a principal spirit (Psal. L). An amplification of this kind we ask daily, 1346Cwhen we say: Confirm, O God, this which thou hast wrought in us (Psal. LXVII). Thou weighest from these things, as I judge, that many fall, by God's dispensing, unto this end, that they may rise again the stronger; and they who before, prior to the fall, had only the assisting grace, after the reparation of the ruin receive at the same time the confirming grace also. Truly, according to that which is written: The Lord killeth and maketh alive, he bringeth down unto hell and bringeth back (I Reg. II). Truly it is he who humbles and lifts up, he who strikes that he may heal (Job V).

CAPUT LI. Quomodo vel qua utilitate oporteat Deum laudare.

Nunc ego Nabuchodonosor laudo et magnifico et glorifico regem coeli, quia omnia opera ejus vera, et 1346Dviae ejus, judicia, et gradientes in superbia potest humiliare (Dan. IV). Superius, uti jam audivimus, benedixit, laudavit atque magnificavit. Hic iterum laudat, magnificat atque glorificat. Superius laudavit cum sensum accipit, et quia beneficiis divinis ingratus non fuit, ad majora provehi meruit. Nam regnum amissum, cum magnificentiae amplificatione accepit. Accepto autem regno, iterum laudat, iterum magnificat. Unde et factum est, ne iterum contumeliose amittat quod et humiliatus recipit et jam cum devotione possedit. Accepto beneficio, lauda et tu, ut merearis ad majora proficere, promotus ad sublimiora nihilominus lauda, ut merearis in ipsis perseverare. Si juxta similitudinem Nabuchodonosor sensum tuum recepisti, si quae 1347Aagenda sunt intelligere potuisti, lauda, glorifica, ut quod accepisti, ex revelante gratia posse intelligere, detur ex aspirante gratia, posse implere. Si juxta ejusdem Nabuchodonosor similitudinem, regnum tuum recepisti, si passionibus tuis dominari potuisti, si bonos affectus in bono posse exercere accepisti, lauda, magnifica, glorifica, ut qui accepisti gratiam proficiendi, accipias pariter et gratiam perseverandi. Libenter ergo lauda summe bonum, glorifica summe beatum, magnifica majestate immensum, aeternitate infinitum. Quia omnia opera ejus vera et viae ejus judicia. Opera ejus, viae sunt ejus. Viae, inquam, per quas ad nos venit, viae per quas nostrae se intelligentiae ingerit et innotescere facit. Ex operibus namque ipsius, ad ejus cognitionem 1347Bproficimus. Omnia igitur opera ejus, quia omnes viae ejus judicia, et omnia ejus vera, quia in veritate facta. Nos saepe in judiciis nostris erramus, unde fit saepe ut nocentes absolvamus et innocentes condemnemus. Sed absque dubio quem latere nihil potest, in suo judicio errare non potest. Sed cum scriptum sit: Universae viae Domini misericordia et veritas (Psal. XXIV), nunquid et illa etiam judicia sunt quae ab ipso misericorditer fiunt? Sed absque contradictione ulla pietas illa divina, in omnibus, quae misericorditer impendit aequitatis metas, non deserit. 1348ANos saepe, cum pietate movemur, aequitatis limites transgredimur, et in eo saepe quod misericordiam impendere nos credimus, contra justitiam actitamus. Sed superna pietas sic misericordiae suae opera dispensat, ut in his omnibus nihil contra justitiam agat, nihil iniquum committat. Viae itaque ejus judicia sunt, quia ab aequitatis tenore et justitiae rectitudine non recedunt, et gradientes in superbia potest humiliare. Quid, quaeso, solum hoc eum posse dicit, de quo veraciter constat, quia omnia possit? Sed quia experto credendum est, forte illud specialius exprimere voluit, quod tertius in seipso proprio experimento probavit. Ante forte illud prae omnibus voluit sensibus nostris imprimere quod maxime expedit memoriter retinere? 1348BScimus autem quia initium omnis peccati superbia (Eccle. X). Scimus quia ante ruinam exaltabitur cor (Prov. XVI). Qui igitur morum ruinam timet, prae omnibus contra superbiam vigilare debet. Valet autem maxime ad conterendam superbiam, et ad verae humilitatis custodiam semper animo retinere, quia ille, qui solet superbis resistere, potest cum voluerit gradientes in superbia humiliare. Hoc eos Nabuchodonosor verbo, hoc nos docuit exemplo, quod quomodo vel quando voluerit, gradientes in superbia humiliare possit.

CHAPTER LI. How, or with what profit, one ought to praise God.

Now I Nabuchodonosor praise and magnify and glorify the King of heaven, because all his works are true, and 1346Dhis ways, judgments, and them that walk in pride he is able to humble (Dan. IV). [Vulg. Nunc igitur ego, "Now therefore I"] Above, as we have already heard, he blessed, praised and magnified. Here again he praises, magnifies and glorifies. Above he praised when he received his sense, and because he was not ungrateful for the divine benefits, he merited to be advanced unto greater things. For he received the lost kingdom with an amplification of magnificence. But the kingdom once received, he praises again, magnifies again. Whence also it came about that he should not again ignominiously lose what he both received when humbled and now possessed with devotion. When a benefit has been received, praise thou also, that thou mayest merit to advance unto greater things; and when promoted unto loftier things, praise none the less, that thou mayest merit to persevere in them. If after the likeness of Nabuchodonosor thou hast received thy sense, if thou hast been able to understand the things 1347Athat are to be done, praise, glorify, that what thou hast received of revealing grace, to be able to understand, may be given thee of the grace that breathes upon thee, to be able to fulfil. If after the likeness of the same Nabuchodonosor thou hast received thy kingdom, if thou hast been able to have dominion over thy passions, if thou hast received the power to exercise good affections in good, praise, magnify, glorify, that thou who hast received the grace of advancing mayest equally receive the grace of persevering also. Gladly, therefore, praise him who is supremely good, glorify him who is supremely blessed, magnify him who is immeasurable in majesty, infinite in eternity. Because all his works are true and his ways judgments. His works are his ways. Ways, I say, by which he comes unto us; ways by which he presses himself upon our understanding and makes himself known. For from his works we advance unto the knowledge 1347Bof him. All his works, therefore, because all his ways are judgments; and all his things are true, because done in truth. We often err in our judgments, whence it often comes about that we absolve the guilty and condemn the innocent. But without doubt he from whom nothing can lie hidden cannot err in his judgment. But since it is written: All the ways of the Lord are mercy and truth (Psal. XXIV), are those things also judgments which are done by him mercifully? But without any contradiction that divine goodness, in all the things which it mercifully bestows, forsakes not the bounds of equity. 1348AWe, when we are moved with pity, often transgress the limits of equity, and often in that very thing wherein we believe ourselves to be bestowing mercy, we act against justice. But the supernal goodness so dispenses the works of its mercy that in all these it does nothing against justice, commits nothing unjust. His ways therefore are judgments, because they depart not from the tenor of equity and the rectitude of justice, and them that walk in pride he is able to humble. What, I ask, does he say that this alone he is able to do, of whom it truly stands that he is able to do all things? But because one must believe him who has had the experience, perhaps he wished to express that thing more especially which he, a third, proved in his own self by his own trial. Beforehand, perhaps, he wished to impress upon our senses before all things that which it is most expedient to retain in memory? 1348BBut we know that pride is the beginning of all sin (Eccle. X). We know that before ruin the heart shall be exalted (Prov. XVI). [Vulg. ante ruinam exaltatur spiritus, "the spirit is exalted"] He therefore who fears the ruin of morals ought before all things to be watchful against pride. But it avails most for the crushing of pride, and for the keeping of true humility, always to retain in the spirit that he who is wont to resist the proud is able, when he will, to humble them that walk in pride. This Nabuchodonosor taught them by word, this he taught us by example: that howsoever or whensoever he will, he is able to humble them that walk in pride.

LIBER TERTIUS.

BOOK THREE.

CAPUT PRIMUM. De triplici vitio mutabilitatis, et inconstantia mentis.

  • 1347C

Videbam in visione mea nocte, et ecce quatuor venti coeli pugnabant in mari magno. Et quatuor bestiae grandes ascendebant de mari diversae inter se. Prima quasi leaena, et alas habebat aquilae. Aspiciebam donec evulsae sunt alae ejus, et sublata est de terra. Et super pedes quasi homo stetit, et cor ejus datum est ei (Dan. VII). Videamus nunc quid sit hoc mare magnum, in quo propheta Daniel vidit hanc, quaecunque sit, pugnam ventorum. Quis autem nescit mare quam sit instabile et inquietum, quod semper movetur ad omnem statum, impulsumque ventorum? Recte itaque in eo, ni fallor, cordis mutabilitas, et inconstantia figuratur, eo quod 1347Dvere sit similis fluctui maris qui a vento movetur et circumfertur. Est autem hoc malum triplex. Est enim mutabilitas propositi, et est mutabilitas promissi, et item mutabilitas voti. Propositum est quod quisque statuit apud semetipsum; promissum est quod quisque statuit apud proximum; votum est quod quisque statuit, sed quasi apud Deum. De mutabilitate propositi est illud: Vult et non vult piger (Prov. XIII). Audi et quomodo mutabilitas promissi arguitur in eo quod dicitur: Nubes et ventus, et pluviae non sequentes, vir gloriosus, et promissa non complens (Prov. XXIII). Voti mutabilitatem 1348Cpraecavere nos voluit qui dicit: Si quid vovisti Deo, ne moreris reddere, displicet enim Deo infidelis, et stulta promissio (Eccle. III). Stulta promissio est quae fit de eo quod fieri non licet, vel quod Deus facere prohibet. Stulta promissio est de eo quod fieri non expedit in eo quod quis vel modestiae metas excedit, vel aliquid contra obedientiam praesumit. De talibus votis dicit Scriptura, quia vota stultorum frangenda sunt. Nec tamen qui tali se voto obligaverunt, pro libitu ejusmodi vinculum abrumpere debent, sed ad eorum arbitrium absolvendi sunt qui potestatem ligandi ac solvendi acceperunt, et habent. Infidelis promissio est quam poenitentia sequitur, vel cordis duplicitas comitatur. Bonum itaque est bona, et bene vovere, ne sit promissio 1348Dstulta. Bonum est, et bene promissa plene exsolvere, ne sit promissio infida: Vovete, inquit, et reddite Domino Deo vestro, qui in circuitu ejus affertis munera (Psal. CLXXV). Alioquin melius est non vovere, quam vota non reddere (Eccle. V). In mutabilitate propositi, notatur vitium inconstantis animi. In mutabilitate promissi, scandalum proximi. In mutabilitate voti, contemptus et injuria Dei. Mala itaque mutabilitas prima, sed pejor secunda, tertia autem omnium pessima. O quam amarum hoc ultimum prae caeteris malum, quam timidam, quam turbidam reddit humanam conscientiam! 1349AHaec est procellosa illa maris aqua, et per timiditatem inquieta, et per amaritudinem salsa. Hoc est illud quod quaerimus, mare magnum, in cujus comparatione non reputatur quodlibet caeterorum. Tenemus mare, restat nunc de ventis, ventorumque pugna inquirere. Quae est autem ista pugna ventorum, nisi fortis impulsus, et vehementia tentationum. Tentatio enim est vita humana super terram. Unde et recte praecipitur, vel admonetur: Fili, accedens ad servitutem Dei sta in timore, et praepara animam tuam ad tentationem (Eccle. II). Diversitas vero ventorum coeli, contrarii affectus sunt animi. Aliter enim spirat concupiscentia, et aliter flat iracundia. Aliter autem et aliter confidentia, vel diffidentia. Quis autem per experientiam 1349Bnon agnoscat quomodo concupiscentia spirat, et anhelat, quam vehementer animum concutiat, quam fortiter inclinet in id quod affectat? Unusquisque enim tentatur concupiscentia sua abstractus, et illectus (Jac. I). Sed saepe inter fortes concupiscentiae aestus violentosque impulsus animus ad semetipsum redit atque redarguit acriterque reprehendit. Sibi irascitur, sibi indignatur, et concupiscentiae flatibus iracundia, ex adverso flando fortiter resistit et reluctatur. Hos iracundiae flatus, atque resultus fieri volebat qui dicebat? Irascimini et nolite peccare (Psal. IV). In hunc itaque modum concupiscentiae et iracundiae flatus; saepe invicem confligunt, et in alterutrum consurgunt. Hujusmodi alternantium flatuum conflictum unusquisque nostrum 1349Cexperiri solet, et sustinere oportet, quandiu caro concupiscit adversus spiritum, et spiritus adversus carnem. Nam quamvis videatur concupiscentia, quandoque vel ad modicum quiescere, incipit iterum et iterum resurgere, et vehementius impugnare. Siluit, inquit Scriptura, terra Juda diebus paucis (I Mach. VII). Si terra et terra Juda, vix potest paucis diebus silere, quanto magis mare? Quid namque per mare, nisi cor instabile. et quid per terram (elementum, scilicet solidum et corpus immobile) rectius intelligimus quam cor firmum et stabile. Si igitur stabile cor raro requiescit, et diebus paucis, quid mirum si cor inquietum et lubricum, et quale denique designatur 1349Dper mare magnum, quid, inquam, mirum si raro silet, et vix paucis momentis? Ventus itaque ille vehementer urens (concupiscentia videlicet spirans et fervens) post momentaneam saepe quietem violentior surgit, et solito validius impellit, et mentis statum pene evertit, in tantum ut incipiat jam animus lassescere et pene deficere, et de perseverantia desperare et in diffidentiam cadere. Flante itaque diffidentiae vento, miserabilius saepe animum periclitari contingit, quam ullis concupiscentiae impulsibus potuit. Timendum est namque valde, ne ventus iste vehemens repente irruat e regione deserti, et concutiens conscientiae domum, totum mentis statum evertat. A facie enim frigoris ejus quis sustinebit? Nisi forte, quod fieri solet, aspirante 1350ADeo ex adverso contrarii flatus exsurgant. Fit namque saepe ut post multam diffidentiae trepidationem, nimiamque fatigationem, animus tandem aliquando redeat ad conscientiam, recurrat ad divinam misericordiam, jactet in Domino curam suam, reparet constantiam, et vix sero resumat fiduciam. Ex tunc incipit confidentia, et diffidentia mutuo rebellare, fortiterque impugnare, et adversis flatibus impellere, et alternis vicibus modo haec, modo illa, superiorem manum habere. Haec illa ventorum pugna, ni fallor, quam propheta Daniel vidit, mysticoque sermone descripsit, et quam forsitan qualis sit nonnullos vestrum, ipsa experientia docuit. Et utinam nossem et possem eam tam utiliter describere quam veraciter constat, me cujusmodi sit per 1350Bexperientiam probasse. Sed videamus quid hanc ventorum pugnam nonnunquam sequi soleat. Videamus qualia illud, quod supra locuti sumus mare magnum monstra gignere contingat.

CHAPTER ONE. Of the threefold vice of mutability, and of the inconstancy of the mind.

  • 1347C

I saw in my vision by night, and behold, the four winds of heaven strove upon the great sea. And four great beasts came up out of the sea, diverse one from another. The first was like a lioness, and had the wings of an eagle. I beheld till its wings were plucked off, and it was lifted up from the earth. And it stood upon its feet as a man, and its heart was given to it (Dan. VII). [Vulg. cor hominis, "a man's heart"] Let us now see what this great sea is, in which the prophet Daniel saw this battle of the winds, whatever it be. But who knows not how unstable and restless the sea is, which is ever moved at every state and impulse of the winds? Rightly therefore, if I mistake not, is the mutability and inconstancy of the heart figured in it, in that 1347Dit is truly like a wave of the sea, which is moved and carried about by the wind. But this evil is threefold. For there is a mutability of purpose, and there is a mutability of promise, and likewise a mutability of vow. A purpose is what each man determines with himself; a promise is what each man determines with his neighbour; a vow is what each man determines, but as it were with God. Of the mutability of purpose is that saying: The sluggard willeth and willeth not (Prov. XIII). Hear also how the mutability of promise is reproved in that which is said: Clouds and wind, and rains not following, a boastful man, and not fulfilling his promises (Prov. XXIII). He wished us to beware 1348Cof the mutability of vow who says: If thou hast vowed anything to God, defer not to pay it; for an unfaithful and foolish promise displeaseth God (Eccle. III). A foolish promise is one that is made concerning that which it is not lawful to do, or which God forbids to be done. A foolish promise is concerning that which it is not expedient to do, in this, that a man either exceeds the bounds of modesty, or presumes something against obedience. Of such vows the Scripture says that the vows of fools are to be broken. Yet they who have bound themselves by such a vow ought not to break a bond of that kind at their own pleasure, but are to be absolved at the judgment of those who have received and have the power of binding and loosing. An unfaithful promise is one which repentance follows, or which duplicity of heart accompanies. It is good, therefore, to vow good things, and to vow them well, lest the promise be 1348Dfoolish. It is good also fully to discharge what has been well promised, lest the promise be faithless: Vow ye, he says, and pay to the Lord your God, ye that round about him bring presents (Psal. CLXXV). [Vulg. omnes qui in circuitu ejus, "all ye that round about him"] Otherwise it is better not to vow, than not to pay the vows (Eccle. V). [Vulg. Multoque melius est non vovere, quam post votum promissa non reddere, "And much better is it not to vow, than after a vow not to perform what is promised"] In mutability of purpose is noted the vice of an inconstant spirit. In mutability of promise, the scandal of one's neighbour. In mutability of vow, the contempt and the wrong of God. Evil, therefore, is the first mutability, but worse the second, and the third the worst of all. O how bitter beyond the rest is this last evil, how fearful, how troubled it renders the human conscience! 1349AThis is that stormy water of the sea, restless through fearfulness, and salt through bitterness. This is that which we seek, the great sea, in comparison whereof none of the rest is reckoned anything. We hold the sea; it remains now to inquire concerning the winds, and the battle of the winds. But what is this battle of the winds, but the strong impulse and vehemence of temptations. For human life upon earth is a temptation. Whence also it is rightly enjoined, or admonished: Son, when thou comest to the service of God, stand in fear, and prepare thy soul for temptation (Eccle. II). [Vulg. sta in justitia et timore, "stand in justice and in fear"] But the diversity of the winds of heaven is the contrary affections of the spirit. For concupiscence breathes in one way, and wrathfulness blows in another. And in one way and another, confidence, or diffidence. But who does not recognize by experience 1349Bhow concupiscence breathes and pants, how vehemently it shakes the spirit, how strongly it bends it toward that which it desires? For every man is tempted, being drawn away and allured by his own concupiscence (Jac. I). But often, amid the strong heats and violent impulses of concupiscence, the spirit returns unto itself, and confutes, and sharply rebukes. It is angry with itself, it is indignant with itself, and wrathfulness, blowing from the opposite quarter, strongly resists and struggles against the blasts of concupiscence. These blasts of wrathfulness, and these recoils, he wished to come about, who said? Be ye angry, and sin not (Psal. IV). In this manner, therefore, the blasts of concupiscence and of wrathfulness; often clash one with the other, and rise up each against the other. A conflict of alternating blasts of this kind each one of us 1349Cis wont to experience, and must endure, so long as the flesh lusteth against the spirit, and the spirit against the flesh. For although concupiscence may seem at some time to rest, at least for a little, it begins again and again to rise up, and to assail more vehemently. It was silent, says the Scripture, the land of Juda, for a few days (I Mach. VII). If the earth, and the land of Juda, can scarcely keep silence for a few days, how much more the sea? For what by the sea, but an unstable heart. and what by the earth (a solid element, that is to say, and an immovable body) do we more rightly understand than a heart firm and stable. If therefore a stable heart rarely rests, and that for a few days, what wonder if a heart restless and slippery, and such in short as is designated 1349Dby the great sea, what wonder, I say, if it is rarely silent, and scarcely for a few moments? That wind, therefore, vehemently burning (concupiscence, that is to say, breathing and boiling), after a momentary quiet often rises the more violent, and drives more strongly than is its wont, and well-nigh overturns the state of the mind, so far that the spirit now begins to grow weary and almost to fail, and to despair of perseverance, and to fall into diffidence. When therefore the wind of diffidence blows, it often befalls that the spirit is more miserably endangered than it could be by any impulses of concupiscence. For it is greatly to be feared lest that vehement wind rush suddenly from the region of the desert, and, shaking the house of conscience, overturn the whole state of the mind. For who shall stand before the face of its cold? Unless perhaps, as is wont to happen, by God's breathing 1350Acontrary blasts rise up from the opposite quarter. For it often comes about that, after much trembling of diffidence and exceeding weariness, the spirit at length at some time returns unto conscience, runs back unto the divine mercy, casts its care upon the Lord, repairs its constancy, and scarcely and late resumes its confidence. From thenceforth confidence and diffidence begin mutually to rebel, and strongly to assail, and to drive with adverse blasts, and by alternate turns, now this, now that, to have the upper hand. This is that battle of the winds, if I mistake not, which the prophet Daniel saw and described in mystical speech, and of what sort it is, experience itself has perhaps taught some of you. And would that I knew and were able to describe it as profitably as it truly stands that I have proved by 1350Bexperience of what kind it is. But let us see what is sometimes wont to follow this battle of the winds. Let us see what monsters that great sea, of which we spoke above, chances to bring forth.

CAPUT II. Qualis, vel quanta conflictio tentationis surgat ex inconstantia mentis.

Quatuor, inquit, bestiae grandes ascendebant de mari diversae inter se. Quid, quaeso, est bestias post ventorum pugnam, de mari ascendere, nisi bestiales motus et irrationabiles impetus, post tentationum violentiam, de cordis instabilitate exsurgere et in apertum prorumpere? Quasi bestialem enim formam sibi induit, qui bestialiter vivit. Nam procul dubio, qui irrationabiliter et bestialiter degit, 1350Cin quantum irrationabiliter, bestialiter. Quid nisi quaedam bestiae sunt, quaelibet vitia? Quid nisi bestiae sunt, quaelibet prava desideria? Ejusmodi bestiae mores lacerant, virtutes jugulant, animas suffocant. Ejusmodi bestiarum incursus Psalmista metuebat, cum psallendo orabat et orando psallebat: Ne tradas bestiis animas confitentes tibi (Psal. LXXIII). Sunt bestiae quae corpus occidunt, animae autem non habent quid faciant. Verumtamen viris sanctis, qui ejusmodi sunt, non sunt valde metuendae, eis praesertim, qui spontaneo nutu, mortificantur tota die, ne propheticum illud improperium videantur incurrere: Illic trepidabant timore ubi non erat timor. Non tradas, ait, bestiae animas confitentes 1350Dtibi (Psal. XIII). Non dicit corpus confitentium tibi, non dicit carnes confitentium tibi, sed ne tradas bestiis animas confitentes tibi. Illae, illae utique bestiae vere timendae sunt, quae animas occidere possunt. Talium bestiarum incursus, cito incurrimus, cum exigentibus meritis divinae illustrationis lumen amittimus: Posuisti, inquit, tenebras, et facta est nox, in ipsa pertransibunt omnes bestiae silvae (Psal. CIII). Talium bestiarum incursus de semetipsis praesumentibus, et de suis meritis gloriantibus, Dominus minatur, cum in Deuteronomii cantico per Prophetam loquitur: Dentes bestiarum immittam in eis (Deut. XXXVIII). Restat autem nunc illud quaerere, cur quatuor tantum dicantur hae bestiae, vel quomodo diversae inter se.

CHAPTER II. What sort, or how great, a conflict of temptation arises out of the inconstancy of the mind.

Four great beasts, he says, came up out of the sea, diverse one from another. What, I ask, is it for beasts to come up out of the sea after the battle of the winds, but for bestial motions and irrational impulses, after the violence of temptations, to rise up out of the instability of the heart and to break forth into the open? For he puts on as it were a bestial form who lives bestially. For without doubt, he who passes his life irrationally and bestially, 1350Cin so far as irrationally, so far bestially. What are any vices whatsoever, but certain beasts? What are any depraved desires whatsoever, but beasts? Beasts of this kind tear morals, cut the throat of virtues, choke souls. The onslaughts of such beasts the Psalmist feared, when in psalmody he prayed and in praying sang: Deliver not up unto the beasts the souls that confess to thee (Psal. LXXIII). There are beasts which kill the body, but have nothing that they can do to the soul. Yet to holy men such as these are not greatly to be feared, and least of all to those who by a spontaneous nod are mortified all the day long, lest they seem to incur that prophetic reproach: There they trembled for fear, where there was no fear. Do not deliver, he says, unto the beast the souls that confess 1350Dto thee (Psal. XIII). [Vulg. trepidaverunt, "they feared"; and at Psal. LXXIII, printed a few lines above, Ne tradas bestiis, "deliver not up unto the beasts", plural] He says not the body of them that confess to thee, he says not the flesh of them that confess to thee, but deliver not up unto the beasts the souls that confess to thee. Those, those beasts assuredly are truly to be feared, which are able to kill souls. The onslaughts of such beasts we quickly run into, when, our deserts requiring it, we lose the light of divine illumination: Thou hast appointed, he says, darkness, and it was night: in it shall all the beasts of the wood go about (Psal. CIII). The onslaughts of such beasts the Lord threatens against them that presume upon themselves and glory in their own merits, when in the canticle of Deuteronomy he speaks by the Prophet: I will send the teeth of beasts upon them (Deut. XXXVIII). But it now remains to inquire why these beasts are said to be four only, or how they are diverse one from another.

CAPUT III. Quod bestialiter vivendi modus sit quadripartitus.

  • 1351A

Ut breviter dicam quid sentiam, omnis bestialiter vivendi modus videtur mihi esse quadripartitus. Alii videntur bona exercere, sed sine modo atque mensura et absque omni discretione. Et quamvis in eis fortassis sit bona intentio, hoc in eis bestiale est, quod vivunt sine modo, et feruntur praecipites, sine rationis judicio et absque consilio. Et quamvis forte zelum rectitudinis habeant, sed secundum scientiam non habent. Alii autem mala exercent, sed timide quidem, et ideo occulte; et idcirco, secundum vocem Psalmistae inhabitabunt, et abscondent (Psal. LV), eo quod argui timeant, vel deprehendi erubescant. Alii item bona exercent in opere, 1351Bsed per hypocrisim quidem, et sine omni bona intentione, utpote amantes primos recubitus in coenis, et primas cathedras in synagogis, et salutationes in foro, et vocari ab hominibus Rabbi. Alii item mala exercent passim, palam, et libere, et absque ullo timore, et pudore, ita ut videantur esse sine fronte, eo quod frons meretricis facta sit illis, et nesciant erubescere. Vides ergo quam sint inter se diversi hujusmodi operarii. Primi et tertii exercent se in bonis, secundi et quarti in malis. Primi et tertii agunt bona, non bene: nam primi indiscrete, tertii simulate, et eo ipso diversi inter se. In malis se exercent tam secundi quam quarti. Sed illi latenter, isti tam patenter quam impudenter, et idcirco ipsi inter seipsos diversi. Primi, sunt qui ambulant 1351Cin magnis et mirabilibus sub se (Psal. CXXX). Secundi sunt, qui sedent in insidiis cum divitibus in occultis, ut interficiant innocentem (Psal. X). Tertii sunt, qui omnia opera sua faciunt ut videantur ab hominibus (Matth. XXIII). Quarti vero, qui nec Deum timent nec homines verentur (Luc. XVIII). In primis itaque est religio indiscreta, in secundis praevaricatio occulta, in tertiis religio ficta, in quartis praevaricatio manifesta. Hi sunt quatuor status eorum, qui irrationabiliter et bestialiter vivunt (si tamen status, et non potius casus dicendi sunt) qui semper hominem de malo in pejus impellunt.

Et notandum quod in his quatuor corruptionum gradibus secundus est pejor quam primus, tertius quam secundus, quartus autem omnium est pessimus. 1351DEt forte quis dubitet de tertio quomodo sit pejor secundo. Sed in tertio statu, quid aliud est quam simulata aequitas? Sed, teste Augustino, didicimus quia simulata aequitas non est aequitas, sed duplex iniquitas.

CHAPTER III. That the manner of living bestially is fourfold.

  • 1351A

That I may briefly say what I think, every manner of living bestially seems to me to be fourfold. Some seem to practise good things, but without mode and measure and apart from all discretion. And although in them there be perhaps a good intention, this in them is bestial, that they live without measure, and are carried headlong, without the judgment of reason and apart from counsel. And although they may perchance have a zeal of rectitude, yet they have it not according to learning. Others, however, practise evil things, but timidly indeed, and therefore secretly; and for that reason, according to the voice of the Psalmist, they will dwell, and will hide themselves (Psal. LV), because they fear to be reproved, or blush to be caught. Others again practise good things in work, 1351Bbut by hypocrisy indeed, and without any good intention, as loving the first couches at feasts, and the first chairs in the synagogues, and salutations in the market place, and to be called Rabbi by men. Others again practise evil things everywhere, openly, and freely, and without any fear and shame, so that they seem to be without a forehead, because a harlot's forehead has been made theirs, and they know not how to blush. Thou seest therefore how diverse among themselves are workmen of this kind. The first and the third exercise themselves in good things, the second and the fourth in evil. The first and the third do good things, but not well: for the first indiscreetly, the third in simulation, and by that very thing they are diverse one from the other. In evil things both the second and the fourth exercise themselves. But those secretly, these as openly as shamelessly, and therefore are they themselves diverse among themselves. The first are they who walk 1351Cin great things and in wonderful things beneath themselves (Psal. CXXX). [Vulg. neque ambulavi in magnis, neque in mirabilibus super me, "nor have I walked in great matters, nor in wonderful things above me"] The second are they who sit in ambush with the rich in secret places, that they may kill the innocent (Psal. X). The third are they who do all their works that they may be seen of men (Matth. XXIII). But the fourth, they who neither fear God nor reverence men (Luc. XVIII). In the first, therefore, there is an indiscreet religion; in the second, a hidden transgression; in the third, a feigned religion; in the fourth, a manifest transgression. These are the four states of them who live irrationally and bestially (if indeed they are to be called states, and not rather falls), which ever drive a man from evil to worse.

And it is to be noted that in these four steps of corruptions the second is worse than the first, the third than the second, and the fourth is the worst of all. 1351DAnd perhaps some may doubt of the third, how it is worse than the second. But in the third state, what else is there than a dissembled equity? But, on Augustine's testimony, we have learned that a dissembled equity is not equity, but a double iniquity.

CAPUT IV. Quibus corruptionis gradibus corruere solet saepe animus humanus.

In primo itaque statu est justitia fatua, in secundo malitia infrenata, in tertio justitia ficta, in quarto malitia effrenata. In primo regnat singularitas, in secundo impietas, in tertio calliditas, in quarto erudelitas. In primo, inquam, regnat singularitas, et dicitur de eo: Vae soli, quia cum ceciderit non habet 1352Asublevantem (Eccle. IV). Vae, inquam, soli, hoc est de se solo, et suo consilio praesumenti, et solitaria quadam praesumptione viventi. In secundo regnat impietas, et de eo dicitur: Vae impio in malum (Isa. III). In tertio regnat calliditas. Callidi autem provocant iram Dei, et dicitur de eis: Vae duplici corde, et terram ingredienti duabus viis (Eccle. II). In quarto regnat crudelitas, et crudelibus dicitur: Vae qui praedaris, nonne et ipse praedaberis (Isai. XXXIII). Crudelitas est rapina, rapina est quando quis tollit res alienas. Quid tibi videtur de eo qui seminat inter fratres discordiam? Impie quidem tecum agit qui tibi aufert pecuniam tuam; quid ille, qui tibi aufert pacem tuam? Nunquid crudeliter agit, qui tibi aufert vestem, 1352Bet non multo magis qui tibi aufert charitatem? Si vero crudele est tollere homini bona exteriora, bona peritura, multo magis crudele erit tollere homini bona interiora, bona in aeternum mansura: Nam charitas est, quae nunquam excidit (II Cor. XIII). Absque dubio, qui homini charitatem tollit, et Deum tollit: Quia Deus charitas est (I Joan. IV). Vae qui praedaris, nonne praedaberis? Quantum tollis, tantum perdis; Deum tollis et Deum perdis. Coelum tollis, coelum amittis. Quid hac impietate crudelius? Quid hac crudelitate vesanius? Ecce unde quo pervenitur, ecce nihilominus et qua descenditur: unde, inquis, et quo? De summa singularitate, ad summam crudelitatem. Sed qua, inquis, et quomodo? Per impietatem et duplicitatem. A primo 1352Csiquidem gradu descenditur ad secundum, de secundo ad tertium, de tertio ad quartum. Nam absque dubio praesumptio dejecta solet inducere aemulationem, aemulatio despecta simulationem, simulatio dejecta, mentis effrenationem. Ad primum namque statum pertinet praesumptio, ad secundum aemulatio, ad tertium simulatio, ad quartum mentis effrenatio. Ad primum insolentia, ad secundum invidentia, ad tertium fraudulentia, ad quartum impudentia, et fortassis hae sunt illae bestiae, quas oportuit quaerere.

CHAPTER IV. By what steps of corruption the human spirit is often wont to fall.

In the first state, therefore, there is a foolish justice; in the second, an uncurbed malice; in the third, a feigned justice; in the fourth, an unbridled malice. In the first reigns singularity, in the second impiety, in the third craftiness, in the fourth erudelitas. In the first, I say, reigns singularity, and of it is said: Woe to him that is alone, for when he shall fall he hath none 1352Ato lift him up (Eccle. IV). Woe, I say, to him that is alone, that is, to him who presumes upon himself alone and upon his own counsel, and lives by a certain solitary presumption. In the second reigns impiety, and of it is said: Woe to the wicked unto evil (Isa. III). In the third reigns craftiness. And the crafty provoke the wrath of God, and of them is said: Woe to the double heart, and to him that goeth on the earth two ways (Eccle. II). In the fourth reigns cruelty, and to the cruel it is said: Woe to thee that spoilest, shalt not thou thyself also be spoiled (Isai. XXXIII). Cruelty is rapine; rapine is when a man takes away another's goods. What seems to thee of him who sows discord among brethren? Impiously indeed he deals with thee who takes away thy money; what of him who takes away thy peace? Does he act cruelly who takes away thy garment, 1352Band not much more he who takes away thy charity? But if it is cruel to take from a man outward goods, goods that shall perish, much more cruel will it be to take from a man inward goods, goods that shall abide unto eternity: For it is charity that never falleth away (II Cor. XIII). Without doubt, he who takes charity from a man takes God also away: Because God is charity (I Joan. IV). Woe to thee that spoilest, shalt not thou be spoiled? As much as thou takest, so much dost thou lose; thou takest God away, and thou losest God. Thou takest heaven away, thou forfeitest heaven. What is crueller than this impiety? What is madder than this cruelty? Behold from whence and whither one comes; behold none the less also by what way one goes down: from whence, sayest thou, and whither? From the height of singularity, unto the height of cruelty. But by what way, sayest thou, and how? Through impiety and duplicity. For from the first 1352Cstep one goes down to the second, from the second to the third, from the third to the fourth. For without doubt presumption cast down is wont to bring in emulation; emulation despised, simulation; simulation cast down, the unbridling of the mind. For to the first state belongs presumption, to the second emulation, to the third simulation, to the fourth the unbridling of the mind. To the first, insolence; to the second, enviousness; to the third, fraudulence; to the fourth, shamelessness; and perhaps these are those beasts which we had to seek.

CAPUT V. De vitio insolentiae.

In descriptione igitur primae bestiae, intelligimus 1352Dvitium insolentiae. Insolitum quasi, non solitum, vel contra solitum dicimus, et inde insolentia, dictum putamus. Recte ergo insolentia dicitur, quando passim et temere, praeter solitum praesumitur. Insolentiae itaque est vitium praeceps, et temeraria praesumptio, sive praeceps et praesumptuosa temeritas, praeter solitum. Est autem multiplex hoc malum. Insolentia siquidem, modo se erigit supra solitum, modo cohibet infra solitum, modo se opponit contra solitum. Sed videamus nunc de prima bestia qualem eam Scriptura sacra describat vel quam convenienter insolentiae proprietatem exprimat. Prima, inquit, quasi leaena, et alas habebat aquilae. Dicitur leo tantae feritatis esse, ut provocatus nec mortem timeat, in tantum ut nec pro periculo mortis, 1353Aa furoris sui impetu se cohibeat. Leo, inquit, fortissimus bestiarum, ad nullius pavebit occursum (Prov. XXX). Vides in leone quam stulta audacia, et quam audax stultitia. Nonne leoninae videtur audaciae et ad faciendum sibi nomen, ambulare in magnis et mirabilibus supra se, omnia ultra vires praesumere, spreto consilio doctorum, contempto praecepto majorum, jejunia, vigilias et ejusmodi observantias ultra modum extendere, et nec saluti corporis parcere, nec animae mortem in debitae obedientiae contemptu horrere?

Nota quod non leoni, sed leaenae assimilatur, quia hic sexus, in impetus sui vehementia protervior et pertinacior invenitur. Vide ergo quam irrationabile et bestiale et vere leoninum simile, nullius consilio, 1353Bnullius praecepto acquiescere, sed in temeritatis suae audacia pertinaciter perdurare. Vultis et hoc breviter audire quid sentiam de ejusmodi bestiae alis? Videtur mihi una esse praecipitationis, altera praesumptionis. Ecce alae duae et ambae aquilinae. Quis hoc de aquila nesciat quod caeteris pene omnibus avibus celerius, vel celsius volare soleat. In praecipitatione attende volatus celeritatem. In praesumptione perpende volatus sublimitatem. Jam superius dictum est, quod insolentia sit praeceps, et praesumptuosa temeritas, praeter solitum. Ut igitur diffusius dicta, compendiosa brevitatis succingam, agnosce leoninum simile in singularitatis temeritate. Adverte in aquilinis alis vitium praesumptionis 1353Cet vitium praecipitationis. Dicitur aquila, rex avium et leo bestiarum. Nunquid ergo non ille videtur aliquid habere leaenae, et aliquid aquilae qui praesumit, tam in activa quam in contemplativa vita, cunctos praecedere et in utraque vita singularem et summum locum tenere? In terra leaena, in coelo est aquila, qui se praesumit caeteris omnibus incedere fortius, volare sublimius, incedere per actionem, volare per contemplationem.

CHAPTER V. Of the vice of insolence.

In the description, therefore, of the first beast we understand 1352Dthe vice of insolence. We say insolitum, as it were non solitum, "not customary", or "contrary to the customary", and thence we suppose insolentia, insolence, to be so named. Rightly therefore is it called insolence, when at random and rashly something is presumed beyond the customary. Insolence, therefore, is a headlong vice, and a rash presumption, or a headlong and presumptuous rashness, beyond the customary. But this evil is manifold. For insolence now raises itself above the customary, now restrains itself below the customary, now sets itself against the customary. But let us now see concerning the first beast, of what sort Holy Scripture describes it, or how fittingly it expresses the property of insolence. The first, he says, was like a lioness, and had the wings of an eagle. The lion is said to be of such fierceness that, when provoked, he fears not even death, so far that not even for the peril of death 1353Adoes he restrain himself from the impulse of his own fury. The lion, he says, the strongest of beasts, will be afraid at the meeting of none (Prov. XXX). Thou seest in the lion how foolish a boldness, and how bold a folly. Does it not seem a matter of lion-like boldness, and done to make oneself a name, to walk in great things and in wonderful things above oneself, to presume all things beyond one's strength, the counsel of teachers being spurned and the precept of elders contemned, to stretch fasts, vigils and observances of this kind beyond measure, and neither to spare the health of the body, nor to shudder at the death of the soul in the contempt of due obedience?

Note that it is likened not to a lion, but to a lioness, because this sex is found more forward and more obstinate in the vehemence of its impulse. See therefore how irrational and bestial and truly lion-like a thing it is to rest in no man's counsel, 1353Bin no man's precept, but obstinately to persist in the boldness of one's own rashness. Do ye wish to hear this also briefly, what I think of the wings of a beast of this kind? It seems to me that the one is of precipitancy, the other of presumption. Behold, two wings, and both of the eagle. Who knows not this of the eagle, that it is wont to fly more swiftly, or more loftily, than almost all other birds. In precipitancy mark the swiftness of the flight. In presumption weigh the loftiness of the flight. It has been said already above that insolence is a headlong and presumptuous rashness, beyond the customary. That I may therefore gird up into a compendious brevity the things said more diffusely, recognize the lion-like likeness in the rashness of singularity. Note in the eagle's wings the vice of presumption 1353Cand the vice of precipitancy. The eagle is called the king of birds, and the lion the king of beasts. Does not he therefore seem to have something of the lioness and something of the eagle, who presumes, in the active life as in the contemplative, to go before all men, and in both lives to hold a singular and highest place? On earth he is a lioness, in heaven he is an eagle, who presumes to walk more strongly than all others, to fly more loftily; to walk by action, to fly by contemplation.

CAPUT VI. Quibus insolentiae gradibus paulatim corrumpitur animus humanus.

Sed videamus nunc quid Salomon de hujusmodi homine sentiat, qui omnia praecipitanter et praesumptuose actitat, qui in conatibus suis nec modum, 1353Dnec modestiam servat: Qui festinus est, inquit, pedibus offendit (Prov. XIX). Hinc est, quod alibi dicit: Haereditas ad quam festinatur in principio, in novissimo benedictione carebit (Prov. XX). Eidem sententiae consonare videtur, quod de antedicta bestia adjungitur: Aspiciebam donec evulsae sunt alae ejus, et sublata est de terra et super pedes quasi homo stetit, et cor ejus datum est ei. Ecce qui de semetipsis ultra modum praesumunt, quomodo gradatim amittunt, unde intumescunt, caeterosque contemnunt. Prius alae evelluntur, postea et ipsius bestiae corpus de terra sublevatur, prius contemplativae, postmodum activae etiam vitae gratia amittitur. Quid est enim alas suas perdere, nisi sublimes illos contemplandi 1354Avolatus amittere? Cum enim post multos jejuniorum et vigiliarum excessus, coeperit corpus tabescere, caput dolere, velis nolis, oportet alas deponere, ad terram redire, et carnis curam agere, et melius est ut sponte deponantur quam invito avellantur. Quid est alas avelli, nisi amittere, cum dolore ingenti, et sine ulla spe recuperandi? Alae evelluntur, quando praecipitationis et praesumptionis audacia, urgente infirmitate, cogente necessitate praeciditur. Nonne ejusmodi alarum evulsionem Dominus minatur, cum per prophetam loquitur: Si exaltatus fueris, ut aquila, et inter sidera coeli posueris nidum tuum, inde traham te, dicit Dominus (Abd. I). Post alarum evulsionem de terra sublevatur; per mentis ergo elevationem, et propter mentis elationem 1354Bde terra excutitur. Et merito, ut credo, eo quod per superbiam inflando, crepando, tabescendo, putrescendo, tamen aliquando fiant tanquam pulvis quem projicit ventus a facie terrae. Sic itaque post alarum amissionem, etiam terrae inhabitatio amittitur, quia post subtractam contemplationis gratiam, ut dictum est, etiam bene operandi gratia superbienti animae subtrahitur. Et qui prius praesumpsit omnes fortiter praecedere, jam incipiat post omnes sequi et tandem remanere. Et qui prius per insolentiam se extendit ultra solitum, postmodum remanet citra solitum, et sic jam de primo insolentiae gradu decidit in secundum, futurum tandem quod timeo, ut de secundo recidat in tertium. Sed ubi, quaeso, de caetero erit dicta jam bestia de coelo amissis 1354Caliis dejecta, et de terra sublevata? An forte illuc redibit unde venit, et iterum in mare descendet, quae de mari ascendit? Sic utique, sic saepe quod ex cordis levitate praesumitur, post in multam mentis fluctuationem in amaritudine. Et hoc forte est terram perdere, omnem propositi sui stabilitatem amittere, et dejectionem suam indigne ferendo, nimiam cordis amaritudinem incurrere. Sic de terra in mare transitur, sic montes in cor maris transferuntur. Quid enim est montes in cor maris transire, nisi de magni conaminis culmine, in intimam cordis malitiam cadere? Nam juxta Veritatis testimonium, abundante iniquitate, refrigescet charitas multorum (Matth. XXIV). Nemo ergo in quantalibet 1354Dvirtutum celsitudine, debet quidquam de suis viribus, sed totum de Domino praesumere, et cum Psalmista cantare: Propterea non timebimus, dum turbabitur terra, et transferentur montes in cor maris (Psal. XXV). Tunc sane montes transferuntur, quando illud Joannis impletur: Exierunt a nobis, sed non erant ex nobis (I Joan. II).

CHAPTER VI. By what steps of insolence the human spirit is little by little corrupted.

But let us now see what Solomon thinks of a man of this kind, who does all things precipitately and presumptuously, who in his endeavours keeps neither measure 1353Dnor modesty: He that is hasty, he says, stumbleth with his feet (Prov. XIX). Hence it is that elsewhere he says: The inheritance which is hastened to in the beginning, in the end shall want a blessing (Prov. XX). To the same sentence there seems to accord that which is added concerning the aforesaid beast: I beheld till its wings were plucked off, and it was lifted up from the earth and stood upon its feet as a man, and its heart was given to it. [Vulg. cor hominis, "a man's heart"] Behold how they who presume upon themselves beyond measure lose by degrees that whereby they swell and despise the rest. First the wings are plucked off, afterwards the body itself of the beast is lifted up from the earth; first the grace of the contemplative life, afterwards that of the active life also is lost. For what is it to lose one's wings, but to lose those lofty 1354Aflights of contemplating? For when, after many excesses of fastings and vigils, the body has begun to waste away and the head to ache, willy-nilly one must lay down the wings, return to the earth, and take care of the flesh; and it is better that they be laid down willingly than plucked off from one unwilling. What is it for the wings to be plucked off, but to lose them with huge grief, and without any hope of recovering them? The wings are plucked off when the boldness of precipitancy and of presumption is cut short, infirmity urging, necessity compelling. Does not the Lord threaten a plucking off of wings of this kind, when he speaks by the prophet: If thou be exalted as an eagle, and set thy nest among the stars of heaven, thence will I draw thee, saith the Lord (Abd. I). [Vulg. et si inter sidera posueris … inde detraham te, "and if thou set thy nest among the stars, thence will I draw thee down"] After the plucking off of the wings it is lifted up from the earth; through the mind's elevation, therefore, and because of the mind's elation, 1354Bit is shaken off from the earth. And deservedly, as I believe, in that by pride, swelling, bursting, wasting, rotting, they yet at some time become as the dust which the wind driveth from the face of the earth. And so, after the loss of the wings, the habitation of the earth also is lost, because after the grace of contemplation has been withdrawn, as has been said, the grace of working well also is withdrawn from the proud soul. And he who before presumed to go strongly before all, let him now begin to follow after all, and at last to be left behind. And he who before through insolence stretched himself beyond the customary, afterwards remains short of the customary; and thus he now falls from the first step of insolence into the second, with this at last to come, which I fear, that from the second he fall back into the third. But where, I ask, will the beast already spoken of be henceforth, cast down from heaven, the others 1354Chaving been lost, and lifted up from the earth? Will it perhaps return thither whence it came, and go down again into the sea, it that came up out of the sea? So indeed, so often what is presumed out of the levity of the heart, afterwards into a great fluctuation of the mind in bitterness. And this perhaps is to lose the earth: to lose all the stability of one's purpose, and, by bearing one's dejection unworthily, to run into an exceeding bitterness of heart. Thus from the earth one passes into the sea; thus are the mountains carried into the heart of the sea. For what is it for mountains to pass into the heart of the sea, but to fall from the summit of a great endeavour into the inmost malice of the heart? For according to the testimony of the Truth, iniquity abounding, the charity of many shall grow cold (Matth. XXIV). No one therefore, in however great a 1354Dloftiness of virtues, ought to presume anything of his own powers, but all of the Lord, and to sing with the Psalmist: Therefore we will not fear when the earth shall be troubled, and the mountains shall be carried into the heart of the sea (Psal. XXV). Then indeed are the mountains carried away, when that saying of John is fulfilled: They went out from us, but they were not of us (I Joan. II).

CAPUT VII. Quomodo ex vitio insolentiae, cadat homo in vitium invidentiae.

Sed si nullus jam locus remanet huic bestiae praeter mare (nam et coelum et terram videtur amisisse), mirum quomodo dicatur, supra pedes stetisse. An forte super cautem, vel rupem aliquam stetisse creditur, ubi et mari undique cingeretur, et fluctibus marinis indesinenter 1355Atunderetur? O quam dura poena, sed praesumptuose singularitati condigna! Ecce, quae prius omnes praecurrere praesumebat, et ex sua praesumptione fatigabat, jam non habet quo eat, nec invenit quomodo requiescat. Et supra pedes, inquit, quasi homo stetit. Et utinam vel sero desinat se angelum credere, et tandem aliquando desistat supra hominem praesumere. Ecce jam angelum exuens non jam volat in summis, sed ambulat in imis, imo jam non ambulat, sed stat qui amorem et spem proficiendi amisit. Non autem jacet, sed stat ut nunquam requiescat. Assidue ergo stat, quia sine intermissione laborat. Pedes sine incessu fatigat, sine fructu laborat. Stat itaque, sed quomodo? Supra pedes, ut homo. Grande spectaculum, sed foedum 1355Bmiraculum, videre quadrupedem bestiam, leoninam denique formam, se supra duos pedes contra coelum erigere, et contra naturam erectam stare. Quae est ista erectio monstruosa, nisi enormis quaedam, et nimis detestanda superbia? Contra Deum se erigit, qui contra supercoelestem majestatem intumescit. Nonne contra Deum intumescit, qui ejus judicia reprehendit? Quis autem nesciat, quomodo praesumptuose mentes soleant contra divina flagella murmurare, aliorum profectibus invidere, et gloriae suae detrimenta impatienter ferre? Murmurant autem non solum contra justitiam Dei, verum etiam contra bonitatem Dei. Contra justitiam Dei in malis suis, contra bonitatem Dei in bonis alienis. Insolens namque animus, et propriae excellentiae cupidus cum 1355Cper nimiam superbiam cadit in invidiam pene molestius fert aliorum profectum quam proprium defectum. O praesumptionem monstruosam quae inde murmurat contra majestatem divinam quod Deus superbis resistat, humilibus autem det gratiam (Luc. IV). An oculus tuus, inquit, nequam est, quia ego bonus sum? (Matth. XX.) Solent autem qui ejusmodi sunt, querelas quasdam quasi rationabiles texere, et murmurationis suae causas (cum verae sint irrationabiles) quasi justas ostendere, et hoc est forte belluinam formam ad hominis se similitudinem erigere, in irrationabili murmuratione contra Deum velut rationes quasdam velle praetendere. Huic sententiae concordare videtur quod adhuc de 1355Dhac bestia dicitur. Cum enim praemissum esset, super pedes stetit quasi homo, statim adjungitur et cor ejus datum est ei. Cujus ejus? Aut cor hominis, aut cor leaenae. Sed cur dicatur leaena cor suum accepisse quod non dicitur amisisse? Accepit cor hominis et carnaliter sapientis cum permittente Deo incidit in prudentiam carnis, vel tunc veraciter cor hominis accipit quando per multam experientiam errorem suum intelligit, et tam suum defectum quam aliorum profectum plenius agnoscit. Sed quod homini divinitus conceditur ad bonum suum, ipse homo saepe abutitur ad malum suum. Et cognitis suis, vel aliorum meritis disparibus, unde deberet compungi ad poenitentiam, inde excrescit ad majorem invidiam. Ecce enim postquam descripta leaena 1356Acor hominis accepit, ei non agnus sed ut sus succedit. Nam saepe cor gloriae cupidum et lividum, aliorum inspecta gloria propriaque ignominia, in invidentiam cadit. Sic vitium vitio succedit, insolentia praecedit, et invidentia succedit, et quod adhuc pejus est, insolentia non recedit.

CHAPTER VII. How from the vice of insolence a man falls into the vice of enviousness.

But if no place now remains to this beast except the sea (for it seems to have lost both heaven and earth), it is a wonder how it is said to have stood upon its feet. Or is it perhaps believed to have stood upon some crag or rock, where it might be girt about on every side by the sea, and unceasingly 1355Abeaten by the waves of the sea? O how hard a punishment, but worthy of presumptuous singularity! Behold, it which before presumed to outrun all, and out of its own presumption wearied them, now has not whither to go, nor finds how it may rest. And upon its feet, he says, it stood as a man. And would that even late it might cease to believe itself an angel, and at length at some time leave off presuming above man. Behold, now putting off the angel, it no longer flies in the highest, but walks in the lowest; nay, now it walks not, but stands, having lost the love and the hope of advancing. Yet it lies not down, but stands, so that it never rests. Continually therefore it stands, because without intermission it labours. It wearies its feet without going forward, it labours without fruit. It stands therefore, but how? Upon its feet, as a man. A grand spectacle, but a foul 1355Bwonder, to see a four-footed beast, a lion-like shape at that, raise itself upon two feet against heaven, and stand upright against nature. What is this monstrous uprearing, but a certain enormous and exceedingly detestable pride? He rears himself against God who swells against the supercelestial majesty. Does he not swell against God who reproves his judgments? But who knows not how presumptuous minds are wont to murmur against the divine scourges, to envy the advances of others, and impatiently to bear the declines of their own glory? But they murmur not only against the justice of God, but even against the goodness of God. Against the justice of God in their own evils, against the goodness of God in the goods of others. For an insolent spirit, and one greedy of its own excellence, when 1355Cthrough excessive pride it falls into envy, bears almost more grievously the advance of others than its own defect. O monstrous presumption, which murmurs against the divine majesty for this, that God resisteth the proud, but giveth grace to the humble (Luc. IV). Is thy eye, he says, evil, because I am good? (Matth. XX.) But they who are of this kind are wont to weave certain complaints as though reasonable, and to display the causes of their murmuring (though in truth they are irrational) as though just; and this perhaps is for the brutish shape to raise itself unto the likeness of a man, to be willing to put forward as it were certain reasons in an irrational murmuring against God. To this sentence there seems to accord what is further said of 1355Dthis beast. For when it had been premised, it stood upon its feet as a man, straightway there is added, and its heart was given to it. Whose, its? Either the heart of a man, or the heart of the lioness. But why should the lioness be said to have received its heart, which it is not said to have lost? It received the heart of a man, and of one savouring carnally, when, God permitting, it fell into the prudence of the flesh; or else it then truly receives the heart of a man when through much experience it understands its own error, and more fully recognizes both its own defect and the advance of others. But what is divinely granted to a man for his own good, the man himself often abuses to his own evil. And when his own merits and those of others are known to be unequal, from that whereby he ought to be pricked unto penitence, thence he grows unto a greater envy. For behold, after the lioness described 1356Areceived the heart of a man, there succeeds it not a lamb, but a bear ut sus. For often a heart greedy of glory and livid, when the glory of others and its own ignominy have been looked upon, falls into enviousness. Thus vice succeeds vice: insolence goes before, and enviousness follows; and what is yet worse, insolence does not withdraw.

CAPUT VIII. De vitio invidentiae.

Plus aliquid sonare videtur invidentia quam invidia. Sicut legens non recte dicitur nisi tunc temporis quando legendo occupatur, sic et invidens tunc quidem recte dicitur quando livore utitur. Ab eo autem quod est invidens, invidentia dicitur. Invidia itaque in invidentiam tunc veraciter excrescit, quando animum eousque exagitat, ut eum 1356Bnullo pene jam tempore quiescere sinat. Invidentia itaque est invidia frequens et animum quiescere non sinens. In secunda autem bestia recte, ni fallor, intelligitur invidentia. Sed, ut hoc melius clarescat, audiamus quomodo eam sermo propheticus describat: Et ecce, inquit, bestia alia, similis urso, in parte stetit, et tres ordines erant in ore ejus et in dentibus ejus, et sic dicebant ei: Surge, comede carnes plurimas (Dan. VII). Est autem ursus, ut omnes novimus, animal deforme, et quod in locis remotis et abditis soleat habitare, et in hoc ipso formam gerit invidiae. Nam et ipsa quidem (utpote foeditatem suam erubescens) semper studet latere. Ursus autem est animal malitiosum et saevum, et forte ejusmodi est invidiae vitium. Invidia est odium 1356Cfelicitatis alienae, et quid magis malitiosum? Quid itaque aliud est invidia quam lucifuga et latebrosa malitia? Invidentia autem est malitia fervens et in occulto saeviens. Est autem ursus animal inquietum valde, ita ut ad stipitem ligatus nunquam pene desinat vel in circuitum pergere, vel pedibus fixis caput nunc in hanc, nunc in illam partem jactitare, et in hoc habet formam non modo invidiae, sed invidentiae, quae animum nullo pene momento sinit requiescere. Dicitur autem de hac bestia quod in parte stetit. Nondum ergo homo in hoc statu funditus bona deserit, nec tamen, ut in superiori, ad omnia se extendit. Stat igitur in parte quia in quibusdam adhuc bonis videtur perdurare. Nemo 1356Denim repente fit turpissimus, sicut e regione nemo repente fit summus. Quasi adhuc itaque in parte consistit, quoniam ad aliqua bona agenda antiqua adhuc hominem consuetudo trahit. Ecce quod in hoc statu animus nondum et apertam malitiam effrenatur in quantum vel timore restringitur, vel pudore cohibetur.

CHAPTER VIII. Of the vice of enviousness.

Enviousness seems to sound something more than envy. As a man is not rightly called a reader save at that time when he is occupied in reading, so too a man is then rightly called envying when he is employing his spite. And from that which is invidens, envying, is invidentia, enviousness, named. Envy, therefore, then truly grows out into enviousness, when it so agitates the spirit that it 1356Bnow scarcely at any time lets it rest. Enviousness, therefore, is a frequent envy, and one that does not let the spirit rest. But in the second beast, if I mistake not, enviousness is rightly understood. But, that this may grow clearer, let us hear how the prophetic word describes it: And behold, he says, another beast, like a bear, stood on one side, and there were three rows in its mouth and in its teeth, and thus they said to it: Arise, eat much flesh (Dan. VII). Now the bear, as we all know, is an ugly animal, and one that is wont to dwell in remote and hidden places; and in this very thing it bears the form of envy. For envy herself too (as blushing at her own foulness) ever strives to lie hidden. But the bear is a malicious and savage animal, and perhaps such is the vice of envy. Envy is the hatred 1356Cof another's happiness; and what is more malicious? What else, therefore, is envy but a light-shunning and lurking malice? But enviousness is a boiling malice, and one that rages in secret. And the bear is a very restless animal, so that when tied to a stake it scarcely ever ceases either to go round about, or, with its feet fixed, to toss its head now to this side, now to that; and in this it has the form not only of envy, but of enviousness, which scarcely for a moment lets the spirit rest. But it is said of this beast that it stood on one side. Not yet, therefore, in this state does a man utterly forsake good things, nor yet, as in the former state, does he extend himself unto all things. It stands therefore on one side, because in certain good things it still seems to persist. For no one 1356Dbecomes suddenly most base, just as, on the opposite side, no one becomes suddenly highest. As it were, therefore, it still stands on one side, since old custom still draws the man to do some good things. Behold that in this state the spirit is not yet unbridled even to open malice, in so far as it is either restrained by fear or checked by shame.

CAPUT IX. De malo invidentiae quibus soleat gradibus accrescere.

Et tres ordines, inquit, erant in ore ejus, et in dentibus ejus. Ipsa Scriptura determinat quomodo tres ordines essent in ore ejus, id est in dentibus ejus. Quid autem in dentibus rectius intelligimus quam malitiae, invidentiae morsus? Quis autem nesciat quam familiare, et domesticum, et velut 1357Aproprie proprium sit invidiae alienam vitam redere et morsibus obtrectationum lacerare? Solet autem hoc malum quibusdam gradibus accrescere. Nam sunt in hominibus quidem quaedam manifeste mala, quaedam autem dubia, ita ut in ambiguum venire possit, utrum sint bona an mala. Solet autem livida mens semper manifesta mala multiplicius exagerare, dubia quaeque in deteriorem partem vertere, aperta autem bona (quia negare non potest) arte qua potest offuscare, et de eis vel aliquid minuere. Primum est oblocutionis, secundum derogationis, tertium detractionis. Oblocutio est quando mordax et reprehensoria locutio versatur circa id, vel fit propter id mali quod vere in homine est. Qui scit quid sit rogare, ex opposito potest agnoscere quid 1357Bsit derogare cum sic sibi per contrarium respondeant quemadmodum suadere et dissuadere. Nonne tibi derogatio videtur cum aestimationem de alio aliquo bonam habenti dicitur noli sic credere, noli ita existimare, falleris, deciperis, longe aliter est quam sentis, tenebras lucem credis et amarum in dulce ponis. Haec utique in apertis bonis locum non habet, quia quae manifesta sunt in alteram partem inflectere non valet. Detrahere autem est de apertis bonis vel aliquid velle minuere, cum non possis penitus evacuare. Detrahit enim qui aliquid inde oblatrando retrahit unde hominis famam gloriosiorem credit. Oblocutio itaque est malae opinionis disseminatio. Derogatio bonae opinionis evacuatio. Detractio bonae opinionis attenuatio.
