Linguam 1357Cobloquentem timebat Psalmista, cum diceret: Tota die verecundia mea contra me est, et confusio faciei meae cooperuit me a facie exprobrantis et obloquentis (Psal. XLIII). Derogatores tangit quod alias dicit: Captabant in animam justi, et sanguinem innocentem condemnabant (Psal. XCIII). Detractores exprimit, cum dicit: Qui retribuunt mala pro bonis, detrahebant mihi, quoniam sequebar bonitatem (Psal. XXXVII). Ecce habes tres ordines dentium in multiplicitate detractionum, derogationum, oblocutionum.

CHAPTER IX. Of the evil of enviousness, by what steps it is wont to increase.

And three rows, he says, were in its mouth, and in its teeth. The Scripture itself determines how there were three rows in its mouth, that is, in its teeth. But what do we more rightly understand by the teeth than the bites of malice, of enviousness? But who knows not how familiar, and homely, and as it were 1357Aproperly proper it is to envy to gnaw redere another's life, and to tear it with the bites of disparagement? But this evil is wont to increase by certain steps. For there are in men some things manifestly evil, and some doubtful, so that it may come into ambiguity whether they are good or evil. But a livid mind is wont always to exaggerate manifest evils the more manifoldly, to turn whatever is doubtful to the worse part, and open goods (since it cannot deny them) to darken by what art it can, and to diminish at least something of them. The first is of speaking-against, the second of derogation, the third of detraction. Speaking-against is when a biting and reproving speech is occupied about that, or is made on account of that evil, which is truly in a man. He who knows what it is rogare, to entreat, can from the opposite recognize what 1357Bit is derogare, to derogate, since they answer to one another by contraries just as to persuade and to dissuade do. Does it not seem to thee derogation when to one who holds a good estimation of some other man it is said, do not so believe, do not so esteem, thou art mistaken, thou art deceived, it is far otherwise than thou thinkest, thou believest darkness to be light and puttest bitter for sweet. This assuredly has no place in open goods, because it cannot bend the things that are manifest to the other side. But to detract is to be willing to diminish at least something of open goods, when thou canst not utterly empty them out. For he detracts who by barking against draws back something from that whereby he believes a man's reputation to be the more glorious. Speaking-against, therefore, is the disseminating of an evil opinion. Derogation, the emptying out of a good opinion. Detraction, the thinning of a good opinion. The tongue 1357Cthat speaks against the Psalmist feared, when he said: All the day long my shame is before me, and the confusion of my face hath covered me, at the face of him that reproacheth and speaketh against me (Psal. XLIII). [Vulg. a voce exprobrantis, "at the voice of him that reproacheth"] He touches the derogators in what he says elsewhere: They were catching at the soul of the just, and were condemning innocent blood (Psal. XCIII). [Vulg. Captabunt … condemnabunt, "they shall catch … they shall condemn"] He expresses the detractors when he says: They that render evil for good detracted from me, because I followed goodness (Psal. XXXVII). Behold, thou hast the three rows of teeth in the manifoldness of detractions, derogations, speakings-against.

CAPUT X. Quomodo ex vitio invidentiae mens cadat in vitium fraudulentiae.

Nunc est facile videre quam recte conveniat huic 1357Dbestiae, quod loco novissimo ponitur dum dicitur: Surge, comede carnes plurimas. Quid est enim carnes comedere, nisi aliena infirma dente maledico lacerare? Qui enim alienam vitam libenter rodunt, qui derogationibus studiose deserviunt, qui proximorum infirmitates delectabiliter carpunt, quid aliud quam carnes comedunt? Ejusmodi carnium corrosores, Propheta jam per experientiam noverat, quando dicebat: Appropiant super me nocentes, ut edant carnes meas (Psal. XXVI). Sed quod dicitur: Surge, comede, quid, quaeso, erit praeceptio an permissio, suggestio an instigatio? Sed si a bonis angelis dictum putamus, consequenter et in eorum verbis solam permissionem factam intelligamus. Tale est illud quod ad Judam 1358Adicitur: Quod facis, fac citius (Joan. XIII). Si autem constat a malis angelis dictum fuisse, possumus in ejusmodi verbis tam suggestionem, quam instigationem intelligere. Solent enim semper mali spiritus malignis mentibus maligna suggerere, et ad omnem malitiam instigare. Quod sit malitiosum, quamve perniciosum detractionibus deservire, et in hoc unumquemque vitio suo satisfacere, ex illa potes Salomonis sententia advertere: Si mordeat serpens in silentio, nihil eo minus habet, qui occulte detrahit (Eccli. X). Nonne huic sententiae concinit, quod Psalmista de ejusmodi dicit: Sepulcrum patens est guttur eorum, linguis suis dolose agebant, venenum aspidum sub labiis eorum (Psal. V). O malum exsecrabile et tam loquenti, quam audienti exitiale: 1358BSurge, inquit, comede carnes plurimas. Et quid aliud ursus, quid aliud haec ferina bellua novit, quam vel suas plantas sugere, vel alienas carnes lacerare? Quid, inquam, invidentiae monstrum aliud officii gerit, quam vel suas molestias plangere, vel aliena infirma carpere? Sed utinam solas carnes lacerarent, utinam vel ossibus parcerent! Nunc autem omnia laniant, omnia lacerant, unde fit ut tandem aliquando omnibus odiosi fiant. Qui enim loquitur, inquam, non potest latere. Sed cum incipiunt agnosci, incipiunt pariter contemni et odio haberi. Sed invida mens atque superba, quanto putas dolore uritur, quanto livore torquetur, cum alios videat de die in diem ad meliora proficere, seipsum quotidie in omni opinione decrescere, et magis magisque 1358Cvilescere? Unde fit saepe ut se ad callida argumenta convertat, dum summo cum studio et sollicitudine quaeritat, quomodo vel suam gloriam dilatare, vel aliorum obnubilare queat. Incipit itaque extunc sanctitatem simulare, et per hypocrisim paulatim se ad omnem fraudulentiam tradere. Sic pardus urso succedit, dum mens lubrica de vitio in vitium defluit, et per invidentiae malum in fraudulentiam cadit.

CHAPTER X. How from the vice of enviousness the mind falls into the vice of fraudulence.

Now it is easy to see how rightly there befits this 1357Dbeast that which is set in the last place when it is said: Arise, eat much flesh. For what is it to eat flesh, but to tear another's weak things with a slanderous tooth? For they who gladly gnaw another's life, who zealously serve derogations, who delightedly pluck at the infirmities of their neighbours, what else do they but eat flesh? Such gnawers of flesh the Prophet had already known by experience, when he said: They that harm me draw near upon me, to eat my flesh (Psal. XXVI). But that which is said, Arise, eat, what, I ask, will it be, a command or a permission, a suggestion or an instigation? But if we think it was said by good angels, let us consequently understand that in their words also only a permission was made. Such is that which is said to Judas 1358A: That which thou dost, do quickly (Joan. XIII). But if it stands that it was said by evil angels, we can understand in words of this kind both a suggestion and an instigation. For evil spirits are ever wont to suggest malignant things to malignant minds, and to instigate them unto every malice. How malicious a thing it is, and how pernicious, to serve detractions, and in this for each man to satisfy his own vice, thou canst note from that sentence of Solomon: If a serpent bite in silence, he that detracteth secretly hath nothing the less (Eccli. X). Does not that accord with this sentence which the Psalmist says of such men: Their throat is an open sepulchre, with their tongues they acted deceitfully, the venom of asps is under their lips (Psal. V). O evil execrable, and deadly as much to him that speaks as to him that hears: 1358BArise, he says, eat much flesh. And what else does the bear, what else does this wild brute know, but either to suck its own paws, or to tear the flesh of others? What other office, I say, does the monster of enviousness discharge, but either to bewail its own troubles, or to pluck at the weak things of others? But would that they tore the flesh only, would that they spared at least the bones! But now they mangle all things, they tear all things, whence it comes about that at length at some time they become hateful to all. For he who speaks, I say, cannot lie hidden. But when they begin to be recognized, they begin likewise to be despised and to be held in hatred. But an envious and proud mind, with how great a grief thinkest thou it is burned, with how great a spite is it tormented, when it sees others advance from day to day unto better things, and itself daily decrease in every opinion, and grow more and more 1358Cworthless? Whence it often comes about that it turns itself to crafty devices, while with the utmost zeal and solicitude it seeks how it may either spread wide its own glory, or becloud the glory of others. It begins therefore from thenceforth to dissemble sanctity, and through hypocrisy little by little to give itself over to every fraudulence. Thus the leopard succeeds the bear, while a slippery mind flows down from vice to vice, and through the evil of enviousness falls into fraudulence.

CAPUT XI. De vitio fraudulentiae.

Tunc procul dubio fraus in fraudulentiam exerescit, quando ex multa jam malitia menti accidit, ut libenter omnino decipere velit cum possit, et post 1358Dmultum usum, et exercitium, nihilominus per multam jam astutiam suppetit, ut decipere facile possit, cum velit. Quid enim aliud est fraudulentia, quam astuta malitia, ad omnem fraudem tam prona quam prompta? Recte hypocritarum fraudulentia in pardo figuratur qui per totum corpus maculis quibusdam respergitur. Nam hypocritae quidem sanctitatem opere praetendunt, sed perversum est quod diligunt, et quod intus vivit in affectione, necesse est quandoque erumpat et appareat in opere. Agunt itaque qui ejusmodi sunt, bona in manifesto, et mala in occulto. Qui igitur ad dissolutionis opera furtive erumpunt, vel quotiescunque excusabiliter id possunt, quid aliud quam actionis suae corpus quibusdam maculis respergunt? Videamus itaque 1359Aqualis esse dicatur bestia haec, quae tertio loco describitur. Post haec, inquit, aspiciebam, et ecce altera quasi pardus, et alas habebat quasi avis quatuor super se, et quatuor capita erant super bestiam, et potestas data est ei (Dan. VII). Attende quam recte alas habere dicantur, quae in hac bestia designantur. Certe eum volucres volant, seipsas supra semetipsas levant. Certe cum volare volunt seipsas per inane suspendunt. Nonne ejusmodi mentes seipsas supra semetipsas attollunt, et per inane suspendunt, cum propter ambitionem et inanem gloriam durae districtionis observantias pene ultra humanae possibilitatis mensuram extendunt? Virtutis enim studia exercere, et virtutum merita, vel praemia non curare, nec quaerere, quid aliud est quam se per 1359Binane in alta suspendere? Et quid aliud est multa capita habere, quam nihil simpliciter agere? Nam, sicut in alis occupationem et exercitationem, sic in capite intelligimus intentionem. Nam caput totum corpus regit, et intentioni omnis nostra actio subservit.

CHAPTER XI. On the vice of fraudulence.

Then without doubt fraud grows exerescit into fraudulence, when out of much malice it now befalls the mind that it is altogether willing to deceive when it can, and after 1358Dmuch use and practice there is nonetheless at hand to it, through much cunning now, that it can easily deceive when it wills. For what else is fraudulence than a cunning malice, as prone to every fraud as it is prompt? Rightly is the fraudulence of hypocrites figured in the leopard, which is sprinkled over its whole body with certain spots. For hypocrites indeed make a show of holiness in their work, but what they love is perverse; and what lives within in the affection must needs at some time break forth and appear in the work. And so they that are of this sort do good things openly and evil things in secret. They therefore who break forth stealthily into works of dissoluteness, or as often as they can do it excusably, what else do they do than sprinkle the body of their own action with certain spots? Let us see, then, 1359Aof what sort this beast is said to be, which is described in the third place. After these things, he says, I was beholding, and lo, another as it were a leopard, and it had wings as of a bird, four upon itself, and four heads were upon the beast, and power was given to it (Dan. VII). [Vulg. Dan. 7:6 reads et ecce alia quasi pardus and quatuor capita erant in bestia, "in the beast"; Migne prints altera for alia, and super bestiam, "upon the beast"] Give heed how rightly those that are designated in this beast are said to have wings. Certainly when eum birds fly, they lift themselves above themselves. Certainly when they will to fly, they suspend themselves through the void. Do not minds of this sort lift themselves above themselves, and suspend themselves through the void, when for the sake of ambition and empty glory they stretch out the observances of a hard strictness almost beyond the measure of human possibility? For to exercise the zeals of virtue, and not to care for or to seek the merits or the rewards of the virtues, what else is it than to suspend oneself on high through 1359Bthe void? And what else is it to have many heads, than to do nothing simply? For as in the wings we understand occupation and exercise, so in the head we understand intention. For the head rules the whole body, and all our action is subservient to the intention.

CAPUT XII. De malo fraudulentiae, quam sit multiforme in opere.

Vultis adhuc plenius nosse quales alas soleat fraudulentia habere? Ut igitur breviter exprimam quid sentiam: Una dicatur simulationis, alia dissimulationis, tertia ostentationis, quarta excusationis. Solent namque simulatrices mentes et hypocrisi deservientes; solent, inquam, non solum jactitare quae habent, verum etiam simulare se habere 1359Cbona, quae omnino non habent. Solent econtrario manifesta mala callide excusare, occulta autem mala (ac si omnino non habeant) astute dissimulare. Simulatio et dissimulatio est de occultis. Sed simulatio de occultis bonis, dissimulatio vero de occultis malis. Econtra autem ostentatio et excusatio solent esse de manifestis, sed illa de manifestis bonis, ista de manifestis malis. Nam in his bonis quae manifesta sunt, et negari non possunt, locum habet ostentatio, sicut et in solis manifestis malis quaeritur excusatio. Sinistra nostra quam dextera in medium rarius profertur, et eo ipso rarius videtur. Recte igitur per sinistram occultiora, et recte per dexteram intelligimus manifestiora. Videntur 1359Ditaque alae simulationis et dissimulationis pertinere ad sinistram, quemadmodum et alae ostentationis et excusationis ad dexteram. Habes ergo in ostentatione et simulatione par alarum primum. Habes et aeque in excusatione et dissimulatione par alarum secundum. Nam ostentatio et simulatio est de solis bonis. Sed illa de veris et manifestis, quasi ala a dextris. Ista de fictis, quasi ala a sinistris. In his duabus alis volant, et se proximis commendantes, opinionemque suam propalantes, gloriam suam circumquaque dilatant. Excusatio autem et dissimulatio est de solis malis, sed illa de manifestis quasi ala una a dextris. Ista de occultis, quasi ala alia a sinistris. Sicut in superioribus duobus, ut dictum est, volant, sic in istis posterioribus corpus suum 1360Avelant. Nam absque dubio simulatores et hypocritae solent hinc inde sub excusationis et dissimulationis obductione posteriora sua et inhonestiora velare.

CHAPTER XII. On the evil of fraudulence, how multiform it is in work.

Do you wish to know yet more fully what kind of wings fraudulence is wont to have? That I may therefore briefly express what I think: let one be called the wing of simulation, another of dissimulation, the third of ostentation, the fourth of excuse. For simulating minds, and those that are slaves to hypocrisy, are wont; they are wont, I say, not only to boast of the goods they have, but even to simulate that they have 1359Cgoods which they have not at all. On the contrary they are wont craftily to excuse manifest evils, but hidden evils (as if they had them not at all) cunningly to dissimulate. Simulation and dissimulation are concerning hidden things. But simulation concerning hidden goods, dissimulation concerning hidden evils. On the other side, however, ostentation and excuse are wont to be concerning manifest things; but the former concerning manifest goods, the latter concerning manifest evils. For in those goods which are manifest and cannot be denied, ostentation has its place, just as in manifest evils alone excuse is sought. Our left hand is brought forth into the midst more rarely than the right, and by that very fact is more rarely seen. Rightly therefore by the left we understand the more hidden things, and rightly by the right the more manifest. And so the wings 1359Dof simulation and of dissimulation seem to belong to the left, even as the wings of ostentation and of excuse to the right. Thou hast therefore in ostentation and simulation the first pair of wings. And thou hast likewise in excuse and dissimulation the second pair of wings. For ostentation and simulation are concerning goods alone. But the former concerning true and manifest ones, as it were a wing on the right; the latter concerning feigned ones, as it were a wing on the left. On these two wings they fly, and commending themselves to their neighbours and publishing abroad their own reputation, they spread their glory round about. But excuse and dissimulation are concerning evils alone: the former concerning manifest ones, as it were one wing on the right; the latter concerning hidden ones, as it were the other wing on the left. As on the two former, as has been said, they fly, so on these latter they veil their own 1360Abody. For beyond doubt simulators and hypocrites are wont, on this side and on that, under the covering of excuse and of dissimulation, to veil their hinder and more shameful parts.

CAPUT XIII. De malo fraudulentiae, quam sit multiplex intentione.

Ecce de numero alarum videamus et de pluralitate capitum. Scimus autem quia hypocritarum fraudulentia in omni eo quod actitat ad honoris sui promotionem anhelat. Omne enim quod agit, vel agendo intendit, ambitioni deservit. Nihil autem aliud est ambitio quam honoris affectatio. Prima autem species hujus mali est affectatio libertatis, secunda affectatio dignitatis, tertia affectatio auctoritatis, quarta affectatio potestatis. Ecce quomodo ambitio 1360Bin quatuor se capita dividit, cui omnis, ut dictum est, hypocritarum actio subservit. Libertatis affectatio est, quando jam quis subterfugitat aliis subesse. Dignitatis affectatio est superiora ambiendo semper de gradu in gradum anhelare. Auctoritatis affectatio est, cum jam ambit quis vir magni consilii et sanctitatis omnibus apparere et statuenda quaeque, vel diffinienda ab ejus consilio vel arbitrio pendere. Potestatis affectatio est, cum jam ambit aliis praeesse. Videmus saepe illos etiam qui voluntariam obedientiam professi sunt, quam libenter, quam desideranter molestas satis et onerosas occupationes suscipiunt, ut exeundi, loquendi, agendi majusculam libertatem ex administrationis suae occasione obtineant. Quid agit hoc, quaeso, nisi libertatis ambitio? 1360CVidemus alios niti semper de gradu in gradum, et qui inferioris gradus sunt aspirare ab subdiaconatum, subdiaconos ad diaconatum, diaconos ad presbyteratum; et cum nihil ex eo fortassis majoris libertatis vel potestatis acquisierint, nonnihil tamen arbitrantur tenere prae caeteris saltem superiorem locum. Sed unde hoc in eis nisi ex ambitione dignitatis? Docet nos ille illius Achitopel loquens quid in quibusdam possit ambitio auctoritatis, docet nos proditor et parricida Absalon quid valet ambitio potestatis.

CHAPTER XIII. On the evil of fraudulence, how manifold it is in intention.

Behold, let us see concerning the number of the wings and concerning the plurality of the heads. Now we know that the fraudulence of hypocrites, in all that it keeps doing, pants after the advancement of its own honour. For all that it does, or intends in doing, is a slave to ambition. But ambition is nothing else than the coveting of honour. Now the first species of this evil is the coveting of liberty, the second the coveting of dignity, the third the coveting of authority, the fourth the coveting of power. Behold how ambition 1360Bdivides itself into four heads, to which, as has been said, the whole action of hypocrites is subservient. The coveting of liberty is when one now shrinks from being under others. The coveting of dignity is, by courting higher things, always to pant from step to step. The coveting of authority is when one now courts to appear to all a man of great counsel and of holiness, and that whatever is to be established or defined should hang upon his counsel or his judgment. The coveting of power is when one now courts to be set over others. We often see even those who have professed a voluntary obedience, how gladly, how eagerly they take upon themselves occupations troublesome enough and burdensome, that from the occasion of their own administration they may obtain a somewhat greater liberty of going out, of speaking, of acting. What does this, I ask, unless the ambition of liberty? 1360CWe see others striving always from step to step, and those who are of a lower grade aspiring from the subdiaconate, subdeacons to the diaconate, deacons to the presbyterate; and though from it they have perhaps acquired nothing of greater liberty or of power, yet they judge it no small thing to hold at least a higher place than the rest. But whence is this in them, unless from the ambition of dignity? That man of that Achitopel, speaking, teaches us what the ambition of authority can do in certain men; the traitor and parricide Absalon teaches us what the ambition of power avails.

CAPUT XIV. Quomodo ex vitio fraudulentiae mens cadat in vitium impudentiae.

1360DNotandum autem quod de hac bestia dicitur: Quia potestas data est ei, et absque dubio miro Dei occultoque judicio majora sunt pene quae hypocritae facere possunt, et faciunt et districtioris vitae observantias custodiunt, quam hi qui se propter Deum affligunt, et cum constet quia omnis caro fenum, et omnis gloria ejus quasi flos feni, plus tamen laborant saepe sectatores vanitatis pro gloria vana et transitoria, quam veri aemulatores pro gloria vera et aeterna. Sed valde est difficile, imo pene impossibile ejusmodi homines diu posse latere. Audi quid hic Salomon sentiat cum dicat: Qui ambulat simpliciter, ambulat confidenter, qui autem depravat vias suas manifestus erit (Prov. X). Oportet ergo dupliciter et fraudulenter ambulantes, et viarum suarum depravatores 1361Amanifestos fieri et illud in eis propheticum impleri: Denuda turpitudinem tuam, discooperi humerum, revela crura, transi flumina, revelabitur ignominia tua, et videbitur opprobrium tuum. (Isa. XLVII). Ejusmodi homines saepe cum in hoc fraudulentiae statu deprehensi fuerint, adhuc in deteriora ruunt: nam, cum ulterius in suis pravitatibus posse latere diffidunt, cum unicum illud gloriationis suae gaudium sine ulla spe recuperandi amittunt, statim ad apertam impudentiam erumpunt. Ecce quomodo per fraudulentiam praecipitatur homo in impudentiam.

CHAPTER XIV. How from the vice of fraudulence the mind falls into the vice of shamelessness.

1360DBut it is to be noted what is said of this beast: That power was given to it; and beyond doubt, by the wonderful and hidden judgment of God, the things which hypocrites can do are almost greater, and they do them and keep the observances of a stricter life, than they who afflict themselves for God's sake; and although it is established that all flesh is hay, and all the glory thereof as the flower of hay [Vulg. Isa. 40:6 flos agri, "the flower of the field"], yet the followers of vanity often labour more for a vain and transitory glory than true emulators for the true and eternal glory. But it is very difficult, nay almost impossible, that men of this sort should be able long to lie hid. Hear what Solomon thinks here, when he says: He that walketh simply, walketh confidently, but he that perverteth his ways shall be made manifest (Prov. X). It behoves therefore that those who walk doubly and fraudulently, and the perverters of their own ways, 1361Abe made manifest, and that that prophetic word be fulfilled in them: Uncover thy shame, lay bare thy shoulder, make bare thy legs, pass over the rivers, thy ignominy shall be revealed, and thy reproach shall be seen. (Isa. XLVII). Men of this sort, when they have been caught in this state of fraudulence, often rush yet into worse things: for when they despair of being able any longer to lie hid in their own depravities, when they lose that one joy of their boasting without any hope of recovering it, straightway they break forth into open shamelessness. Behold how through fraudulence a man is cast headlong into shamelessness.

CAPUT XV. De vitio impudentiae.

Attende, obsecro, quam sit bestialis, et plus 1361Bquam bestialis vita, rejecto humano pudore, ad omnia se praecipitem dare, et conspectum divinum vel vultus hominum, nec in erubescendis erubescere, et tandem aliquando ad omnem se malitiam effrenare. Quid enim aliud est impudentia quam praeceps, et effrenata malitia? Audiamus nunc itaque quid de hac ultima bestia dicatur: Post haec aspiciebam, inquit, in visione noctis, et ecce bestia quarta terribilis atque mirabilis et fortis nimis. Dentes ferreos habebat magnos, comedens atque comminuens, et reliqua pedibus suis conculcans. Dissimilis autem erat caeteris bestiis quas videram ante eam, et habebat cornua decem (Dan. VII). Ecce bestiam dicit et tamen quae bestia sit, vel cui 1361Cbestiae similis sicut in superioribus non dicit. Vide ergo quod genus monstri in vitiis sit, quod modo mystice describitur cui nulla in tot bestiarum monstris comparabilis invenitur. Scimus autem quia mens, quae invidia et impudentia corrupta fuerit, de caetero nulli omnino quia nec sibi, nec aliis parcit. Nam invidia quidem tollit homini amorem et compassionem proximi; impudentia vero moderantiam et circumspectionem sui. Et saepe qui ejusmodi sunt, multos labores, multos dolores patienter imo et libenter tolerant, ut alios affligant, et malunt ipsi miseriam sustinere, quam aliis non inferre. Vide ergo quam sit mirabile, quamve horribile, hominem humanitatis oblitum, hominem exuere, et cor belluinum, imo plus quam belluinum induere. 1361DEt ecce, inquit, bestia quarta terribilis atque mirabilis et fortis nimis. Nunquid non est fortis et fortis nimis, quae ob mala proximis inferenda, tam patiens est ad mala toleranda? Dentes autem et ungues ferreos habere dicitur, qui ad omnem crudelitatem effrenatur. Ferrum enim quod caetera omnia metalla domat, recte satis crudelitatem designat. Ferreos ergo dentes et ungues accipit, qui verbis et operibus in proximos saevit, De alia bestia dictum est, quod carnes plurimas comedat. De hac autem dicitur, quod omnia comminuat. Quae ergo omnia comminuit, non solum aliorum infirma, sed et fortia capit, damnat, annihilat. Omnia itaque per diffamationem lacerat, et quod infamare non potest, per despectum calcat. Ut autem caetera 1362Ataceam, in hoc maxime videtur haec bestia caeteris dissimilis esse, quod in hoc novissimo statu nescit mens improba erubescere. Dissimilis erat, inquit, caeteris bestiis quas videram ante eam, et habebat cornua decem.

CHAPTER XV. On the vice of shamelessness.

Give heed, I beseech thee, how bestial, and more 1361Bthan bestial, a life it is, when human shame has been cast off, to give oneself headlong to all things, and the divine gaze or the countenances of men, not to blush even at things to be blushed at, and at length at some time to unbridle oneself unto every malice. For what else is shamelessness than a headlong and unbridled malice? Let us therefore now hear what is said of this last beast: After these things I was beholding, he says, in the vision of the night, and lo, a fourth beast, terrible and wonderful, and exceeding strong. It had great iron teeth, eating and breaking in pieces, and treading down the rest with its feet. But it was unlike to the other beasts which I had seen before it, and it had ten horns (Dan. VII). Behold, he says a beast, and yet what beast it is, or to what 1361Cbeast it is like, he does not say, as in the foregoing. See therefore what kind of monster among the vices this is, which is now mystically described, to which none among so many monsters of beasts is found comparable. Now we know that the mind which has been corrupted by envy and by shamelessness spares thenceforth no one at all, since it spares neither itself nor others. For envy indeed takes away from a man love and compassion for his neighbour; but shamelessness, moderation and circumspection of himself. And often they that are of this sort bear many labours, many griefs, patiently, nay even gladly, that they may afflict others; and they had rather themselves endure misery than not inflict it upon others. See therefore how wonderful, or rather how horrible a thing it is, that a man, forgetful of humanity, should put off the man, and put on a beastly heart, nay one more than beastly. 1361DAnd lo, he says, a fourth beast, terrible and wonderful, and exceeding strong. Is it not strong, and exceeding strong, which, for the sake of inflicting evils upon its neighbours, is so patient in bearing evils? But he is said to have iron teeth and nails who is unbridled unto every cruelty. For iron, which tames all the other metals, rightly enough designates cruelty. He therefore receives iron teeth and nails who rages in words and in works against his neighbours, Of another beast it was said that it devoured very much flesh. But of this one it is said that it breaks all things in pieces. It therefore which breaks all things in pieces seizes, condemns, annihilates not only the weak things of others, but the strong as well. And so it tears all things by defamation, and what it cannot defame it treads down by contempt. But that I may be silent about the rest, 1362Ain this chiefly does this beast seem to be unlike the others, that in this last state the wicked mind knows not how to blush. It was unlike, he says, to the other beasts which I had seen before it, and it had ten horns.

CAPUT XVI. Quomodo mens obduratur per impudentiae vitium, et primo contra Deum.

O cruenta bellua, quae et per despectum conculcat quosque inferiores, et rebellionis cornibus oppugnat superiores! Utinam de hujusmodi cornibus impleatur, quod utiliter minatur qui in psalmo loquitur: Et omnia cornua peccatorum confringam, et exaltabo cornua justi (Psal. LXXIV). Quaeramus ergo diligentius qualia cornua impudentia habeat 1362Bin quibus repugnare vel impugnare soleat. Illud autem novimus, quia cornua a carne excrescunt, et supra carnis sensibilitate obdurescunt. Decem autem modis, ut mihi videtur, anima impudens obdurescit, et illam quam habere decet cordis sensibilitatem amittit. Tribus namque modis mens obdurescit erga Deum, tribus apud semetipsum, quatuor autem modis contra proximum. Erga Deum per arrogantiam, per extollentiam, per contumaciam. Arrogantia est, quando quod divinitus accepit, sibi ascribit. Extollentia est, quando semetipsum supra id quod accepit attollit: per arrogantiam in se gloriatur de eo quod habet, per extollentiam de eo etiam quod non habet. Per arrogantiam et extollentiam 1362Cagitur, ut gratia divina merito subtrahatur, et ad contumaciam obduretur, desertus autem praevaricator efficitur. Nunquam autem est spontanea praevaricatio praecepti sine contemptu Dei. Contumacia itaque est, quando Deum et praecipientem despicit et minantem contemnit. Per arrogantiam impugnatur bonitas Dei, per extollentiam veritas Dei, per contumaciam potestas Dei. Pro certo scimus, et absque ulla haesitatione, quia Deus est qui operatur omnia pro bona voluntate (Ephes. I). Ex sola itaque Dei bonitate est, quidquid in nobis boni est, vel esse potest. Qui igitur sibi arrogat unde Deum glorificare debuerat, quid aliud quam Dei bonitatem sua gloria spoliat? Qui se attollit supra id quod est, vult falsum credi quod verum est, et 1362Dverum quod falsum est. Qui vero falsitati innititur, veritati Dei reniti convincitur. Ad divinae autem potentiae contumeliam maxime spectat, quidquid improba mens per contumaciam praesumit.

CHAPTER XVI. How the mind is hardened through the vice of shamelessness, and first against God.

O bloody beast, which both by contempt treads down all its inferiors, and with the horns of rebellion assails its superiors! Would that of horns of this kind might be fulfilled that which he who speaks in the psalm profitably threatens: And I will break all the horns of sinners, and I will exalt the horns of the just (Psal. LXXIV). [Vulg. Ps. 74:11 exaltabuntur cornua justi, "the horns of the just shall be exalted"; the received text is passive, not a first-person promise] Let us therefore ask more diligently what sort of horns shamelessness has, 1362Bwith which it is wont to fight back or to assail. But this we know, that horns grow out of the flesh, and harden above the sensibility of the flesh. Now in ten ways, as it seems to me, the shameless soul grows hard, and loses that sensibility of heart which it is fitting to have. For in three ways the mind grows hard toward God, in three toward itself, and in four ways against its neighbour. Toward God, through arrogance, through self-exaltation, through contumacy. Arrogance is when it ascribes to itself that which it has received from God. Self-exaltation is when it lifts itself above that which it has received: through arrogance it glories in itself over what it has, through self-exaltation even over what it has not. Through arrogance and self-exaltation 1362Cit comes about that the divine grace is deservedly withdrawn, and that it is hardened unto contumacy; and being deserted, it is made a transgressor. But there is never a willing transgression of a precept without contempt of God. Contumacy therefore is when it despises God commanding and contemns him threatening. Through arrogance the goodness of God is assailed, through self-exaltation the truth of God, through contumacy the power of God. For certain we know, and without any hesitation, that it is God who worketh all things according to good will (Ephes. I). [Vulg. Eph. 1:11 reads qui operatur omnia secundum consilium voluntatis suae, "who worketh all things according to the counsel of his will"; pro bona voluntate is the phrase of Phil. 2:13] And so from the goodness of God alone is whatever of good is, or can be, in us. He therefore who arrogates to himself that whereby he ought to have glorified God, what else does he do than despoil the goodness of God of its glory? He who lifts himself above that which he is wills that to be believed false which is true, and 1362Dtrue which is false. But he who leans upon falsehood is convicted of striving against the truth of God. But whatever the wicked mind presumes through contumacy looks chiefly to the contumely of the divine power.

CAPUT XVII. Quomodo per impudentiae vitium, mens obdurescit apud semetipsam.

Tribus nihilominus praecipitationis gradibus, solet animus humanus apud semetipsum obrigescere, mentis obligatione, mentis obduratione, mentis effrenatione. Mentis obligatio est, quando assueta eam delectatio ita insolubiliter implicat, ut se a peccati consuetudine implicare, etiam cum velit, non valeat; cum jam omnino peccare non desistit, in quantum eam impunitas sinit. Ecce quia quem arrogantia 1363Aspoliat, extollentia enervat; et quem extollentia enervat, contumacia praecipitat; et quem contumacia ad peccandum praecipitat, peccandi consuetudo postmodum paulatim ligat. Tali animae, taliterque ligatae, clamat vox illa prophetae: Solve vincula colli tui, captiva filia Sion (Isa. LII). Talibus vinculis soluta et libera canere potest cum Psalmista: Dirupisti vincula mea, tibi sacrificabo hostiam laudis (Psal. CXV). Cordis autem obduratio est quando nullius jam poenae timore, nulla vindictae severitate a suis excessibus coerceri potest, qui ejusmodi est. Et, ut apertis utamur exemplis, sciunt flagitiosi qualem damnationem in suis flagitiis deprehensi sustineant vel sustinere debeant, et tamen a flagitiorum perpetratione nec se cohibere valent. Aliud est desidorium 1363Bsuum cohibere non posse, cum impunitas blanditur, atque aliud est illud etiam cavere non posse, quod sine summo periculo non committitur. In superiori igitur gradu animus insolubiliter ligatur ad culpam, in isto velut insensibiliter obduratur ad poenam. In illo pudor, in isto calcatur et timor. Audi quid de his dicatur, qui in hoc gradu ad poenam obdurantur: Percussi, inquit, eos, et non doluerunt (Jer. V). Mentis effrenatio est, quando absque ulla haesitatione, vel retractatione animus se ad omnem praevaricationem exponit, et pudoris chamo, frenoque timoris excusso, praeceps in omnia ruit, et fit de reliquo sicut equus et mulus quibus non est intellectus (Psal. XXXI). In duobus superioribus, animus a suis desideriis, etiam cum reluctatur, 1363Cvincitur. In isto autem gradu penitus non reluctatur, sed, quovis tentationis vento flante, quaquaversum raptatur, secundum quod propheta imprecatur: Deus meus, pone illos ut rotam, et sicut stipulam ante faciem venti (Psal. LXXXII). Sicut autem in tribus primis cornibus Deum impugnat, sic in tribus sequentibus Deo repugnat. Nam obligatione, obduratione, effrenatione sui mens male munita fortiter resistit, ne ullus divini timoris impulsus facile eam humiliare possit.

CHAPTER XVII. How through the vice of shamelessness, the mind grows hard in regard to itself.

By three steps of headlong fall, nonetheless, the human spirit is wont to grow stiff in regard to itself: by the binding of the mind, by the hardening of the mind, by the unbridling of the mind. The binding of the mind is when a habitual delight so insolubly entangles it that it cannot, even when it wills, entangle itself from the custom of sin; when it now does not at all cease from sinning, so far as impunity permits it. See how him whom arrogance 1363Adespoils, self-exaltation enervates; and him whom self-exaltation enervates, contumacy casts headlong; and him whom contumacy casts headlong into sinning, the custom of sinning afterwards little by little binds. To such a soul, and so bound, that voice of the prophet cries: Loose the bonds of thy neck, O captive daughter of Sion (Isa. LII). Loosed from such bonds and free, it can sing with the Psalmist: Thou hast broken my bonds, to thee will I sacrifice the sacrifice of praise (Psal. CXV). But the hardening of the heart is when he that is of this sort can now be restrained from his own excesses by fear of no punishment, by no severity of vengeance. And, that we may use open examples, the shameful know what condemnation they undergo, or ought to undergo, when caught in their shameful deeds, and yet neither are they able to restrain themselves from the perpetration of shameful deeds. It is one thing not to be able to restrain one's own desire desidorium 1363Bwhen impunity allures, and another not to be able to beware even of that which is not committed without the utmost peril. In the former step, therefore, the spirit is insolubly bound to fault; in this one it is hardened, as it were insensibly, against punishment. In that one shame, in this one fear also, is trodden underfoot. Hear what is said of those who in this step are hardened against punishment: I have struck, he says, them, and they grieved not (Jer. V). [Vulg. Jer. 5:3 Percussisti eos, "Thou hast struck them"; the received text addresses God in the second person, and the same verse is printed at 1276C of this work as Percussi filios vestros] The unbridling of the mind is when without any hesitation or drawing back the spirit exposes itself to every transgression, and, the bit of shame and the bridle of fear being shaken off, rushes headlong into all things, and becomes thenceforth as the horse and the mule which have no intellect (Psal. XXXI). In the two former the spirit, by its own desires, even when it resists, 1363Cis overcome. But in this step it does not resist at all, but, whatever wind of temptation blows, is snatched in every direction, according to that which the prophet imprecates: My God, set them as a wheel, and as stubble before the face of the wind (Psal. LXXXII). But as in the three first horns it assails God, so in the three following it fights back against God. For by the binding, the hardening, the unbridling of itself, the ill-fortified mind strongly resists, lest any impulse of divine fear should be able easily to humble it.

CAPUT XVIII. Quomodo per impudentiae vitium, mens obdurescit erga proximum.

Quatuor adhuc restant de cornibus praedictis, cornu 1363Dabjectionis, cornu objectionis, cornu dejectionis, cornu subjectionis. His quatuor gradibus mens improba obduratur adversus proximum suum, et quod pejus est, adversus eos quibus debet humilem famulatum. Nam, sicut in ferreis unguibus conculcat inferiores, sic quatuor his rebellionis cornibus, armatur contra superiores. Impudens siquidem anima, quae jugum timoris semel excussit, improba jam temeritate contra magisterium suum insurgit. Cornu itaque abjectionis rebellat, quando jam dedignatur obedire; cornu objectionis, quando praelatorum suorum conatibus conatur semper et ubique fortiter resistere; cornu dejectionis, quando praelatos suos nititur dejicere; cornu subjectionis, quando jam nititur etiam sibi subjicere. Ecce quomodo malum 1364Agrassatur, ecce quibus gradibus prava anima ad summam impudentiam obduratur, ecce qualiter homo, in id usque producitur, ut nec Deum timeat, nec hominem vereatur (Luc. XVIII).

CHAPTER XVIII. How through the vice of shamelessness, the mind grows hard toward its neighbour.

Four still remain of the aforesaid horns: the horn 1363Dof abjection, the horn of objection, the horn of dejection, the horn of subjection. By these four steps the wicked mind is hardened against its neighbour, and, what is worse, against those to whom it owes a humble service. For as with iron nails it treads down its inferiors, so with these four horns of rebellion it is armed against its superiors. For the shameless soul, which has once shaken off the yoke of fear, rises up now with wicked rashness against its own mastery. And so it rebels with the horn of abjection, when it now disdains to obey; with the horn of objection, when it strives always and everywhere strongly to resist the endeavours of its own prelates; with the horn of dejection, when it strives to cast down its own prelates; with the horn of subjection, when it now strives even to subject them to itself. Behold how the evil 1364Arages abroad; behold by what steps the depraved soul is hardened unto the utmost shamelessness; behold in what manner a man is brought even to this, that he neither fears God nor reverences man (Luc. XVIII). [Vulg. Luc. 18:2 qui Deum non timebat, et hominem non reverebatur, "who feared not God, nor regarded man"]

CAPUT XIX. Quomodo mens cum in profundum malorum venerit, saepe etiam in desperationem cadit.

Sed, depravata anima saepe, cum in profundum malorum venerit post omnia mala, spem etiam resipiscendi amittit, et in desperationem cadit. Hoc est illud cornu novissimum, desperationis videlicet malum; cornu, inquam, et per diffidentiam parvulum, et prae caeteris, per diffidentiae pertinaciam durum. Considerabam, inquit, cornua, et ecce cornu aliud parvulum ortum est de medio eorum, et tria de 1364Bcornibus primis evulsa sunt a facie ejus. Et ecce oculi quasi oculi hominis erant in cornu isto, et os loquens ingentia (Dan. VII). Hoc cornu de medio aliorum oritur, quia cordis obligatione, cordis obduratione, et effrenatione ad desperationem mens maxime praecipitatur. Cum enim ex his tribus coeperit homo sui potestatem amittere, incipit et pariter paulatim de salute desperare. Malum autem desperationis animum non tam tumidum quam timidum reddit, et in eo ipso contumaciae cornu dejicit. Malum desperationis animum solet, non tam attollere quam dejicere, et idcirco extollentiae cornu non potest ante faciem ejus subsistere. Malum desperationis animum facit suis viribus magis derogantem, quia plus semper 1364Ccogit timere quam oportet: unde est quod arrogantiae cornu ante ejus praesentiam subsistere non valet. Anima de sua semel salute desperata, et a gratia divina penitus destituta, cum sentit se inveteratae consuetudini non posse resistere, et a sua se pravitate non posse cohibere, solet saepe seipsam excusabilem ostendere, et culpam suam in Creatorem refundere. Et hoc est, desperationis cornu, oculos quasi hominis et os loquens ingentia habere. Solus homo inter caetera animantia ratione pollet, et prudentia callet. Cum igitur, post desperationis malum, coeperit quisque ad excusationis suae argumentum, quasi rationabiles causas, astute texere, argute praetendere, videtur oculos quasi hominis habere. Oportet, inquiunt, esse, quomodo praedestinatum 1364Dest, quis Dei voluntati, quis ejus ordinationi resistere potest? Quales vero voluit, tales nos fecit. Omnia enim quaecunque voluit fecit, in coelo et in terra, in mari et in omnibus abyssis (Psal. CXXXIV). Quid ergo nos possumus, quid nos sumus? Nunquid merita nostra nobis creare possumus? Non est utique volentis neque currentis sed miserentis Dei (Rom. IX). Cur ergo non miseretur? Cur in nobis, quod sibi placet non operatur, qui operatur omnia in omnibus prout vult? (Ephes. I.) Os loquens ingentia, et tam vesana quam inania. Ut te excuses, Deum accusas; ut te justifices, Deum blasphemas? Nonne hoc esse videtur, quod in sequentibus legitur, et sermones contra Altissimum loquetur. Ecce, quibus gradibus, ad ima descenditur. Ecce unde, 1365Aquo, quomodo, pervenitur de nimia animi levitate ad impudentem animi effrenationem, de multa cordis praesumptione ad ultimam desperationem.

Audivimus quomodo mens de malo semper in pejus corruens, in profundum tandem malorum deducitur. Audiamus quomodo, vel unde eadem ipsa quandoque, post multa et ultima mala, ad rectitudinis integritatem reformatur. Si enim praecedentia et subsequentia hujus visionis recte censemus, puto quia in hac mystica descriptione recognoscimus, quod in Apostolo legimus: Quoniam ubi abundavit 1366Ainiquitas, superabundavit et gratia. Ex hoc Scripturae loco facile advertere est quod nulla nostra malitia divinam misericordiam vincere potest. Quid enim est quod post succedentes sibi bestias, posito throno, antiquus dierum sedit, nisi quod post multa saepe vitiorum detrimenta, ille qui est ante omnia tempora aeternus, per visitantem gratiam, cor lubricum in firma propositi stabilitate figit, et sibi mansionem constituit? Et quid est quod bestiarum potestas aufertur, nisi, quod per visitantem gratiam, vitiorum tyrannide sopita, regnum peccati destruitur?

CHAPTER XIX. How the mind, when it has come into the depth of evils, often falls even into despair.

But the depraved soul often, when it has come into the depth of evils, after all evils loses even the hope of coming to itself again, and falls into despair. This is that last horn, namely the evil of despair; a horn, I say, both little through diffidence, and, beyond the rest, hard through the obstinacy of diffidence. I was considering, he says, the horns, and lo, another little horn sprang out of the midst of them, and three of 1364Bthe first horns were plucked up before its face. And behold, eyes as it were the eyes of a man were in this horn, and a mouth speaking great things (Dan. VII). This horn arises out of the midst of the others, because by the binding of the heart, the hardening of the heart, and unbridling, the mind is chiefly cast headlong into despair. For when out of these three a man has begun to lose power over himself, he begins likewise little by little to despair of salvation. But the evil of despair renders the spirit not so much swollen as timid, and in that very thing casts down the horn of contumacy. The evil of despair is wont not so much to lift the spirit up as to cast it down, and therefore the horn of self-exaltation cannot stand before its face. The evil of despair makes the spirit the more disparaging of its own powers, because it always 1364Ccompels it to fear more than is fitting: whence it is that the horn of arrogance is not able to stand before its presence. The soul that has once despaired of its own salvation, and is utterly forsaken by divine grace, when it feels that it cannot resist an inveterate custom, and cannot restrain itself from its own depravity, is often wont to show itself excusable, and to pour back its own fault upon the Creator. And this is for the horn of despair to have eyes as it were of a man, and a mouth speaking great things. Man alone among the other living things is strong in reason and shrewd in prudence. When therefore, after the evil of despair, anyone has begun cunningly to weave, and shrewdly to put forward, for the proof of his own excuse, causes as it were reasonable, he seems to have eyes as it were of a man. It must be, they say, as it has been predestined; 1364Dwho can resist the will of God, who his ordinance? And such as he willed us, such he made us. For whatsoever he would he hath done, in heaven and in earth, in the sea and in all the deeps (Psal. CXXXIV). [Vulg. Ps. 134:6 Omnia quaecumque voluit Dominus fecit in caelo, in terra, in mari, et in omnibus abyssis: the received text names the Lord as the subject, and does not join the members with et] What then can we do, what are we? Can we create our own merits for ourselves? It is not, assuredly, of him that willeth, nor of him that runneth, but of God that showeth mercy (Rom. IX). Why then does he not show mercy? Why does he not work in us that which pleases him, who worketh all things in all as he wills? (Ephes. I.) [Vulg. Eph. 1:11 reads qui operatur omnia secundum consilium voluntatis suae; the words printed here are those of I Cor. 12:6 and 12:11, qui operatur omnia in omnibus … dividens singulis prout vult] A mouth speaking great things, and things as mad as they are empty. That thou mayest excuse thyself, thou accusest God; that thou mayest justify thyself, thou blasphemest God? Is not this seen to be that which is read in what follows, and he shall speak words against the Most High. [Vulg. Dan. 7:25 sermones contra Excelsum loquetur; Migne prints Altissimum for Excelsum] Behold by what steps one descends to the lowest things. Behold whence, 1365Awhither, and in what manner one comes from an excessive levity of spirit to a shameless unbridling of spirit, from much presumption of heart to the last despair.

We have heard how the mind, falling ever from evil into worse, is at last led down into the depth of evils. Let us hear how, or whence, that same mind is sometimes, after many and last evils, reformed to the integrity of rightness. For if we rightly judge the things that go before and that follow after in this vision, I think that in this mystical description we recognize what we read in the Apostle: Since where 1366Ainiquity abounded, grace also did more abound. [Vulg. Rom. 5:20 Ubi autem abundavit delictum, superabundavit gratia, "where the offence abounded"; the noun printed here is iniquitas, that of Matt. 24:12] From this place of Scripture it is easy to observe that no malice of ours can conquer the divine mercy. For what is it that, after the beasts succeeding one another, a throne being set, the Ancient of days sat, unless that after many and frequent declines of the vices, he who is eternal before all times, through visiting grace, fixes the slippery heart in a firm stability of purpose, and establishes for himself a dwelling? And what is it that the power of the beasts is taken away, unless that through visiting grace, the tyranny of the vices being lulled to sleep, the kingdom of sin is destroyed?

On this text

Cruces for this work — where the plate is defective or the reading uncertain →

Richard reads Nebuchadnezzar's dreams as a map of the inner life. The statue of gold, silver, bronze, iron and clay (Daniel 2) becomes the stages by which a man of virtue declines — each metal baser than the one above it, and the whole standing on feet that crumble; the stone cut without hands that shatters it is the fear of God, and the mountain it becomes is the kingdom that is not destroyed. The second book turns to the great tree of Daniel 4, felled but with the shoot of its roots left in the earth, and the third to the four beasts of Daniel 7, each read as a vice: the lioness of insolence, the bear of enviousness, the leopard of fraudulence, and the fourth beast, shamelessness, with its ten horns of hardening. Throughout, the argument is that the fall is gradual and the repair is gradual, and that a man who has fallen and been instructed rises stronger than he stood: quanto eruditior quantoque humilior, tanto et fortior resurgit. The text breaks off unfinished. Its last chapter announces the restoration half of the argument, quotes Romans 5:20 as its programme, asks two of its questions on Daniel 7:9–12, and stops at column 1366 — never reaching the Son of man, and never reaching the kingdom that shall not be destroyed, which is the figure the whole treatise is built toward from Book I. The last sentence Migne prints is complete; the argument is not.

Du bon, à bon marché — carried to its limit: the good, free.
